# Survey of discovery mechanisms

This is the record of a survey run on 2026-11-08, after the maintainer said he was not tied to Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) and asked whether something better could be very widely adopted. The rulings and the design that followed are in [the README](../../README.md#rulings) and [the design](../design.md).

**The question put to every family.** For a definition identified by a digest (a SAID), can a stranger holding only the digest find *who else declares it*, without anyone operating an index or site built for the purpose? Generic shared infrastructure that many independent parties already operate was acceptable. The maintainer's values, in order: no purpose-built aggregator; very wide adoption; local-first discovery from a single introduction; identity that can move to KERI; nothing for a solo maintainer to run.

**Method.** One researcher per family worked from current primary sources with web access. A skeptic per family then re-opened the sources, walked the reverse lookup as a stranger would have to, and re-scored. All were read-only: nothing was published to any network, so no write path was tested end to end. The text below is the agents' own, unedited apart from formatting. Where the skeptic corrected a claim, trust the correction. Figures are as of 2026-11-08 or 2026-11-09 UTC. A few researchers cite copies of sources saved under a session-temporary scratch folder; those copies are gone, and the URLs beside them are the durable reference.

**Conclusion.** No family answers the question with a lookup that is keyed by the digest, run by many independent parties, and widely adopted. Each ends at somebody's index. The two that come closest, IPFS's distributed hash table and Nostr relays, fail on durability and adoption.

## Scores

Scores run from 0 to 5. *Adoption today* and *adoption trajectory* are about real use. *No aggregator*: 5 means the reverse lookup needs nothing purpose-built. *Reverse lookup*: 5 means native, complete and cheap. *Publisher effort*: 5 means trivial. *KERI fit*: how naturally self-held keys and SAIDs ride on it. Each cell shows the researcher's score, then the skeptic's.

<a id="agent-ecosystem"></a>

## Agent-ecosystem standards (AI Catalog, ARD, MCP, A2A, AGNTCY)

**Researcher's summary.** No mechanism in the agent-ecosystem family answers "who else declares digest H" natively and completely; every one is either a per-domain file with no query, or a search service that somebody must crawl and index. The family has in practice converged on one publishing format, the Linux Foundation AI Catalog (/.well-known/ai-catalog.json): its steering committee holds seats for Google, Microsoft, Anthropic and PulseMCP, nominated by the MCP maintainers and the A2A TSC, and MCP's Server Cards extension (SEP-2127, Final, merged 2026-10-06) tells clients doing domain-level discovery to fetch it. ARD (v0.91, status Proposal, 2026-08-26; authors from Google and Hugging Face plus R.V. Guha) is the search layer on top, and its architecture is crawl-and-index by design: "Web Ingestion (Required)" by registries exposing POST /search, so the draft spec's Lambda plus DynamoDB index was in effect a private ARD registry. ARD is a risky bet, neither sound nor overtaken: GitHub Copilot's Agent Finder and Hugging Face Discover shipped on 2026-06-17 and Chrome Lighthouse audits for it, but Anthropic and OpenAI are absent, and v0.91 renamed the path to ard.json while MCP standardised on ai-catalog.json. Measured uptake is small: in my own probe of 65 hand-picked domains on 2026-11-08, 9 served a valid ai-catalog.json (GitHub, Hugging Face, Vercel, Notion, Cloudflare docs, Supabase, Zapier, Neon, Buildkite) and 3 a valid ard.json; third-party censuses put the worldwide total at a few hundred manifests. The AI Catalog data model fits the job better than the draft assumed: free-form identifier and type, an entry digest, and an array of independently signed Trust Manifests whose signed subject carries the digest ("A signed manifest MAY endorse only the artifact release identified by that subject"), with signer profiles that are extensible, so a KERI profile can be added later without breaking the did:web one. Published catalogs already list non-agent resources with ordinary media types (OpenAPI, text/markdown), and both specs accept any type string. Reverse lookup through existing generic registries is partial at best: on 2026-11-08 the Hugging Face reference registry returned only its own Hub resources, rejected a namespaced filter (422) and answered 501 on /explore, while the independent Neuronto registry (180,538 entries) enforced an exact tags filter but only together with matching text, and silently ignored a namespaced filter. The only native digest-keyed provider lookup in the family is AGNTCY's libp2p DHT, but it is limited to OASF agent records with sha256 CIDs, needs a Go node federated with Cisco Outshift's network, and has no published deployment numbers. MCP Registry and A2A Agent Cards are the widely adopted pieces (88.4M and 6.2M weekly npm downloads of their TypeScript SDKs) but neither can carry a protocol definition or answer a reverse query; llms.txt is widely served and almost never read; ANS, NANDA, NLWeb and agents.json are marginal for this job; the IETF DAWN working group is chartered but explicitly excludes Internet-wide indexing and identity. The safer horse is therefore the AI Catalog document, served identically at both well-known paths so that MCP clients, ARD registries and Lighthouse all read it. Copies of the primary sources I read are under /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-seriouscoderone-code-bpm/0df03f74-64d8-4f0b-9040-da278e74b62c/scratchpad/dl/ (ard/spec_ard.md, aic/specification_ai-catalog.md, mcp/sep2127.md, mcp/discovery.md, iana/well-known.csv, probe/).

**Researcher's best pick.** AI Catalog as the publication and crawl substrate, served as one document at both /.well-known/ai-catalog.json and /.well-known/ard.json, with ARD registries used only as an optional source of hints. For the crux itself, a native digest-keyed reverse lookup that nobody has to index, the honest answer for this family is none.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** This does not deliver the thing ranked first. The reverse lookup is either a partial walk of whoever linked to whom, or a query to someone else's crawler index, which is the aggregator the maintainer wanted to avoid with a different owner. The registries that exist today either index only their own or curated content (Hugging Face, GitHub) or are small independents (Neuronto, allagents.app); none is obliged to index a custom media type or a said: tag, the reference registry rejects namespaced filters, and exact-match lookup is a workaround on a ranked semantic search that requires text. The base is also thin and moving: a few hundred publishing domains worldwide, ARD at v0.91 Proposal, AI Catalog untagged with its trust design still under an open ADR, a live fork between ard.json and ai-catalog.json, no IANA registration for either path, and no evidence that Claude or ChatGPT read either file. Finally the identity model is domain-anchored end to end (urn:air plus did:web plus ES256 JWS) and the digest field means sha256 of bytes, so both KERI AIDs and Blake3 SAIDs ride as passengers in tags and private profiles. A mechanism with a native tag-keyed or digest-keyed query that many independent nodes already answer would satisfy the first value where this family cannot, and that lies outside the agent ecosystem's standards.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** The report is factually reliable: of the weight-bearing claims I re-opened, almost every figure matched the primary source to the digit, and its central admission, that nothing here answers 'who else declares H' without an index, is correct. Its errors are of optimism: 'converged' overstates a pre-release format whose adoption vote is still pending, the adoption and no-aggregator scores run 0.5 to 1.5 points high, the Trust Manifest and peer-link features it builds on have no observed use, and it missed ERC-8004, UCP, the Agent Skills index and RFC 9727. For the job as the maintainer ranked it, trust this family for a cheap secondary advertisement only: emitting one static catalog entry per definition costs little and may be picked up by a few registries. Do not trust it for the reverse lookup, for identity, or for 'very widely adopted'; roughly 200 to 250 domains publish these files and no major assistant is shown to read them. Building mad-discovery on it means either running the index the maintainer wanted to avoid or accepting an answer limited to whoever already links to whom. My registry checks were eleven read-only search requests using a made-up digest string, and Neuronto states it logs query text for 90 days.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

No. For the best pick (AI Catalog entries, served at both well-known paths, with ARD registries as hints), a reverse lookup with nothing purpose-built does not work; somebody must run an index.

Walking it as a stranger who holds only the 44-character digest H:

1. Entry point. Nothing in this family maps a digest to anything. AI Catalog has no query, and there is no registry of registries (registries are found through application/ai-registry+json entries in catalogs you already hold; Neuronto counts 18 such entries). The stranger needs a seed domain or a registry address from outside.

2. With a seed domain D. GET https://D/.well-known/ai-catalog.json, answered by D's own static host. It is free, accepts any type string and tag, and lasts as long as D keeps its domain. It yields D's own declaration about H and nothing else.

3. Walking outward. Follow D's nested-catalog entries to other domains, each answered by that domain's static host. Links point forward only, so the walk reaches parties D already knew. A party that began relying on H later is invisible unless it told someone in the graph, and no such notice exists in the spec. The spec recommends a depth limit of 4, and none of the nine live catalogs I fetched links to another domain. The walker itself (match the tag, check the sha256 digest, recompute the SAID, verify each signature) is MAD-specific code, though it can be a CLI with nothing hosted.

4. Asking registries. Hugging Face Discover (run by Hugging Face) indexes only its Hub, rejects namespaced queries with 422 and returns 501 on /explore. GitHub Agent Finder (run by GitHub) reads a curated catalog or your own private registry. Google's and AWS's registries are per-customer, and Google's ARD support is not in its release notes as of 2026-11-06. Neuronto (operator not named, no stated funding or uptime) is open, takes domain submissions and indexes arbitrary media types and tags. There an exact match works only as text equal to H plus a tags filter, unknown filter keys are silently dropped so every hit must be re-verified, and queries are logged for 90 days.

So today one hobby-scale index could answer, and only for domains submitted to it or reached by its crawl. A complete answer needs either that third party to keep running and to ingest every declarer, or the maintainer's own crawler and index, which is the draft's Lambda and DynamoDB again under an ARD-shaped API.

The one way to avoid an index inside this format is to make the definition's author the rendezvous: each adopter sends its signed Trust Manifest to the author, who appends it to the author's own entry for H, so the lookup becomes a forward fetch. That needs three things nobody has built: a way to get from H to the author's domain, a submission channel to the author, and an author who stays online and cooperative. It also makes each author a small aggregator for its own definitions.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

This family supplies a file format and a set of search services, and neither is a lookup. Every member is either a per-domain document with no query or an index somebody crawls, so the maintainer's first value is unmet by construction, and the stated objective (nobody needs a protocol aggregation site) fails the moment enumeration or reverse lookup has to be complete.

The 'generic shared infrastructure' on offer is one unnamed operator's registry plus vendor registries scoped to their own content; measured cross-vendor federation was zero in July 2026.

What adoption buys is small: roughly 200 to 250 publishing domains worldwide, no evidence that Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor or VS Code read either file, and a path fork that the same Google author sits on both sides of. The features that make AI Catalog look fit for MAD (entry digest, Trust Manifests, cross-domain nesting) have no observed use and rest on an ADR still marked Proposed. MAD would be the first implementer and would need its own crawler, which makes it a private protocol under a standard's file name.

Identity and hashing run against the project's direction at every layer: urn:air and did:web bind to DNS, signatures must be ES256 JWS for any generic consumer, and digest means sha256 over served bytes. KERI AIDs and Blake3 SAIDs would travel in tags and private profiles that nothing else verifies.

The objection is to building on this family, not to emitting into it: one extra static catalog entry per definition is cheap and harmless as a secondary advertisement.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (agent-ecosystem)

1. Each party serves static files only (S3 plus CloudFront or GitHub Pages; no compute): the definition bytes at https://&lt;domain&gt;/mad/&lt;SAID&gt;.json, a Markdown reference beside it, and ONE catalog document served byte-identically at /.well-known/ai-catalog.json and /.well-known/ard.json (Buildkite and Supabase already do this), advertised with Link rel=ai-catalog and rel=ard and an Agentmap line in robots.txt.

2. One entry per declared definition, valid under both specs: identifier urn:air:&lt;own-domain&gt;:mad:&lt;SAID&gt;; type application/vnd.mad.definition+json; url; displayName; a description that contains the SAID; tags [mad-definition, said:&lt;SAID&gt;]; 2 to 5 representativeQueries; digest sha256:&lt;hex of the served bytes&gt;; and one trustManifests item {contributor: did:web:&lt;own-domain&gt;, subject: {identifier, type, digest}, extensions: {&lt;reverse-DNS key&gt;: {said, role: issues | relies-on | implements | endorses}}, signature: did-web-v1 ES256 JWS over JCS}. The join key across publishers is the said:&lt;SAID&gt; tag, since urn:air identifiers are per-domain.

3. Peer links: for each counterparty it knows, an entry of type application/ai-catalog+json pointing at that party's catalog. This is the thing reviewers said was missing: something a crawler can follow from one publisher to the next.

4. mad-discovery is a TypeScript library and CLI, no service. who(H, seeds) does a breadth-first walk of catalogs from the seed domain (depth cap and visited set as the spec recommends), keeps entries tagged said:H, fetches each url, checks the sha256 digest, recomputes the SAID, verifies each Trust Manifest, and returns the set of (contributor, role, domain).

5. Optional wider net: for each application/ai-registry+json entry met on the walk, plus a short configurable list (Hugging Face Discover, Neuronto, GitHub Agent Finder, later Google and AWS), POST /search {query: {text: "&lt;SAID&gt; MAD definition", filter: {tags: ["said:&lt;SAID&gt;"]}}, federation: "auto"}. Hits are treated only as names of more domains to fetch and verify. Enumeration is the same call filtered by type.

6. KERI later, additively: a second Trust Manifest per entry under a MAD-defined profile URI whose signer is a did:webs or did:keri AID, with the JWS key checked against the KEL, or an attestations[] item pointing at an ACDC. The did:web manifest stays for generic consumers.

What the maintainer still runs: nothing. The price is that the answer is complete only over the peer-link graph, plus whatever third-party registries happen to have indexed.

### Mechanisms (agent-ecosystem)

#### AI Catalog (ai-catalog.io, Linux Foundation Agent Card Working Group)

A typed, nestable JSON container (application/ai-catalog+json) listing AI artifacts. Entry fields: identifier (open text, any URI or URN), type (open string, media type recommended), exactly one of url or data, and optional displayName, description, tags, version, updatedAt, extensions, publisher, digest, trustManifests[]. It is a publishing format only; the spec says dynamic discovery is out of scope.

- **Publish.** The party hosts a static JSON file, by convention at /.well-known/ai-catalog.json (optional; any URL is conformant; also advertised with Link rel=ai-catalog). To declare definition H it adds an entry pointing at the bytes (url) or inlining them (data), with digest, and a Trust Manifest {contributor: &lt;absolute URI&gt;, subject: {identifier, type, digest}, signature: {signer, profile: did-web-v1, issuedAt, jws}}. A third party endorses the same release by adding its own manifest; manifests are independent and travel separately from the entry. There is no core field for the role (issue / rely on / implement / endorse); it goes in the manifest's extensions map under a reverse-DNS or URL key, which the manifest signature covers. Cost: static hosting.
- **Fetch by digest.** No hash-keyed fetch. A client fetches entry.url (or reads entry.data), computes the digest named by entry.digest (over the exact bytes, or over the JCS-canonicalised data value) and compares. The signed subject omits the URL, so mirrors serving identical bytes are allowed. SAID self-verification is a separate MAD-side step.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not native. Steps: (1) collect catalogs, either by crawling outward from known domains through nested entries of type application/ai-catalog+json (the spec recommends a depth limit of 4 and a visited set) or from someone's index; (2) keep entries whose identifier, digest or tag equals H; (3) the declarers are the catalog hosts and the trustManifests[].contributor values; (4) verify each signature and the subject-to-entry binding. A global answer needs an index: an ARD registry, or the informative OCI binding where Trust Manifests are OCI referrers of the entry manifest, which only works inside one shared repository.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None built in. A new publisher is found only if an already-known catalog links to it with a nested catalog entry, if a registry crawls its domain's well-known path, or if it is submitted to a registry.
- **Identity.** Layer 0 is HTTPS on the serving domain. The one interoperable signer profile is did-web-v1: a root did:web DID with an ES256 key, detached JWS over the JCS-canonicalised manifest; the Publisher Profile additionally requires the signer to equal did:web:&lt;publisher segment of the urn:air identifier&gt;. The spec allows independently defined profiles named by an absolute URI, and an entry may carry several manifests from the same contributor under different profiles, so a KERI profile (signer a did:webs or did:keri AID, key state from the KEL) or an attestations[] pointer to an ACDC is additive.
- **Digest fit.** identifier is free-form, so the 44-character SAID can be the identifier or a segment of it, and can also sit in tags and extensions. The digest field has its own format, algorithm:hex-value in lowercase hex, with SHA-256 expected (Consumers MUST reject algorithms shorter than SHA-256). Two mismatches: generic verifiers will not implement Blake3, and digest covers the exact served bytes whereas a SAID is computed over the serialization with the SAID field holding placeholders, so the two values differ even with the same hash. Practical answer: digest = sha256 of the bytes for generic consumers, SAID carried alongside. If the raw Blake3 bytes are wanted in this format: replace the leading E of the SAID with A, base64url-decode to 33 bytes, drop the first byte, hex-encode the remaining 32 as blake3:&lt;64 hex&gt;.
- **Governance.** Linux Foundation project with a four-seat TSC drawn from the MCP and A2A communities; the README says the A2A and MCP steering committees will vote on adopting it once final. No version label or tag on the spec; a 0.9 release candidate was being targeted in October 2026 per a secondary source. The Trust Manifest and did:web text is on main but ADR-0027 is still marked Proposed (2026-09-04), so that part can still change.
- **Publisher cost.** One static JSON file plus the definition bytes; a did.json and an ES256 key if signing. No account, no registration.
- **TypeScript tooling.** No official TypeScript SDK (official: Go, Rust; community: C#, Python). The npm package ai-catalog 0.1.2 is a community package with 13 weekly downloads. The format is plain JSON; signing needs only general-purpose maintained libraries for JCS and JWS (for example canonicalize and jose).
- **Failure modes.** Unsigned entries are only as trustworthy as the domain; did:web inherits DNS and TLS trust. A declaration disappears when its publisher goes offline unless someone mirrored the signed manifest. No revocation or freshness mechanism beyond optional expiresAt. Catalog poisoning and manifest substitution are discussed in the spec and only mitigated by signatures. Pre-1.0 spec; path fork with ARD (ard.json); fetching a catalog reveals interest to the publisher.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Publishing and a local crawl from one introduction need nothing. A complete answer to who else declares H needs somebody's crawler and index, because the format defines no query.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Technical Steering Committee seats: Google (Junjie Bu), Microsoft (Darrel Miller), Anthropic (David Soria Parra), PulseMCP (Tadas Antanavicius); two nominated by the MCP Core Maintainers and two by the Linux Foundation A2A TSC. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/Agent-Card/ai-catalog/blob/main/GOVERNANCE.md
  - MCP SEP-2127 header reads 'Status: Final' (merged 2026-10-06) and says: 'For domain-level discovery, an AI Catalog can link to or embed the relevant Server Cards.' (as of 2026-10-06). Source: https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/blob/main/seps/2127-mcp-server-cards.md
  - MCP Server Card extension: 'Clients performing domain-level discovery SHOULD attempt to retrieve this well-known URL.' (/.well-known/ai-catalog.json) (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/ext-server-card/blob/main/docs/discovery.md
  - Own probe of 65 hand-picked domains: 9 serve valid AI Catalog JSON at /.well-known/ai-catalog.json (github.com, huggingface.co, vercel.com, notion.so, developers.cloudflare.com, supabase.com, zapier.com, neon.tech, buildkite.com). Several list non-agent resources with types such as application/openapi+json and text/markdown. google.com, microsoft.com, anthropic.com, openai.com returned 404. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/.well-known/ai-catalog.json
  - Neuronto tutorial: of 178 publishers crawled, 157 serve ai-catalog.json and 14 serve ard.json. (as of 2026-08-31). Source: https://neuronto.com/blog/publish-your-agent-to-ard-in-10-minutes
  - Independent probe of 50 domains: 31 serve a manifest; 'All 31 publishers serve ai-catalog.json'; six also serve ard.json. (as of 2026-09-04). Source: https://agenticresources.dev/
  - ai-catalog.json is not in the IANA Well-Known URIs registry (107 entries read); the spec only contains a registration request. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris-1.csv
  - Repository: 241 stars, 40 forks, created 2025-10-29, last push 2026-11-08, no release tags. Official SDKs are Go and Rust only. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/Agent-Card/ai-catalog
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Adoption today down from 2.5: two censuses put publishers at roughly 200 to 250 worldwide, no mainstream client is shown to read the file, and the digest and Trust Manifest features have no observed use. Trajectory down from 4: the backing is real (four-company committee, a Final MCP extension) but the repository is still 'temporary', the adoption vote is pending, there is no tag or IANA entry, and ARD and Lighthouse steer publishers to a different path. No-aggregator down from 2 and reverse lookup down from 1.5: the format has no query, and a walk over forward links cannot find a declarer nobody links to. Publisher effort down from 4.5: the version that does the job needs did:web, ES256, JCS and detached JWS with no TypeScript SDK. KERI fit down from 3: profiles are extensible, but signatures must be JWS, the publisher profile is bound to did:web and urn:air domains, and digest means sha256 of bytes.

#### Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD, agenticresourcediscovery.org)

A discovery and search layer over catalog entries: a manifest at /.well-known/ard.json plus registries that crawl manifests and expose POST /search (required), POST /explore and GET /agents (optional), with federation modes auto, referrals and none. An ARD entry is a JSON-LD node: the AI Catalog core plus flat namespaced extensions via @context. The spec calls AI Catalog its predecessor format and couples to it only loosely (ADR-0010, 2026-07-09).

- **Publish.** The party hosts /.well-known/ard.json containing an entries array, and may also emit link rel=ard, an Agentmap line in robots.txt, in-page JSON-LD or DNS SVCB records. Each entry MUST carry identifier (schema pattern ^urn:air:[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+(:[a-zA-Z0-9._-]+)+$, publisher segment an FQDN), displayName, type and exactly one of url or data; it SHOULD carry 2 to 5 representativeQueries, without which the spec says it cannot be found by search. A declaration about H would be an entry of a custom media type with H in the identifier's last segment, in tags, and in a namespaced term. Cost: static hosting.
- **Fetch by digest.** None. ARD has no entry-level digest; the only digest is on attestation items and covers the attestation document. The spec also says retrieving a complete entry by identifier is out of scope for this draft. Bytes come from entry.url on the publisher's domain.
- **Reverse lookup.** Only through a registry that has crawled the publishers. Query: POST /search with query.text (required) and query.filter on a term holding H, or POST /explore with a filter and a facet on publisher. The spec says a registry that indexes a term makes it filterable, that support for extension terms is registry-defined, and that a registry MAY reject an unsupported filter with 400. Tested read-only on 2026-11-08. Hugging Face Discover: search works but every result is a Hub resource; @context in the query returns 422; /explore returns 501; /agents returns 404. Neuronto (independent): an exact tags filter is enforced, but only ANDed with text relevance, so unrelated text plus a matching tag returns nothing; a namespaced filter was silently ignored and unrelated results came back; /explore ignored the tags filter. So an exact, complete digest lookup is not available from registries that exist today.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A registry must crawl the new domain. The spec requires web ingestion from 'discovered URIs' but does not say how domains are discovered; in practice the publisher submits the domain to a registry, is linked from an indexed catalog, or is reached through a referral.
- **Identity.** Optional trustManifest with a required identity (SPIFFE ID, DID or HTTPS FQDN) whose trust domain MUST align with the publisher domain in the URN. ARD defines no signing procedure and defers to the declared trust framework. A DID is allowed, so a did:webs AID bound to the domain could sit there, but the model is domain-anchored by construction.
- **Digest fit.** The 44-character SAID fits the URN segment pattern (base64url characters are all allowed) and fits tags, capabilities or a namespaced term. No hash function is imposed because there is no content digest at all.
- **Governance.** Apache-2.0 repository under the ards-project GitHub organisation with three leads; normative changes need an issue and a maintainer lands them. Acknowledgements name people at AWS, Cisco, Databricks, GitHub, GoDaddy, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia, Salesforce and Snowflake; Anthropic and OpenAI appear nowhere in the spec. Status is Proposal; media types and the ard.json well-known path are not IANA-registered. v0.91 diverged from AI Catalog on path (ard.json), identifier strictness (urn:air required) and trust shape (singular trustManifest with identity versus an array of contributor manifests).
- **Publisher cost.** One static JSON file. Being indexed may require submitting the domain to each registry.
- **TypeScript tooling.** JSON Schema, CDDL and an OpenAPI 3.1 file in the repo (a TypeScript client can be generated; the spec suggests ajv-cli for validation). The conformance tool is a Python script. Community npm packages are tiny (pi-ard 66 and ard-explorer 12 weekly downloads); ard-publish is Python.
- **Failure modes.** Coverage and term indexing are at each registry's discretion, so results are incomplete and differ between registries. Search is semantic and ranked; text is mandatory, so an exact lookup is a workaround. Spam: anyone can publish a manifest; the squatting defence depends on registries verifying trust manifests. Registries see and may log queries (Neuronto states it logs query text for 90 days). Centralisation risk if one or two large registries dominate. Spec churn: path rename within three months of launch.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. The architecture is aggregators by design, but generic ones operated by several parties. Today the large ones index their own or curated content (Hugging Face Hub, GitHub's curated catalog) and the ones crawling the open web are small independents, so completeness for MAD definitions would in practice mean running a registry, which is the draft's index again.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Spec header: 'Version: v0.91', 'Status: Proposal', 'Date: August 26, 2026'; authors Junjie Bu (Google), R.V.Guha, Shaun Smith (Hugging Face). (as of 2026-08-26). Source: https://github.com/ards-project/ard-spec/blob/main/spec/ard.md
  - Repository created 2026-05-19; 499 stars, 74 forks; CODEOWNERS names three project leads (@rvguha @mindpower @evalstate); no releases. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/ards-project/ard-spec
  - GitHub: 'Agent finder implements the open Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) specification.' Available on all GitHub Copilot plans; points at GitHub's curated public catalog or a private registry. (as of 2026-06-17). Source: https://github.blog/changelog/2026-06-17-agent-finder-for-github-copilot-now-available/
  - Hugging Face: the Discover Tool 'is our reference implementation of ARD'; its catalog is published at huggingface.co/.well-known/ai-catalog.json. (as of 2026-06-17). Source: https://huggingface.co/blog/agentic-resource-discovery-launch
  - Google: 'Native support for ARD will be available in Agent Platform in the coming months.' The post thanks the AI Catalog Working Group under the Linux Foundation. (as of 2026-06-17). Source: https://developers.googleblog.com/en/announcing-the-agentic-resource-discovery-specification/
  - Chrome Lighthouse has an ARD audit that fetches /.well-known/ard.json and warns when only the legacy ai-catalog.json path is found. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://developer.chrome.com/docs/lighthouse/agentic-browsing/ard
  - Scan of 102 sites on 2026-09-13: '24 on ard.json, 78 only on ai-catalog.json.' (as of 2026-09-13). Source: https://www.webfx.com/blog/ai/agentic-resource-discovery/
  - Of the 11 launch backers, 'Only Hugging Face serves a manifest at its root domain'; 'The spec's own domain serves none.' (as of 2026-09-04). Source: https://agenticresources.dev/
  - Own probe of 65 domains: 3 serve valid JSON at /.well-known/ard.json (buildkite.com, developers.cloudflare.com, supabase.com). github.com returned 404, huggingface.co 401, agenticresourcediscovery.org 404. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://buildkite.com/.well-known/ard.json
  - Neuronto registry GET /agents reports totalSize 180538; its type facet shows 161,833 MCP server cards and also 2,696 application/vnd.oai.openapi+json and 322 text/markdown entries, so arbitrary media types are indexed. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://neuronto.com/agents?pageSize=1
  - ADR-0010: 'we will only have a loose coupling between ARD entries and `ai-catalog` entries.' (as of 2026-07-09). Source: https://github.com/ards-project/ard-spec/blob/main/adr/0010-ard-and-ai-catalog-loose-coupling.md
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Adoption today down from 2: about 25 domains serve ard.json, the spec's own site serves none, and the large registries index only their own or curated content. Trajectory down from 3.5: still a Proposal with no release or foundation home, zero cross-vendor federation measured in July, and Google's promised native support is absent from its release notes five months on. No-aggregator down from 2.5: the design requires registries to crawl, and the 'several generic operators' reduce in practice to one unnamed independent. Reverse lookup down from 2: one registry can answer, only for domains it has ingested, only as a text-plus-tag workaround, and it silently drops unknown filters. Publisher effort down from 4.5 for the strict urn:air identifier, representative queries and per-registry submission. KERI fit down from 2: identity must align with the publisher's domain and there is no content digest or signing procedure.

#### MCP Registry (server.json) and MCP Server Cards

registry.modelcontextprotocol.io is the official central metadata registry of MCP servers (server.json: name, description, version, packages, remotes). Server Cards (SEP-2127, Final) are static per-server documents for remote servers, located through an AI Catalog entry of type application/mcp-server-card+json. Earlier well-known proposals (SEP-1649 at .well-known/mcp.json, SEP-1960 at .well-known/mcp) were closed in favour of this.

- **Publish.** POST /v0/publish to the registry after proving a namespace through GitHub OAuth or OIDC, a DNS signature or an HTTP signature. Only MCP servers can be published; there is no way to register a protocol definition, short of wrapping it in an MCP server and using _meta, which nothing can query.
- **Fetch by digest.** None. packages[].fileSha256 is an integrity hash for package files only.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not possible. GET /v0/servers offers search described as 'Search servers by name (substring match)', plus updated_since and version filters. No query by hash, tag or metadata.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Publishing to the one registry makes a server findable to every client and downstream registry that syncs it.
- **Identity.** Reverse-DNS or GitHub namespace ownership checked at publish time; the registry issues a JWT. No signed endorsements by third parties.
- **Digest fit.** No field for an arbitrary digest apart from free-form _meta.
- **Governance.** MCP project under the Agentic AI Foundation (Linux Foundation). Specification releases 2025-11-25 and 2026-07-28. The registry API is at v0 and v0.1.
- **Publisher cost.** Free, but requires a real MCP server and namespace verification.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @modelcontextprotocol/sdk (official, heavily used); the Server Card schema source is TypeScript.
- **Failure modes.** Central chokepoint run by the MCP project; scope limited to MCP servers; name squatting within verified namespaces only.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. The registry is shared infrastructure but accepts only MCP servers and cannot be queried by digest, so definitions would still need their own index.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Registry OpenAPI, search parameter: 'Search servers by name (substring match); nonempty searches require at least 3 characters'. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.modelcontextprotocol.io/openapi.yaml
  - Third-party counts of latest-version listings: 52,042 on 2026-10-28 and 57,041 on 2026-11-05 (not official figures). My own pagination read the first 1,500 listings before a timeout: 1,483 active, 1,358 with remotes. (as of 2026-10-28). Source: https://eseospace.com/blog/mcp-statistics/
  - @modelcontextprotocol/sdk: 88,418,428 npm downloads in the week 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-07. (as of 2026-11-07). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/@modelcontextprotocol/sdk
  - SEP-2127 MCP Server Cards merged 2026-10-06 with Status Final on the Extensions Track; extension repository modelcontextprotocol/ext-server-card (TypeScript schema). (as of 2026-10-06). Source: https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/pull/2127
  - MCP is one of ten projects hosted by the Agentic AI Foundation, alongside A2A, AGENTS.md, goose and Agent Skills. (as of 2026-10-22). Source: https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/agentic-ai-foundation-announces-four-new-projects-agent-skills-open-responses-openenv-and-opensandbox
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Adoption today down from 5 and trajectory from 5: the 88.4M weekly downloads measure the protocol SDK, not discovery; the registry size is a third-party figure and Server Cards are one month old with no client evidenced. Publisher effort down from 3: a definition would have to be wrapped in a real MCP server with a verified namespace. Other scores unchanged; the name-substring-only search is confirmed.

#### A2A Agent Card

A JSON self-description of an A2A agent (identity, endpoints, skills, auth), served at /.well-known/agent-card.json. Version 1.0 added signed Agent Cards (JWS).

- **Publish.** The agent operator serves the card on its own domain. A card describes one agent; a declaration about a protocol definition could only ride in an extension field that nothing indexes.
- **Fetch by digest.** None.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not possible. The A2A discovery guide says: 'The current A2A specification does not prescribe a standard API for curated registries.'
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** The client must already know the domain, or use a registry outside the spec.
- **Identity.** Domain over HTTPS, optionally a JWS signature on the card. A KERI signature could be an extension but no consumer would check it.
- **Digest fit.** No digest field; arbitrary strings only in extensions.
- **Governance.** Linux Foundation project, listed among Agentic AI Foundation projects in the 2026-10-22 press release; its TSC nominates two AI Catalog TSC seats.
- **Publisher cost.** One static JSON file, but only meaningful if you run an A2A agent.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @a2a-js/sdk 1.3.0 (official).
- **Failure modes.** Wrong object for the job; widely used inside platforms but rarely published at public well-known paths.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Per-domain file with no query interface and no registry API.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - IANA Well-Known URIs: agent-card.json, status permanent, change controller Linux Foundation, registered 2025-08-01. It is the only entry of this family in the registry besides RFC 9727 api-catalog. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris-1.csv
  - Linux Foundation: 'more than 150 organizations supporting the standard'; integrated into Azure AI Foundry, Copilot Studio and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Runtime; repository past 22,000 stars. (as of 2026-04-09). Source: https://linuxfoundation.org/press/a2a-protocol-surpasses-150-organizations-lands-in-major-cloud-platforms-and-sees-enterprise-production-use-in-first-year
  - Releases v1.0.0 on 2026-03-12 and v1.0.1 on 2026-05-28; @a2a-js/sdk had 6,216,246 npm downloads in the week to 2026-11-07. (as of 2026-11-07). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/@a2a-js/sdk
  - Own probe: 1 of 65 domains served an Agent Card JSON at the root well-known path (modelcontextprotocol.io). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://modelcontextprotocol.io/.well-known/agent-card.json
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Adoption today down from 4: the protocol is widely used inside platforms (6.2M weekly SDK downloads, IANA permanent registration) but cards are rarely published at public well-known paths, which is what matters for open discovery. Publisher effort down from 3 because a card is only meaningful with a running agent. KERI fit left at 2; I did not re-check the signed-card text.

#### AGNTCY Agent Directory Service (ADS) and OASF

A peer-to-peer directory of OASF records (agents, MCP servers, skills). Records are stored as OCI artifacts and addressed by a CID derived from the SHA-256 OCI digest; nodes announce CIDs and skill labels on a libp2p Kad-DHT. Since v1.5 it also presents records as AI Catalog entries and calls itself a reference implementation of ARD.

- **Publish.** Run or connect to a directory node (Go server, Zot or GHCR as the store), push a record that validates against the OASF schema to get its CID, then run routing publish so the node announces the CID and its labels on the DHT; announcements have a TTL and must be repeated. Cross-organisation discovery means joining the federation at ads.outshift.io, which involves a SPIRE trust-bundle exchange.
- **Fetch by digest.** dirctl pull &lt;cid&gt;, or name@cid with a hash check, over OCI distribution from the node or registry that holds the record.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native for one narrow case: the DHT maps skill labels to CIDs and CIDs to provider peers, so nodes on the network can answer which peers hold record CID X. It is keyed by the CID of an OASF record, not by an arbitrary digest; structured routing search filters are skill, domain, module and locator only; results are a cached, possibly stale view.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A bootstrap peer puts a node on the DHT; announced records are then findable by label by other federated nodes.
- **Identity.** Cosign-compatible signatures attached as OCI referrers (Sigstore keyless OIDC, self-managed or KMS keys); ownership claims for subjects such as did:web verified against DNS TXT, a JWKS file, a DID document or a SPIFFE bundle. Workload identity is SPIFFE. KERI would be foreign here.
- **Digest fit.** Imposes its own: CIDv1 from the SHA-256 OCI digest of the record. This matches the earlier draft's CIDv1 sha2-256 choice, not a Blake3 SAID; the SAID could only be carried as an annotation.
- **Governance.** Linux Foundation project led by Cisco Outshift; Cisco and AWS staff maintain the AI Catalog Go and Rust SDKs.
- **Publisher cost.** A running Go daemon or Kubernetes deployment plus an OCI store; not serverless, not zero idle cost.
- **TypeScript tooling.** npm agntcy-dir 1.8.1 (a gRPC client for a running node); @agntcy/dir-mcp.
- **Failure modes.** No published node or record counts; one dominant operator; announcements expire; schema forces agent-shaped records; heavy operational footprint for a solo maintainer.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. The DHT is shared, but every declarer must run or use a directory node, the public network is effectively one operator's federation, and a definition would have to be dressed as an OASF agent record.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Docs: 'ADS uses libp2p Kad-DHT for server and content discovery.' and 'Join the public network — connect to the production Directory at `ads.outshift.io`'. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/agntcy/dir/blob/main/docs/content/dir/dir-component-routing.md
  - agntcy/dir v1.8.0 released 2026-10-15; 206 stars. agntcy/oasf 344 stars. npm agntcy-dir 4,723 downloads in the week to 2026-11-07. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/agntcy/dir
  - IETF draft-mp-agntcy-ads-02, published 2026-07-06, individual informational submission by Cisco authors, no IETF standing; states artifactType MUST be application/vnd.agntcy.dir.record.v1+json. (as of 2026-07-06). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/draft-mp-agntcy-ads-02
  - AGNTCY blog: 'ADS is a reference implementation of ARD'; ARD POST /search available through the directory's MCP server only at that time. (as of 2026-06-17). Source: https://blogs.agntcy.org/technical/2026/06/17/ai-catalog-over-directory.html
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Adoption today down from 1.5: 206 stars, 4,723 weekly npm downloads and no published node or record counts. No-aggregator down from 3: the 'public network' is a federation centred on one operator's host and every declarer must run or rent a Go node. Reverse lookup down from 3: the provider lookup is keyed by the CID of an OASF agent record only, label search is limited to skill, domain, module and locator, and results are a cached, expiring view. Publisher effort down from 1.5 and KERI fit from 2 for the daemon, OCI store and SPIFFE or Sigstore identity.

#### GoDaddy Agent Name Service (ANS)

A naming and identity registry for agents: ans://v&lt;version&gt;.&lt;agentHost&gt; names bound to a verified domain, X.509 identity certificates issued by a registration authority, lifecycle events sealed in a SCITT-style transparency log, and _ans TXT and TLSA records in DNS.

- **Publish.** Register an agent version with a registration authority (GoDaddy operates one), prove domain control, receive certificates, publish DNS records. It registers agents, not arbitrary resources.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not defined. The draft leaves search to a separate Discovery Service that 'indexes agents for searchability'; content hashes appear only for integrity checks of agent cards.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Registration with the authority, then DNS.
- **Identity.** Domain control plus X.509 and DANE. PKI-centric and domain-anchored; a KERI AID has no natural place.
- **Digest fit.** No field for an arbitrary digest; names are FQDN-based.
- **Governance.** Vendor-led with individual Internet-Drafts; GoDaddy is also acknowledged in the ARD spec, which mentions ANS registrations as one kind of transparency-log receipt.
- **Failure modes.** Single practical operator; agent-only; no registration counts found.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Requires a registration authority and log operator, and still delegates discovery to an unspecified index.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - draft-narajala-courtney-ansv2-00 published 2026-04-13 as an Independent Submission, intended status Informational; authors from GoDaddy, OWASP, DistributedApps.ai and Cisco. (as of 2026-04-13). Source: https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-narajala-courtney-ansv2-00.html
  - Reference registry repository: 34 stars, 16 forks, Apache-2.0; no usage numbers published. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/godaddy/ans-registry
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Trajectory down from 2: an independent-submission draft and a 34-star reference repository with no usage numbers. Other scores kept; I verified only the repository figures.

#### IETF DAWN working group (Discovery of Agents With Names) and DNS-AID

The IETF's chartered venue for agent discovery, considering DNS and mDNS as the base. DNS-AID is an individual draft (SVCB and TXT records under an _agents label) that is one candidate input.

- **Publish.** Nothing to publish yet; no protocol document exists. Expected shape is DNS records on the publisher's own domain.
- **Reverse lookup.** Explicitly out of scope: the charter excludes 'AI resource indexing and searching across the wider Internet' and limits discovery to a client obtaining minimum public information for a named target.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** The client must already hold a name.
- **Identity.** The charter also excludes identity management of agents, tools and skills.
- **Digest fit.** Unknown; no specification text yet.
- **Governance.** IETF working group, the only formal standards-body track in this family; first protocol output is at least a year away.
- **Failure modes.** Too late and too narrow for this job; nothing deployable in 2026.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Per-name resolution only; any who-else query would need an index outside the working group's scope.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Datatracker shows group dawn in the Internet Area: 'State Active', 'Charter charter-ietf-dawn-01 Approved'; chairs Dapeng Liu and Frank Brockners. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/dawn/about/
  - Milestones: protocol specification to the IESG by 2027-12-31, architecture by 2028-01-31. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/dawn/about/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Trajectory down from 3: the charter is confirmed, but the protocol milestone is 2027-12-31 and the charter rules out both Internet-wide search and identity, so even success would not serve this job. Publisher effort is a guess for a protocol that does not exist yet; kept at 3.

#### MIT NANDA (Index and AgentFacts)

A research project proposing a lean index that resolves an agent name to an AgentFacts document, with a federation of registries.

- **Publish.** Register an agent through the project's onboarding and publish an AgentFacts document. Agents only.
- **Reverse lookup.** Name-to-facts resolution through the project's index; no digest-keyed query found.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Registration in the NANDA index.
- **Identity.** AgentFacts are described as verifiable documents; details not verified from a primary source.
- **Digest fit.** Not verified.
- **Governance.** MIT Media Lab research pilot with individual Internet-Drafts.
- **Failure modes.** Research-stage; no platform adoption found.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Depends on the project's own index and registries.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - projnanda/projnanda repository: 126 stars, last push 2026-11-05. No primary adoption figures found; a secondary source mentions a pilot registry with over 1,000 agents. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/projnanda/projnanda
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. Only the repository figure (126 stars) was re-checked; nothing found that would move it.

#### llms.txt

A Markdown file at /llms.txt that points language models at a site's key documents.

- **Publish.** Write a Markdown file on your own domain; a definition could be linked from it.
- **Reverse lookup.** None; free text with no structure, no query and no index.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None.
- **Identity.** Domain only.
- **Digest fit.** Any string can appear in prose; nothing interprets it.
- **Governance.** Informal community proposal (AnswerDotAI/llms-txt, 2,682 stars); no standards body.
- **Failure modes.** Widely served, rarely fetched; useful only as a human and LLM readable pointer.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Unstructured per-site file; any lookup would need a bespoke crawler and parser.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Ahrefs study of 137,210 domains: 28% publish an llms.txt (stated as an upper bound), and '97% of those files received zero traffic in May 2026.' (as of 2026-06-15). Source: https://ahrefs.com/blog/llmstxt-study/
  - Own probe: 32 of 65 hand-picked domains serve a plausible llms.txt, far more than serve any catalog manifest. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/llms.txt
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Adoption today down from 3.5: the 28 percent figure comes from a self-selected sample and 97 percent of the files were never fetched, and the maintainer's test includes being read. Other scores unchanged.

#### agents.json (Wildcard) and NLWeb

agents.json is an OpenAPI-based file describing API flows for agents. NLWeb is Microsoft's open project that gives a site a natural-language /ask endpoint over its schema.org data and doubles as an MCP server.

- **Publish.** agents.json: a static file on your domain. NLWeb: run a service with a vector store over your content.
- **Reverse lookup.** None in either. NLWeb answers natural-language questions about one site.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None.
- **Identity.** Domain only.
- **Digest fit.** No relevant field.
- **Governance.** Single-vendor open-source projects; NLWeb's discovery ideas have effectively moved into ARD.
- **Failure modes.** agents.json is stale; NLWeb needs a running service and solves a different problem.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Neither has a cross-site query or an index.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - wild-card-ai/agents-json: 1,317 stars, last push 2025-08-21 (dormant for over a year). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/wild-card-ai/agents-json
  - nlweb-ai/NLWeb: 6,258 stars, last push 2026-08-11; nlweb.ai did not accept connections in my probe. R.V. Guha, NLWeb's creator, co-authors ARD, and the ARD spec's examples use an nlweb.ai registry. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/nlweb-ai/NLWeb
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. Repository figures confirmed (agents-json dormant since 2025-08-21; NLWeb last pushed 2026-08-11); API Evangelist separately reports that the public NLWeb finder host does not resolve.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (agent-ecosystem)

The skeptic re-checked 27 claims; 18 held as stated. The rest:

- *partly* · AI Catalog · claim: The family has in practice converged on one publishing format, the Linux Foundation AI Catalog.
  - Correction: Convergence is on a data model only. The README calls the repository 'a temporary working repo maintained by the Linux Foundation' and says 'When the specification is finalized, A2A and MCP steering committees will vote on adoption', so the vote has not happened. There are 0 tags and 0 releases. ARD v0.91 says a publisher on the ai-catalog.json path 'SHOULD move to ard.json' and that consumers need not read the old path; Lighthouse warns when only ai-catalog.json is found. Neither path is in the IANA registry. The LF press release of 2026-10-22 lists ten Agentic AI Foundation projects and neither AI Catalog nor ARD is among them.
  - Checked: Raw README.md of Agent-Card/ai-catalog; ARD spec section 5.1; Chrome Lighthouse ARD audit page; IANA well-known registry CSV (107 rows); GitHub API for tags and releases.
- *partly* · AI Catalog · claim: The data model fits: free-form identifier, open type string, entry digest, an array of independently signed Trust Manifests ('A signed manifest MAY endorse only the artifact release identified by that subject'), signer profiles extensible by absolute URI.
  - Correction: Every quoted sentence is present. Three qualifications. ADR-0027 is still 'Status: Proposed' (2026-09-04). No live catalog I fetched uses digest or trustManifests, so MAD would be a first implementer. A Signature object MUST contain a detached compact JWS, so a KERI profile would have to wrap KERI keys in JWS rather than use CESR signatures, and the spec says of independent profiles 'Consumers need an implementation of those requirements', meaning only MAD's own tooling would verify it.
  - Checked: Raw specification/ai-catalog.md on main (identifier and type definitions, Trust Manifest, Manifest Validity, Signature Object, Profile Selection, did:web Signer Profile); ADR-0027 header.
- *partly* · Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) · claim: Google: 'Native support for ARD will be available in Agent Platform in the coming months.'
  - Correction: The quote is accurate. Nearly five months later the Agent Registry release notes (latest entries 2026-11-06, 2026-10-30, 2026-07-23, 2026-06-18) contain no mention of ARD, ai-catalog or ard.json.
  - Checked: Google Developers Blog post of 2026-06-17; Google Cloud Agent Registry release notes.
- *partly* · Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) · claim: ARD is a risky bet, neither sound nor overtaken.
  - Correction: Risky understates it; stalled on the consumer side is closer. API Evangelist measured 'Cross-vendor federation referrals across four registries: 0', found the public Agent Finder host finder.nlweb.ai does not resolve, and found that of the eleven launch backers two serve a manifest on any of 127 domains. I found no release, no foundation home and no new large adopter since launch.
  - Checked: API Evangelist research page (2026-07-31); a practitioner post of 2026-06-19; web search for any v1.0 release, foundation donation or adoption by Anthropic or OpenAI.
- *partly* · Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) · claim: One document can be served byte-identically at both well-known paths and be valid under both specs; Buildkite and Supabase already do this.
  - Correction: Dual serving is real and the schema tolerates extra members such as digest and trustManifests. But the two specs name trust differently: AI Catalog uses an array trustManifests keyed by contributor, ARD a single trustManifest with a required identity that must align with the publisher domain. A document meant to satisfy a verifying ARD registry needs both shapes, and neither live example carries either.
  - Checked: Fetched both paths on buildkite.com (925 bytes each) and supabase.com (3,615 bytes each); read the ARD entry JSON Schema (additionalProperties is true; identifier pattern requires urn:air with a domain-shaped publisher segment).
- *partly* · MCP Registry (server.json) and MCP Server Cards · claim: @modelcontextprotocol/sdk had 88,418,428 npm downloads in the week to 2026-11-07, supporting an adoption score of 5.
  - Correction: The number is right but measures use of the protocol SDK, not use of the registry or of Server Cards for discovery. Server Cards became Final one month ago with no client implementation evidenced. The registry size figures (52,042 and 57,041) are third-party and I could not verify them.
  - Checked: npm downloads API returned exactly 88,418,428.
- *partly* · A2A Agent Card · claim: agent-card.json is IANA-registered (permanent, Linux Foundation, 2025-08-01) and is the only entry of this family in the registry besides RFC 9727 api-catalog.
  - Correction: The registration is as stated. The 'only entry' part is wrong: 'ucp' (Universal Commerce Protocol business profile) was registered as provisional on 2026-10-26, and the researcher did not cover it.
  - Checked: IANA well-known URIs CSV, 107 rows; npm downloads API for @a2a-js/sdk (6,216,246, exact); GitHub API (26,457 stars).
- *refuted* · AGNTCY Agent Directory Service (ADS) and OASF · claim: The only native digest-keyed provider lookup in the family is AGNTCY's libp2p DHT.
  - Correction: ERC-8004 (Trustless Agents; EIP status Draft, live on Ethereum mainnet since 2026-01-29) is an agent-ecosystem mechanism the report omits. Its Validation Registry emits events with an indexed bytes32 requestHash and its Reputation Registry an indexed tag, and any node of the chain answers a log query on those topics. It is a poor fit for other reasons (see missed mechanisms) but it is a second native keyed lookup in the family.
  - Checked: EIP-8004 text at eips.ethereum.org; an empirical study of its deployment (arXiv 2606.26028).
- *partly* · Best pick (sketch) · claim: Peer links (entries of type application/ai-catalog+json pointing at counterparties) give a crawler something to follow from one publisher to the next, and the maintainer then runs nothing.
  - Correction: The spec permits it, but nested catalogs are specified for a publisher's own hierarchy with a recommended maximum depth of 4, none of the nine live catalogs links to another domain's catalog, and Neuronto counts 93 nested-catalog entries in 180,538. More important, links point forward only: a party that starts relying on H is reachable only if someone already in the graph learns of it and adds a link, and no mechanism for that notice exists. 'Runs nothing' is true of hosting only; every declarer still needs a did:web document, an ES256 key, JCS and JWS code with no TypeScript SDK, and a domain submission to each registry it hopes to appear in.
  - Checked: AI Catalog sections on nested catalogs and depth limits; the nine live catalogs; Neuronto type facet.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (agent-ecosystem)

- **ERC-8004 Trustless Agents (on-chain identity, reputation and validation registries).** It is an agent-ecosystem mechanism with a native keyed lookup that no purpose-built index has to serve: events carry an indexed bytes32 requestHash and an indexed tag, and any node of the chain answers a log query on them. That refutes the report's 'only AGNTCY' claim and its 'none in this family' conclusion. Nothing in the text prevents registering a non-agent resource. It does not rescue the family for this project: the EIP is still Draft, declaring costs gas and a wallet, identity is an Ethereum address, the hash convention is keccak256, and a study through 2026-05-13 found only 3 to 15 percent of registrations had a valid file with a live endpoint and 59 to 91 percent of reviewers were Sybil. One tracker claims 1.33M registrations across 24 chains on 2026-10-23; registration is one cheap transaction, so that is not usage. Source: https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-8004 ; https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2606.26028
- **Universal Commerce Protocol business profile (/.well-known/ucp).** It is the closest precedent in the family for a party declaring which protocol definitions it implements: capabilities are named in reverse-domain form with a version, a spec URL and a schema URL, vendors may define their own, and the spec says this 'eliminates the need for a central registry'. It was registered with IANA (provisional) on 2026-10-26, which neither ai-catalog.json nor ard.json has managed. It has no reverse lookup either, which shows the family's best-backed declaration pattern lives without one. I found no count of merchants serving it. Source: https://ucp.dev/latest/specification/overview/ ; https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris-1.csv
- **Agent Skills discovery index (/.well-known/agent-skills/index.json).** A Cloudflare draft (v0.2.0) whose entries require name, type, description, url and a sha256 digest. Cloudflare, Neon, Zapier and LangChain docs serve it, Agent Skills itself is an Agentic AI Foundation project contributed by Anthropic, and Google's Agent Registry made standalone skills generally available on 2026-11-06. Since MAD is authored through a CLI driven by an AI with a Skill, a definition's readable reference could ship as a skill, and this is the piece of the family most likely to be read by Anthropic and OpenAI tooling. It offers no reverse lookup. Source: https://github.com/cloudflare/agent-skills-discovery-rfc ; https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/agentic-ai-foundation-announces-four-new-projects-agent-skills-open-responses-openenv-and-opensandbox
- **RFC 9727 api-catalog (/.well-known/api-catalog, Linkset).** The only IETF-published catalog format in the family, IANA permanent since 2024-12-23, carrying arbitrary link relations and media types. The report mentions it only as a registry row. Neuronto already indexes 61 application/linkset+json entries. For 'what could a publisher emit that several would read at once' it is a more settled companion than ard.json. Source: https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris-1.csv
- **Sigstore Rekor transparency log (adjacent; AGNTCY and AI Catalog's OCI layer both lean on Cosign).** Rekor v1's public instance has a hash-to-entries index, which is a generic 'who signed the artifact with digest H' service run by a foundation. Rekor v2 removes that search endpoint and leaves finding entries to clients that read the log tiles, while the public instance stays on v1 'for the foreseeable future' (Sigstore blog, 2026-06-28). It matters mostly as a caution: the one free, generic, digest-keyed reverse index near this family is being designed out by its own operators. The removal is verified only through a vendor's documentation. Source: https://blog.sigstore.dev/rekor-evolution/ ; https://docs.nvidia.com/aicr/latest/contributor-guide/rekor-v-2-signing
- **Signed-event networks outside this family (Nostr relays, AT Protocol).** The researcher's closing sentence points here without naming them: a tag-keyed query that many independent nodes answer, over records signed by self-held keys. That is the shape the first and fourth values ask for and this family lacks. I checked only peripheral facts: nostr.json is IANA-provisional (2024-03-18), and weekly npm downloads are 599,453 for nostr-tools and 350,049 for @atproto/api, against 1,499 for signify-ts. Relay counts I found ranged from about 70 to over 1,000 with one source saying most cannot cover costs, so durability needs its own check by whoever covers that family. Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/nostr-tools ; https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris-1.csv

### What the researcher could not verify (agent-ecosystem)

- Whether ARD is formally a Linux Foundation project: a third-party page credits the 'ARD Project (Linux Foundation)', but the repository shows only the ards-project organisation and three leads, and Google's post only thanks the LF AI Catalog Working Group.
- Whether Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor or VS Code fetch ai-catalog.json or ard.json: no evidence found either way. GitHub Agent Finder's registry configuration, and whether it ever ingests third-party domains automatically, was not verified; GitHub describes its public source as a curated catalog.
- Current status of Google Cloud Agent Registry's native ARD support (announced 2026-06-17 as 'in the coming months') and of ARD support in AWS Agent Registry; no AWS primary source found.
- Worldwide manifest counts: the Neuronto census (375,995 domains checked, 246 manifests, 207 publishers) and the API Evangelist audit (43,834 domains, 12 manifests) are relayed by agenticresources.dev without dates; not checked at source.
- Total size of the MCP Registry: 52,042 (2026-10-28) and 57,041 (2026-11-05) are third-party counts taken from search summaries; my own pagination stopped at 1,500 listings after an SSL timeout.
- That a generic registry would actually index a new domain's custom-type entry with a said: tag: inferred from Neuronto indexing other non-agent media types and enforcing tag filters, not demonstrated, because the task was read-only and nothing was published.
- AI Catalog text on Trust Manifests, digest and the did:web profile was read from the main branch on 2026-11-08; the spec has no version tag and ADR-0027 is still marked Proposed, so it may change. The claim that the README says the A2A and MCP steering committees will vote on adoption came through a summarising fetch.
- The date A2A became an Agentic AI Foundation project (reported as 2026-08-17) comes from secondary sources; the Linux Foundation press release of 2026-10-22 lists A2A among AAIF projects, read through a summarising fetch.
- AGNTCY public network size, federation membership and exact joining requirements; NANDA adoption figures and AgentFacts identity details; ANS registration counts. No primary numbers found.
- Outcome of IETF 127 for DAWN; the datatracker shows the group Active with an approved charter but no approval date.
- The API Evangelist A2A report (65 organisations publishing an Agent Card, ten conformant) returned HTTP 410 and could not be read.
- Quotes marked verbatim=false were relayed by the fetch tool's summarising model or are my paraphrase; verbatim=true is used only for text I read directly from raw files or API responses.
- My domain probe is a hand-picked, non-random sample, one request per path on 2026-11-08; domains that returned 403 to my client (godaddy.com, cisco.com, nvidia.com, gitlab.com, docker.com) may serve manifests to others.
- Method note: the registry tests were read-only POST /search and /explore queries to huggingface-hf-discover.hf.space and neuronto.com containing a made-up SAID-shaped string; Neuronto's response states that it logs query text for 90 days.

<a id="dns-web"></a>

## Plain DNS and HTTPS

**Researcher's summary.** Plain DNS and HTTPS solve the forward half of the job cheaply: a publisher can serve a definition at a URL containing its digest, plus a static manifest of what it issues, relies on, implements or endorses, and anyone can verify the bytes against the digest. Nothing in the family answers the reverse question natively, because HTTP and DNS only answer "what does this name say", never "which names say H". The standards confirm the gap: RFC 9727 defines the catalog but no way to learn which domains have one, DNS-AID puts capability search out of scope, and the IETF DAWN working group (charter approved, page last updated 2026-10-22) excludes "AI resource indexing and searching across the wider Internet" and identity management. So the reverse lookup in this family is always somebody's crawl; the only choice is whose. Existing generic crawls answer part of it today: Common Crawl's URL index (monthly, 40.2 million hosts in August 2026, keyed by URL so the digest must be in the path), HTTP Archive's well-known census (already fetches ard.json, ai-catalog.json and agent-card.json), and full-text web search. In this session a bare quoted KERI SAID returned no relevant hits in one search mode and four relevant pages in another, missing the canonical repository, so web search is partial and non-deterministic. Programmatic web search is also contracting: Bing's APIs were retired in August 2025 and Google's Custom Search JSON API ends 2027-01-01 with a partner-only replacement. The widest real deployments are robots.txt (85%) and JSON-LD (43% of home pages); llms.txt reached 8.6% of the top 100,000 domains, while every agent-specific well-known file is negligible (api-catalog on 4 of 74 probed providers, ai-catalog.json on nine of 37,751 domains). ARD has the strongest backers and tooling, but it is a Proposal, its identifiers are domain-anchored URNs rather than digests, and a July 2026 field probe found "Federation is specified, implemented and empty." No mechanism here natively carries a key-signed endorsement; everything is the domain's say-so except did:webs, which roots trust in a KERI AID and treats the web server as untrusted, and KERI's own OOBI model explicitly allows DNS and web search as untrusted discovery. The 44-character SAID fits verbatim in URL paths and TXT values but not in DNS names (case-insensitive; re-encode as 52-character base32). Verdict: this family is the right publication, fetch, human-reference and identity-bootstrap layer and supports a local-first walk from one introduction if manifests list peers, but it cannot give a complete reverse lookup without an index someone operates.

**Researcher's best pick.** Static digest-in-path publication on each publisher's own HTTPS origin: a well-known path for the bytes, a peer-linked declaration manifest that can be KERI-signed, and a generated HTML plus JSON-LD reference page per definition, with ARD and api-catalog emitted as generated adapters and Common Crawl, HTTP Archive and web search used opportunistically. It is the strongest option in this family, but it does not make the reverse lookup native. Use this family as the publication, fetch, human-reference and identity-bootstrap layer, and take the reverse lookup from another family if one offers a digest-keyed query that existing nodes answer.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** The reverse lookup is borrowed, not provided. The peer walk sees only the component connected to your introduction, so two communities that independently adopt the same definition, which is the case where "same digest means interoperable" matters most, will not find each other. The global fallbacks are crawls that owe you nothing: Common Crawl samples about 40 million hosts a month by link centrality and says "we do not generally archive any entire website"; web search gave zero relevant hits for a bare SAID in one mode and a partial list in another; and programmatic search access is being withdrawn (Bing's APIs retired, Google's ending 2027-01-01). Anyone who needs a complete answer will rebuild the crawler and index that the reviewers objected to. The second objection is adoption: what is widely adopted is the carrier (HTTPS, HTML, JSON-LD, sitemaps), not the convention. A new /.well-known/mad/ path starts with zero readers and no agent tooling reads it, while the one option with large backers, ARD, has nine publishers, no digest semantics and domain-anchored identity.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** Trust this family for four jobs: publishing definitions on the publisher's own origin, fetching and verifying them by digest, carrying a generated human-readable reference page, and locating KERI identity material. Do not trust it for the reverse lookup. The researcher's sourcing held up well: I re-read nearly every cited source, matched the ARD, KERI, RFC 8615, Webmention and DAWN quotes against raw text, and found only small corrections (a newer ANS draft, two newer Common Crawl crawls, an over-absolute claim about DNS datasets, and three missed conventions that do carry key-signed statements). The researcher's own verdict, that a complete answer needs an index someone operates, is correct, but the scores were about a point too generous on reverse lookup: the Common Crawl route was never run, and when I tested its nearest analog it captured none of three live manifests on hosts it crawled that month. ARD is weaker than the report suggests in one respect and stronger in another: it has named agent-side readers, yet the path the current spec requires was live on 1 of 21 hosts I probed today. Build the publication layer here if you like, and take the reverse lookup from a family whose existing nodes answer a digest-keyed query; if no such family exists, the honest design is a small reproducible index, stated as such.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

No. For the best pick (static digest-in-path files plus a peer-linked manifest), a stranger holding only H has four routes, and none is a reverse lookup that works today without something purpose-built.

1. Peer walk. Infrastructure: each publisher's own static host. H contains no origin, so the walk cannot start without an introduction. From one, it returns only the origins that people typed into peers lists, and an issuer learns of a relier only if told out of band. That is the hand-curated list moved to every publisher. ARD shipped the same idea with eleven vendors behind it; the July 2026 probe found every registry referral pointing at the same operator and zero DNS pointers.

2. Common Crawl's columnar index. Operator: Common Crawl Foundation, data on AWS S3. It accepts any URL path string. The data is free; the querier needs their own AWS account and pays Athena $5 per TB, at most about $1 per monthly partition. It is monthly (latest: 5 to 18 October 2026, 41.4 million hosts) and returns a host only if CCBot fetched that exact URL. I tested the nearest real analog: the October index holds captures for zapier.com/, huggingface.co/ and airtable.com/ but "No Captures found" for the ai-catalog.json manifest each serves live today. The free CDX endpoint returned HTTP 504 on most requests, and Cloudflare's managed robots.txt disallows CCBot.

3. Web search (Google, Microsoft, Brave, or an agent's search tool). It accepts any string and is free interactively, while API access is being withdrawn. My replication gave zero relevant hits in standard mode for two long-public vLEI SAIDs and seven pages without the canonical source in extended mode. There is no completeness or determinism, and any page can carry the string.

4. Candidate lists (Certificate Transparency logs, zone files, HTTP Archive, Common Crawl's robots.txt archive) followed by a probe of each host's manifest. This works, and it is a crawler plus an index that somebody runs. The cheapest form is a monthly batch over Common Crawl's robots.txt archive (0.15 TiB compressed, every visited host) looking for a pointer line, with the result published as a static file. Anyone can reproduce it, but it is still an aggregator.

What must exist: a public spec and provisional IANA registration for the suffix, the publish/who/verify CLI, and, for anything beyond the hand-linked neighbourhood of your introduction, an index that somebody operates. The honest answer is that somebody must run an index.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

This family cannot deliver the maintainer's first-ranked requirement, and the best pick hides that by relocating the curation. DNS and HTTP answer "what does this name say"; "which names say H" needs an inverted index, and an inverted index always has an operator.

The evidence is the closest live analog. ARD and AI Catalog are this exact pattern (a static well-known manifest, optional robots, link and DNS pointers, referrals between indexes) with Google, Microsoft, GitHub and eight others behind it. After about five months: nine publishers in roughly 38,000 domains probed, every referral pointing at the same operator, zero DNS records, and today the path the current spec requires is live on 1 of the 21 hosts I probed. Common Crawl visited three of those publishers in October 2026 and captured none of their manifests. Web search returned nothing relevant in its default mode for digests that have been public since 2022. The IETF working group chartered for this problem excludes Internet-wide indexing, and its BoF minutes record "We should exclude global Internet-wide discovery, as its unsolvable."

So mad-discovery on this family either returns only the hand-linked neighbourhood of one introduction, where the peers list is the openhaven-style list the maintainer wants to abolish, now kept by every publisher, or it rebuilds the crawler and index the reviewers rejected. Two communities that adopt the same definition independently, the case where a shared digest matters most, will not find each other.

Two further problems. What is widely adopted is the carrier, not the convention: a new suffix starts with zero readers, and no agent tooling reads it. And a domain can claim any H for nothing, so a count of declarers means little until claims are KERI-signed; once they are, the domain is only a locator and the reverse index belongs wherever those signed statements are anchored, which is outside this family.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (dns-web)

What is written where (all static, produced by one CLI command and uploaded to any static host):

1. https://&lt;origin&gt;/.well-known/mad/&lt;SAID&gt; holds the definition bytes, immutable.
2. https://&lt;origin&gt;/.well-known/mad/index.json is the declaration manifest: a list of {SAID, role: issue | rely | implement | endorse, date, page URL} and a peers list of other origins this publisher knows declare the same SAIDs. The peers list is what lets a crawler step from one publisher to the next, which the reviewers found missing.
3. https://&lt;origin&gt;/mad/&lt;SAID&gt;/ is an HTML reference page rendered from the definition itself, with the SAID in the title and visible text, a single-token alias (hex or base32), a JSON-LD block using schema.org identifier, and links to the issuer's and peers' pages for the same SAID. This is the piece that removes the need for an openhaven-style reference site: every publisher carries the reference, generated from the definition.
4. sitemap.xml, a Sitemap line in robots.txt, and an llms.txt list, so Googlebot, Bingbot and CCBot find the pages.
5. Optional generated adapters from the same data: /.well-known/ard.json (entries with the SAID as an extension term) and /.well-known/api-catalog. They cost nothing and make the publisher visible to Lighthouse, ARD registries and HTTP Archive's existing well-known census.
