6. Later, additively: a principal field (did:webs:&lt;origin&gt;:&lt;AID&gt;), a CESR signature over the manifest, and the KEL at the did:webs location. Verifiers then count AIDs, not domains, and the domain is only a locator.

Which query answers "who else declares H":
- Local-first walk, run by the client: GET index.json at the one known origin, collect declarations for H, follow peers breadth-first with a depth and host bound, verify bytes and signatures. This needs one introduction and no infrastructure.
- Issuer shortcut: the issuer's manifest entry for H lists the reliers it has been told about (by pull request or email; by Webmention only if the issuer chooses to run one endpoint).
- Wider net on other people's infrastructure: an Athena query on Common Crawl's URL index, for example SELECT url_host_name, fetch_time FROM ccindex WHERE crawl = 'CC-MAIN-2026-34' AND subset = 'warc' AND url_path = '/.well-known/mad/&lt;SAID&gt;'. A prefix filter on '/.well-known/mad/' enumerates every definition seen. An agent's own web search for the SAID and its alias is a second net. Every hit is an untrusted hint to fetch and verify.

What the maintainer still runs: no service. The deliverables are a TypeScript library and CLI (publish, who, verify) and a short public specification so the mad suffix can be registered as provisional with IANA. No Lambda, no DynamoDB, no seed list; the project's own site is just one more publisher with a peers list.

### Mechanisms (dns-web)

#### Static digest-in-path publication under a well-known URI (RFC 8615; precedents RFC 6920 ni and the KERI well-known OOBI)

A publisher serves each definition at a URL on its own HTTPS origin whose path contains the digest, plus one static manifest listing the digests it declares. RFC 8615 (Standards Track, May 2019) reserves /.well-known/; the IANA registry holds about 107 suffixes (last updated 2026-10-26) and registration is Specification Required, with provisional entries for non-standards. Two precedents already put a hash or identifier in the path: ni (RFC 6920, permanent) and /.well-known/keri/oobi/&lt;AID&gt; in the KERI specification v1.1 (keri is not in the IANA registry).

- **Publish.** Static files only. (1) Definition bytes at https://pub.example/.well-known/&lt;suffix&gt;/&lt;SAID&gt;. (2) A manifest at https://pub.example/.well-known/&lt;suffix&gt;/index.json listing {SAID, role: issue|rely|implement|endorse} and a peers list of other origins the publisher knows declare the same SAIDs. Hosted by the publisher on any static host (S3 plus CloudFront, GitHub Pages, Cloudflare Pages).
- **Fetch by digest.** GET the path at any origin that claims H, recompute the Blake3-256 SAID over the returned document and compare. The origin is untrusted; the hash check carries the trust. KERI spec: "an OOBI itself is not trusted but MUST be verified." RFC 6920 gives the same pattern as http://example.com/.well-known/ni/sha-256/&lt;base64url digest&gt;.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not native. HTTP has no query for "which origins serve path P". Three routes: (a) the client walks: read one known origin's manifest, follow peers breadth-first, HEAD /.well-known/&lt;suffix&gt;/&lt;H&gt; on each; this answers only for the component reachable from the introduction and uses nobody's infrastructure but the publishers'. (b) Common Crawl's columnar URL index (Common Crawl Foundation, on AWS S3) queried with Athena on url_path = '/.well-known/&lt;suffix&gt;/&lt;H&gt;'; this returns only hosts where CCBot captured that URL. (c) Someone crawls a seed list, which is the purpose-built aggregator of the draft.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher becomes findable when an existing publisher adds it to peers (one introduction), or when a sitemap plus one inbound link gets generic crawlers to its digest URLs. Certificate Transparency exposes its hostname, but someone must then probe it.
- **Identity.** By default the claim is the domain's say-so over TLS. Key identity is additive: sign the manifest with a KERI AID (CESR attached signatures or an ACDC) and host the KEL at a did:webs location; a verifier then ignores the domain. The KERI spec defines an OOBI as a mechanism that "associates a given URI or URL with a given AID or SAID" and says "OOBIs may safely use DNS/CA, web search engines, social media, email, and messaging as discovery mechanisms."
- **Digest fit.** Path segments are opaque and case-sensitive, so the 44-character CESR SAID (base64url alphabet) goes in verbatim. RFC 6920 ni is a poor fit: IANA's Named Information Hash Algorithm Registry has sha-256, sha-384, sha-512, sha3, blake2s-256, blake2b-256, blake2b-512, k12-256 and k12-512 but no BLAKE3 (Expert Review; last updated 2025-10-14). Re-encoding for ni would be the raw 32 bytes as unpadded base64url (43 characters): replace the leading CESR code character E with A, base64url-decode to 33 bytes, drop the leading zero byte, re-encode; the result is not a substring of the SAID. Multihash has blake3 at 0x1e (status draft). Caveat (my reasoning): a SAID is computed over the document with the SAID field filled by placeholder characters, so a verifier that hashes the served bytes (ni or CID semantics) would not reproduce it; use a suffix of your own.
- **Governance.** RFC 8615 is an IETF standard; a new suffix needs a public specification and expert review, and a provisional registration is routine.
- **Publisher cost.** A domain and static hosting; effectively zero idle cost and no code running.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Global fetch in Node 18+, @noble/hashes for BLAKE3, signify-ts and WebOfTrust CESR code for SAIDs. Library names are from memory and were not checked this session.
- **Failure modes.** Completeness is limited to the reachable component. A publisher that goes offline takes its declaration with it, though anyone can mirror hash-verified bytes and signed manifests. Any domain can claim any H for almost nothing, so counts of domains are meaningless until claims are key-signed. Declaring reliance is public. A brand-new suffix has no readers.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Fetch, forward declaration and the component walk need nothing purpose-built. A complete "who else" answer needs a crawl, and generic crawls cover a monthly sample only.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - RFC 8615 is Standards Track (May 2019); registration is Specification Required, with provisional status for values not from open standards. (as of 2019-05). Source: https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8615.html
  - IANA Well-Known URIs registry holds about 107 suffixes; ni (RFC 6920) is permanent; keri, ard.json, ai-catalog.json and llms.txt are not registered. (as of 2026-10-26). Source: https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris.xhtml
  - KERI spec example path: /.well-known/keri/oobi/EPR7FWsN3tOM8PqfMap2FRfF4MFQ4v3ZXjBUcMVtvhmB; "A secondary use case for OOBIs is to provide service endpoints or URIs for SAD (items identifier by their SAID)." (as of spec v1.1). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - Named Information Hash Algorithm Registry contains no BLAKE3 entry. (as of 2025-10-14). Source: https://www.iana.org/assignments/named-information/named-information.xhtml
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 3 to 2 and trajectory 3 to 2: only the carrier is adopted. A new /.well-known/mad/ suffix has no publishers and no readers, ni is negligible, the KERI suffix is not even registered, and the two bodies converging on agent discovery (ARD, IETF DAWN) both chose domain-anchored names, not digests. reverse_lookup 2 to 1: the peers walk follows only hand-typed edges, and the Common Crawl route failed its nearest real test (three hosts crawled in October 2026, none of their live well-known manifests captured). publisher_effort 5 to 4: static files, but also an own domain, a hand-kept peers list, a sitemap, HTML pages and a registered suffix. no_aggregator and keri_fit unchanged: fetch, forward declaration and additive KERI signing all check out against the KERI spec.

#### RFC 9727 api-catalog with Linkset (RFC 9264)

The only IETF Standards Track catalog convention in the family. A domain serves /.well-known/api-catalog as application/linkset+json with profile https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc9727, listing its APIs and links to their descriptions and documentation.

- **Publish.** One static JSON file. Each definition becomes a link context (anchor is the definition's URL on the publisher's origin) with service-desc pointing at the bytes, service-doc at an HTML page, and extension relation types (RFC 9264 allows relation types that are URIs) for issue, rely, implement or endorse. RFC 9264 "supports 'third-party links'", and RFC 9727 says "An API catalog may itself contain links to other API catalogs" and a publisher "MAY include a link to the API catalog of the third-party".
- **Fetch by digest.** Not defined. Targets are ordinary URLs; put the digest in the target path and verify after fetch.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. The RFC does not mention crawlers or any way to learn which domains have a catalog. A reverse answer comes only from walking cross-catalog links or from someone's crawl. Cloudflare's scanner and Radar check this path on the top 200,000 domains, but the published output is aggregate adoption.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None beyond another catalog linking to yours.
- **Identity.** Domain's say-so over TLS. No signature. Link targets may be any URI, so an entry can point at a did:webs identifier or a signed KERI stream.
- **Digest fit.** Anchors and targets must be URI references, so the digest rides inside a URL path or a URI scheme; there is no digest field.
- **Governance.** IETF Standards Track; stable.
- **Publisher cost.** One static file; the host must serve an extensionless path with the linkset media type, which some static hosts cannot do.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Plain JSON; no dedicated maintained library known (not checked this session).
- **Failure modes.** Almost nobody publishes or reads it. Semantics are APIs, so a protocol definition is a stretch. No integrity or signature.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Cross-catalog links permit a walk from one introduction, but there is no reverse query and no generic index of catalog contents.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - RFC 9727 is Standards Track, published June 2025; api-catalog is a permanent IANA registration dated 2024-12-23. (as of 2025-06). Source: https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9727.html
  - "Four out of seventy-four" probed API providers serve a valid RFC 9727 Linkset: Cloudflare, Memesio, Merge.dev, Zuplo. (as of 2026-05-22). Source: https://apievangelist.com/blog/2026/05/22/four-providers-publishing-well-known-api-catalog/
  - MCP Server Cards and API Catalogs "together appear on fewer than 15 sites in the entire dataset" of the 200,000 most visited domains. (as of 2026-04-17). Source: https://blog.cloudflare.com/agent-readiness/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** No change. Every claim checked out: Standards Track, four of 74 hand-picked providers, fewer than 15 of 200,000 sites together with MCP Server Cards, and no discovery of which domains have one. HTTP Archive does not even census it.

#### Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) v0.91 with AI Catalog (the draft's current choice)

A manifest of JSON-LD entries at /.well-known/ard.json plus a Registry search API. The spec is v0.91, status Proposal, dated August 26, 2026; ai-catalog.json was the path at Google's announcement on 2026-06-17 and is now the predecessor path. AI Catalog is "Maintained by the Agent Card Working Group under the Linux Foundation."

- **Publish.** A static manifest at /.well-known/ard.json. Alternates listed by the spec: "Embedding entry JSON-LD in a web page describing the resource", a link element with rel="ard", a robots.txt directive (example: Agentmap: https://example.com/entries.json), and Service Binding DNS records at _entries._agents.&lt;domain&gt;. Each entry's identifier is "a domain-anchored URN form, urn:air:&lt;publisher&gt;:[&lt;namespace&gt;:]&lt;agent-name&gt;". The schema is open ("additionalProperties: true" by design), so a SAID can ride as an extension term.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not defined. No entry-level digest of the resource; the only digest field "covers the referenced attestation document itself."
- **Reverse lookup.** Through Registries, which crawl: "Crawling entry sources — hosted manifests and in-page markup — from discovered URIs." POST /search takes a natural-language text (required) and an optional filter on term paths, but "support for metadata.* and other extension terms is registry-defined", and retrieving an entry by identifier "is out of scope for this draft". So "who declares H" works only if some registry crawled the publisher and chose to index the SAID term.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A registry's crawler has to find the domain; the spec describes no submission. Generic census exists: HTTP Archive's well-known custom metric already fetches ard.json and ai-catalog.json on each crawled origin (recording entry count and types), and Lighthouse audits against the v0.91 conformance suite.
- **Identity.** Optional trustManifest; "ARD requires only trustManifest.identity", and "The cryptographic trust domain asserted in trustManifest.identity MUST align with the &lt;publisher&gt; domain." SPIFFE, DID methods and enterprise PKI are given as examples. A did:webs identifier on the same domain fits; a bare AID with no domain does not.
- **Digest fit.** Poor. Identifiers are publisher-domain URNs, so two publishers of the same H necessarily have different identifiers, which is the opposite of "same digest means same definition". The SAID must be an extension term or part of the name.
- **Governance.** Industry working group; AI Catalog under the Linux Foundation; Apache 2.0. Not at a standards body; the well-known path changed once within about ten weeks of launch.
- **Publisher cost.** One static file.
- **TypeScript tooling.** A JavaScript port of the conformance suite ships in Lighthouse (per Chrome's documentation).
- **Failure modes.** Specification churn. Registries decide what is indexed and ranked. Domain-anchored identity conflicts with key-based identity. Entries carry no content integrity.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. The architecture is aggregators by design. Registries run by others exist, but none indexes MAD digests, so the maintainer would operate one or persuade an operator to index an extension term.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "ARD is being developed by a working group with participants from Microsoft, Google, Hugging Face, GoDaddy, and others." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://agenticresourcediscovery.org/
  - Spec v0.91, status Proposal, dated August 26, 2026; repository shows 499 stars. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/ards-project/ard-spec
  - Probe of /.well-known/ai-catalog.json: "43,834 domains, 37,751 reachable", "Nine publishers" (Zapier, ClickHouse, Airtable, DataRobot, Roboflow, Hugging Face, Opus, Artie, Fundraise Up); "None is fully conformant." (as of 2026-07-31). Source: https://standards.apievangelist.com/research/agentic-resource-discovery/
  - "Federation is specified, implemented and empty." and "Three of four discovery mechanisms are effectively unused." (as of 2026-07-31). Source: https://standards.apievangelist.com/research/agentic-resource-discovery/
  - Lighthouse audit: "Verification is performed using a JavaScript port of the ARD v0.91" conformance suite. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://developer.chrome.com/docs/lighthouse/agentic-browsing/ard
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_trajectory 4 to 3: the backers and the Lighthouse audit are real and there are reported readers (GitHub Copilot's Agent Finder, Hugging Face), but ten weeks after v0.91 moved the manifest, my probe found the new path on 1 of 21 hosts, the spec's own backers mostly serve nothing, and one of the nine July publishers has gone. keri_fit 2 to 1: identifiers are urn:air:&lt;publisher domain&gt; and the trust identity MUST align with that domain, so moving to AIDs later is not additive. adoption_today stays 1: nine publishers on the old path, and I found no fresher census. Other scores confirmed by the raw spec text.

#### WebFinger (RFC 7033)

A Standards Track query at /.well-known/webfinger?resource=&lt;URI&gt; that returns a JSON document of typed links about that resource from one host.

- **Publish.** The publisher answers queries for a URI naming H with links such as an issuer, relier or endorser relation and a link to the bytes. The resource can be any URI: "WebFinger is neutral regarding the scheme of such a URI".
- **Fetch by digest.** Indirect: a returned link points at the bytes; verify after fetch.
- **Reverse lookup.** Per host only. "The host to which a WebFinger query is issued is significant." It can tell you whether a known host declares H; it cannot tell you which hosts do.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None.
- **Identity.** Domain's say-so over TLS.
- **Digest fit.** The SAID must be wrapped in a URI (a URL or a URI scheme) to be a resource value.
- **Governance.** IETF Standards Track; stable and static.
- **Publisher cost.** Needs query-string routing, so it is not purely static hosting.
- **TypeScript tooling.** webfinger.js (from memory; not checked this session).
- **Failure modes.** Adds a dynamic endpoint for no gain over a static path; harvesting is by design.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. There is no cross-host query and no linking convention between hosts.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - RFC 7033 is Standards Track; webfinger is a permanent IANA registration dated 2013-03-15. (as of 2013-09). Source: https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7033.html
  - Secondary figure: about 10,475 active Mastodon instances, all of which answer WebFinger. (as of 2026-04-17). Source: https://marketful.com/mastodon-statistics
- **Skeptic's re-score.** No change. The standard's status is confirmed; the Mastodon count is unverified but the score does not depend on its exact value, and the per-host limit is in the RFC.

#### DNS records: TXT, SVCB/HTTPS (RFC 9460), DNS-SD (RFC 6763) and the agent-discovery drafts (DNS-AID, AID, ANS, AgentDNS, IETF DAWN)

A domain declares in its zone what it publishes. Current state of the drafts: DNS-AID is draft-mozleywilliams-dawn-dnsaid-00 (individual, 2026-11-02; it replaced the dnsop draft, which replaced BANDAID) and has been a Linux Foundation project since 2026-05-27. AID uses a TXT record at _agent.&lt;domain&gt;. ANS v2 (draft-narajala-courtney-ansv2-01, 2026-04-13) is an informational independent submission layering PKI, DANE and a transparency log. AgentDNS (draft-liang-agentdns-00) expired 2026-04-12. The IETF DAWN working group ("Discovery of Agents With Names") is active with an approved charter.

- **Publish.** For example a TXT record at _mad.pub.example carrying the manifest URL and an AID, or an existence record at &lt;base32(H)&gt;._mad.pub.example. DNS-AID style: an SVCB record at _index._agents.&lt;domain&gt; pointing at the organisation's catalog, whose contents the draft leaves out of scope. Requires control of the zone.
- **Fetch by digest.** DNS does not carry the definition; it points at HTTPS.
- **Reverse lookup.** None, and none is planned. DNS answers name to records only. DNS-AID states known-capability search "is out of the scope for DNS-AID". The DAWN charter excludes "AI resource indexing and searching across the wider Internet." At the DAWN BoF (2026-07-21) Eric Rescorla said "Internet scale is not plausible" and Jonathan Rosenberg said "This has to be narrowed down to be inside a single domain or organization." Zone files list delegations, not the contents of underscore records.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None natively. DNS-SD enumerates service types and instances inside a domain you already know.
- **Identity.** DNSSEC signs with the zone's key, which is still the domain's say-so. DNS-AID adds optional TLSA; ANS adds X.509 and a SCITT transparency log. A TXT record can carry an AID or OOBI pointer, which the KERI spec permits as untrusted discovery.
- **Digest fit.** Names are case-insensitive ("comparisons on name lookup for DNS queries should be case insensitive") and labels are at most 63 octets, so a base64url SAID is unsafe in a name; re-encode the raw 32 bytes as base32 (52 characters). TXT strings (each "limited to 255 bytes", value "opaque binary data" in DNS-SD) carry the SAID verbatim.
- **Governance.** Base record types are IETF standards. Agent-specific use is pre-standard: one chartered working group with deliverables in 2027 and 2028, and several competing individual drafts.
- **Publisher cost.** Free with DNS hosting, but many publishers on shared or static hosting do not control a zone, and SVCB support varies by DNS provider.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Node dns/promises for TXT; SVCB via DNS-over-HTTPS JSON or dns-packet (from memory; not checked). DNS-AID's reference SDK is Python.
- **Failure modes.** No reverse lookup at all. Wildcard DNS produces false positives. Records are small. Without DNSSEC the answer is spoofable.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. No dataset indexes TXT or SVCB contents across domains, so any reverse answer needs someone probing a list of domains.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - DAWN: charter-ietf-dawn-01 Approved, WG state Active; milestone to submit protocol specification(s) to the IESG by 2027-12-31. (as of 2026-10-22). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/dawn/about/
  - DAWN charter exclusions: "AI resource indexing and searching across the wider Internet." and "Identity management of AI agents, tools, and skills." (as of 2026-10-22). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/dawn/about/
  - DNS-AID current draft is individual with "no formal standing"; cross-domain search is left to "upper-layer work". (as of 2026-11-02). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-mozleywilliams-dawn-dnsaid/
  - Linux Foundation DNS-AID project launched with initial members Cloudflare, CSC, Equinix, GoDaddy, IDC, Indeed, Infoblox, Internet Systems Consortium and WWT; reference implementation is a Python SDK, CLI and MCP server. (as of 2026-05-27). Source: https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/linux-foundation-announces-dns-aid-project-to-advance-decentralized-ai-agent-discovery
  - RFC 9460 (SVCB and HTTPS records) is Standards Track, November 2023; RFC 6763 (DNS-SD) is Standards Track, February 2013. (as of 2023-11). Source: https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9460.html
- **Skeptic's re-score.** No change. The charter exclusion and the BoF remarks are verbatim in primary sources and rule out cross-domain search by design. The claim that no dataset holds TXT contents is too absolute (OpenINTEL measures apex TXT), but that covers fixed names and is not a query service, so reverse_lookup stays 0.

#### schema.org JSON-LD in HTML pages plus sitemaps, as read by general search engines

The most widely deployed machine-readable convention on the web. A page describes a thing in an embedded JSON-LD block; crawlers find pages through links and sitemaps announced in robots.txt.

- **Publish.** One HTML page per definition at a URL containing the digest, with the digest in the title and visible text and a JSON-LD block using identifier (schema.org: "The identifier property represents any kind of identifier for any kind of Thing, such as ISBNs, GTIN codes, UUIDs etc.", expected types PropertyValue, Text, URL). List the pages in a sitemap referenced from robots.txt. This page is also the human-readable reference, generated from the definition itself.
- **Fetch by digest.** The page links to the bytes; verify after fetch.
- **Reverse lookup.** A full-text query for the digest string on a general search engine (Google, Microsoft, Brave), then fetch and verify every hit. Tested this session with the vLEI schema SAIDs, which have been public for years: a bare quoted SAID returned zero relevant results in standard mode; in extended mode a bare quoted SAID returned four relevant pages (a GitHub repository, vlei.wiki, two W3C list posts) plus noise and missed the canonical GLEIF repository. So it works for well-linked hosts, incompletely and non-deterministically. Any agent with a web-search tool can run this query today.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A sitemap plus at least one inbound link from an already-crawled page. IndexNow pushes URLs to Bing and several smaller engines; Google does not take part (secondary source).
- **Identity.** None; a page is content on a domain. It can link to signed artifacts.
- **Digest fit.** Any string. The hyphen and underscore in base64url appear to hurt tokenised search, so also print a single-token alias (64-character hex or 52-character base32) in visible text. That recommendation is my inference from the test, not a documented rule.
- **Governance.** schema.org is a community vocabulary sponsored by the search engines; the Sitemap protocol is a de facto standard.
- **Publisher cost.** Static HTML and a sitemap.
- **TypeScript tooling.** schema-dts, jsonld, sitemap (from memory; not checked this session).
- **Failure modes.** Engines may never index thin pages on new domains. Results are unordered, unauditable and can be gamed by putting the string on any page. Completeness cannot be checked.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Nothing purpose-built is needed, but the engines owe no completeness or freshness and API access is being withdrawn.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "JSON-LD format is used on 43% of desktop home pages and 43% of mobile home pages." "Overall usage of structured data grew to 50% on both desktop and mobile in 2025". (as of 2025 crawl, published 2026-01-15). Source: https://almanac.httparchive.org/en/2025/seo
  - "In 2025, 85% of requests for robots.txt files returned valid 200 status codes, up from 84% in 2024." (as of 2025 crawl). Source: https://almanac.httparchive.org/en/2025/seo
  - Google's Custom Search JSON API "is closed to new customers and will be discontinued" on January 1, 2027; the Web Search Service API is "limited to partners." (as of 2026-09-11). Source: https://www.searchenginejournal.com/google-documents-partner-only-api-for-full-web-search-results/
  - Bing Search APIs were retired on 2025-08-11 (secondary sources). (as of 2025-08-11). Source: https://ppc.land/microsoft-ends-bing-search-apis-on-august-11-alternative-costs-40-483-more/
  - Brave Search API: "$5 per 1,000 requests"; "Every result comes from Brave's own independent index, crawled and ranked by Brave." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://brave.com/search/api/
  - IndexNow: "There are currently over 3.5 billion URLs submitted per day". (as of 2024-12-12). Source: https://blogs.bing.com/webmaster/December-2024/Look-How-Far-We-ve-Come-IIndexNow-Expands-Adoption-Across-Industries
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_trajectory 4 to 3: the markup is growing but the access a lookup tool needs is shrinking (Bing APIs retired, Google's JSON API closed to new customers and ending 2027-01-01). reverse_lookup 2 to 1: I replicated the test and got zero relevant hits in standard mode for two SAIDs that have been public for years, and seven pages without the canonical source in extended mode. No engine uses the JSON-LD identifier for lookup; only visible text matters. Other scores unchanged: this is the one mechanism every agent already reads.

#### Registry-less text conventions: llms.txt, security.txt and robots.txt directives

Examples of conventions that spread without any registry, and of what spreading does and does not buy.

- **Publish.** A line in /llms.txt linking the definition pages, or a robots.txt directive pointing at the manifest (ARD's Agentmap is one such).
- **Fetch by digest.** None; these are pointers.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. Each file is fetched per host; reverse answers come only from a crawl.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None beyond generic crawling.
- **Identity.** security.txt is the family's precedent for a key-signed file: "It is RECOMMENDED that a 'security.txt' file be digitally signed using an OpenPGP cleartext signature". In practice 11% of the files found were signed and half of those validated.
- **Digest fit.** Free text; any string.
- **Governance.** llms.txt is a community proposal with no standards body; security.txt is an Informational RFC.
- **Publisher cost.** A text file.
- **Failure modes.** The lesson from both: a convention spreads when a platform emits it by default, and being emitted is not the same as being read.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. A text file on each host has no reverse query.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - llms.txt: "8,598 of 100,000 top domains publish an llms.txt"; "At least 29.8% of the files carry a platform signature" with Shopify at 18.2% of all files; "No major AI vendor has documented consuming it". (as of 2026-09-13). Source: https://seomator.com/blog/llms-txt-adoption
  - llms.txt a year earlier: "We can see that the adoption rate is relatively low at 2%." (2.13% desktop, 2.10% mobile). (as of 2025 crawl). Source: https://almanac.httparchive.org/en/2025/seo
  - security.txt: "1.25% (12,510 domains) now hosting a security.txt file" among the top one million; "only 44% of these domains meet RFC standards"; "11% of domains have implemented a signature". (as of 2025-01-24). Source: https://www.uriports.com/blog/security-txt-in-2025/
  - RFC 9116 (security.txt) is Informational, April 2022. (as of 2022-04). Source: https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9116.html
- **Skeptic's re-score.** No change. Figures confirmed, with the caveat that the llms.txt numbers are not a like-for-like growth rate. One upgrade in usefulness that does not move the score: Common Crawl archives the robots.txt of every host it visits, so a robots.txt pointer is the emission an existing open dataset captures most completely, but someone still has to scan it.

#### did:web, did:webvh and did:webs (identity carried on plain HTTPS)

Identity documents hosted as static files. did:web resolves to /.well-known/did.json or &lt;path&gt;/did.json and trusts DNS and TLS. did:webvh and did:webplus add verifiable history. did:webs (Trust over IP, v0.10.3) is did:web plus a KERI AID: it hosts did.json and keri.cesr side by side.

- **Publish.** Host did.json and keri.cesr at the path derived from did:webs:&lt;host&gt;:&lt;path&gt;:&lt;AID&gt;. The stream must contain a designated-aliases ACDC, and "KERI-aware applications MAY use the KERI event stream for capabilities beyond DID document verification", so signed endorsement ACDCs can sit in the same stream or in sibling files.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not applicable; this carries keys, not definitions.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. This is not a discovery mechanism.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None.
- **Identity.** This is the family's answer to key-signed claims. did:webs: "Unlike did:web, this method's trust is not rooted in DNS, webmasters, X509, and certificate authorities." and "any given target system in isolation can be viewed by this method as a dumb, untrusted server of content." A first cut on did:web or bare TLS upgrades additively: "Any did:webs can be expressed as a did:web but the inverse is not true".
- **Digest fit.** AIDs and SAIDs are native CESR in did:webs.
- **Governance.** did:web is a W3C Credentials Community Group draft; did:webs is a Trust over IP specification; did:webvh has DIF's recommendation.
- **Publisher cost.** Static files after generation, but generating did:webs artifacts needs KERI tooling.
- **TypeScript tooling.** The did:webs reference resolver is Python on keripy; I know of no maintained TypeScript implementation (from memory; not checked this session).
- **Failure modes.** did:web alone is the domain's say-so with no key history. did:webs has few implementations and is not yet DIF-recommended.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. It provides no lookup of any kind; the zero scores for lookup mean "not offered", not "offered badly".
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - did:webvh and did:webplus are the first two DIF Recommended DID methods; "The initial three methods submitted to the process were did:webvh, did:webplus, and did:webs." (as of 2026-06-19). Source: https://blog.identity.foundation/two-recommended-did-methods/
  - did:webs "Specification Status: v0.10.3". (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-did-method-webs-specification/
  - did.json is a provisional IANA well-known registration (Credentials Community Group, 2023-01-31). (as of 2026-10-26). Source: https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris.xhtml
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Scored for did:webs, the member that earns keri_fit 5. adoption_trajectory 3 to 2: it is the only one of DIF's three initial submissions not recommended, and it is still v0.10.3. publisher_effort 3 to 2: producing keri.cesr and the designated-aliases ACDC needs the Python reference stack; the TypeScript package is 0.0.1 and only derives the identifier. If scored for did:webvh, trajectory would be 3 and keri_fit 2.

#### Existing open indexes used as-is: Common Crawl URL index, HTTP Archive well-known census, Certificate Transparency logs, zone files

Generic datasets that already observe the web and are run by independent parties: Common Crawl Foundation (monthly crawl, hosted on AWS S3), HTTP Archive (monthly crawl of popular origins, public in BigQuery), CT log operators (every publicly trusted certificate), ICANN's Centralized Zone Data Service and the gTLD registries (daily zone files).

- **Publish.** Nothing specific; these observe. To be seen: put the digest in a URL path listed in a sitemap (Common Crawl: "The crawler supports the Sitemap Protocol and utilizes any Sitemap announced in the robots.txt file."); use a well-known path that HTTP Archive's well-known.js metric already fetches; obtain a TLS certificate.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not applicable.
- **Reverse lookup.** Common Crawl: the columnar URL index has url_host_name, url_host_registered_domain and url_path columns and can be queried "via Amazon Athena or Apache Spark if your query involves broad or large-scale filtering"; a filter on url_path returns every captured host serving that digest path, and a prefix filter enumerates every definition seen. It is keyed by URL, not by content, it is monthly, and "we do not generally archive any entire website". The public CDX API "is frequently abused and therefore heavily rate limited." HTTP Archive: its well-known.js fetches ard.json, ai-catalog.json, agent-card.json, security.txt, did-configuration.json and others per origin, but for ard.json records entry count and types, not entries, so it yields which origins have a manifest, not which digests. CT logs yield hostnames only, and zone files yield delegations only; both give candidates that someone must still probe.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new host enters Common Crawl only if the crawl frontier reaches it; fetch lists are ranked by harmonic centrality, so an unlinked new publisher is likely absent. CT shows the hostname soon after issuance.
- **Identity.** None. Hits are untrusted hints, which matches KERI's OOBI model of verify-after-discovery.
- **Digest fit.** Common Crawl url_path matches any path string, so the SAID works verbatim. A CT route would need the digest in a hostname label (base32, 52 characters), one certificate per declaration; I do not recommend it and could not verify that any CT search service supports a leading-label query.
- **Governance.** Independent non-profits and CAs; none has any obligation to this use.
- **Publisher cost.** Nothing beyond a sitemap. The querier pays: a full scan of one monthly index partition is about 0.20 TiB, roughly one US dollar at Athena's list price as I remember it (not checked).
- **TypeScript tooling.** @aws-sdk/client-athena, or DuckDB over the Parquet files (from memory; not checked this session). Querying needs the querier's own AWS account.
- **Failure modes.** Monthly lag, sampled coverage, no content search, and coverage decisions outside your control. Common Crawl's page count has been falling (third-party analysis).
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. No purpose-built index is needed for a partial answer; coverage is a sample and freshness is a month or worse.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - August 2026 crawl CC-MAIN-2026-34: "contains 2.14 billion web pages (or 360 TiB of uncompressed content)", "Page captures are from 40.2 million hosts" "or 33.1 million registered domains", "crawled between August 7th and August 20th"; columnar URL index is 900 files, 0.20 TiB compressed. (as of 2026-08). Source: https://commoncrawl.org/blog/august-2026-crawl-archive-now-available
  - Columnar index schema includes url_host_name, url_host_registered_domain, url_path, url_query, fetch_status, content_digest (SHA-1) and subset (warc, crawldiagnostics, robotstxt). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/commoncrawl/cc-index-table/main/src/main/resources/schema/cc-index-schema-flat.json
  - HTTP Archive well-known.js fetches /.well-known/ard.json, ai-catalog.json, agent-card.json, security.txt, did-configuration.json, http-message-signatures-directory, ucp and others from each crawled origin. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/HTTPArchive/custom-metrics/blob/main/dist/well-known.js
  - Let's Encrypt shut down its RFC 6962 CT logs on 2026-02-28 and now operates static CT logs. (as of 2026-02-28). Source: https://letsencrypt.org/2025/08/14/rfc-6962-logs-eol
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 5 to 3: the datasets exist at scale, but nobody uses them as a lookup, agents do not read them, the free endpoint timed out on most of my requests and the dependable path needs an AWS account and SQL. adoption_trajectory 3 to 2: Cloudflare's managed robots.txt disallows CCBot, and HTTP Archive only crawls origins popular enough to be in the Chrome UX Report. reverse_lookup 2 to 1: keyed by exact URL, monthly, and 0 of 3 live well-known manifests were captured on hosts crawled that month. no_aggregator stays 4: these really are generic and independently run.

#### Webmention to the issuer (the web's one standard reverse-link primitive)

A W3C Recommendation (12 January 2017): "A Webmention is a notification that one URL links to another." The issuer of a definition becomes the rendezvous for it. Not in the brief's list; included because it is the only plain-HTTP standard that addresses the reverse direction.

- **Publish.** The relier's declaration page (source) links to the issuer's URL for H (target). The relier discovers the issuer's endpoint from a Link header or link element with rel webmention and POSTs form-encoded source and target. The issuer fetches the source, checks that it contains "an exact match of the target URL provided", and adds it to a public backlinks list for H.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not applicable.
- **Reverse lookup.** Ask the issuer's page for H for its list of sources. This is native for "who links to this URL" at one target and is answered by the issuer's own endpoint or a hosted receiver. It is complete only for parties that notified the issuer, and it requires that the definition or the introduction names the issuer's home.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new relier becomes findable the moment the issuer accepts its mention; no crawler is involved.
- **Identity.** The receiver checks only that the link exists, so it is the domain's say-so; the source document can itself be KERI-signed.
- **Digest fit.** The target is a URL with the digest in its path.
- **Governance.** W3C Recommendation; stable; niche (IndieWeb).
- **Publisher cost.** Sender: one HTTP POST. Issuer: one endpoint, for example a single Lambda.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Small community packages exist (not checked this session); the protocol is one form POST.
- **Failure modes.** The list dies or is censored with the issuer. Mention spam is a known problem. Mirrors and multiple issuers fragment the list.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. No third-party index, but each issuer must run a small receiving endpoint (or use a hosted one), which conflicts with running no service.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "W3C Recommendation 12 January 2017". (as of 2017-01-12). Source: https://www.w3.org/TR/webmention/
  - Third-party tracker figure from a search summary: about 2,019 live sites using the hosted receiver webmention.io. (as of 2026-11). Source: https://trends.builtwith.com/framework/Webmention.io
- **Skeptic's re-score.** no_aggregator 4 to 3: every issuer runs a receiving endpoint or rents one, which is a service per issuer. reverse_lookup 3 to 2: the Recommendation standardises sending and verifying only, with no way to read back the list, and a stranger holding H still needs the issuer's URL, which a digest does not carry.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (dns-web)

The skeptic re-checked 27 claims; 13 held as stated. The rest:

- *partly* · Static digest-in-path publication under a well-known URI (RFC 8615; precedents RFC 6920 ni and the KERI well-known OOBI) · claim: Reverse route (b): an Athena query on Common Crawl's columnar index with url_path = '/.well-known/&lt;suffix&gt;/&lt;H&gt;' returns the hosts serving that digest path; a sitemap gets CCBot to the digest URLs.
  - Correction: The query is well formed but the researcher ran none, and in the one observable analog capture was 0 of 3 on hosts that were crawled that month. Treat expected recall as near zero unless the digest URL is linked from crawled HTML and selected. Sitemap-listed digest URLs remain untested by either of us.
  - Checked: Schema confirmed (url_path, url_host_name, subset, crawl present). Then tested the nearest live analog on the Common Crawl CDX API for CC-MAIN-2026-43 (October 2026). Positive controls returned captures for zapier.com/, huggingface.co/, airtable.com/ and github.com/robots.txt. The same index returned "No Captures found" for zapier.com, huggingface.co and airtable.com /.well-known/ai-catalog.json, all three of which I fetched live today as JSON, and also for github.com and securitytxt.org security.txt and apple.com's apple-app-site-association.
- *partly* · Static digest-in-path publication under a well-known URI (RFC 8615; precedents RFC 6920 ni and the KERI well-known OOBI) · claim: Reverse route (a): the client walks peers lists breadth-first from one introduction and this needs nobody's infrastructure but the publishers'.
  - Correction: The walk only follows edges humans typed. The analog with eleven vendors behind it was found "specified, implemented and empty", with "zero DNS records", one robots.txt directive and one link tag. Nothing tells an issuer who relies on its definition, so the reverse edges exist only by out-of-band notice.

- *partly* · Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) v0.91 with AI Catalog (the draft's current choice) · claim: Probe of 43,834 domains (37,751 reachable) found nine publishers; "None is fully conformant"; "Federation is specified, implemented and empty".

Today /.well-known/ard.json returned a JSON manifest on 1 of 21 hosts (clickhouse.com). The predecessor path returned JSON on 6 (github.com, huggingface.co, zapier.com, clickhouse.com, airtable.com, datarobot.com). roboflow.com now returns 404 on both. google.com, microsoft.com, databricks.com, salesforce.com and snowflake.com returned 404; cisco, godaddy and nvidia returned 403 to my client. agenticresourcediscovery.org and ai-catalog.io serve neither file.

- *partly* · WebFinger (RFC 7033) · claim: RFC 7033 is Standards Track with a permanent IANA registration dated 2013-03-15; about 10,475 Mastodon instances answer WebFinger.

- *partly* · DNS records: TXT, SVCB/HTTPS (RFC 9460), DNS-SD (RFC 6763) and the agent-discovery drafts (DNS-AID, AID, ANS, AgentDNS, IETF DAWN) · claim: No dataset indexes TXT or SVCB contents across domains.
  - Correction: Too absolute. OpenINTEL actively measures a fixed query set that includes TXT for hundreds of millions of domains daily (per paper summaries; I did not read the papers or confirm access terms). It covers fixed names such as the apex, not an underscore label like _mad, and it is a file dump, not a query service. The practical conclusion stands.
  - Checked: Search results describing OpenINTEL's published measurement papers and the openintel.nl data page.
- *partly* · schema.org JSON-LD in HTML pages plus sitemaps, as read by general search engines · claim: Programmatic search is contracting: Bing Search APIs retired 2025-08-11; Google's Custom Search JSON API is closed to new customers and ends 2027-01-01 with a partner-only replacement; Brave charges $5 per 1,000 requests.
  - Correction: Dates and prices are right. Google names Vertex AI Search (up to 50 domains) as the alternative; the partner-only Web Search Service API is not designated as the successor. Brave also includes $5 of free credit a month.

- *partly* · Registry-less text conventions: llms.txt, security.txt and robots.txt directives · claim: llms.txt is on 8,598 of the top 100,000 domains (2026-09-13), up from about 2% a year earlier; security.txt is on 1.25% of the top million with 11% signed.
  - Correction: All figures are quoted correctly. The two llms.txt numbers use different populations (Cloudflare Radar top 100,000 versus all HTTP Archive sites), so they are not a growth rate, and the 8.6% comes from one SEO vendor's single-day census. The security.txt data is from January 2025.
  - Checked: The seomator study, the Web Almanac 2025 SEO chapter, and the URIports post.
- *partly* · did:web, did:webvh and did:webs (identity carried on plain HTTPS) · claim: No mechanism in this family natively carries a key-signed endorsement; everything is the domain's say-so except did:webs.
  - Correction: Three in-family conventions carry key-signed statements at well-known paths: DID Configuration (a Domain Linkage Credential signed by the DID's controller, provisional IANA registration), OpenID Federation (signed entity statements and Trust Marks), and AI Catalog's trust manifests and catalog signature. The claim holds only in the narrower sense that none of them roots the signer in a self-certifying identifier.
  - Checked: OpenID Federation 1.0 (raw spec text), the DIF Well-Known DID Configuration page, and ai-catalog.io.
- *partly* · did:web, did:webvh and did:webs (identity carried on plain HTTPS) · claim: No maintained TypeScript did:webs implementation is known; the reference resolver is Python.
  - Correction: @gleif-it/did-webs-ts exists at version 0.0.1 and only derives the identifier; it does not resolve or generate the hosted artifacts. The reference resolver is Python (did-webs-resolver 0.3.5 on PyPI).

- *partly* · Existing open indexes used as-is: Common Crawl URL index, HTTP Archive well-known census, Certificate Transparency logs, zone files · claim: Fetch lists are ranked by harmonic centrality, so an unlinked new publisher is likely absent; a sitemap is enough for CCBot to see a publisher.
  - Correction: The FAQ says only that the data is "a sample of the web" and a "randomly selected subset" of each site; harmonic centrality appears in the web graph statistics and a 2026 paper, not in the FAQ. Missed risk: Cloudflare's managed robots.txt lists CCBot among the user agents it disallows, so a publisher behind that setting is invisible to Common Crawl whatever its sitemap says.
  - Checked: Common Crawl FAQ, web graph release notes, and Cloudflare's managed robots.txt documentation (last updated 2026-11-02).
- *partly* · Existing open indexes used as-is: Common Crawl URL index, HTTP Archive well-known census, Certificate Transparency logs, zone files · claim: Let's Encrypt shut down its RFC 6962 CT logs on 2026-02-28; a CT route would need the digest in a hostname label and no CT search is known to support a leading-label query.
  - Correction: The shutdown date is confirmed. On crt.sh, community reports say leading-label wildcard matching is inconsistent and slow queries are cancelled; I found no primary documentation, so the CT route stays unverified and should stay rejected.
  - Checked: Let's Encrypt's post of 2025-08-14 and a search on crt.sh query behaviour.
- *partly* · Webmention to the issuer (the web's one standard reverse-link primitive) · claim: Webmention is a W3C Recommendation of 12 January 2017; the receiver checks for "an exact match of the target URL"; asking the issuer's page for its list of sources is a native reverse lookup at one target.
  - Correction: Status and quotes are exact. The Recommendation standardises sending and verification only: "the receiver MAY publish content from the source page". There is no standard way to read back the accepted mentions, so a stranger must scrape the issuer's HTML or use one vendor's API. The webmention.io site count is unverified.
  - Checked: Raw text of https://www.w3.org/TR/webmention/.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (dns-web)

- **OpenID Federation 1.0: signed entity statements, Trust Marks and the Trust Marked Entities Listing endpoint.** It is the standardised form of the sketch's "issuer shortcut" and it contradicts the claim that nothing in the family carries a key-signed endorsement. The specification is Final (17 February 2026). An entity publishes a signed statement at /.well-known/openid-federation; a Trust Mark is a signed JWT from an issuer saying an entity meets a named requirement; and section 8.5 defines a GET with trust_mark_type that returns a JSON array of the entities holding it. With the digest in the trust mark type, that is "who holds H" answered by the issuer with signed evidence. Limits: the issuer must run a query endpoint, entity identifiers are HTTPS URLs, and the signatures are JWS, not CESR, so KERI fit is low. Deployment evidence (Italy's national federation, an eduGAIN pilot) is from secondary sources. It does not give a reverse lookup without the issuer's cooperation, so it does not change the conclusion. Source: https://openid.net/specs/openid-federation-1_0.html
- **DIF Well-Known DID Configuration (/.well-known/did-configuration.json).** A Domain Linkage Credential signed by the DID's controller binds a DID to an origin. It is a provisional IANA registration and HTTP Archive's well-known.js already fetches it. It is a ready-made, registered bridge from a domain-based first cut to key-based identity, so the sketch need not invent its own manifest-signature format for step 6. Source: https://identity.foundation/well-known-did-configuration/resources/did-configuration
- **Common Crawl's robots.txt archive as the publisher census.** Common Crawl stores the robots.txt of every host it visits as a separate dataset (October 2026: 100,000 files, 0.15 TiB compressed), independent of which pages it samples; I confirmed github.com/robots.txt in the October index. A pointer line in robots.txt (ARD already defines Agentmap) is therefore the one publisher emission an existing open dataset captures for all roughly 41 million visited hosts, whereas my test showed unlinked well-known files are not captured. It changes what the publisher should emit, not the verdict: someone still runs a monthly batch scan and publishes the result. Source: https://commoncrawl.org/blog/october-2026-crawl-archive-now-available
- **Backlink infrastructure: Common Crawl host and domain web graphs, Google Search Console and Bing Webmaster link reports.** This is the web's actual reverse-link index, and the researcher considered only Webmention. Common Crawl publishes host-level and domain-level link graphs (May to July 2026: 240.4 million host nodes, 3.7 billion edges). Search Console shows a site owner its top 1,000 linking sites, and Bing Webmaster Tools shows a sampled backlink list for any site (both from secondary SEO sources, not re-checked first-hand). An issuer could learn its reliers from these without running an endpoint. The open graph is host-level and quarterly and the owner reports are sampled, so it is a hint source, not a lookup. Source: https://commoncrawl.org/blog/host--and-domain-level-web-graphs-may-june-and-july-2026
- **Active DNS measurement datasets (OpenINTEL) for apex TXT records.** It qualifies the researcher's statement that no dataset holds TXT contents across domains. A declaration placed in an apex TXT record, the way site-verification tokens are, would be collected daily for the zones OpenINTEL measures. I could not confirm access terms or current coverage, it covers fixed names only, and it is a file dump that someone must scan, so it does not change the conclusion. Source: https://openintel.nl/data/

- All page reads went through a fetch tool that summarises; quotations are as that tool returned them and I could not compare them against raw page text.
- The ARD specification text was read through that summarising tool from the raw GitHub markdown; the quoted sentences and the v0.91 / Proposal / August 26, 2026 header are as it reported.

- TypeScript library names in ts_tooling are from memory and were not checked for current maintenance.
- The statement that vLEI tooling serves schema bytes at /oobi/&lt;SAID&gt; is from memory; the KERI spec text I retrieved shows the well-known AID form and a sad: URI scheme, not that path.

- Whether the IANA NI registry would accept a BLAKE3 entry, and the status of any BLAKE3 IETF draft, were not checked.

<a id="keri-native"></a>

## KERI-native mechanisms

**Researcher's summary.** KERI has no native reverse lookup: nothing in KERI, ACDC, OOBI, did:webs or TSP answers 'who else declares SAID X' without somebody crawling and indexing, and that is deliberate (discovery is out of band, disclosure is need-to-know). I checked this in the reference implementation: witness and watcher queries are keyed by AID or registry id (routes logs, ksn, mbx, tels, tsn), and the only by-schema index (Reger.schms) covers credentials the local node already holds. What the family does supply is a forward fetch-and-verify by SAID (data OOBI at /oobi/{SAID}) and a strong statement format: an untargeted, self-issued ACDC ('to whom it may concern') with a TEL registry for revocation; did:webs is the publication precedent, since its designated-aliases ACDC sits in a web-hosted keri.cesr stream and is found by a pinned schema SAID. Percolated discovery is a sharing principle, not a query: it gives a walk from one introduction that reaches only what somebody linked, and IPEX apply, the one by-schema question in the stack, is addressed to a single known peer. The flagship deployment (vLEI) publishes centrally: schema SAIDs in a GLEIF governance document, GitHub repositories and GLEIF's web server, and Qualified vLEI Issuers must report issued credentials to GLEIF, which publishes them on the LEI's page; the community registry WebOfTrust/schema is a pull-request-curated registry.json last pushed 2023-11-21. KERI's own DHT discovery project was archived on 2023-04-21, and the specification now says 'KERI does not, therefore, need its own dedicated discovery network; OOBIs with URLs will do'. Deployment is small: GLEIF's page listed 9 Qualified vLEI Issuers, keripy has 81 GitHub stars, signify-ts had 3,950 npm downloads in the last month, 40 repositories carry the GitHub topic 'keri', I found no published vLEI issuance count, and no mainstream AI agent tooling reads OOBIs or CESR. The smallest thing that must be added is a place to post unsigned OOBIs keyed by H, that is (URL, H) pairs, on infrastructure that already exists; the KERI-signed declaration then rides as payload and is verified after fetch. A test on 2026-11-08 shows where such a place already exists: a general web search for the most-deployed ACDC schema SAID returned no relevant hit, while GitHub code search for the same 44-character string returned 415 hits across at least 15 owners. So this family is the right identity and signature layer, added later (did:web to did:webs is exactly the domain-first, keys-later path), and the wrong discovery substrate.

**Researcher's best pick.** None for the reverse lookup: no KERI-native mechanism answers 'who else declares H' without an index. As the identity and statement layer the strongest combination is an untargeted, self-issued ACDC declaration with a TEL registry, published in a did:webs-layout static location, with the definition served as a SAID-verified data OOBI and peer introductions for a single-introduction walk. The lookup keyed by H has to come from outside this family.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** KERI gives verifiable statements and no place to find them, so building 'on KERI' leaves the crux untouched: the walk reaches only publishers somebody already linked, which is the hand-kept seed list redistributed into every publisher's peers file, and completing it needs an outside index whose most effective tested instance (GitHub code search) is a single company's service. On the maintainer's second value the family fails by orders of magnitude: about 9 qualified issuers in the one production ecosystem, 81 stars on the reference implementation, 3,950 monthly downloads of the only established TypeScript client, no published issuance count, and no AI agent tooling that reads OOBIs or CESR. Publisher effort for the signed form is high (AID, registry, anchoring, stream export, repeated on rotation), the pure-TypeScript verification libraries were published days ago by one maintainer, and the flagship KERI ecosystem itself solved this same problem with a central registry document, a central repository and mandatory reporting to GLEIF. Committing to KERI now would spend the project's effort on identity plumbing while discovery, the stated goal, still depends on something else.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** The report can be trusted on its facts and on its conclusion: nearly every figure reproduced exactly (stars, downloads, code search counts, issuer count, spec statuses, code line references), and the central finding, that no KERI mechanism answers 'who else declares H' without an index, holds in the reference implementation. It was wrong or incomplete in four places that matter: GLEIF's production well-known OOBI URLs and catalog do exist; the well-known path question is resolvable (three variants are live); the web-search test is engine-dependent; and the GitHub code search lookup is weaker than presented (login-gated, non-exhaustive, blind past character 1,024 of a line, and in the live test it surfaced no actual issuer). Trust this family for two things only: the SAID as the digest rule, and, later and additively, an untargeted ACDC as the signed form of a declaration. Do not build discovery on it. If the maintainer wants a KERI-compatible publisher layout today, copy GLEIF's (host-meta.json, a catalog, and /.well-known/oobi/{said}/index.json) and take the lookup keyed by H from another family. The one missed mechanism with a real public index, CIP-0170 on Cardano, is too small and too far from agent tooling to change that.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

No. The best pick (static files plus a generic index keyed by the literal SAID, plus a peers walk) does not give a reverse lookup with nothing purpose-built in the sense the maintainer set; it gives leads from one company's index.

Walked as a stranger holding only H:

1. Find anything that mentions H. Nothing in KERI answers this: keripy's query routes are keyed by AID or registry id (confirmed in code). General web search is unreliable: the session backend and Bing returned nothing relevant for the most-deployed schema SAID, Brave returned about ten HTML pages that mention it, and no engine returned the URL that actually serves it (www.gleif.org/.well-known/oobi/&lt;SAID&gt;/index.json returns 200 but is in no result, and the Wayback index has no capture). That leaves GitHub code search: run by GitHub alone, free, accepts any text in any public repository's default branch, but requires a login or token (10 requests a minute, 1,000 results by API, 100 in the web UI), is documented as 'not all code is indexed' and 'Exhaustive search is not supported', and truncates lines over 1,024 characters. I reproduced the truncation on a one-line schema file: a SAID at character 893 is found, one at character 1,509 is not. There is no durability commitment.

2. Turn hits into declarers. For the Qualified vLEI Issuer schema, the 415 hits are demo scripts, docs, fixtures and mirrors; none of the nine organisations that actually issue under it appears in the first 100. A path convention fixes precision: the same SAID with path:.well-known returned 6 hits in 2 repositories, both mirrors of GLEIF's one catalog. GLEIF's own production site is invisible because it is not a GitHub repository.

3. Fetch and verify. HTTPS plus a Blake3 SAID recompute works today. Verifying a signed declaration in TypeScript depends on keri-ts (one maintainer, 642 downloads last month) or signify-ts with a running KERIA agent, and did:webs has not defined protocol v2 streams.

4. Completeness is unknowable. A publisher on its own domain, GitLab or S3 is found only by the peers walk, which reaches only publishers somebody already listed.

What would have to exist or be run: a published path convention with H in the path or on its own short line; a GitHub token for every querier, human or agent; a TypeScript verifier; and acceptance that non-GitHub publishers need an introduction. For an answer that is complete and independent of one company, somebody must run an index, or the (URL, H) pointer must be posted to a multi-operator store from another family. The Nostr option was checked only against NIP-01, which says tags are indexed 'as a convention' and promises neither acceptance nor retention.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

The family contributes nothing to the function the project exists for, and its one empirical demonstration cuts the other way. For the most-deployed KERI schema SAID, the proposed lookup returns 415 files that are scripts, docs, fixtures and mirrors, with none of the nine real issuers among the first 100 hits; the authoritative publisher's production site is invisible to it; the index needs a GitHub login, is documented as non-exhaustive, and silently misses a SAID past character 1,024 of a line, which is where references sit in compact SAID-serialised JSON. So after adopting the whole KERI stack the maintainer still depends on a single company's best-effort index, or runs one.

What KERI adds in exchange is cost and churn. Three different well-known OOBI paths are live at once (spec head, keripy, GLEIF production) and the IANA registration is drafted but not filed. did:webs does not yet say how to handle the protocol v2 streams keripy now emits. The pure TypeScript libraries have one maintainer and about 640 downloads a month. Every publisher, not only the maintainer, would need an AID, a registry and a regenerated event stream. And the one production KERI ecosystem answers 'who issues under this schema' with a mandatory report to GLEIF, a central registry.

On wide adoption the gap is orders of magnitude: 9 qualified issuers (one added in 2026), 81 stars on the reference implementation, 3,950 monthly downloads of the established TypeScript client, no published issuance count, and no agent tooling that reads OOBIs or CESR beyond an individual IETF draft and demo scripts.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (keri-native)

Keep H as the workspace's SAID (Blake3-256, CESR code E, 44 characters) and make sure the literal string appears in every published artifact, because the lookup depends on it.

What a publisher writes (static files on any host, or a public git repository with pages hosting; no service):
1. Definition bytes at &lt;base&gt;/oobi/&lt;H&gt;. Anyone who relies on H may mirror it; every copy self-verifies.
2. &lt;base&gt;/.well-known/mad/declarations.json, unsigned (it is a list of OOBIs, which by KERI's own rule need no signature): for each declaration {definition: H, stance: issues | relies-on | implements | endorses, oobi: URL of the bytes, since: date, acdc: SAID of the signed declaration once one exists}, plus peers: [{url or did:webs, definitions: [H, ...]}] naming other publishers this one knows to declare the same definitions. The peers list is the link a walker follows from one publisher to the next, which the ARD draft lacked.
3. Later, additively: &lt;base&gt;/&lt;AID&gt;/did.json and keri.cesr (did:webs layout) holding the key event log, the registry events and one untargeted ACDC per declaration: issuer i = the publisher's AID, schema s = SAID of a 'MAD definition declaration' schema, attributes a = {d, dt, definition: H, stance}, registry ri (rd in ACDC version 2). Revoking the ACDC withdraws the declaration. Step 2's file keeps working unchanged; verified entries simply outrank domain-only ones.

Queries:
- Fetch H: GET /oobi/&lt;H&gt; from any known publisher, recompute the SAID, compare.
- Who else declares H: (a) walk outward from the publishers already known, starting from one introduction, following peers and keeping entries for H; (b) widen with an existing generic index keyed by the literal string H (tested: GitHub code search; a relay tag query or whatever the other families find stronger); (c) treat every result as an unverified OOBI, fetch that publisher's declarations, verify the SAID and, where present, the ACDC against the key event log and registry, and report each declarer as key-verified, domain-only or mention-only.
- Enumeration and human-readable reference: each publisher's declarations.json enumerates what it stands behind, and a client-side renderer (MAD itself, or one static page) draws the reference for any H from the definition bytes, so no curated site holds the content.

What the maintainer still runs: no service. One TypeScript library and CLI with three commands: publish (compute SAIDs with @noble/hashes, write the files), resolve H, and who H (walk, optional index query, verify). Key-based signing can start with keripy's kli or the new keri-ts tooling on the maintainer's machine and never needs a hosted agent.

One thing to settle first: /Users/seriouscoderone/code/bpm/mad/src/services/crypto.ts line 15 describes its encoding as 'not a KERI/SAID implementation', and /Users/seriouscoderone/code/bpm/mad-discovery/MAD-DISCOVERY-SPEC.md specifies CIDv1 with sha2-256 over JCS; the digest rule must be one thing before anything is published.

### Mechanisms (keri-native)

#### OOBI and data OOBI (SAID-addressed fetch)

An out-of-band introduction is an unsigned (URL, AID-or-SAID) pair. A data OOBI maps a URL to a SAID-addressed document. It is defined in an annex of the Trust over IP KERI specification v1.1; ACDC schemas are resolved this way in practice.

- **Publish.** Host the definition bytes as a static file at &lt;base&gt;/oobi/&lt;H&gt; on any web host. keripy recognises exactly that path shape (DOOBI_RE = '\A/oobi/(?P&lt;said&gt;[^/]+)\Z', /Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/end/ending.py:56). Nothing is signed, and nothing says 'I issue / rely on / endorse': a data OOBI only says 'bytes for H are here'. Cost is static hosting.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET the URL, then recompute the SAID: replace the embedded SAID field with 44 '#' characters, take Blake3-256, CESR-encode with code E, compare with the SAID in the URL. Any mirror is as good as the origin. From the code (/Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/app/oobiing.py:530-563): for a JSON body on a bare-SAID OOBI keripy accepts only a JSON Schema (Schemer, SAID in $id) or a rpy message; a CESR body is fed to the KERI parser. A MAD definition therefore needs its own small resolver, which is a few lines of TypeScript.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. An OOBI is forward-only: you must already hold the URL. No KERI node answers 'which URLs or AIDs are associated with SAID X'. The specification hands this to outside infrastructure ('enables any internet and web search infrastructure to act as an out-of-band infrastructure'). Tested 2026-11-08: a general web search for the exact Qualified vLEI Issuer schema SAID returned no relevant hit; GitHub code search for the same string returned total_count 415.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Out of band by definition: QR code, configuration file (the 'durls' bucket), a link, a message. A new publisher is findable only by whoever was handed its OOBI, or through whatever third-party index happens to have crawled the URL.
- **Identity.** None at this layer ('An OOBI itself is not signed or otherwise authenticatable by KERI'). Byte integrity comes from the SAID; authorship must come from a separate KERI-signed statement.
- **Digest fit.** Native. The 44-character Blake3-256 CESR SAID is the path segment, unchanged. CESR also has codes for SHA2-256 (I), SHA3-256 (H) and Blake2b-256 (F), but the result is still a SAID (digest over the serialization with the SAID field dummied out), never a CID over the final bytes.
- **Governance.** Trust over IP (Linux Foundation) KERI Suite Working Group; specification v1.1 with a Zenodo DOI. Not an IETF, W3C or ISO standard in its own right. The local copy says /.well-known/oobi/{aid} while keripy and the live page show /.well-known/keri/oobi/{aid}; neither suffix is in the IANA registry.
- **Publisher cost.** One static file per definition. No keys, no service.
- **TypeScript tooling.** SAID computation needs only Blake3 and base64url: @noble/hashes (456,833,624 npm downloads 2026-10-09 to 2026-11-07) has blake3. signify-ts 0.4.1 and cesr-ts 2.0.0 (published 2026-11-07) carry Saider/Diger primitives.
- **Failure modes.** Link rot when the host goes away (no replication unless others mirror). No poisoning of content, since bytes self-verify, but anyone can serve H, which says nothing about endorsement. A spec caveat: an OOBI can be used for denial of service but not for cache poisoning. No privacy issue for public definitions.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. The forward fetch needs no aggregator. The reverse lookup does not exist in the mechanism, so somebody must crawl and index, or an outside search index must be used.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - KERI specification status is v1.1 (Trust over IP KERI Suite Working Group; a PDF is titled 'KERI V1.1 2026 01 21'). OOBI is an annex of that document, not a separate standard. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - KERI does not, therefore, need its own dedicated discovery network; OOBIs with URLs will do. (as of 2026-08-01 (local copy, lines 6296-6297 of /Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keri-claude/scripts/markdown/keri-specification.md)). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - A secondary use case for OOBIs is to provide service endpoints or URIs for SAD (items identifier by their SAID). (as of 2026-08-01 (local copy)). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - The IANA well-known URI registry, as returned by my fetch, has no 'keri' or 'oobi' suffix (and no 'ai-catalog' or 'ard.json'); it does have did.json (provisional, 2023-01-31) and agent-card.json (Linux Foundation, permanent, 2025-08-01). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.iana.org/assignments/well-known-uris/well-known-uris.xhtml
  - GitHub code search for the exact Qualified vLEI Issuer schema SAID string returns total_count 415; the first 100 hits span 37 repositories and 15 owners (WebOfTrust, GLEIF-IT, veridian-id, healthKERI, cardano-foundation, berkmancenter, HL7, THCLab, keri-foundation and others). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/search?q=%22EBfdlu8R27Fbx-ehrqwImnK-8Cm79sqbAQ4MmvEAYqao%22&type=code
- **Skeptic's re-score.** One change. publisher_effort 5 to 4: the path is not settled (spec head /.well-known/oobi/{aid}, keripy /.well-known/keri/oobi/ and /oobi/{said}, GLEIF production /.well-known/oobi/{said}/index.json) and keripy rejects responses by Content-Type, so a bare extensionless file is not interoperable everywhere. Other scores stand: forward-only is confirmed in code, and the IANA suffix is drafted but not filed.

#### Witnesses and watchers

Witnesses are designated in an AID's key event log to receipt and serve its events. Watchers are chosen by a validator to keep copies of other parties' logs and detect duplicity.

- **Publish.** A party could anchor a digest seal for H, or for the SAID of a declaration about H, in an interaction event of its own key event log; its witnesses receipt it. That needs an AID with witnesses and tooling to rotate and interact.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not a content store for definitions. Witnesses serve key event logs, transaction event logs and receipts, not arbitrary documents.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. In keripy the KEL query routes are logs, ksn and mbx, all keyed by AID (qry['i']); logs accepts an anchor 'a' only to check that the named AID has anchored that seal (/Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/core/eventing.py:5566-5587). TEL routes are tels and tsn, keyed by registry id (/Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/vdr/eventing.py:1602-1619). No route is keyed by a digest across AIDs. A watcher holds the raw material (seals in the logs it watches), but no implementation indexes or exposes it, and I found no public production watcher network.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A witness OOBI (/oobi/{aid}/witness/{wit}) handed over out of band.
- **Identity.** The strongest the stack has: a commitment anchored in the principal's key event log and receipted by witnesses.
- **Digest fit.** A digest seal carries the SAID unchanged.
- **Governance.** Defined in the KERI specification v1.1. Witness pools are operated per controller or per ecosystem; there is no shared public pool with open governance.
- **Publisher cost.** An AID, a witness pool (own or rented), and KERI software to anchor events. Hours of setup; a running witness is a service.
- **TypeScript tooling.** No maintained TypeScript witness or watcher that I found. keri-ts 2.0.0 (npm, published 2026-11-07, 642 downloads in the last month) describes itself as a 'KERI TypeScript protocol and runtime library'; maturity not assessed.
- **Failure modes.** Witness outage makes the log unavailable. No spam surface, because nothing is queryable by content. Building the missing index would recreate the central chokepoint.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. A watcher extended with a seal-digest index would be a new component that somebody must write and run; run for these definitions, it is a purpose-built aggregator under another name.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Watchers are not AID-specific; instead, they watch the KELs of any or all AIDs that are shared with them. (as of 2026-08-01 (local copy, lines 582-583)). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - vLEI Ecosystem Governance Framework v4.0, Technical Requirements Part 1 (2026-03-25): GLEIF must set up and maintain its own witness pool; validators may choose any number of watchers. Taken from a search summary; the PDF itself returned 404 to my fetch. (as of 2026-03-25). Source: https://www.gleif.org/media/pages/organizational-identity/become-a-vlei-issuer-qvi/vlei-ecosystem-governance-framework/8262a823f3-1774607082/2026-03-25_vlei-egf-v4.0-technical-requirements-part-1-keri-infrastructure_v1.4_final.pdf
  - GLEIF's testnet documentation lists five witnesses (wit1 to wit5.testnet.gleif.org) and one KERIA server, all operated by GLEIF. (as of 2025-07-11). Source: https://www.gleif.org/newsroom/events/gleif-vlei-hackathon-2025/2025-07-11-gleif-testnet-schema-server-documentation-v1.0._final.pdf
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. Query routes keyed only by AID or registry id are confirmed in keripy, and Cardano Foundation's CIP-0170 independently says watcher networks are not yet widely deployed.

#### Percolated discovery (PID, SPED, just-in-time / need-to-know) and IPEX apply

KERI's stated discovery model: verifiable information spreads along the exchanges that need it, and each party passes on what it has already verified. It is a principle with supporting message types, not a query service.

- **Publish.** Nothing is published to a shared place. A party hands OOBIs and proofs to its counterparties at the moment of an exchange. The nearest thing to a by-schema question is the IPEX apply message, which carries 'Schema or its SAID, Attribute field label list' and is sent to one known peer.
- **Fetch by digest.** The counterparty supplies the document or an OOBI for it; verification is by SAID as above.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not native. As specified, you can only ask peers you already know, one at a time. If each publisher also listed introductions (OOBIs) for other parties it knows to declare H, a client could walk outward from one introduction: fetch A's list, verify, follow A's peers, repeat. That walk needs no index and is local-first, but it reaches only what somebody linked, it is never known to be complete, and its cost grows with the graph. KERI does not define such a peers list; it would be a new convention.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher becomes findable when one already-known publisher lists it. Until then it is invisible. This is the same seed-list problem, moved into every publisher's file.
- **Identity.** Everything percolated is end-verifiable against the originating AID's key event log, so intermediaries need not be trusted.
- **Digest fit.** SAIDs are carried unchanged.
- **Governance.** Part of the KERI and ACDC specifications v1.1 (Trust over IP).
- **Publisher cost.** None beyond taking part in exchanges; a peers-list convention would add upkeep of that list.
- **TypeScript tooling.** signify-ts 0.4.1 exposes oobis() and IPEX through a KERIA agent; a static-file walk needs only fetch plus SAID and signature checks.
- **Failure modes.** Partition: publishers nobody links to are never found. Link spam in peers lists costs clients fetches, though unverifiable claims are discarded. Offline publishers drop out of the walk. Good privacy, which is the design goal and the reason the global question is unanswered.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** no. By design nothing is aggregated; the price is that the question 'who else, globally' is not answered at all.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Percolation means that each discoverer, in turn, may share what it discovers with any subsequent discoverers. (as of 2026-08-01 (local copy, lines 6658-6659)). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - This avoids the need to have a dedicated global infrastructure for the discovery of verifiable data and the associated proofs. (as of 2026-08-01 (local copy, lines 6674-6675)). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - The KERI DHT discovery project is archived: 'This repository was archived by the owner on Apr 21, 2023. It is now read-only.' Its description is 'DHT mechanism for KERI written in Py'. (as of 2023-04-21). Source: https://github.com/decentralized-identity/keri-dht-py
  - Comment recorded in a community note ('Peer DIDs with KERI', speaker not named on the page): 'we started down the path of using a DHT for global discovery with KERI' and 'all I needed was an OOBI that was in a web searchable location'. (as of undated). Source: https://hackmd.io/@dhh1128/S1PC_EWlq
