- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. keripy's '/oobis' exchange message shows OOBI forwarding exists between known peers, but nothing publishes a crawlable list, so the walk still needs a new convention and still reaches only what somebody linked.

#### did:webs and did:keri

did:webs is did:web plus KERI: a domain hosts did.json and a keri.cesr event stream for an AID, and the resolver verifies the stream rather than trusting the domain. did:keri names the same AID with no location, so it needs an OOBI to resolve.

- **Publish.** Static files under https://&lt;host&gt;/&lt;path&gt;/&lt;AID&gt;/: did.json and keri.cesr. The stream carries the key event log, the registry events and a self-issued 'designated aliases' ACDC. A MAD declaration ACDC could be added to the same stream or a sibling file; the specification does not define that, so it would be a convention. Cost is static hosting plus KERI tooling to produce the stream.
- **Fetch by digest.** did:webs does not fetch arbitrary documents by digest; pair it with a data OOBI on the same host.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. did:webs makes one publisher's full signed claim set fetchable from one URL, which is what a walk or a crawler needs, but finding the publishers is left to the web. The specification says 'web searching could yield either the current location of the DID document, or a copy'; my web-search test for a SAID string found nothing.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A domain name or URL passed out of band; for did:keri, an OOBI.
- **Identity.** This is the additive path the maintainer asked about. A first cut can be did:web (domain authority, did.json registered at IANA); adding keri.cesr turns the same URL into did:webs with key-based verification, and 'did:webs DIDs MUST provide the corresponding did:keri as an alsoKnownAs identifier'.
- **Digest fit.** AIDs and SAIDs appear unchanged in paths and in the stream.
- **Governance.** Trust over IP KERI Suite Working Group draft (v0.10.3), with open questions about KERI/ACDC version 2. did:keri is listed in the W3C DID methods note (per a search summary); I could not establish a current status for a did:keri specification.
- **Publisher cost.** Static hosting, plus producing and refreshing the event stream with keripy or equivalent after every key or registry event.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @gleif-it/did-webs-ts 0.0.6 (2025-04-08) exists but looks unmaintained; the reference resolver is Python (GLEIF).
- **Failure modes.** Domain loss or takedown removes the location (the AID survives). An eclipse of the stream is possible without watchers. The pinned schema SAID problem in issue #216 shows the ecosystem itself relying on one implementation as the source of a SAID.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. It standardises where one principal publishes, not how to find all principals that mention H.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - did:webs specification status is v0.10.3; the repository has 18 stars and was last pushed 2026-08-11. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-did-method-webs-specification/
  - Open issue #216 (2026-10-06): 'The spec never identifies the designated-aliases schema by SAID.' and 'The one every implementation pins, EN6Oh5XSD5_q2Hgu-aqpdfbVepdpYpFlgz6zvJL5b_r5, comes from GLEIF's reference resolver.' (as of 2026-10-06). Source: https://github.com/trustoverip/kswg-did-method-webs-specification/issues/216
  - the keri.cesr stream MUST contain at least ONE designated aliases ACDC in which the host and path are committed to. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-did-method-webs-specification/
  - npm package @gleif-it/did-webs-ts is at 0.0.6, last published 2025-04-08. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/-/v1/search?text=keri%20cesr&size=20
  - GitHub code search for the designated-aliases schema SAID returns total_count 44. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/search?q=%22EN6Oh5XSD5_q2Hgu-aqpdfbVepdpYpFlgz6zvJL5b_r5%22&type=code
- **Skeptic's re-score.** reverse_lookup 1 to 0: the researcher's own text says 'None', and nothing I found contradicts it. publisher_effort 3 to 2: issue #216 shows resolvers are v1-only while keripy 2.x emits v2 and the spec does not say what to do; the TypeScript library is 0.0.6 from April 2025; the stream must be regenerated with Python tooling after every key or registry event.

#### Trust Spanning Protocol (TSP)

An authenticated, optionally confidential messaging protocol between verifiable identifiers, from Trust over IP. It moves messages between parties that already know each other's identifiers.

- **Publish.** Nothing. TSP has no notion of a public record or announcement.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not applicable.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. The specification defines no directory, search or topic subscription: 'Detailed specifications of OOBI methods are out of scope for this specification.' and 'Native TSP multicast messages are out of scope for this specification.'
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Out of band, by an OOBI, which TSP defines only as 'Any method of discovering VIDs and making an initial (insecure) connection to an Endpoint.'
- **Identity.** Verifiable identifiers, including KERI AIDs; a signed declaration could be sent over TSP to a known party, but not found through it.
- **Digest fit.** Payload-agnostic.
- **Governance.** Trust over IP Technology Stack Working Group. Status wording differs between the specification page and the deliverables list.
- **Publisher cost.** A TSP endpoint or intermediary, which is a running service.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @openvtc/vti-tsp-js 0.4.0 (2026-10-22); the main SDK is Rust.
- **Failure modes.** Irrelevant to the job: no public lookup exists to fail.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. It does not address discovery at all, so everything would have to be built beside it.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - The specification page shows 'Specification Status: Release 1.0' with no date; a Trust over IP deliverables list (via search summary) still labels it 'Implementers Draft, 11 Apr 2024'. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/tswg-tsp-specification/
  - The OpenWallet Foundation TSP project describes itself as an early implementation of the draft protocol, written in Rust (search summary). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://tac.openwallet.foundation/projects/tsp/
  - npm package @openvtc/vti-tsp-js 0.4.0, 'Pure-TypeScript TSP 1.0 primitives', last published 2026-10-22. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/-/v1/search?text=keri%20cesr&size=20
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. Status and both out-of-scope sentences confirmed; it has no bearing on discovery.

#### ACDC endorsement credential with a TEL registry (untargeted, self-issued)

The signed-statement format. An ACDC with an issuer and no issuee is an attestation 'to whom it may concern'; its issuance and revocation state lives in a transaction event log anchored in the issuer's key event log.

- **Publish.** The principal issues an ACDC whose schema SAID names the declaration type and whose attributes carry {definition: H, stance: issues | relies-on | implements | endorses, dt}. Revoking it later means 'I no longer do'. KERI defines no public place to put it: IPEX is a two-party exchange. The only precedent for public placement is did:webs, where a self-issued ACDC sits in the web-hosted keri.cesr stream. A lighter KERI-native form is a signed reply (rpy) message under the best-available-data rules, with a date stamp and no registry.
- **Fetch by digest.** The definition is fetched separately (data OOBI). The ACDC itself is fetched from wherever the issuer hosts its stream and verified against the issuer's key event log and registry.
- **Reverse lookup.** Local only. keripy indexes credentials by schema (Reger.schms, /Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/vdr/eventing.py:2342, used by 'kli vc list --schema'), but only over credentials that node has already received. There is no network query for 'all ACDCs whose attribute equals H' or 'all issuers under schema S'.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** The issuer's OOBI or did:webs URL, passed out of band.
- **Identity.** Exactly what the maintainer wants: a publication is an endorsement signed by a principal's AID, survives key rotation, and is revocable. A domain-only first cut can publish the same attribute block as plain JSON and wrap it in an ACDC later without changing H.
- **Digest fit.** Native. H is an attribute value; the declaration type is itself identified by a schema SAID ('type-is-schema'); the declaration has its own SAID.
- **Governance.** ACDC specification v1.1, Trust over IP KERI Suite Working Group, with a Zenodo DOI. Version 2 changes registry fields (rd replaces ri; rip, bup and upd events), which is unsettled for did:webs.
- **Publisher cost.** High next to dropping a JSON file: incept an AID, optionally arrange witnesses, incept a registry, issue and anchor, export the stream, and repeat on rotation. In practice keripy's kli or a KERIA agent; KERIA is a long-running service.
- **TypeScript tooling.** signify-ts 0.4.1 (2026-10-12; maintainers from GLEIF and the community) needs a KERIA agent. keri-ts 2.0.0, cesr-ts 2.0.0 and @keri-ts/tufa 2.0.0 ('Trust utilities for agents CLI package') were all published 2026-11-07 by one maintainer; @kerits/core 0.3.4 (2026-05-19) claims KEL/TEL/ACDC logic. None is proven as an independent verifier at scale.
- **Failure modes.** Statements are unforgeable, but the set of statements is invisible without a lookup. Stale streams on static hosts hide revocations. Tooling complexity is the main risk for a solo maintainer.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. It is a record format. Finding the records needs a crawl and index, or an outside lookup keyed by H.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - the issuance is effectively addressed "to whom it may concern." An Untargeted ACDC enables verifiable authorship by the Issuer of the data in the Attributes section (as of 2026-08-01 (local copy, lines 1460-1463 of /Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keri-claude/scripts/markdown/acdc-specification.md)). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-acdc-specification/
  - GLEIF's 'Get a vLEI' page, as returned by my fetch, lists 9 Qualified vLEI Issuers: Provenant (2022-12-08), CFCA, FINEMA, Certizen, Global vLEI, TradeGo, TOPPAN Edge, SHECA (all 2025) and TrustAsia (2026-10-12). Nord vLEI, announced by GLEIF as the first European issuer, was not in the returned list, so the count may be low by one or two. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.gleif.org/en/organizational-identity/get-an-lei-vlei/get-a-vlei
  - signify-ts: 3,950 npm downloads from 2026-10-09 to 2026-11-07; 31 GitHub stars; description 'Typescript prototype of Simple KERI for Web Apps'. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-month/signify-ts
  - keripy (reference implementation): 81 stars, 80 forks, 242 open issues, last push 2026-11-06. KERIA: 33 stars. PyPI 'keri': 2,728 downloads in the last month. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://api.github.com/repos/WebOfTrust/keripy
  - GitHub repositories carrying the topic 'keri': 40; the most starred is veridian-wallet with 146 stars. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://api.github.com/search/repositories?q=topic:keri&per_page=1
  - ISO 17442-3:2024 standardises the vLEI and names ACDC and KERI as its basis (secondary sources: GLEIF press release and trade press; the ISO text is paywalled). (as of 2024-10). Source: https://gleif.org/en/newsroom/press-releases/iso-standardizes-gleif-s-pioneering-digital-organizational-identity-offering-with-publication-of-vlei-technical-standard
- **Skeptic's re-score.** reverse_lookup 1 to 0: the by-schema index is local to a node; over the network there is none. publisher_effort 2 to 1: every publisher, not only the maintainer, needs an AID, a registry, anchoring and a re-exported stream, through Python kli or a running KERIA agent; the pure TypeScript route is one maintainer's library at about 640 downloads a month; ACDC v2 changes registry fields. Trajectory stays 2: QVIs went from 1 to 9 but only one was added in 2026, and no issuance count is published.

#### vLEI-style schema publication (governance registry document, GitHub repositories, GLEIF web server and reporting API; WebOfTrust/schema registry.json)

How the one production KERI ecosystem publishes and finds schemas today, plus the community's attempt at a general schema registry.

- **Publish.** GLEIF lists each official schema with its SAID in a governance document (Technical Requirements Part 3: vLEI Credential Schema Registry) and keeps the files in GitHub (WebOfTrust/vLEI under schema/acdc, GLEIF-IT/vLEI-schema) and on its web server. Anyone with a custom schema hosts it themselves. For the community registry you open a pull request adding a folder and a row in /registry.json.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET &lt;server&gt;/oobi/&lt;SAID&gt; from a schema server or GLEIF's well-known URLs, then verify the SAID. Verifiers preload the OOBIs in configuration.
- **Reverse lookup.** Central where it exists. For credentials: 'Legal Entity vLEI credentials and Legal Entity Official Organizational Role vLEI credentials must be reported by the Qualified vLEI Issuer to GLEIF in order to be published by GLEIF on the reference data page of the respective LEI.' For schemas in general there is none; registry.json lists schemas, not their users.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Be listed by the governing body, or get a pull request merged.
- **Identity.** Authority is the governing body's publication (document, repository, domain), not a signature per schema.
- **Digest fit.** Schema SAIDs unchanged; they are plain 44-character strings in JSON files, which is why code search finds them.
- **Governance.** GLEIF's ecosystem governance framework (v4.0, 2026-03) for vLEI; volunteer maintainers for WebOfTrust/schema, dormant since 2023.
- **Publisher cost.** A pull request, or acceptance into a governance framework.
- **TypeScript tooling.** None needed beyond fetch.
- **Failure modes.** Single curator, single repository, single domain. Dormancy: the community registry has had no push for three years.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. It is a curated central registry and site: precisely what the maintainer wants to avoid. The ACDC specification anticipates it: 'The function of an extensible Registry of ACDC types now becomes merely Schema discovery or schema blessing for a given context or ecosystem.'
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - It is a development convenience and should not be relied upon in production since it would be a single point of failure. Instead of using the vLEI Schema server in production the well-known OOBI URls for vLEI schemas from GLEIF's main public webserver should be used. (as of 2025-07-11). Source: https://www.gleif.org/newsroom/events/gleif-vlei-hackathon-2025/2025-07-11-gleif-testnet-schema-server-documentation-v1.0._final.pdf
  - a team or company will need to host their own credential schemas if they want to create and use custom ACDCs. (as of 2025-07-11). Source: https://www.gleif.org/newsroom/events/gleif-vlei-hackathon-2025/2025-07-11-gleif-testnet-schema-server-documentation-v1.0._final.pdf
  - Legal Entity vLEI credentials and Legal Entity Official Organizational Role vLEI credentials must be reported by the Qualified vLEI Issuer to GLEIF in order to be published by GLEIF on the reference data page of the respective LEI. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.gleif.org/en/about/gleif-services/gleif-service-catalog
  - WebOfTrust/schema README: 'This repo holds a registry for ACDC schemas that might be useful to a broad audience.' and 'The registry is simply a well known and convenient place to make data discoverable.' The repository has 3 stars, 40 commits, and was last pushed 2023-11-21. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/WebOfTrust/schema
  - WebOfTrust/vLEI has 40 stars and 15 forks; GLEIF-IT/vLEI-schema has 3 stars and 3 commits; provenant-dev/public-schema (3 stars) says 'All released schemas are enumerated in registry.json.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/WebOfTrust/vLEI
  - The vLEI governance schema registry document pairs each schema version with its SAID and a GitHub URL in GLEIF-IT/vLEI-schema (search summary of the v3.0 document dated 2023-12-15; the PDF returned 404 to my fetch and I could not retrieve the v4.0 edition). (as of 2023-12-15). Source: https://www.gleif.org/vlei/introducing-the-vlei-ecosystem-governance-framework/2023-12-15_vlei-egf-v3.0-technical-requirements-part-3-vlei-credential-schema-registry_v1.1-final.pdf
- **Skeptic's re-score.** The researcher missed that GLEIF production serves a self-hosted, machine-readable layout (host-meta.json, an OOBI catalog and {said}/index.json), added in mid 2026 alongside a spec change and a drafted IANA request. That lifts adoption_trajectory 1 to 2, no_aggregator 0 to 1 (the layout needs no registry and any publisher can copy it; 'who issues' is still a mandatory report to GLEIF) and publisher_effort 3 to 4 (static files, no pull request). reverse_lookup stays 1: central where it exists.

#### Hybrid, crossing the family boundary: KERI-signed declarations as plain files on web or public git hosting, found through an existing generic index keyed by the literal SAID string

The answer to 'which outside substrate carries KERI-signed endorsements most naturally'. Hosting: static HTTPS files in the did:webs layout, which a public git repository with pages hosting also satisfies. Lookup: an unsigned OOBI, the pair (URL, H), surfaced by an index that already exists. This is how the KERI community already works in practice: its schemas live in GitHub repositories.

- **Publish.** Commit or upload three kinds of file: the definition at /oobi/&lt;H&gt;; a declarations file that contains the literal string H, the stance and optional peers; later, the AID's did.json and keri.cesr with the declaration ACDC. Hosting is free on any static host or public repository.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET /oobi/&lt;H&gt; from any publisher and recompute the SAID.
- **Reverse lookup.** Search an existing full-text or tag index for the exact 44-character string, then treat every hit as an unverified OOBI: fetch that publisher's declarations and verify signature, key event log and registry state; discard the rest. Tested on GitHub code search (one operator, authenticated API): 415 hits for the Qualified vLEI Issuer schema SAID, 374 for the Legal Entity schema SAID, 44 for the did:webs designated-aliases schema SAID. Verified at specification level only: Nostr relays answer a filter on any single-letter tag with an arbitrary string value ('all single-letter (only english alphabet letters: a-z, A-Z) key tags are expected to be indexed by relays'), which is a native keyed query on nodes run by many independent operators. A general web search for the same SAID found nothing.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher becomes findable when the index picks up its file (git code search) or the moment it posts a tagged pointer (relay), and in any case when an existing publisher lists it as a peer.
- **Identity.** Domain or repository ownership in the first cut; a KERI AID and an untargeted ACDC are added later in the same location without changing H or the lookup. The index hit is never trusted: it is an OOBI, and trust comes from KERI verification.
- **Digest fit.** The SAID is used as an opaque string, unchanged, which is what makes exact-match lookup work. Do not re-encode it as a content identifier for IPFS-style fetch: a SAID is the digest of the document with its own SAID field replaced by '#' characters, so it is not the digest of the served bytes and block verification would fail. The raw 32 bytes can be recovered (replace the leading E with A, base64url-decode to 33 bytes, drop the first zero byte) and wrapped as a Blake3 multihash only for use as a lookup key (multicodec code 0x1e is from memory, not checked this session).
- **Governance.** HTTPS and git need none. GitHub code search is a proprietary service with rate limits and a 1000-result cap. The KERI layer is governed as above.
- **Publisher cost.** One commit or upload; nothing to run. The KERI signature adds the costs listed under the ACDC mechanism and can be deferred.
- **TypeScript tooling.** fetch, @noble/hashes for the SAID, the GitHub REST API or gh for code search, nostr-tools if a relay pointer is used; keri-ts or signify-ts primitives once declarations are signed.
- **Failure modes.** Index coverage: code search covers default branches of indexed public repositories only, so a publisher on a bare static host is invisible to it. A string match is not a declaration: test fixtures, forks and vendored copies all match, and anyone can plant the string, so verification and a stance field are mandatory. One-company chokepoint for the git index. Relay retention is not guaranteed.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Nothing is built or run for these definitions. But GitHub code search is one company's index, not infrastructure run by many independent parties, and its coverage is best-effort; relays are run by many parties but are lightly adopted. Which outside index is best belongs to the other families' research.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - GitHub code search total_count for three ACDC schema SAIDs: 415 (Qualified vLEI Issuer), 374 (Legal Entity), 44 (did:webs designated aliases). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/search?q=%22ENPXp1vQzRF6JwIuS-mp2U8Uf1MoADoP_GqQ62VsDZWY%22&type=code
  - all single-letter (only english alphabet letters: a-z, A-Z) key tags are expected to be indexed by relays (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/nostr-protocol/nips/blob/master/01.md
  - Rekor v2 removed its search-by-hash index, so the Sigstore transparency log is not a native reverse lookup by digest (search summary of the Sigstore announcement). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://blog.sigstore.dev/rekor-v2-ga/
  - The AT Protocol backlink index Constellation is lexicon-agnostic, but its public instance is run by a single maintainer (search summary of the project readme). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://constellation.microcosm.blue/
  - @noble/hashes, which provides Blake3 for TypeScript, had 456,833,624 npm downloads from 2026-10-09 to 2026-11-07. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-month/@noble/hashes
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 3 to 2: the substrate (git hosting) is everywhere, but the convention has no adopters and the lookup works only for GitHub-hosted publishers and logged-in queriers. adoption_trajectory 3 to 2: no standards body or second ecosystem is converging on 'grep a code index for a digest'. reverse_lookup 3 to 2: GitHub documents the index as non-exhaustive, I reproduced silent misses past character 1,024 of a line, and in the one live test the hits contained no actual issuer. no_aggregator stays 3: nothing purpose-built is run, but one company operates the index, which is not what the maintainer's first value asks for.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (keri-native)

The skeptic re-checked 29 claims; 17 held as stated. The rest:

- *partly* · OOBI and data OOBI (SAID-addressed fetch) · claim: Which well-known OOBI path is current is unresolved (/.well-known/oobi/{aid} in the local copy, /.well-known/keri/oobi/{aid} in keripy and the live page).
  - Correction: Resolved: the spec head moved to /.well-known/oobi/{aid} on 2026-05-29; keripy and the rendered page lag. GLEIF production uses a third shape, /.well-known/oobi/{said}/index.json. Three variants are live at once.
  - Checked: keripy ending.py:57 (WOOBI_RE = /.well-known/keri/oobi/); live rendered spec (build 2026-03-06) shows /.well-known/keri/oobi/; GitHub code search inside trustoverip/kswg-keri-specification finds only 'well-known/oobi' (spec/spec-body.md) and none for 'well-known/keri/oobi'; commit dated 2026-05-29 'updates to well-known oobis for provisional IANA registration'.
- *refuted* · vLEI-style schema publication (governance registry document, GitHub repositories, GLEIF web server and reporting API; WebOfTrust/schema registry.json) · claim: GLEIF's production 'well-known OOBI URLs for vLEI schemas' could not be located; a guessed URL on www.gleif.org returned 404.
  - Correction: They exist. host-meta.json (RFC 6415) links a template https://www.gleif.org/.well-known/oobi/{said}/index.json ('Type-agnostic OOBI lookup by SAID/AID') and a 'GLEIF OOBI Discovery Catalog' at /.well-known/oobi/index.json (updated 2026-06-08, 22 resources: 3 AIDs, 9 schemas, 10 witnesses). The same catalog is mirrored at gleif-it.github.io and weboftrust.github.io (first commit 2026-05-27). So the flagship does publish a machine-readable per-publisher enumeration on a standard well-known file; it is still one publisher.
  - Checked: curl against www.gleif.org: /.well-known/oobi/EBfdlu8R27Fbx-ehrqwImnK-8Cm79sqbAQ4MmvEAYqao/index.json returns 200 application/json, 2933 bytes (the QVI schema); /.well-known/host-meta.json returns 200; /.well-known/oobi/index.json returns 200.
- *partly* · OOBI and data OOBI (SAID-addressed fetch) · claim: A general web search for the exact schema SAID returned no relevant hit.
  - Correction: Engine-dependent. Brave Search returned about ten relevant URLs for the same string (docs.veridian.id pages, github.com/healthKERI/vital, keripy issue 799, a cesr-decoder issue). None was a URL that serves the bytes, and the Wayback CDX index has no capture under gleif.org/.well-known/oobi/. Web search finds HTML pages that mention a SAID, not JSON at well-known paths.
  - Checked: Repeated the query on the session search backend (8 unrelated results), Bing (unrelated), DuckDuckGo HTML (nothing returned, possibly bot-blocked) and Brave Search.
- *partly* · Witnesses and watchers · claim: No public production watcher network was found.
  - Correction: A negative cannot be proven, but there is corroboration from inside the ecosystem: CIP-0170 states 'as KERI is a maturing technology, wide-spread deployment of watcher networks is not yet guaranteed' and falls back to publishing an OOBI 'via a known persistent channel'.
  - Checked: One web search (only demos and governance text) and CIP-0170 in cardano-foundation/CIPs, written by Cardano Foundation KERI implementers.
- *unverifiable* · Witnesses and watchers · claim: vLEI EGF v4.0 Technical Requirements Part 1: GLEIF must run its own witness pool; validators choose watchers. GLEIF testnet lists five witnesses and one KERIA server.
  - Correction: Independent data point: GLEIF's production catalog at www.gleif.org/.well-known/oobi/index.json lists 10 witness OOBIs, all GLEIF's.
  - Checked: The Part 1 PDF URL returned 404 again; the hackathon PDF downloads but its text could not be extracted (no PDF tools, installs not allowed).
- *partly* · Percolated discovery (PID, SPED, just-in-time / need-to-know) and IPEX apply · claim: KERI does not define a peers list; it would be a new convention.
  - Correction: keripy implements an '/oobis' exchange message (OobiRequestHandler, oobiRequestExn) that forwards one OOBI to a known AID, and GLEIF publishes a per-host OOBI catalog. Neither is a spec-defined, crawlable list of other publishers, so the substance of the claim holds: the walk needs a new convention.
  - Checked: keripy /Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/app/oobiing.py lines 198-239 and the GLEIF catalog.
- *partly* · ACDC endorsement credential with a TEL registry (untargeted, self-issued) · claim: GLEIF lists 9 Qualified vLEI Issuers; Nord vLEI was missing, so the count may be low by one or two.
  - Correction: The count of 9 is right. Nord vLEI was rebranded and requalified as Global vLEI, which is in the list (2025-05-26). Seven of the nine qualified in 2025 and only one (TrustAsia, 2026-10-12) in 2026.
  - Checked: https://www.gleif.org/en/organizational-identity/get-an-lei-vlei/get-a-vlei (9 entries, same names and dates); nordlei.org announcement found by search.
- *partly* · ACDC endorsement credential with a TEL registry (untargeted, self-issued) · claim: keri-ts 2.0.0, cesr-ts 2.0.0 and @keri-ts/tufa 2.0.0 were published 2026-11-07 by one maintainer ('published days ago').
  - Correction: Version 2.0.0 is dated 2026-11-07 and the single npm maintainer is confirmed, but keri-ts and cesr-ts were first published 2026-02-13, so they are nine months old, not days. Use is tiny: 642 and 622 downloads last month.
  - Checked: registry.npmjs.org for each package.
- *partly* · ACDC endorsement credential with a TEL registry (untargeted, self-issued) · claim: No published vLEI issuance count exists; ISO 17442-3:2024 standardises the vLEI on ACDC and KERI.
  - Correction: Issuance count remains unverifiable (GLEIF reports are PDFs I could not parse). ISO status is confirmed from secondary listings only.
  - Checked: Two searches for an issuance figure (only LEI counts came back); iso.org returned 403, a search summary of the ISO listing gives Edition 1, 2024-10, and ACDC in the scope text.
- *partly* · Hybrid, crossing the family boundary: KERI-signed declarations as plain files on web or public git hosting, found through an existing generic index keyed by the literal SAID string · claim: GitHub code search finds the literal SAID: 415, 374 and 44 hits for three schema SAIDs; limits are rate limits, a 1000-result cap and default branches of indexed public repositories.
  - Correction: The limits are heavier than stated. GitHub documents: login required even for public code; REST needs a token and allows 10 requests per minute; 'not all code is indexed'; 'Exhaustive search is not supported'; vendored and generated code excluded; 100 results in the web UI; 'Lines over 1,024 characters long are truncated'. I reproduced the truncation: the QVI schema file is one 2,933-character line; a SAID at character 893 gets 3 hits in that repo, a SAID at character 1,509 gets 0. SAID-serialised JSON is compact and single-line by construction, so any H past character 1,024 is invisible unless files are pretty-printed or H is in the path.
  - Checked: gh api search/code (415, 374, 44 reproduced); https://docs.github.com/en/search-github/github-code-search/about-github-code-search and the REST search docs; a controlled test on GLEIF-IT/GLEIF-IT.github.io.
- *partly* · Hybrid, crossing the family boundary: KERI-signed declarations as plain files on web or public git hosting, found through an existing generic index keyed by the literal SAID string · claim: Searching the index for H and treating each hit as an unverified OOBI yields the declarers of H.
  - Correction: Unqualified, the result is noise for this purpose (scripts, docs, fixtures; no actual issuer). With a path convention it sharpens: the path-qualified query returned 6 hits in 2 repositories, both mirrors of GLEIF's single catalog. The authoritative publisher, www.gleif.org, is invisible because it is not a GitHub repository. So a convention makes the query precise, but only for publishers who host on GitHub.
  - Checked: Per-repository breakdown of the 415-hit result, and a path-qualified query: "&lt;SAID&gt;" path:.well-known.
- *partly* · Hybrid, crossing the family boundary: KERI-signed declarations as plain files on web or public git hosting, found through an existing generic index keyed by the literal SAID string · claim: Nostr relays give a native keyed query: 'all single-letter ... key tags are expected to be indexed by relays'.
  - Correction: The sentence begins 'As a convention,' which the quote dropped, and adds 'Only the first value in any given tag is indexed'. NIP-01 does not oblige a relay to accept or keep an event of an unfamiliar kind. No relay was queried, so behaviour and retention are unverified here; adoption figures found by search disagree widely (3,675 to 216,000 daily active).
  - Checked: Raw text of https://github.com/nostr-protocol/nips/blob/master/01.md line 84.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (keri-native)

- **GLEIF's production well-known layout: host-meta.json (RFC 6415) + OOBI Discovery Catalog + /.well-known/oobi/{said}/index.json.** This is how the flagship actually publishes, and the researcher reported it as not found. One IANA-permanent well-known file links a SAID-keyed URL template and a machine-readable catalog of everything the publisher serves (22 resources). It works on plain static hosting including GitHub Pages, and the KERI spec (since 2026-05-29) describes publishing the same well-known OOBI on a domain and on a third-party platform. It should replace the sketch's invented /oobi/&lt;H&gt; and /.well-known/mad/declarations.json: same idea, but an existing layout with H in the path, which also makes a path-qualified index query precise. It covers fetch and per-publisher enumeration only; the reverse lookup conclusion does not change. Use is tiny and new: code search finds the layout in 3 repositories (GLEIF-IT, WebOfTrust, bakobo), first committed 2026-05-27. Source: https://www.gleif.org/.well-known/host-meta.json ; https://www.gleif.org/.well-known/oobi/index.json ; https://github.com/trustoverip/kswg-keri-specification/blob/main/iana/well-known-oobi-registration.md
- **CIP-0170, KERI-backed metadata attestations on Cardano (transaction metadata label 170).** The only place in the KERI orbit where a KERI-signed attestation about a digest lands in a public store that third parties already index. An ATTEST record carries a CESR Blake3-256 digest (code E, the same form as the workspace's SAIDs), the signer's AID and the KEL sequence number that anchors it; when no on-chain metadata is attested, the digest may refer to an off-chain document defined by the use case. Generic chain indexers answer 'list every label-170 record' free and without a purpose-built index: Koios returned 12,315 label-170 transactions, 449 since the CIP's creation date, the latest on 2026-08-17 and a demo with a fake digest. It does not change the conclusion: the query is by label, not by digest, so the client filters; the label had unrelated use since 2021; each publication costs a transaction fee and needs a credential chain; status is Proposed with two implementers; and no agent tooling reads Cardano metadata. Indicative scores: adoption_today 1, trajectory 2, no_aggregator 4, reverse_lookup 2, publisher_effort 1, keri_fit 5. Source: https://github.com/cardano-foundation/CIPs/tree/master/CIP-0170 (Status: Proposed, created 2025-11-05, version 1.1) ; https://api.koios.rest/api/v1/tx_by_metalabel
- **keripy '/oobis' exchange message (OOBI forwarding between known peers).** The nearest implemented thing to the peers link the sketch invents: one controller sends another an OOBI in a signed exn, and the receiver queues it. It is a push to an AID you already know, through a mailbox, not a published list, so it does not give a walk from one introduction. It shows the peers file would be a new convention, as the researcher said, though not one without precedent. Source: /Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/app/oobiing.py lines 198-239
- **Trust Registry Query Protocol (TRQP) v2.0, Trust over IP.** The Trust over IP answer to 'is party X authorised for Y under framework Z'. It is a read-only point query against a registry somebody operates, so it is an aggregator by design and offers no 'list everyone' query that I could confirm. It does not rescue the family. Status is from the PDF's metadata ('(Approved)', 2026-04-23); the query types are from a search summary because the PDF body could not be parsed. Source: https://trustoverip.org/wp-content/uploads/TRQPv2.0-Archival-PDF-2026.04.23.pdf

### What the researcher could not verify (keri-native)

- The keri.host chat tool (mcp__plugin_keri_keri-chat__ask_keri_chat) returned HTTP 403, so no answer came from it; specification text was read from the live Trust over IP pages and from the local copies under /Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keri-claude/scripts/markdown (dated 2026-08-01).
- WebFetch summarises pages. Quotes from the KERI, ACDC and CESR specifications were checked verbatim against the local copies. Quotes from did:webs, TSP, NIP-01, GitHub READMEs, the did:webs issue and the HackMD note are as reported by the fetch tool and were not independently checked. The GLEIF service catalog sentence was returned by the tool in two fragments that I joined.
- No published count of vLEI credentials issued was found. The GLEIF annual report PDF downloaded but its text could not be extracted (no PDF tools installed).
- The Qualified vLEI Issuer count (9) comes from a model-summarised fetch of GLEIF's page; Nord vLEI, announced by GLEIF as a qualified issuer, was not in the returned list, so the count may be incomplete.
- The vLEI governance framework v4.0 Technical Requirements Part 1 and the Part 3 schema registry PDF both returned 404; statements about GLEIF's witness pool, watchers and the registry table come from search summaries, not from the documents.
- I could not locate GLEIF's production 'well-known OOBI URLs for vLEI schemas'; one guessed URL on www.gleif.org returned 404.
- Well-known OOBI path: the local specification copy says /.well-known/oobi/{aid} 'using the registered oobi suffix', keripy's regex and the live-page fetch show /.well-known/keri/oobi/{aid}, and the IANA registry fetch showed neither suffix. Which is current is unresolved.
- Trust Spanning Protocol status: the specification page says 'Release 1.0' with no date, while a Trust over IP deliverables list (search summary) says Implementers Draft, 11 Apr 2024.
- No current status found for a did:keri method specification; the W3C DID methods listing is from a search summary.
- Whether any public production watcher network exists: I found none, but absence of evidence is all I have.
- Outside substrates were checked only lightly because they belong to other families: Nostr at specification level (NIP-01 text) with no primary adoption figure (a search summary gave 144,202 daily active pubkeys for late December 2025); AT Protocol and Constellation, Rekor v2 and IPFS provider-record facts are from search summaries. The Blake3 multicodec code 0x1e is from memory.
- GitHub code search counts are total_count values from the authenticated API on 2026-11-08; they count file matches, not distinct declarers, and include tests, forks and vendored copies. The 37-repository and 15-owner figures cover only the first 100 hits.
- The web-search result of zero hits for a SAID reflects this session's search backend only; other engines were not tried.
- Vereign's production figures are self-reported and the Veridian install estimate is from a third-party tracker; neither is used in scoring.
- Maturity of keri-ts 2.0.0, cesr-ts 2.0.0, @keri-ts/tufa and @kerits/core as independent KEL/TEL/ACDC verifiers was not assessed; only their npm metadata was read.
- Nothing was published or signed (read-only task), so the sketch is untested end to end; in particular I did not confirm how existing did:webs resolvers treat extra ACDCs in keri.cesr.
- ISO 17442-3:2024 content is from secondary sources; the standard is paywalled.
- The speaker of the 'OOBI in a web searchable location' comment is not named on the HackMD page.

<a id="p2p-content"></a>

## Content-addressed peer-to-peer networks

**Researcher's summary.** This family does have a native reverse lookup. In IPFS, "who announces key K" is a first-class query (GET_PROVIDERS on the Amino DHT, or GET /routing/v1/providers/{cid} over HTTP), keyed by any multihash up to 80 bytes, and nothing checks that the announcer holds the bytes. A SAID's Blake3-256 digest re-encodes losslessly as a CIDv1 (raw 0x55, blake3 0x1e); on 2026-11-09 03:00 UTC both delegated-ipfs.dev and cid.contact answered such a CID with HTTP 200 and provider lists. If the publisher serves the SAID pre-image (the document with its d field set to 44 '#' characters), that CID is also a verifiable content address; otherwise the digest is only a rendezvous key. What comes back is a libp2p peer ID (an Ed25519 key in 180 of 184 results I sampled) plus addresses: unauthenticated, carrying no role, and on the DHT gone after 48 hours (default re-announce 22 hours), so every publisher needs a recurring job holding a libp2p key, and the HTTP routing API has no write endpoint for providers. IPNI avoids the cron (a static signed advertisement chain plus one HTTP PUT), but it is an index with one production operator: cid.contact listed 162 providers, 82 active in the last 30 days, runs on Filecoin ProPGF grants, and has paid-tier ingest and per-publisher metering on its roadmap. The decisive adoption fact is recent and negative: Shipyard, which maintained Kubo, Helia, Boxo and Someguy and ran ipfs.io, dweb.link, delegated-ipfs.dev and the bootstrap nodes, ended all IPFS work on 2026-09-30 after Protocol Labs declined to renew funding. Kubo's last release is v0.43.1 (2026-09-15), Someguy's README says "There is no dedicated maintainer at the moment", and in my probes trustless-gateway.link returned 521/526 and dist.ipfs.tech timed out. ProbeLab's last figures (July 2026) were 23k-25k distinct DHT server IPs and about 400k weekly clients, with only about 4k servers generally online in February. BitTorrent Mainline is far larger, but get_peers returns only an IP and port taken from the announcer's UDP source address, and BEP 44 / pkarr items are keyed by public key, so they answer "does K declare H" but never "who declares H". Iroh is BLAKE3-native and its core is healthy (1.0 on 2026-06-15), but iroh-blobs calls itself "not yet considered production quality" and its content discovery is "experimental"; HyperDHT has the best semantics (lookup of a 32-byte topic returns signed public keys) but needs a long-lived UDP node. The only place this family meets AI-agent tooling is AGNTCY's Agent Directory (Linux Foundation; libp2p Kad-DHT, CIDs, OCI; v1.5 ships a reference ARD implementation), which is a network of directory servers keyed by skill labels and SHA-256 CIDs. Net: the family can supply an optional rendezvous layer with no purpose-built aggregator, but it is weak on wide adoption, agent readership and publisher effort, and the signed, role-typed declaration has to be a separate KERI-signed document that the routing layer only points at.

**Researcher's best pick.** IPFS content routing: Amino DHT provider records for announcing, read through the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API (/routing/v1/providers/{cid}), with an IPNI advertisement as an optional second path that needs no cron. This is a qualified pick. It is the only mechanism in the family that gives a native reverse lookup on infrastructure many independent parties run and that a TypeScript publisher on Lambda can plausibly reach. I would use it only as an optional rendezvous layer underneath KERI-signed declarations, not as the foundation of mad-discovery.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** It builds on infrastructure whose stewardship collapsed six weeks ago, and the routing layer is not where the value is. Shipyard stopped all IPFS work on 2026-09-30; Kubo has had no release since 2026-09-15, Someguy has no maintainer, the public trustless gateway returned 521/526 and dist.ipfs.tech timed out in my probes, and ProbeLab had already reported the stable DHT server set declining to about 4k. For a browser or an AI agent, the only practical way to ask the question is an HTTP endpoint, and both endpoints (delegated-ipfs.dev and cid.contact) are now run by one grant-funded team that is adding per-publisher metering and a paid tier, so "no aggregator" reduces to "a generic aggregator run by someone else". A provider record is an unauthenticated, role-less hint that costs nothing to forge and expires in 48 hours, so every publisher must run a job forever and every reader must fetch and verify a signed declaration anyway; once that declaration format exists, the DHT is just one replaceable way to find it. No AI-agent tooling reads the Amino DHT (AGNTCY's directory uses the same library in what appears to be its own network), so this does nothing for the maintainer's second value, very wide adoption. And a Blake3 CID is second-class in today's tooling: multicodec lists blake3 as draft, Helia's verified-fetch does not hash it by default, and the IPFS Foundation's own DASL profile forbids it outside BDASL.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** The report is accurate where it can be checked: of roughly thirty weight-bearing claims I re-opened, the adoption figures, constants, spec statements and service-status probes held, several to the digit. Its errors all lean the same way, toward making the best pick look sturdier than it is: cid.contact does not serve DHT announcements (so there is one HTTP endpoint, not two), the "23k-25k servers" figure is 4k servers online at once, the provider record is not verifiable by the reader, and the test that "both routers answered" used a digest other parties already announce. The researcher's own conclusion is the right one and should be read as a no: this family can supply an optional rendezvous layer, not a foundation, and the step everything depends on (a serverless publisher announcing a blake3 key that a stranger then finds) is untested. I would not build mad-discovery on it, and I would not spend time prototyping the DHT path until the signed-declaration format exists and some other family has been shown unable to carry it. If a hash-keyed lookup from this family is ever wanted, IPNI is simpler for publishers but is exactly a single-operator index, and Arweave's tag index deserves the look it did not get.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

Best pick: Amino DHT provider records read through Delegated Routing. Nobody has to run an index, but that is true only for readers that run libp2p, and the lookup has not been shown to work end to end. Walking it as a stranger who holds only H:

1. Derive the key. Nothing in IPFS understands a SAID. The stranger needs mad-discovery's own rule (drop the CESR code, wrap the 32 digest bytes as a CIDv1 with the raw and blake3 codes). I recomputed the report's example and it is correct.

2. Ask who provides that CID. Without a libp2p node there is exactly one public HTTP endpoint that covers DHT announcements: delegated-ipfs.dev. The network's own autoconf file lists delegated-ipfs.dev for 'AminoDHT' and 'IPNI' and cid.contact for 'IPNI' only, and in my probe cid.contact returned 4 providers where delegated-ipfs.dev returned 10. That hostname belongs to Protocol Labs and has been run since October 2026 by a three-person IPNI team on Filecoin grants. It is free, sends CORS headers, accepted a blake3 CID, and has no published terms or lifetime. A reader with libp2p (Node or a CLI) can skip it and ask the DHT directly: about 4k servers online at once, run by whoever runs Kubo, with no measurement since the maintainers left.

3. What comes back is a list of peer IDs with addresses: unsigned, with no role, gone after 48 hours. In my sample of 169 DHT-origin records for a popular CID, 69 had only relay addresses, 6 had none and 1 had an HTTPS address. The scheme needs every MAD publisher's record to carry an HTTPS address announced by a short-lived client. One live record shows such an address can be carried; nobody has done it from Lambda.

4. Fetch a declarations file from each address and verify signatures. The DHT accepts no record types, only key to peer, so the file, its format, its roles and its verification are all new work. This file is the actual answer to 'who declares H'.

5. The result is only the declarers whose announce job ran in the last 48 hours. Miss two days and a declaration vanishes from the lookup while its file is still published. There is no history.

6. Enumeration works by looking up one shared key and then fetching every publisher's file, so each reader is its own crawler; at any real size someone will cache that, and the aggregator is back.

What must exist or run: a key-derivation and declaration library, a blake3 hasher in every client, a static origin laid out as a trustless gateway, and a scheduled libp2p announce job at every declarer, forever. For browser and agent readers, the honest description is 'one generic router run by one team answers the question'. Neither the researcher nor I (read-only) has shown that a new blake3 key announced from a serverless client is returned by delegated-ipfs.dev.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

This family answers "which network peers were recently online and claimed key K", which is a different question from "which principals declare H, and in what role". Every part of the answer the maintainer wants (principal, role, signature, a readable reference, enumeration) has to live in a signed document on the publisher's own web origin. Once that document exists, the DHT contributes only one thing: letting strangers find each other without an introduction. It charges a high price for that. Each declarer must run a libp2p job at least every 48 hours for as long as the declaration should be findable, which turns "run no service" into "every publisher runs a cron". The node software has had nothing merged since 2026-09-30. The single HTTP door to DHT records is one hostname owned by the company that defunded the maintainers. The records are unsigned, so a reader cannot verify even the peer ID. And nothing that AI agents use reads this network, so it scores worst on the maintainer's second value, wide adoption. The one mechanism here with any agent connection, AGNTCY's directory, turns out to have no public network to join. Local-first discovery from a single introduction does not need a global hash table at all; it needs publishers to link to each other, which this family does not provide.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (p2p-content)

1. Keys. For a SAID H, take the 32 raw digest bytes (replace the leading 'E' with 'A', base64url-decode, drop the first byte). C(H) = CIDv1(raw 0x55, blake3 0x1e, digest), a 'bafkr4i...' string. One enumeration key E = CIDv1(raw, sha2-256, sha256('mad-discovery/1/definitions')), the same namespace-to-CID construction go-libp2p's RoutingDiscovery uses.

2. What each publisher writes, on its own static HTTPS origin (S3 plus CloudFront):
- /ipfs/{C(H)}: the SAID pre-image, that is the definition with its d field set to 44 '#' characters. blake3 of those bytes equals the digest, so any blake3-capable IPFS client verifies it.
- /.well-known/mad/declarations.json: a list of {said, role: issue | rely | implement | endorse, aid, signature, KEL or OOBI URL}, each entry signed by the publisher's KERI AID and naming the publisher's libp2p peer ID. This file is the actual claim. A first cut can leave entries unsigned and lean on the domain; adding AID signatures later changes no keys and no URLs.

3. Announce. A scheduled Lambda (EventBridge, every 12 hours; TypeScript with libp2p and @libp2p/kad-dht in client mode; Ed25519 peer key in SSM) calls contentRouting.provide(C(H)) for every H in the declarations file, and provide(E), announcing the address /dns4/&lt;origin&gt;/tcp/443/https. Records last 48 hours. Optional second path with no cron: write an IPNI advertisement chain to the same bucket under /ipni/v1/ad/ and send one HTTP PUT /announce to cid.contact per change.

4. The query that answers "who else declares H": GET https://delegated-ipfs.dev/routing/v1/providers/{C(H)} with Accept: application/x-ndjson (the same path works on cid.contact; from Node or a CLI, a direct DHT findProviders involves no HTTP party at all). For each {ID, Addrs} returned, GET https://&lt;host&gt;/.well-known/mad/declarations.json and keep the entries for H whose AID signature verifies and whose peer ID matches. The verified set, grouped by role, is the answer. Enumeration: the same query on E lists publishers, their declarations files list definitions, and the client renders each definition's human-readable reference from the definition itself.

5. Fetch by hash: from any provider, GET /ipfs/{C(H)}?format=raw, check blake3, substitute H for the placeholder.

6. What the maintainer still runs: nothing shared. mad-discovery ships as a TypeScript library, a CLI and a SAM template that each publisher deploys (one Lambda, one bucket). No crawler, no DynamoDB index, no seed list. Browser readers depend on a third-party Delegated Routing endpoint; Node readers do not.

Untested parts of this sketch: js-libp2p provides from inside Lambda, cid.contact accepting a small static publisher, and end-to-end gateway verification of a pre-image block.

### Mechanisms (p2p-content)

#### IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API

The public IPFS Kademlia DHT (protocol /ipfs/kad/1.0.0). DHT servers store provider records mapping a key (a multihash) to a peer ID and its multiaddrs. Any libp2p node can read them by walking the DHT; HTTP clients read them through a Delegated Routing V1 server at GET /routing/v1/providers/{cid}.

- **Publish.** A libp2p node holding the publisher's key sends ADD_PROVIDER for key K to the roughly 20 DHT servers closest to sha256(K). The receiver checks only that the key is valid (go-libp2p-kad-dht rejects keys over 80 bytes) and that the provider entry's peer ID equals the sender; it does not check that the sender has the content. So a record can be announced for any multihash without serving a block. Constants in go-libp2p-kad-dht amino/defaults.go: DefaultProvideValidity 48h, DefaultReprovideInterval 22h, DefaultProviderAddrTTL 24h. There is no HTTP write path: the Delegated Routing spec's only PUT is /routing/v1/ipns/{name}. Kubo's CLI will not announce a key it does not hold ('CIDs must already exist in the local blockstore'), so arbitrary keys need the libp2p API directly (js-libp2p contentRouting.provide or go-libp2p Provide). go-libp2p's own RoutingDiscovery does exactly this for rendezvous: namespace string -&gt; sha256 -&gt; CIDv1 raw -&gt; Provide. Pinning services announce under their own peer IDs, so they cannot express 'I declare H'. Hosting: the publisher's own process (a scheduled Lambda or a CLI run). Cost: seconds of compute per key per day.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET /routing/v1/providers/{cid}, pick a provider, then fetch from its trustless gateway (GET https://host/ipfs/{cid}?format=raw) or over Bitswap, hash the bytes and compare with the multihash. For blake3 the client must add a hasher: @helia/verified-fetch supports sha256, sha512 and identity by default and takes a custom hasher option. With the SAID pre-image served, verification is blake3(bytes) == digest, then the client substitutes the SAID for the 44 '#' placeholder.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native to the mechanism, not crawl-dependent. (1) Derive K from H. (2) Either walk the DHT with GET_PROVIDERS (Kubo findprovs default is 20 providers), or GET https://delegated-ipfs.dev/routing/v1/providers/{cid} or https://cid.contact/routing/v1/providers/{cid}. JSON responses 'SHOULD be limited to 100 providers'; NDJSON streams more (I received 184 for a popular CID, 169 of them DHT-origin peers with raw IP addresses and 15 from IPNI). (3) Each result is {Schema: peer, ID, Addrs, Protocols}. (4) These are hints only: the reader must fetch a signed declaration from each provider to learn role and principal. Infrastructure: the Amino DHT servers (whoever runs Kubo and similar nodes), plus bootstrap nodes and delegated-ipfs.dev, which since October 2026 are stewarded by the IPNI / Compass team. Only announcers from the last 48 hours appear.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher is findable as soon as its ADD_PROVIDER lands; no registration and no seed list, only bootstrap peers to join the DHT. The DHT cannot enumerate keys, so enumeration needs a convention: every publisher also provides one well-known key (for example sha256 of 'mad-discovery/1/definitions'), and a provider lookup on that key lists publishers.
- **Identity.** A libp2p peer ID. For Ed25519 keys the peer ID embeds the public key (the 12D3KooW... form), and DHT servers accept a provider record only from the peer it names, so a record proves 'the holder of key P announced K within 48 hours' and nothing more: no role, no principal. A KERI AID rides alongside: the AID signs a declaration {said, role, peerId} served at the provider's address, and the reader verifies that signature and the KEL. A first cut can rely on the HTTPS DNS name in the multiaddr; adding AID signatures later is additive.
- **Digest fit.** Any multihash up to 80 bytes. Blake3 is multicodec 0x1e (status 'draft' in the multicodec table). Re-encoding a 44-character SAID: replace the leading 'E' with 'A', base64url-decode to 33 bytes, drop the first byte to get the 32 raw digest bytes, then CID = 'b' + base32(0x01 0x55 0x1e 0x20 + digest). Computed locally for the BLAKE3 digest of empty input: SAID EK8TSbn1-aGmoEBN6jbcyUmbyyXJrcESt8yak8rkHzJi corresponds to CID bafkr4ifpcne3t5pzugtkaqcn5i3nzskjtpfslsnnyejlpte2spfoihzsmi, and both public routers answered that CID. As a routing key this always works. As a verifying content address it works only if the bytes served are the SAID pre-image (digest field filled with '#'), because that is what the digest was computed over.
- **Governance.** Specifications live in ipfs/specs (IPIP process) and libp2p/specs. Stewardship moved in 2026 from Shipyard to what Protocol Labs called 'lighter-weight stewardship' through IPFS Foundation grants to individual maintainers. The Go and JS implementations lost their dedicated maintainers on 2026-09-30; js-libp2p still shipped releases on 2026-10-19.
- **Publisher cost.** One libp2p key, a scheduled job at least every 22 hours, one DHT walk per key, and static HTTPS hosting for the bytes and the declarations. AWS cost is cents per month. js-libp2p provide time inside Lambda is untested.
- **TypeScript tooling.** libp2p 3.5.1 and @libp2p/kad-dht 16.4.9 (2026-10-19), helia 7.1.16, @helia/verified-fetch 8.1.2, @helia/delegated-routing-v1-http-api-client 9.0.2, multiformats 14.0.5 (4.16M downloads per week), @noble/hashes 2.4.0 for blake3. All TypeScript. Most maintainers were Shipyard staff.
- **Failure modes.** Spam and poisoning: announcing on any key is free and needs no proof of holding, so a Sybil flood can bury real providers and every hint must be verified by fetching a signed declaration. Liveness: a publisher vanishes 48 hours after its job stops (addresses after 24). Chokepoints: bootstrap peers, delegated-ipfs.dev and cid.contact are now under one team; the implementations have no committed maintainers. Privacy: lookups reveal interest in H to DHT servers or to the router operator, and records publish the announcer's addresses. In one probe a cached GET from delegated-ipfs.dev lacked the access-control-allow-origin header, and one request of about eight timed out.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** no. A Node, CLI or Lambda reader answers the query by walking a DHT that about 23k-25k independent server IPs already operate. Caveat: browsers cannot walk the DHT and need an HTTP delegated router, which today means delegated-ipfs.dev or cid.contact, both under one team; that is generic third-party infrastructure, not something built for these definitions.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Distinct DHT server IP counts have consistently maintained their baseline, hovering right in the 23k-25k range. (as of 2026-07-13). Source: https://discuss.ipfs.tech/t/probelab-s-notable-ipfs-performance-results-week-29-2026/20276
  - plateaued into a steadier network baseline of around 400k client nodes seen over a week (as of 2026-07-13). Source: https://discuss.ipfs.tech/t/probelab-s-notable-ipfs-performance-results-week-29-2026/20276
  - the number of generally online server nodes having stabilised at ~4+k nodes (described as following a declining pattern over the last few months) (as of 2026-02-20). Source: https://discuss.ipfs.tech/t/probelabs-notable-ipfs-performance-results-week-07-2026/20048
  - Content publishing times are holding tight at under 1 second (Kubo v0.39+); DHT lookups average just north of 0.3 seconds (as of 2026-07-13). Source: https://discuss.ipfs.tech/t/probelab-s-notable-ipfs-performance-results-week-29-2026/20276
  - Protocol Labs has informed us that it will not be renewing Shipyard's funding. Our final day of our IPFS related work will be September 30, 2026. Affected: Kubo, Helia, Boxo, Rainbow, Someguy and others, plus ipfs.io, dweb.link, delegated-ipfs.dev and the bootstrap nodes. (as of 2026-08-24). Source: https://ipshipyard.com/blog/2026-the-end-of-ipfs-at-shipyard/
  - Latest Kubo release is v0.43.1 (15 Sep 2026); its notes say 'v0.43 is the last Kubo by the Shipyard'. No release after 2026-09-30. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/ipfs/kubo/releases
  - Someguy README: 'There is no dedicated maintainer at the moment.' and 'Someguy was the backend of https://delegated-ipfs.dev/routing/v1 until 2026 Q3' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ipfs/someguy/main/README.md
  - IPFS blog names the IPNI / Compass team as stewarding indexing, routing, 'delegated routing and resilient bootstrapping' (the delegated routing link points to delegated-ipfs.dev) (as of 2026-10-14). Source: https://blog.ipfs.tech/2026-10-new-stewards-public-network-services/
  - Rate limiting has begun on ipfs.io and dweb.link for hotlinked and backend traffic, and will be increasing. (as of 2026-08-25). Source: https://blog.ipfs.tech/2026-08-beyond-sponsored-gateways/
  - My GET probes: delegated-ipfs.dev providers lookup HTTP 200; ipfs.io and dweb.link 301 to trustless-gateway.link, which returned 521 and 526; ipfs.io path gateway 429; dist.ipfs.tech timed out after 30 s; conf.ipfs-mainnet.org autoconf 200 (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://delegated-ipfs.dev/routing/v1/providers/bafybeigdyrzt5sfp7udm7hu76uh7y26nf3efuylqabf3oclgtqy55fbzdi
  - Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API spec is marked status 'reliable', dated 29 July 2026 (as of 2026-07-29). Source: https://specs.ipfs.tech/routing/http-routing-v1/
  - npm: @libp2p/kad-dht 16.4.9 published 2026-10-19, 35,104 downloads in the week to 2026-11-07; helia 7.1.16 published 2026-09-29, 19,147 downloads (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/@libp2p/kad-dht
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 3 to 2: the infrastructure that answers a query is 4k servers online at once (ProbeLab, 2026-09-08), one ecosystem, and no AI-agent tooling reads it. adoption_trajectory stays 1: Kubo has merged nothing since 2026-09-30 and the gateways are failing; a maintained DHT library (v0.43.0, 2026-10-28) is not enough to raise it. no_aggregator 4 to 3: a libp2p reader needs no index, but the only HTTP door to DHT records is delegated-ipfs.dev (cid.contact serves the index only), so browser and agent readers depend on one team's router. reverse_lookup 3 to 2: results are unsigned peer IDs from the last 48 hours, mostly without a usable web address in my sample, and the role and principal come from a file the project must still invent; not shown end to end. publisher_effort stays 2 but is untested in Lambda. keri_fit 3 to 2: the record is not signed, so a reader cannot verify even the peer ID; the digest fits as a key, the identity model does not help.

#### IPNI (InterPlanetary Network Indexer, cid.contact)

Indexer nodes that ingest signed advertisement chains from providers and answer multihash-to-provider lookups over HTTP. The production instance is cid.contact, which also backs delegated routing responses.

- **Publish.** The publisher builds an IPLD chain of Advertisements {PreviousID, Provider, Addresses, Signature, Entries (link to chunks of multihashes), ContextID, Metadata, IsRm}, serves it as files over HTTPS under /ipni/v1/ad/ (head at /ipni/v1/ad/head), then sends 'HTTP PUT requests to /announce on the indexer node's ingest server' or a gossipsub message on /indexer/ingest/mainnet. The indexer pulls the chain. The spec does not say indexers verify the provider has the content. The chain can sit on S3; elastic-ipfs/publisher-lambda is a precedent (Lambdas write ads to S3 and announce 'via an http PUT to INDEXER_NODE_URL'). No periodic re-announce, but the origin must stay reachable.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET https://cid.contact/cid/{cid}, take Provider.Addrs (usually an HTTPS trustless gateway), fetch the block, verify the hash.
- **Reverse lookup.** A native query, but against an index that one team operates. GET https://cid.contact/cid/{cid}, /multihash/{multihash} or /routing/v1/providers/{cid} returns MultihashResults[].ProviderResults[] = {ContextID, Metadata, Provider: {ID, Addrs}}. My lookup of a blake3 CID took about 0.4 s. The answer is whatever cid.contact has ingested: ProbeLab reported 'CIDs are ingested with a delay of more than 5 mins (but less than 60mins) 15% of the time' in February 2026.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher's first HTTP announce makes the indexer fetch its chain. The spec defines no allowlist, only 'The indexer will maintain a policy for when advertisements from a provider are considered valid.'
- **Identity.** Each advertisement is signed with a libp2p key; the provider address is typically an HTTPS DNS name; ContextID is free bytes chosen by the publisher (I saw the base64 of 'FILEBASE_ROCKS'), so it could carry a role tag. A KERI AID would ride as a signed declaration at the HTTPS address.
- **Digest fit.** Any multihash; blake3 is accepted (observed). Same re-encoding as for the DHT.
- **Governance.** Specification in ipni/specs. Operations are grant-funded (Filecoin ProPGF); 'Financial Federation' and 'Technical Federation' designs to spread cost across operators are milestones, not yet reality.
- **Publisher cost.** A few S3 objects and one HTTP PUT per change. Free today; a paid tier for fast ingest is being built and the free-tier terms were truncated on the page I could read.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @web3-storage/ipni (JavaScript, encodes and signs EntryChunks and Advertisements) is effectively unmaintained. The maintained tooling is Go (storetheindex and related).
- **Failure modes.** Single operator and grant funding; metering and paid tiers arriving; ingest delays; severe outages were reported earlier in 2025. Policy risk: advertising multihashes one does not serve, as declarations, could be treated as abuse. Entries are unverified, so spam is possible. Lookups are visible to the operator.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Nothing would be built for these definitions, but IPNI is itself a crawling index, and in practice one operator. It does not meet 'many independent parties already operate'.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - cid.contact/providers listed 162 providers, 82 with an advertisement in the previous 30 days, 156 with HTTP publisher addresses (my GET and count) (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://cid.contact/providers
  - Requests to cid.contact have officially doubled compared to mid 2025; daily IPNI requests scaling past the 198M mark; IPNI uptime has successfully maintained its &gt;99% record (as of 2026-07-13). Source: https://discuss.ipfs.tech/t/probelab-s-notable-ipfs-performance-results-week-29-2026/20276
  - Funded by three Filecoin ProPGF grant batches (approved 2026-06-03, 2026-06-08, 2026-07-22); milestone 'Build immediate sync-on-announce for paid publishers and route their ingest to a dedicated lane'; per-provider metering; 'serving ~220M requests/day' (as of 2026-10-10). Source: https://www.karmahq.org/project/ipni-interplanetary-network-indexer
  - Example policy in the spec: 'If a provider cannot be dialed for 2 days, its advertisements will no longer be returned to clients.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/ipni/specs/blob/main/IPNI.md
  - npm @web3-storage/ipni 3.1.0 was last published 2023-12-15 and had 9 downloads in the week to 2026-11-07 (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/@web3-storage/ipni
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption and trajectory unchanged (162 publishers, 82 active, about 200M lookups a day, active commits, grant funding). no_aggregator 2 to 1: this is an index, and one team operates the only production instance; it is the thing the maintainer's first value excludes, just not purpose-built. reverse_lookup 4 to 3: the HTTP query is fast and CORS-enabled, but acceptance of a tiny publisher advertising multihashes as declarations is untested, ingestion rules are being tightened, and metering and a paid lane are on the roadmap. publisher_effort 3 to 2: there is no maintained TypeScript library (9 downloads a week), so the chain encoder and signer must be written, and the origin must stay dialable or the provider is dropped after 2 days.

#### BitTorrent Mainline DHT: get_peers / announce_peer (BEP 5)

The Kademlia DHT run by BitTorrent clients. It maps a 20-byte infohash to the IP addresses and ports of peers that announced it.

- **Publish.** A UDP node calls get_peers to obtain tokens, then announce_peer(id, info_hash, port, token). 'the queried node should store the IP address of the querying node and the supplied port number under the infohash'. The IP is the packet's source address; only the 16-bit port is chosen by the announcer, and the token is bound to the IP. BEP 5 gives no storage lifetime; libtorrent re-announces every 15 minutes by default.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not provided by the DHT. The reader connects to the returned IP:port and speaks some transfer protocol.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native and index-free: get_peers(infohash) returns 'values', a list of 6-byte compact peer entries (IPv4 address plus port). Any Mainline node answers it. It returns network locations, not principals: to learn who announced, the reader must connect to that socket or use a helper service. A serverless or NATed publisher has no meaningful IP:port, so the mechanism does not work for them.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Immediate on announce. BEP 51 sample_infohashes (status Draft) lets a crawler survey stored infohashes, so enumeration exists only by crawling.
- **Identity.** None. A record is an IP and a port.
- **Digest fit.** Keys are 20 bytes. A Blake3-256 digest must be reduced, for example SHA-1(digest), which is what iroh-content-discovery does, or the first 20 bytes. Deterministic but lossy.
- **Governance.** BitTorrent Enhancement Proposals at bittorrent.org; the core BEP is long frozen.
- **Publisher cost.** A long-lived UDP node on a stable public address, re-announcing every few minutes.
- **TypeScript tooling.** bittorrent-dht (webtorrent), Node only because it needs UDP.
- **Failure modes.** Sybil and poisoning are easy; announcer IPs are public (iroh's README: 'there is no privacy'); UDP is unencrypted and easy to block; records are short-lived; no browser access.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. The lookup itself needs nothing, but turning IP:port into an identity needs a live server at that address or a helper index such as iroh's address index.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - BEP 5 status: Accepted, Standards Track (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.bittorrent.org/beps/bep_0005.html
  - pkarr README: 'With 15 years of proven reliability and 10+ million active nodes' (self-reported, not a measurement) (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/pubky/pkarr
  - IPinfo saw 3,152,801 Rostelecom IP addresses on the Mainline DHT over 30 days; 'The lowest estimates always start in the millions of peers' (as of 2025-09-27). Source: https://torrentfreak.com/bittorrents-dht-and-how-customers-of-major-isps-help-keep-it-vibrant-alive-250927/
  - libtorrent defaults: dht_announce_interval 15*60 seconds, dht_max_peers_reply 100, dht_max_peers 500 per torrent (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.libtorrent.org/reference-Settings.html
  - npm bittorrent-dht 11.0.12 published 2026-05-25, 41,011 downloads in the week to 2026-11-07 (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/bittorrent-dht
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 4 to 3: the network is in the millions, but the only current evidence is one ISP's IP count and a project's own claim, the last full measurement is from 2013, and all of it is file-sharing use; nothing agent-facing reads it. Other scores unchanged: the lookup returns an IP and port and nothing else, and a serverless publisher has no address to announce.

#### BEP 44 items and pkarr (public-key addressable resource records) on Mainline

BEP 44 stores small values in the Mainline DHT: immutable items under the SHA-1 of the value, and mutable items signed with Ed25519 under SHA-1(public key + salt). Pkarr packs DNS records into a mutable item so an Ed25519 key acts as a domain name.

- **Publish.** Sign a packet of at most 1000 bytes and put it to the DHT over UDP, or PUT it to an HTTP pkarr relay ('Web apps use HTTP relays since browsers cannot open UDP sockets'). 'Without re-announcement, these items MAY expire in 2 hours' and 'they SHOULD be re-announced once an hour'.
- **Fetch by digest.** Immutable items are fetched by the SHA-1 of their bencoded value, up to about 1000 bytes. SHA-1 only.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. The lookup target is derived from the publisher's public key and salt. With a known key K and salt H one can ask 'does K have a record for H', which is a forward check. No query returns the set of keys that mention H, and BEP 44 items cannot be enumerated.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Only for a key you already know. That suits discovery from a single introduction (the key's record lists its endpoints), not discovery of strangers.
- **Identity.** An Ed25519 signature by a self-held key, the same primitive as a basic KERI identifier, but with no rotation and no KEL. A record could point to an AID's OOBI.
- **Digest fit.** A digest can ride as salt or as record content, but it is not a lookup key.
- **Publisher cost.** A signed packet republished about hourly; a relay makes this possible from Lambda or a browser.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @synonymdev/pkarr (JavaScript/WASM bindings); bittorrent-dht supports BEP 44 put and get.
- **Failure modes.** Records vanish within hours without republishing; relays cache aggressively; a 1000-byte ceiling; relay operators become chokepoints for browser users.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Reverse lookup would require someone to collect and index every publisher's records.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - BEP 44 status: Draft (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.bittorrent.org/beps/bep_0044.html
  - pkarr repository: 469 stars; 'Records are ephemeral' - 'The DHT drops records after hours; republish periodically' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/pubky/pkarr
  - npm @synonymdev/pkarr 8.1.0 published 2026-10-02, 123 downloads in the week to 2026-11-07 (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/@synonymdev/pkarr
  - iroh publishes signed pkarr records by default to an iroh-dns-server 'run by n0'; Mainline DHT address lookup 'is opt-in' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://docs.iroh.computer/concepts/discovery
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 2 to 1 and trajectory 3 to 2: BEP 44 is still Draft, the JavaScript package has 123 weekly downloads, and pkarr's largest user (iroh) publishes to n0's own DNS server by default with the DHT opt-in. Other scores unchanged. Confirmed there is no reverse lookup; a SAID does fit as a salt (64-byte limit), which makes the forward check possible.

#### Iroh (iroh-blobs plus iroh-content-discovery)

n0's QUIC library that dials peers by Ed25519 EndpointId. iroh-blobs transfers BLAKE3-addressed blobs with verified streaming. iroh-content-discovery adds global 'who has this BLAKE3 hash' on top of the Mainline DHT.

- **Publish.** Run a daemon (iroh-share) that keeps announcing SHA-1(blake3_hash) on Mainline from its UDP socket and stores a signed record with its EndpointId in a small 'udp address index' service keyed by its verified host:port.
- **Fetch by digest.** Dial the EndpointId with iroh, request the blob over iroh-blobs, and 'verify every byte against the BLAKE3 hash'.
- **Reverse lookup.** get_peers(SHA-1(H)) on Mainline returns host:port pairs; the address index translates each into a candidate EndpointId; the reader dials them. Mainline is native; the address index is a helper service whose public operators I could not identify (servers announce themselves under a rendezvous infohash).
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Through Mainline; no registration.
- **Identity.** EndpointId is an Ed25519 public key with a signed record in the address index. It could be bound to a KERI AID by a signed statement; it is not an AID.
- **Digest fit.** The iroh hash is a 32-byte BLAKE3 digest, so the SAID's raw digest bytes are the key with no re-hashing. Verified transfer succeeds only if the blob served is the SAID pre-image.
- **Governance.** A single company (n0) with open-source crates; no standards body.
- **Publisher cost.** A continuously running daemon with a UDP socket.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @number0/iroh (Node.js binding for iroh core). I found no JavaScript binding for content discovery; the browser path is a local gateway plus an extension.
- **Failure modes.** Inherits Mainline's IP exposure and blockable UDP; depends on the address index; the content discovery and blobs layers are pre-production; no serverless publisher.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Needs the address index helper. It is generic to iroh, not to these definitions, and is experimental.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Iroh 1.0 released 2026-06-15: 'The public relays we run have seen more than 200 million endpoints created, in the last 30 days alone.' (as of 2026-06-15). Source: https://iroh.computer/blog/v1
  - In addition to the Rust crate, we now officially support Python, Node.js, Swift, and Kotlin. (as of 2026-06-15). Source: https://iroh.computer/blog/v1
  - iroh-blobs README: 'this version of iroh-blobs is not yet considered production quality'; for production 'use iroh-blobs 0.35'; 'Connection establishment is left up to the user or higher level APIs.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/n0-computer/iroh-blobs
  - iroh-content-discovery README: 'This is experimental.' and 'A provider announces SHA-1(blake3_hash) on Mainline.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/n0-computer/iroh-content-discovery
  - npm @number0/iroh 1.2.0 published 2026-10-22, 21,286 downloads in the week to 2026-11-07 (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/@number0/iroh
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 2 to 1 and trajectory 3 to 2: iroh's core is healthy, but the component that does this job is a 15-star repository marked experimental, on top of a blobs crate marked not production quality. no_aggregator 3 to 2: the lookup needs address-index servers whose operators and discovery the README does not describe. Others unchanged; BLAKE3-native keys remain its one real advantage.

#### Hypercore / HyperDHT (Holepunch Pear)

Holepunch's stack: HyperDHT (a Kademlia DHT with hole punching), Hyperswarm (topic-based peer discovery) and Hypercore (signed append-only logs).

- **Publish.** A Node or Bare process with UDP calls announce(topic, keyPair): 'Announce that you are listening on a key-pair to the DHT under a specific topic'; 'When announcing you'll send a signed proof to peers that you own the key-pair'. The topic is a '32-byte topic buffer'. Announcements expire (TTL not documented) and are refreshed while the process runs.
