- **Fetch by digest.** immutableGet(hash) returns small values stored with immutablePut, addressed by the DHT's own hash of the value; larger data is replicated as Hypercores from the discovered peers.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native and keyed by an arbitrary 32 bytes: lookup(topic) returns a stream whose values contain peers: [{ publicKey, nodes: [{ host, port }] }]. HyperDHT nodes answer it; no index. It returns public keys with a signed ownership proof, the best semantic fit in this family, but only for announcers that are online now.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Join through the default bootstrap nodes (three hosts at hyperdht.org, 'publicly served on behalf of the commons'), then announce.
- **Identity.** An Ed25519 key pair with a signed announce. A KERI AID could sign a binding to that key; the key itself has no rotation or KEL.
- **Digest fit.** A 32-byte topic takes the raw Blake3-256 digest directly as a rendezvous key. It is not usable as the content address for immutable values.
- **Governance.** A single company (Holepunch); no standards body.
- **Publisher cost.** A long-lived process with UDP.
- **TypeScript tooling.** hyperdht and hyperswarm (JavaScript, actively released).
- **Failure modes.** Announcers must stay online; no production browser path; bootstrap run by one company; network size unpublished; announcer addresses are exposed.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** no. The DHT answers the topic lookup directly.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - npm hyperdht 6.36.0 published 2026-11-04, 52,474 downloads in the week to 2026-11-07 (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/hyperdht
  - holepunchto/hyperdht: 417 stars; 'The default bootstrap servers are publicly served on behalf of the commons.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/holepunchto/hyperdht
  - @hyperswarm/dht-relay (the browser path) 0.4.3, last published 2023-10-30; described in search results as experimental and not for production (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/@hyperswarm/dht-relay
- **Skeptic's re-score.** no_aggregator 4 to 3: the DHT answers directly, but all three default bootstrap hosts belong to one company and there is no HTTP path for browsers or agents beyond a relay last published in 2023. Other scores unchanged. Its lookup returning public keys is the best semantics in the family, limited to announcers that are online at that moment.

#### AGNTCY Agent Directory Service (libp2p Kad-DHT, CIDs, OCI)

A Linux Foundation project (originated at Cisco) for publishing and discovering AI agent records. Records follow the OASF schema, are stored as OCI artifacts named by CID, and are routed among directory servers with a libp2p Kad-DHT. It is where this family touches agent tooling.

- **Publish.** Push a record to a Directory server's store to get its CID, then run routing publish 'so the server advertises the CID and its skill taxonomy in the DHT'. 'Announcements are processed asynchronously and have a TTL; republish periodically to keep routing data fresh.' The publisher needs a Directory server (docs suggest running an instance on AWS EKS) or write access to one; the public instance is read-only.
- **Fetch by digest.** Pull the OCI artifact by its SHA-256 digest from a server the DHT names.
- **Reverse lookup.** routing search by skill labels returns 'matching CIDs + server addresses' ('a two-phase lookup: capability to CIDs, then CID to servers'). It answers 'which servers host records with skill S', not 'who references digest H'; the paper says 'ADS does not natively index arbitrary free-form tags.'
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Join the directory network through operator-supplied bootstrap peers.
- **Identity.** 'Records can be signed using OIDC flows or private keys'; verification through the dirctl tool. No self-certifying identifier model.
- **Digest fit.** SHA-256 CIDs over OCI artifacts. A Blake3 SAID would be a field inside a record, not the record's address.
- **Governance.** AGNTCY, a Series of LF Projects, LLC; the specification is an individual IETF draft with no IETF standing.
- **Publisher cost.** A Go directory server plus an OCI registry, or dependence on someone else's.
- **TypeScript tooling.** A JS/TS SDK is listed (npm install agntcy-dir).
- **Failure modes.** The paper assumes 'DHT peers assumed honest-but-curious'; no deployment numbers; heavy to operate; keyed to an agent skill taxonomy that does not model these definitions.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Nothing MAD-specific, but the network is purpose-built for agent records and a publisher must operate or borrow a directory server.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Directory v1.0 announced 2026-02-19: 'Agent Directory uses libp2p Kad-DHT for both server and content discovery.' (as of 2026-02-19). Source: https://blogs.agntcy.org/technical/2026/02/19/dir-v1.html
  - ADS v1.5 ships with a reference implementation of the ARD specification; records exposed as AI Catalog entries with identifiers of the form urn:ai:org.agntcy:cid:&lt;cid&gt; (as of 2026-06-17). Source: https://blogs.agntcy.org/technical/2026/06/17/ai-catalog-over-directory.html
  - draft-mp-agntcy-ads-03, dated 2026-11-02: individual Internet-Draft, intended status Informational, authors from Cisco, not adopted by a working group (as of 2026-11-02). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-mp-agntcy-ads/
  - Linux Foundation accepted AGNTCY on 2025-07-29 with Cisco, Dell Technologies, Google Cloud, Oracle and Red Hat as formative members (as of 2025-07-29). Source: https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/linux-foundation-welcomes-the-agntcy-project-to-standardize-open-multi-agent-system-infrastructure-and-break-down-ai-agent-silos
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 2 to 1: the source shows a separate DHT with an empty default bootstrap list and a TODO to deploy bootstrap nodes; the public instance is read-only and restricted to AGNTCY organization members, so there is no open network to publish into. no_aggregator 2 to 1: a publisher must run or borrow a directory server. reverse_lookup 2 to 1: the lookup maps skill labels to records to servers; it has no query for 'who references digest H'. Trajectory stays 3 (Linux Foundation, active commits, draft revised 2026-11-02).

#### DASL / RASL with FISL and ATFISL (IPFS Foundation, 2025-2026)

The IPFS Foundation's simplified content-addressing profile. DASL CIDs allow only SHA-256; BDASL adds BLAKE3; RASL fetches a CID from hinted hosts over HTTP; FISL is an HTTP lookup API from CID to locations; ATFISL announces locations as records in the publisher's AT Protocol repository. It crosses into the atproto family.

- **Publish.** RASL: serve the bytes at https://host/.well-known/rasl/{cid}. ATFISL: write a record in the collection dev.fisl.at, keyed by the CID, with addrs (each a url and optional expires) in your own atproto repository.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET /.well-known/rasl/{cid} on a hinted host and verify the hash.
- **Reverse lookup.** FISL: GET /routing/locations/{cid}/{format} on an indexer, which builds its table by 'subscribing to a firehose or relay for the dev.fisl.at collection'. Without an indexer, a getRecord call on a known publisher's PDS 'answers "does this DID serve this CID?" in one request'. So reverse lookup depends on someone running a FISL indexer; I found none confirmed.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Write the record; any indexer on the atproto relay firehose sees it.
- **Identity.** An atproto DID with signed repository commits; domain-verified handles. Not KERI.
- **Digest fit.** DASL CID: 'If hash type is not equal to 0x12 (SHA-256), throw an error.' BDASL allows 0x1e (BLAKE3) but says 'it isn't available in browsers' and recommends avoiding it 'in arbitrary open environments'. ATFISL keys must be DASL or BDASL CIDs, so the blake3 CID of a SAID fits as a record key.
- **Governance.** IPFS Foundation editors; community specifications, no IETF draft cited on the pages I read.
- **Publisher cost.** One record write to an atproto PDS.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @atcute/cid 2.5.0 (2026-09-24) for DASL CIDs; standard atproto SDKs for records.
- **Failure modes.** Brand new and unproven; reverse lookup needs an indexer on the relay firehose; blake3 is second-class.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Needs a FISL indexer that is generic to all CIDs, not to these definitions; the spec does not say who runs one.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - DASL CID status 'Stable'; RASL and BDASL 'Stabilising'; FISL and ATFISL 'Hot New Stuff'; all last updated 2026-11-03 (as of 2026-11-03). Source: https://dasl.ing/
  - ATFISL is a content discovery system for CIDs built with the AT Protocol. (as of 2026-11-03). Source: https://dasl.ing/atfisl.html
  - FISL: 'The lookup API path and shape are modelled on the IPFS Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API.' and it 'is not conformant with that API and does not intend to be.' (as of 2026-11-03). Source: https://dasl.ing/fisl.html
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_trajectory 3 to 2: the lookup parts are at the least mature label, no indexer and no adopter is named, and the sponsoring foundation is the residual steward of an ecosystem that just lost its main engineering team. A direction without named adopters is not credible convergence. Other scores unchanged.

#### Veilid and Willow / Earthstar

Two newer peer-to-peer projects that do not offer content routing. Veilid is a privacy-oriented application framework with a DHT of owned records. Willow is a family of specifications (data model, Meadowcap capabilities, confidential sync, drop format) for syncing data among consenting peers.

- **Publish.** Veilid: write a value (at most 1 MiB) into a DHT record whose key derives from a public key and a schema digest. Willow: write entries into a namespace and sync with peers you already know.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not available in either. Veilid looks records up by record key only. Willow has no global discovery: 'Only devices which have been given explicit consent to store data can receive it.'
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Veilid: you must already know the record key. Willow: you must already know a peer.
- **Identity.** Veilid: updates signed by the record owner's key pair. Willow: Meadowcap capabilities signed by namespace and subspace keys.
- **Digest fit.** A digest can be stored as data but is not a lookup key in either.
- **Failure modes.** Small networks, no browser or serverless publisher story that I could confirm, and no reverse lookup.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Neither has a 'who has X' query, so reverse lookup would need an index built for the purpose.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Veilid latest release 0.5.7; VeilidChat public beta announced 2026-09-28; no node counts published (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://veilid.com/
  - Willow was funded through the NGI Assure Fund established by NLnet; the home page states no specification status (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://willowprotocol.org/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** No change. Confirmed that neither offers any lookup by content digest.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (p2p-content)

The skeptic re-checked 29 claims; 16 held as stated. The rest:

- *partly* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: The Go and JS implementations lost their dedicated maintainers; 'the implementations have no committed maintainers'.
  - Correction: True for Kubo, Boxo, Helia and Someguy. Not true for the DHT library itself: go-libp2p-kad-dht released v0.43.0 on 2026-10-28, its README says 'Maintained by @guillaumemichel', and a CODEOWNERS file was added 2026-11-03. js-libp2p published packages on 2026-10-19 and had commits to 2026-10-27. The routing library is in better shape than the node software around it.
  - Checked: GitHub API for libp2p/go-libp2p-kad-dht and libp2p/js-libp2p.
- *partly* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: Since October 2026 the bootstrap nodes and delegated-ipfs.dev are stewarded by the IPNI / Compass team.
  - Correction: The post names three people (Alex Firmani, Bart Święcki, Takwa Gnenna) as 'stepping up as an infrastructure provider' for the Service Worker Gateway and delegated routing, and credits them with 'resilient bootstrapping' while also saying the IPFS Project keeps operating 'the bootstrappers'. It states no funding, terms or duration. The endpoint is up (Envoy headers, CORS '*') and the bootstrap DNS records still resolve.
  - Checked: IPFS blog post of 2026-10-14; live response headers from delegated-ipfs.dev; DNS for bootstrap.libp2p.io.
- *partly* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: ProbeLab: 23k-25k distinct DHT server IPs and about 400k weekly clients (July 2026), about 4k servers generally online (February 2026); the DHT is something 'about 23k-25k independent server IPs already operate'.
  - Correction: The quotes are accurate, but the Week 36 report defines them: 23k-25k is server IP addresses as a weekly average over crawls, 12k-13k is the count of server peer IDs, and 'the number of servers at a specific point in time stands at 4k nodes simultaneously online'. The honest size of the infrastructure that answers a query is 4k servers, not 23k-25k. Clients spiked to about 800k in mid-August, which ProbeLab links to bot traffic.
  - Checked: Both forum posts, plus a newer one the researcher did not find: 'Week 36, 2026', posted 2026-09-08.
- *refuted* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: No ProbeLab report after July 2026 was found.
  - Correction: A Week 36 report dated 2026-09-08 exists (topic 20346). It is still the newest; there is no published measurement of the DHT after Shipyard's exit on 2026-09-30.
  - Checked: Forum search API on discuss.ipfs.tech.
- *partly* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: A provider record 'proves the holder of key P announced K within 48 hours'.
  - Correction: It proves that only to the DHT server that received the announcement over an authenticated connection. The stored record carries no signature (the code comment says 'we should sign them and check signature later'), so a reader, or anyone using the HTTP router, is taking the word of the DHT servers or the router. A dishonest server can name any peer ID.
  - Checked: handlers.go.
- *partly* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: A SAID's Blake3-256 digest re-encodes losslessly as a CIDv1 (raw, blake3), and both delegated-ipfs.dev and cid.contact answered such a CID with HTTP 200 and provider lists.
  - Correction: The conversion is right and both routers accept blake3 keys for reads. But the test CID is the digest of empty input, which other parties already announce (10 providers on delegated-ipfs.dev, 4 on cid.contact). For an unannounced blake3 CID, delegated-ipfs.dev returned 200 with an empty list in 0.9 s and cid.contact returned 404. Nothing here shows that a new MAD publisher's announcement becomes visible.
  - Checked: Recomputed the conversion locally (SAID EK8TSbn1-... gives CID bafkr4ifpcne3t5pz...; exact match). Queried both routers at about 03:15 UTC on 2026-11-09 with that CID and with a blake3 CID nobody has announced.
- *refuted* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: The reverse-lookup query on delegated-ipfs.dev 'works the same on cid.contact'.
  - Correction: The network's own configuration lists cid.contact for the system 'IPNI' only and delegated-ipfs.dev for 'AminoDHT' and 'IPNI'. In my probe cid.contact returned only the 4 index-origin providers where delegated-ipfs.dev returned 10. A publisher that announces on the DHT (the sketch's main path) is visible over HTTP through one hostname only.
  - Checked: conf.ipfs-mainnet.org/autoconf.json and side-by-side queries.
- *partly* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: Browsers cannot walk the DHT and need an HTTP delegated router.
  - Correction: The practical conclusion stands (Helia in browsers defaults to delegated routing), but it is a default and a performance limit, not an impossibility.
  - Checked: Provider records in my probes include browser-dialable addresses (/tls/ws on libp2p.direct, WebTransport), so a browser libp2p node can reach some DHT servers in principle. I did not test a browser DHT walk.
- *unverifiable* · IPFS Amino DHT provider records, read via the Delegated Routing V1 HTTP API · claim: A scheduled Lambda running js-libp2p in client mode can announce each key for 'cents per month', advertising only an HTTPS address.
  - Correction: This is the step the whole pick rests on and nobody has run it: a cold-started js-libp2p node must bootstrap, walk the DHT and store a record on about 20 servers per key within a Lambda invocation, and the record must keep an HTTPS address that short-lived client never listens on.
  - Checked: Nothing can be tested read-only. The researcher lists this as untested too.
- *partly* · IPNI (InterPlanetary Network Indexer, cid.contact) · claim: A publisher needs only a static signed advertisement chain plus one HTTP PUT, with no periodic re-announce; elastic-ipfs/publisher-lambda is a precedent; the JavaScript library @web3-storage/ipni is effectively unmaintained.
  - Correction: All quoted facts check out. Two things weaken 'no cron, three S3 objects': the spec's example policy drops a provider that cannot be dialed for 2 days and discards its chain after 2 weeks, and the indexer team merged an 'ingestion-hardening' branch on 2026-11-06. Whether cid.contact ingests a tiny publisher that advertises multihashes it uses as declarations is untested, and the publisher would have to write the chain encoder and signer itself.
  - Checked: ipni/specs IPNI.md, the publisher-lambda README, npm (@web3-storage/ipni 3.1.0, published 2023-12-15, 9 downloads last week), and recent storetheindex commits.
- *partly* · BitTorrent Mainline DHT: get_peers / announce_peer (BEP 5) · claim: Mainline is 'far larger': '10+ million active nodes' (pkarr), 3,152,801 Rostelecom IPs in 30 days (IPinfo via TorrentFreak).
  - Correction: Both quotes are accurate. The first is a project's own claim; the second is one ISP's 30-day IP count. I found no measurement of total network size newer than the 2013 study (16-28 million) that the article and Wikipedia still cite. 'Millions' is safe; a current figure does not exist.
  - Checked: pkarr README, the TorrentFreak article of 2025-09-27, and a search for any 2025-2026 size measurement.
- *partly* · Hypercore / HyperDHT (Holepunch Pear) · claim: lookup(topic) on a 32-byte topic returns peers as public keys with a signed ownership proof; three bootstrap hosts at hyperdht.org; hyperdht 6.36.0 (2026-11-04), 52,474 weekly downloads, 417 stars; the browser relay was last published 2023-10-30.
  - Correction: API shape, bootstrap list, version, downloads and stars all confirmed. I did not find the 'signed proof' sentence in my read, and the README states no announce lifetime. The relay package still has 6,496 weekly downloads, so it is in use, but its 'not for production' label came from a search summary and I could not confirm it.
  - Checked: hyperdht README, npm, GitHub API.
- *partly* · DASL / RASL with FISL and ATFISL (IPFS Foundation, 2025-2026) · claim: DASL CID is Stable, RASL and BDASL Stabilising, FISL and ATFISL 'Hot New Stuff', all updated 2026-11-03; ATFISL keys records by CID in the collection dev.fisl.at; reverse lookup needs a FISL indexer and none is confirmed.
  - Correction: Status labels and quotes confirmed; neither spec names a running indexer or any adopter. The index page shows no dates; only the FISL page showed 2026-11-03.
  - Checked: dasl.ing index, fisl.html, atfisl.html.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (p2p-content)

- **Arweave transaction tags queried through gateway GraphQL.** The researcher set Arweave aside as payment-based without examining it. It is the one mechanism in this family where the record is signed by the publisher's key, is permanent (no 48-hour heartbeat, no cron) and is found by an arbitrary tag: a query for tag name 'said' with value H returns every data item carrying it, with the owner's address. I ran a tag query against arweave.net/graphql and got signed owners back; turbo-gateway.com failed the same broad query on a row limit. It does not rescue the family: full tag indexes are run by two or three operators (ar.io's docs say gateways 'only return data they've indexed' and name turbo-gateway.com and Goldsky; arweave.net went back to Forward Research on 2026-03-01), it ties the project to a token ecosystem, records cannot be withdrawn, and no agent tooling reads it. It does beat the best pick on publisher effort and on what the lookup returns. Source: https://docs.ar.io/build/access/find-data
- **Storacha content claims and indexing service.** Closest thing in the IPFS world to 'signed statements about a multihash': claims signed by a DID, queried by multihash over HTTP. It is one company's service (default endpoint indexer.storacha.network) and its claim types are about where bytes live, so it is another single-operator index, not a way out. I confirmed only the package documentation, not the live service. Source: https://pkg.go.dev/github.com/storacha/indexing-service
- **WebTorrent WebSocket trackers as a browser rendezvous keyed by a 20-byte hash.** Answers the brief's question about browser publishers on BitTorrent without UDP. It finds only peers that are online at the same moment and relies on a few volunteer trackers, so it does not change the conclusion. From background knowledge; not re-verified in this session.
- **ProbeLab Week 36 report (2026-09-08).** Not a mechanism, but the report's adoption evidence missed it. It is the newest measurement and it defines the headline numbers: 4k DHT servers online at once, 12k-13k server peer IDs a week, 23k-25k server IP addresses a week. Source: https://discuss.ipfs.tech/t/probelab-s-notable-ipfs-performance-results-week-36-2026/20346

### What the researcher could not verify (p2p-content)

- My fetch tool summarises pages. Entries marked verbatim are text it returned inside quotation marks; I did not check them character for character, and it shortened some long quotes.
- No ProbeLab report after July 2026 was found, so I have no Amino DHT server count from after Shipyard's exit on 2026-09-30.
- Who exactly operates delegated-ipfs.dev, ipfs.io, dweb.link and the bootstrap nodes today. The 2026-10-14 IPFS blog credits the IPNI / Compass team with delegated routing and bootstrapping and links delegated-ipfs.dev; Shipyard said Protocol Labs owns the domains. No per-service statement found.
- Whether js-libp2p can complete DHT provides inside an AWS Lambda invocation, how long each key takes, and whether an announce-only HTTPS multiaddr is accepted end to end. Not tested: the task was read-only.
- Whether cid.contact will ingest a small static HTTPS publisher, especially one advertising multihashes it uses as declarations; its publisher policy and free-tier terms (the Karma page was cut off at 'with the free tier continuing on...').
- The IPNI ContextID length limit: the spec file I read states none.
- That go-libp2p-kad-dht requires at least one address in an ADD_PROVIDER record, and whether DHT servers cap provider records per key: both come from a summarised read of handlers.go.
- Current Mainline DHT size: no 2025-2026 measurement found; '10+ million' is pkarr's own claim. BEP 5 gives no storage lifetime for announce_peer; I have only libtorrent's 15-minute re-announce default. BEP 44 salt size limit not checked.
- Who runs public instances of iroh's udp address index, its record TTL, and the repository's last commit date.
- HyperDHT node count, announce TTL and the hash used by immutablePut; Keet user numbers; the 'do not use in production' wording for the DHT relay came from a search summary.
- Whether AGNTCY's Kad-DHT is separate from the Amino DHT (docs imply operator-supplied bootstrap peers but do not say), its TTL values, and the 'nearly 150 members' figure, which came from a search summary of a Cisco Outshift page.
- Whether any public FISL indexer is running.
- The missing access-control-allow-origin header on a cached delegated-ipfs.dev GET and the single 30-second timeout are observations from one session.
- The SAID pre-image approach (serving the placeholder-filled document under the blake3 CID) follows from how a SAID is computed; I did not test it against a gateway or Helia.
- Veilid node counts and crypto details, and Willow specification status labels, were not on the pages I could read.
- Not examined: Autonomi, Ethereum Swarm, Codex and Arweave (token or payment based). Nostr and AT Protocol proper belong to other families; ATFISL is noted only because the IPFS Foundation publishes it.

<a id="atproto"></a>

## AT Protocol

**Researcher's summary.** atproto gives every party a signed, publicly readable repository of typed records at no cost, and a record key may be any 1-512 character string over A-Za-z0-9.-_:~, so a 44-character Blake3 CESR SAID can be the key itself (at://&lt;did&gt;/&lt;collection&gt;/&lt;SAID&gt;). "Does publisher P declare H" is therefore one native GET to P's own server (com.atproto.repo.getRecord), and com.atproto.sync.getRecord returns a signed proof of existence or non-existence; a live Bluesky PDS accepted a SAID-shaped key as valid syntax (RecordNotFound, not a syntax error). "Who else declares H" is NOT native to a PDS or a relay: a PDS answers only for its own repos, and relays are now non-archival event forwarders. Two generic indexes that already exist answer it: com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection (DIDs holding any record of a collection; served today by Bluesky PBC, microcosm's lightrail and waow.tech, while five other public relays I probed return 404), and Constellation, microcosm's all-network backlink index, which answers "which records reference this URI" for arbitrary record types in one free request and is run by one person. Neither is guaranteed complete: for the same collection the four directory hosts returned 753, 695, 665 and 421 DIDs today, and Constellation's README says no instance has a full network backfill and uptime is best-effort. atproto's own lexicon publication system (NSID to DNS TXT to DID to a com.atproto.lexicon.schema record) works (verified live) but is by-name only, only 753 repos publish schemas against about 3,470 collection NSIDs active in the last week, and directory sites (Lexicon Garden, lexicon.store, UFOs) appeared anyway, generated from the firehose rather than hand-curated. Scale: 47.5M Bluesky accounts and about 4,000 independent PDS hosts; the IETF ATP working group charter was approved 2026-03-19 covering repository, sync and the at: URI, with lexicon publication and application schemas out of scope and no WG-adopted drafts yet. Counter-signals: Similarweb puts the Bluesky app's monthly actives about 52% below peak, and Bluesky PBC still operates plc.directory, the default relay and the most complete collection directory. Identity is did:plc or hostname-level did:web with the repo signing key held by the PDS, so a KERI AID cannot be the account identifier; it rides as a signed payload plus cross-references, which is additive but leaves two identity systems. Record CIDs are sha-256 only and records are re-encoded as DAG-CBOR with floats disallowed, so the SAID travels as key, string and URI, and definition bytes travel as an exact string or blob verified client-side. Net: strong on "no purpose-built aggregator" and local-first, middling on wide adoption (one ecosystem; AI tooling reads it only through Lexicon Garden's MCP endpoint), weak on KERI-native identity.

**Researcher's best pick.** A composition, not a single endpoint: digest-keyed declaration records in each party's own atproto repository (record key = the SAID), with reverse lookup through the generic backlink index (Constellation) as the one-request fast path and the relay collection directory (listReposByCollection) plus per-repo getRecord as the verifiable fallback. Within this family that is the only arrangement where the maintainer operates nothing and every answer can be re-checked against the publisher's own signed repository. Taken alone, no single atproto mechanism solves the job: the PDS has no cross-repo query, the relay has no content query, and lexicon resolution has no reverse direction.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** It does not remove the aggregator; it outsources it to very few hands, inside one company's ecosystem. The by-digest reverse lookup depends on a single volunteer-run index whose own README says the public instance 'currently runs on a little raspberry pi in my house' with best-effort uptime, an unstable API and no full backfill; the fallback depends on a collection directory where Bluesky PBC's instance is the most complete and the two independent ones returned 8 to 44 percent fewer DIDs for the same query today. Neither backlinks nor lexicon publication nor application schemas are in the IETF working group's scope, and no WG document is adopted yet. Underneath, identity is did:plc in a directory still operated by Bluesky PBC, with repo signing keys held by the hosting provider, which is the opposite of the self-held-key direction; KERI would be a second identity system bolted on, and every party would need an atproto account purely to be discoverable. On the 'very widely adopted' criterion atproto is one ecosystem (large, but with Similarweb showing the flagship app's monthly actives about 52% below peak), and I found no evidence that mainstream AI agent tooling reads atproto records natively; the only agent-facing surface found is the MCP endpoint of a lexicon directory site, which is itself an aggregator. Finally, atproto's own experience argues against the stated objective in its strict form: with name-based resolution in production, at least three lexicon directory sites still appeared.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** The report's facts can be trusted: of about thirty load-bearing claims I re-checked against primary sources or live endpoints, every number reproduced, most to the digit, and my corrections are to framing, not to data. The functional core held under a test the researcher did not run: Constellation returned 60 of 60 randomly sampled third-party records, and opaque non-http URIs with case-sensitive paths are indexed, though lookups are exact-match with no query normalization. What the checks do not rescue is the fit to the maintainer's ranked values. The by-digest lookup has one verifiable public operator, the fallback leans on one company and left about 9% of publishers unanswered in my timed run, and the ecosystem is a single one that AI agent tooling does not read. So this family is a working, zero-cost rendezvous layer that could be used today without running anything, but it is not a foundation: it satisfies "no purpose-built aggregator" only by borrowing someone else's general one, and it does not satisfy "very widely adopted" in the sense the maintainer just asked for. If it is used at all, the declaration should be a self-contained, SAID-addressed, KERI-signed object for which an atproto record is one carrier among several, so the carrier can be dropped without touching identity or content.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

Yes, somebody must run an index. It works today without the maintainer running one, because one volunteer and one company already do.

Walking "given digest H, list everyone who declares H" as a stranger:

0. **Convention.** The stranger must already know the declaration collection NSIDs and the exact URI spelling of H. That comes from MAD's own spec and lexicons. It is a document, not a service, and the lexicon is resolvable by name through DNS TXT and one PDS (verified live).
1. **By digest.** GET `https://constellation.microcosm.blue/links/all?target=<URI of H>`, then `blue.microcosm.links.getBacklinks`. This is the only step that is keyed by the digest.
   - Operator: one person (fig, microcosm), on home hardware with cloud gateways, funded by sponsors, a Bluesky grant and Blacksky. No SLA; the README says best-effort uptime.
   - Arbitrary record types: yes. Every string in every record that parses as an at-uri, DID or URI is indexed. In my test it returned 60 of 60 randomly sampled third-party records from 31 PDS hosts.
   - Arbitrary digests: yes if H is spelled as a URI with the SAID in the path. A bare SAID is not indexed. Lookup is an exact string match with no query normalization, so one canonical spelling must be fixed; a SAID in the host position would be lower-cased.
   - Free: yes, no published rate limit. Durable: no commitment; no backfill, so anything published during an outage is missed for good.
   - Not verified: a SAID-bearing URI end to end, since that needs a write.
2. **Confirm each hit.** Resolve the DID (plc.directory, one central directory, or the publisher's domain for did:web), then fetch the record and proof from the publisher's real PDS host. Constellation's input is Jetstream, so its output is an unsigned hint. This step uses only the publisher's own server.
3. **Fallback by collection.** `com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection` on bsky.network (Bluesky PBC), lightrail.microcosm.blue or relay.waow.tech, then getRecord with rkey = H on every DID returned. It is not keyed by digest. The directories disagree (753, 695, 665, 421; union 763), and in my timed run 150 publishers took 26 seconds with 8.7% giving no answer.
4. **Second fallback, not in the report's pick.** Replay one collection from Bluesky's Jetstream v2 archive and fold it client-side. Exhaustive by design, no service to run, but it needs a Bluesky-issued API key and the quota and price are unpublished.

What still has to be built: a TypeScript client and CLI, the lexicons, a canonical URI rule for H, and a trust filter. The filter is not optional: 52.7 million did:plc identities came from one unreachable host, so a raw "everyone who declares H" list can be inflated at will.

What has to be run: nothing, for as long as fig's instance or Bluesky's directory stays up and free. If both go, the maintainer runs Constellation or lightrail, which is an index.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

The reverse lookup that makes this family attractive is borrowed, and from very few hands. By digest it has exactly one public operator I could verify, a volunteer whose README promises only best-effort uptime and whose index is fed by an unsigned stream. By collection it is led by one company, and the three operators' answers for the same query differ (union 763, intersection 389). None of the pieces that do the work (backlinks, the collection directory, lexicon publication) is in the IETF working group's scope, and that group has adopted no draft in nearly eight months.

Against the maintainer's ranked values:
- **No purpose-built aggregator:** met only in the narrow sense that the aggregator is someone else's general one. The stated test, infrastructure that "many independent parties already operate", is not met.
- **Very wide adoption:** this is one ecosystem. Its flagship app's actives are about half of peak, the company replaced its CEO in March 2026, and weekly third-party use is 270 collections with ten or more writers. I found no AI agent tooling that reads atproto repositories; the agent-discovery work on atproto is a handful of community proposals.
- **KERI direction:** every party must hold an atproto account whose identifier is did:plc (one directory) or DNS, and whose signing key is normally held by the host and can only be secp256k1 or P-256. The principal's own signature has to be smuggled in as a string. That is a second identity root, not a step toward the first.
- **The objective itself:** atproto already ran this experiment for its own schemas. With registry-free, name-based publication in production, a directory site was still built, and its author's stated reason was that there was no central place to browse. Two more followed. The protocol made the directories automatic and replaceable; it did not make them unnecessary.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (atproto)

WRITTEN, AND WHERE. (1) One-off by the maintainer: pick an NSID authority under a domain you control and publish two or three lexicon schemas through the standard route (DNS TXT _lexicon.&lt;authority&gt; = did=&lt;DID&gt;, plus com.atproto.lexicon.schema records). Record types: &lt;ns&gt;.definition (key type any) and either one &lt;ns&gt;.declaration collection with a roles array or, better for lookups, one collection per verb: &lt;ns&gt;.issue, &lt;ns&gt;.rely, &lt;ns&gt;.implement, &lt;ns&gt;.endorse. (2) By each party, in its own repo on any PDS (free hosted account, self-hosted, or did:web): a record at at://&lt;did&gt;/&lt;ns&gt;.&lt;verb&gt;/&lt;SAID&gt;. Fields: said (the 44-character string), subject (the same digest as a URI, so backlink indexes pick it up), optional definition bytes as an exact string or blob with title and description (this is the human-readable reference; generic record browsers render it, no site needed), peers (DIDs of other publishers the party knows, which gives a crawler something to follow from one publisher to the next), and an optional keri block (AID, OOBI, CESR signature or ACDC SAID over the declaration).

QUERIES. Fetch and verify H from a known publisher: GET &lt;pds&gt;/xrpc/com.atproto.repo.getRecord?repo=&lt;did&gt;&collection=&lt;ns&gt;.definition&rkey=&lt;H&gt;, recompute Blake3 over the bytes. Local-first from one introduction: listRecords and describeRepo on that DID's PDS, then follow peers; this touches nobody's index. Who else declares H, fast path: GET https://constellation.microcosm.blue/links/all?target=&lt;digest URI&gt; gives per-verb counts and distinct DIDs in one response; /xrpc/blue.microcosm.links.getBacklinks?subject=&lt;digest URI&gt;&source=&lt;ns&gt;.&lt;verb&gt;:subject lists the records. Who else declares H, fallback: GET &lt;directory&gt;/xrpc/com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection?collection=&lt;ns&gt;.&lt;verb&gt; on bsky.network, lightrail.microcosm.blue or relay.waow.tech, then getRecord with rkey = H on each returned DID's PDS. In both paths treat index output as hints: confirm each hit with com.atproto.sync.getRecord (signed proof of existence, or of non-existence) and, where present, the KERI signature. Enumeration: listReposByCollection on &lt;ns&gt;.definition plus listRecords per repo.

WHAT THE MAINTAINER STILL RUNS. No service. A TypeScript library and CLI (@atproto/api or @atcute/client, @atproto/identity, @atproto/repo for proofs, a Blake3 implementation), the domain and DNS record for the NSID authority, and a trust policy for filtering results by identity, because raw counts are sybil-cheap. Escape hatch if the public indexes disappear: self-host lightrail or Constellation (Raspberry Pi class by their authors' account), which would mean running an index, though a generic one.

KERI PATH. Start with the atproto DID as the publisher identity. Later, without changing record locations: add the keri block to records, list the AID's key or a did:webs URI in the DID document (did:plc verificationMethods and alsoKnownAs allow this), and have the AID issue a designated-aliases ACDC naming the atproto DID. Verifiers that understand KERI then ignore the atproto signature and trust the CESR one; atproto is reduced to transport and rendezvous.

### Mechanisms (atproto)

#### Digest-keyed records in a PDS repository (per-publisher lookup and local-first crawl)

Each account has a signed repository (Merkle search tree, commit signed by the key in its DID document) hosted on a Personal Data Server. Records live at at://&lt;did&gt;/&lt;collection NSID&gt;/&lt;record key&gt; and are readable by anyone over HTTP without auth. This is the base layer every other atproto mechanism sits on.

- **Publish.** The party calls com.atproto.repo.putRecord or createRecord on its own PDS with a collection such as &lt;your-domain-reversed&gt;.declaration and rkey = the 44-character SAID. Body carries the SAID, the roles (issue, rely, implement, endorse), a URI-shaped form of the digest, optional definition bytes and optional peer DIDs. The createRecord lexicon says validation is skipped for unknown lexicons when `validate` is unset, so no registration of the record type is required. Hosted by the party's PDS: a free bsky.social account, another host (eurosky.social, blacksky.app, tngl.sh), or self-hosted.
- **Fetch by digest.** Given a DID: resolve the DID document (plc.directory or https://&lt;host&gt;/.well-known/did.json), read the #atproto_pds endpoint, GET /xrpc/com.atproto.repo.getRecord?repo=&lt;did&gt;&collection=&lt;nsid&gt;&rkey=&lt;SAID&gt;. Verify twice: (1) recompute Blake3-256 over the embedded definition bytes and compare to the SAID; (2) optionally GET com.atproto.sync.getRecord, which returns a CAR with the blocks 'needed to prove the existence or non-existence of record in the current version of repo', and check the commit signature against the DID document key. Bytes must be stored as an exact string or a blob, not as a structured object, because records are re-encoded as DAG-CBOR and floats are disallowed.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native only per publisher. Step 1: you hold a DID. Step 2: getRecord with rkey = H on that DID's PDS; a hit means it declares H, RecordNotFound means it does not (provable with sync.getRecord). Step 3: listRecords and describeRepo on the same PDS enumerate everything that publisher declares and which collections it has. Step 4: follow peer DIDs named in its records and repeat. Infrastructure used: only the publisher's own PDS plus DID resolution. There is no cross-repository query on a PDS, so this never answers 'everyone who declares H', only 'everyone reachable from my introductions'.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher is findable the moment someone is handed its DID or handle (handle resolves through a DNS TXT _atproto record or /.well-known/atproto-did). Without an introduction it is invisible at this layer; visibility to strangers comes from the relay and index layers below. If records carry peer DIDs, one introduction yields a crawlable graph, which is exactly what the reviewers said the ARD draft lacked.
- **Identity.** A claim is authenticated by the repository commit signature, made with the #atproto signing key in the account's DID document (did:plc or hostname-level did:web only). On hosted and default self-hosted setups that key is held by the PDS: 'The signing key is entrusted to the PDS so that it can manage the user's data.' A KERI AID cannot be the account DID. It rides in three additive ways: (1) put a CESR-signed endorsement (or ACDC SAID plus OOBI) inside the record as a string, verified against the KEL independently of atproto; (2) cross-reference from the atproto side: did:plc alsoKnownAs is a 'priority-ordered list of URIs' and since v0.2 'verificationMethods keys may now use any syntactically-valid did:key:, regardless of key format', so the AID's current Ed25519 key or a did:webs/did:keri URI can be listed; (3) cross-reference from the KERI side with a did:webs designated-aliases ACDC naming the did:plc or did:web. did:webs itself cannot be an atproto account DID because atproto says 'only hostname-level did:web DIDs are supported: path-based DIDs are not supported' and did:webs requires the AID as a path element.
- **Digest fit.** Good as a string, poor as a native content address. Record key rule: 'the allowed characters are alphanumeric (A-Za-z0-9), period, dash, underscore, colon, or tilde', 1 to 512 characters, case-sensitive; an unpadded base64url CESR SAID fits, and a live PDS returned RecordNotFound (not a syntax error) for a 44-character SAID-shaped rkey. atproto's own CIDs are fixed: CIDv1, dag-cbor or raw, 'Hash type: sha-256 (0x12) with size of 256 bits'; Blake3 is not in the blessed set ('Additional hash types are likely to be included' is future tense). So the SAID cannot be a strongRef cid or a cid-link field; carry it as rkey, as a string field and as a URI. The same 32 Blake3 bytes could be wrapped as a multihash/CIDv1 for other systems, but atproto tooling would treat that as a non-blessed CID.
- **Governance.** Specifications are written by Bluesky Social PBC; the core (repository, sync, at: URI) entered the IETF in 2026 but nothing is adopted yet. did:plc is a single directory at plc.directory; Bluesky announced on 2025-09-19 that an independent Swiss association would take it over, and an April 2026 podcast says a board was still being set up.
- **Publisher cost.** Zero on a hosted account (bsky.social and several independents are free). Self-hosting: the PDS README recommends 1 GB RAM, 1 CPU core, 20 GB SSD for 1 to 20 users, plus a domain; the production guide notes 'Relays limit new signups on new PDSes until you've established a track record.' One HTTP call per declaration.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @atproto/api (350k weekly downloads), @atproto/identity, @atproto/repo and @atproto/sync for proof verification, @atproto/lex for typed clients; atcute (@atcute/client, 142k weekly) as a lighter alternative. All maintained in 2026.
- **Failure modes.** Publisher's PDS offline means its declarations are unreadable unless cached (Slingshot) or mirrored (Hubble, a Bluesky-funded mirror run by one person). Records are mutable and deletable, so an endorsement can vanish without trace unless you keep the proof CAR. Anyone can create accounts and declare anything; the PLC statistics page labels about 54M identities 'Other / Junk', so reverse-lookup results are claims to be filtered by who you trust, not facts. plc.directory is a single service; the hosted signing key is custodial. Everything is public, including 'rely on' relationships.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. The per-publisher check, enumeration and graph crawl need nothing but the publishers' own servers. A complete, global 'who else declares H' is not answerable at this layer; it needs one of the generic indexes described in the next two mechanisms, or your own firehose consumer.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - 47,470,413 total Bluesky users shown on the live counter (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://bsky-users.theo.io/
  - PLC directory: 128.4M total identities; 3.4K active PDS providers (13.4K all-time); Bluesky-hosted about 72.1M (57.1%), 'Other / Junk' about 54.0M; largest independent provider Blacksky with 44,152 current accounts (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://sifa.id/stats
  - Independent PDS directory: 'Total PDSes: 4013', 'Accounts: 134175', 'Excluding Bridgy: 85642'; largest hosts eurosky.social 19404, certified.one 14346, tngl.sh 9017, blacksky.app 5078 (as of 2026-11-08 (page states no date)). Source: https://blue.mackuba.eu/directory/pdses
  - IETF Authenticated Transfer (atp) working group: charter state changed to Approved on 2026-03-19; chairs Mallory Knodel and Shuping Peng; deliverables are the repository data structure, a low-latency sync protocol, the at: URI scheme and requirements for identifier resolution systems; out of scope includes 'Any application-specific data schemas' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/atp/about/
  - The WG documents page lists no adopted draft-ietf-atp-* document; draft-newbold-atp-aturi-01 dated 2026-10-30 is an individual draft (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/atp/documents/
  - Similarweb via TechCrunch: Bluesky mobile app 10.4M monthly actives in June 2026, down 27.2% year over year; quarterly average 22.1M in Q4 2024 versus 10.7M in Q2 2026 (about -52%) (as of 2026-08-11). Source: https://techcrunch.com/2026/08/11/blueskys-active-user-base-is-shrinking-as-its-focus-expands-beyond-the-app/
  - 39,915 distinct collection NSIDs observed on the firehose since 2025-05-30, 3,470 of them seen in the last 7 days across 574 two-segment authorities (my count by paging the API; includes junk NSIDs such as 'C.d.e.f') (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://ufos-api.microcosm.blue/collections
  - createRecord `validate`: "Can be set to 'false' to skip Lexicon schema validation of record data, 'true' to require it, or leave unset to validate only for known Lexicons." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bluesky-social/atproto/main/lexicons/com/atproto/repo/createRecord.json
  - Bluesky-hosted PDS write limits: 'The limit is 5,000 points per hour and 35,000 points per day.' Create costs 3 points; blob upload limit 52,428,800 bytes (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bluesky-social/bsky-docs/main/docs/advanced-guides/rate-limits.md
  - npm downloads for the week 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-07: @atproto/api 350,049; @atcute/client 141,854; @atproto/repo 66,267; @atproto/identity 21,400 (as of 2026-11-07). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/@atproto/api
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 3 to 2.5: the account and host counts are real, but they describe one ecosystem; measured weekly third-party use is 270 collections with 10 or more writers, and nothing shows AI agent tooling reading repositories. adoption_trajectory 3.5 to 3: the IETF charter is confirmed, but no draft is WG-adopted after nearly eight months, the flagship app's actives are about half of peak, and the company changed CEO in March 2026. no_aggregator 5 to 4.5: nothing is aggregated, but did:plc resolution is a lookup against one central directory whose operational handover I could not confirm. reverse_lookup stays 2: the per-publisher check and the non-existence proof both worked live, and that is all this layer does. publisher_effort stays 4. keri_fit 3 to 2.5: the additive path is real, but rotation keys and repo signing keys are limited to secp256k1 and P-256 and normally held by the host, so the atproto signature is never the principal's, and the SAID cannot be a native CID.

#### Relay collection directory (com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection) plus per-repo fan-out

A standard sync endpoint that 'Enumerates all the DIDs which have records with the given collection NSID.' It is served by a sidecar next to a relay (Bluesky's collectiondir, microcosm's lightrail), fed by the firehose. It was built for backfilling AppViews and is what Bluesky's Tap tool calls to find repos for a custom collection.

- **Publish.** Same record as in the first mechanism; nothing extra. The publisher's PDS announces itself to relays (the reference PDS defaults PDS_CRAWLERS to https://bsky.network) and the directory picks up the new collection from the commit event.
- **Fetch by digest.** Enumerate DIDs for the collection, then getRecord with rkey = SAID on each DID's PDS until one returns the record; verify the Blake3 SAID over the bytes locally. Any holder will do, because the digest authenticates the bytes regardless of who served them.
- **Reverse lookup.** Semi-native: the existing nodes answer a query keyed by collection, not by digest. Step 1: GET https://&lt;directory&gt;/xrpc/com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection?collection=&lt;your declaration NSID&gt; (limit up to 2000, cursor paging) to get every DID that has made any declaration. Step 2: resolve each DID to its PDS. Step 3: getRecord with rkey = H on each; hits are the answer. Cost is one request per publisher in the ecosystem, so it is cheap at hundreds to low thousands of publishers and degrades beyond that; using one collection per verb (…issue, …rely, …implement, …endorse) narrows the fan-out. Operators answering today (probed 2026-11-08): bsky.network, relay1.us-east and relay1.us-west.bsky.network (Bluesky PBC), lightrail.microcosm.blue (microcosm, one maintainer), relay.waow.tech and zlay.waow.tech (one individual). relay.fire.hose.cam, relay3.fr.hose.cam, atproto.africa (Blacksky), relay.upcloud.world and relay.feeds.blue return 404 for it.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Automatic: a brand-new account on any PDS that a relay crawls appears in the directory after its first record in the collection. No seed list, no submission. A self-hosted PDS must be crawled by the relay behind the directory you query.
- **Identity.** The directory returns bare DIDs with no proof; authenticity comes from re-fetching each record from the author's PDS and checking the repo signature, as in the first mechanism. KERI rides the same way (signed payload inside the record plus DID cross-references).
- **Digest fit.** The digest is not part of the query at all; it only appears as the rkey in the fan-out step, where a 44-character SAID is valid. A collection NSID cannot carry a base64url digest (authority segments are case-insensitive letters, digits and hyphens; the name segment is letters and digits only), so 'one collection per digest' would require re-encoding the 32 bytes as lowercase base32 inside an authority segment; that is syntactically possible but an abuse of the namespace I would not build on.
- **Governance.** The endpoint is a com.atproto.sync lexicon owned by Bluesky PBC. Sync is an IETF ATP deliverable but this particular query is not named in the charter text I read. Implementations: Bluesky's Go collectiondir and microcosm's Rust lightrail (experimental).
- **Publisher cost.** Nothing beyond publishing the record.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Plain XRPC GET through @atproto/api or @atcute/client; @atproto/tap (5,194 weekly downloads) wraps the same enumerate-then-backfill pattern if you ever want a local mirror.
- **Failure modes.** Completeness differs by operator (753 versus 421 for the same query today), and Bluesky's copy may also be over-inclusive because, per the lightrail README, only lightrail 'actually removes repos from the index when their last record from a collection is removed'. Fan-out cost grows linearly with publishers and hits PDS rate limits at scale. A spammer can inflate the DID list with junk accounts, turning every lookup into thousands of wasted requests. Accounts taken down at a relay vanish from that relay's directory. Results are unauthenticated hints until each record is re-fetched.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** no. The directory is generic, already running and indexes every collection on the network; nothing is operated for MAD. The honest qualifier is that it is an index operated by three parties, of which Bluesky PBC's is the most complete, and it is self-hostable (lightrail) if those disappear.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Lexicon description: 'Enumerates all the DIDs which have records with the given collection NSID.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bluesky-social/atproto/main/lexicons/com/atproto/sync/listReposByCollection.json
  - Live count of DIDs returned for collection com.atproto.lexicon.schema: 753 (relay1.us-west.bsky.network), 695 (lightrail.microcosm.blue), 665 (relay.waow.tech), 421 (zlay.waow.tech); same query for site.standard.publication on the Bluesky relay returned 14,758 and sh.tangled.repo 5,684 (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://relay1.us-west.bsky.network/xrpc/com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection?collection=com.atproto.lexicon.schema&limit=2000
  - lightrail README: 'almost working well but not stable yet!!'; relays 'don't usually implement listReposByCollection themselves' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://tangled.org/microcosm.blue/lightrail
  - Sync spec: the endpoint 'can be used to get a list of accounts (by DID) which have records in a specific collection' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://atproto.com/specs/sync
  - Backfilling guide: 'Backfilling the Bluesky Lexicons in particular is resource-intensive and time consuming.' 'Backfilling other Lexicons typically is not.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://atproto.com/guides/backfilling
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 3 to 2.5: I confirmed exactly three operators serve it (one company, two individuals) and five other relays return 404. no_aggregator 4 to 3: it is generic and already running, but it is an index, and 'many independent parties' is not met; the only stable instance is Bluesky PBC's. reverse_lookup 3 to 2.5: the four directories disagree (union 763, intersection 389, none a superset), and my timed fan-out left 8.7% of publishers with no answer, plus stale entries; it is keyed by collection, never by digest. trajectory and publisher_effort unchanged. keri_fit 3 to 2.5 for the same reason as the base layer.

#### Generic backlink index (Constellation, microcosm.blue)

An open-source service that reads the firehose, extracts every link-shaped string from every record of every lexicon (at:// URIs, DIDs and any string that parses as a URI) and answers 'which records link to this target', filtered by source collection and JSON path. The README calls it 'A global atproto backlink index'.

- **Publish.** Same declaration record, with one field holding the digest as a URI (for example a urn-style or custom-scheme string containing the SAID, or an https URL). A bare SAID string is not indexed, because the parser requires an at-uri, a DID or a URI with a scheme. Nothing is sent to Constellation; it sees the record on the firehose.
- **Fetch by digest.** Ask Constellation for records linking to the digest URI, take any returned (did, collection, rkey), fetch it from that DID's PDS and verify the Blake3 SAID over the bytes locally.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native to the index and keyed by an arbitrary URI. Step 1: GET https://constellation.microcosm.blue/links/all?target=&lt;digest URI&gt; returns, per source collection and JSON path, the count of records and distinct DIDs; with one collection per verb this single response is the issue/rely/implement/endorse breakdown. Step 2: GET /xrpc/blue.microcosm.links.getBacklinks?subject=&lt;digest URI&gt;&source=&lt;collection&gt;:&lt;path&gt; returns the list of {did, collection, rkey} (default 16, max 100 per page, cursor paging); /links/distinct-dids returns just the DIDs. Step 3: re-fetch each record from its PDS to verify. I ran this live for a third-party record type: subject https://atproto.com with source site.standard.publication:url returned 3 records. An unknown target returns an empty result with HTTP 200. Infrastructure: one public instance operated by one person (fig, @bad-example.com), community funded.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Automatic for any account whose PDS is reachable through the relay Constellation consumes; a first record containing the digest URI is enough.
- **Identity.** Constellation returns unsigned pointers. Trust comes from fetching the pointed-to record with its repo proof and from whatever KERI-signed payload the record carries. The index is agnostic to identity method.
- **Digest fit.** Any digest that can be written inside a URI works; the hash function is irrelevant to the index. Source: parse_any_link tries at-uri, then DID, then `Uri::parse(s).map(|u| u.normalize()...)` with no scheme restriction. Live evidence that non-http schemes are indexed: a mailto: target and a did:web target both return index entries. Normalization touches scheme and host, not the path, so a case-sensitive SAID in the path should survive; I could not confirm a urn or custom scheme end to end without writing a record.
- **Governance.** A personal open-source project (microcosm-rs), not a standard. The query API is microcosm's own lexicon (blue.microcosm.links.*), described by its author as unstable. No standards body, no second independent implementation that I found. Bluesky PBC has funded the same author's Hubble mirror with a $20,000 grant and committed to take that over if needed, which shows goodwill but is not a commitment about Constellation.
- **Publisher cost.** Nothing beyond publishing the record with a URI-shaped digest field.
- **TypeScript tooling.** No client library is needed: two GET endpoints returning JSON. Self-hosting is a Rust binary; the README claims the full firehose on a Raspberry Pi 4B with under 2 GB of disk per day.
- **Failure modes.** Single-operator public instance on home hardware with best-effort uptime; if it is down the by-digest lookup is gone until you fall back to the collection directory fan-out. Not guaranteed complete (no full backfill; an open issue reports short pages after deletions). Open to poisoning in the obvious way: any account can publish a record that links to H, so counts are sybil-inflatable and only meaningful after filtering by trusted identities. Queries reveal to the operator which digests you are interested in.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** no. Nothing is built or operated for MAD: the index already exists and covers all record types. But it is an aggregator in every other sense, and in practice one volunteer's instance. If it goes away the options are another party's instance or self-hosting one, at which point the maintainer is running an index after all.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Public instance statistics: 19,676,132,479 links indexed, between 4,344,810,198 targets and sources, from 29,467,318 identities, over 649 days (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://constellation.microcosm.blue/
  - 'microcosm has grown to serve hundreds of requests per second from dozens of apps' 'like Blacksky, npmx.dev, and PDS MOOver.' (as of 2026-03-20). Source: https://atproto.com/blog/introducing-hubble-a-public-mirror-for-the-whole-atmosphere
  - README: 'the public instance currently runs on a little raspberry pi in my house'; 'works! api is unstable and likely to change, and no known instances have a full network backfill yet.'; 'best-effort uptime, no commitment to not breaking the api for now' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/at-microcosm/microcosm-rs/tree/main/constellation
  - Year-end post: 'Indexed over 11 BILLION links!' and 'At least a half-dozen third-party self-hosted instances!'; funding is GitHub Sponsors, Ko-fi, a Bluesky grant and a monthly Blacksky contribution (as of 2026-01-02). Source: https://updates.microcosm.blue/3mbfnzmdl7s2k
  - Usage policy on the instance page: 'You're welcome to use this public instance!'; no numeric rate limit published; asks callers to identify themselves in the user-agent (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://constellation.microcosm.blue/
  - Link parser source (parse_any_link: at-uri, then DID, then any URI accepted by fluent_uri) (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/at-microcosm/microcosm-rs/main/links/src/lib.rs
- **Skeptic's re-score.** reverse_lookup stays 4, and on better evidence than the report had: 60 of 60 sampled third-party records were found, opaque non-http URIs are indexed and path case is preserved. The one new defect is that queries are exact-match and not normalized. no_aggregator 3 to 2: it is precisely a global aggregator with one verifiable public operator, a volunteer; that fails the maintainer's own test of infrastructure 'many independent parties already operate'. adoption_trajectory 3 to 2.5: growth is real (11 billion links in January, 19.7 billion now) but there is no second implementation, no standards track, the landing page still says backfill is 'coming soon', and continuity depends on donations. adoption_today and publisher_effort unchanged. keri_fit 3 to 2.5 as for the base layer.

#### Firehose, Jetstream and Tap (subscribe or replay, index it yourself)

The relay firehose (com.atproto.sync.subscribeRepos) is the signed event stream of all repository commits; Jetstream is Bluesky's JSON re-encoding with collection and DID filters, and since v2 (2026-08-13) it 'keeps a compressed archive of the whole network' that can be replayed over HTTP; Tap is Bluesky's tool that enumerates repos for a collection, backfills them and tails the firehose. This is how every atproto AppView, Lexicon Garden and Constellation itself are built.

- **Publish.** Same record; the PDS emits the commit to relays automatically.
- **Fetch by digest.** Your consumer stores records as they arrive (or backfills each repo with com.atproto.sync.getRepo) and serves bytes by SAID from its own store; verify the SAID locally. Raw firehose and getRepo data carry repo proofs; Jetstream does not (the docs say to use the raw firehose for 'the cryptographic proofs to verify repository state yourself').
- **Reverse lookup.** Only by crawling and indexing. Step 1: subscribe to Jetstream with collections=&lt;your NSID or a namespace wildcard&gt; (up to 100 collections per subscription), or run Tap with a signal collection. Step 2: fold creates, updates and deletes into your own table keyed by digest. Step 3: answer queries from that table. Variants: a long-running service (the draft spec's Lambda plus DynamoDB is exactly this), or an ephemeral client-side replay of the Jetstream v2 archive for one collection, which needs an API key on Bluesky-hosted instances. The live socket only looks back 36 hours on Bluesky's instances and relays are non-archival, so without the archive or a backfill you miss history.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Automatic once the publisher's PDS is crawled by the relay you consume.
- **Identity.** Raw firehose commits are signed by the account's repo key and verifiable against the DID document; Jetstream output is unsigned and trusted on the operator's word. KERI payloads ride inside records unchanged.
- **Digest fit.** Irrelevant to the transport; filters are by collection and DID only, never by record content, so the digest is matched in your own code.
- **Governance.** Firehose sync is the part of atproto headed for IETF standards track. Jetstream and Tap are Bluesky PBC open-source products, self-hostable; the hosted archive is gated by Bluesky-issued API keys with unpublished quotas.
- **Publisher cost.** Nothing beyond publishing the record. The indexing cost falls on whoever runs the consumer; for a small custom collection that is tiny, but not zero and not idle-free (a WebSocket consumer does not fit Lambda well).
- **TypeScript tooling.** @atproto/sync (firehose with verification), @atproto/tap (client for Tap), @skyware/jetstream, and the new Jetstream SDK announced with Protocol Services.
- **Failure modes.** You become the single point of failure for your own lookup. Gaps while your consumer is down must be repaired by replay or backfill. Jetstream is unauthenticated data from one operator. The hosted archive depends on Bluesky's API-key policy. Same spam exposure as every other layer.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. The infrastructure delivers events, not answers. Someone has to run the consumer and keep the index, which is the purpose-built aggregator the reviewers objected to, even if atproto makes it far smaller than a web crawler with a seed list.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Jetstream v2 launched with 'Bluesky Protocol Services' on 2026-08-13; archive access requires 'an API token just for these requests'; 'The live tail remains open and unauthenticated.' (as of 2026-08-13). Source: https://atproto.com/blog/introducing-bluesky-protocol-services
  - Replay docs: Jetstream 'keeps a compressed archive of the whole network'; 'The planner guarantees no false negatives'; HTTP replay endpoints require an API key on Bluesky-hosted instances; live lookback is 36 hours; usage is 'metered in bytes, not requests' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://bsky.network/docs/jetstream-replay
  - 'All relays in the network now run this way, and are called non-archival:' 'lightweight and cheap to run, but only retaining recent events, not the full history of the network.' (as of 2026-03-20). Source: https://atproto.com/blog/introducing-hubble-a-public-mirror-for-the-whole-atmosphere
  - Public relays answering /xrpc/_health on 2026-11-08 (my probe): bsky.network and relay1.us-east.bsky.network (Bluesky PBC), relay.fire.hose.cam and relay3.fr.hose.cam (microcosm), atproto.africa (Blacksky, rsky-relay 0.3.2), relay.upcloud.world, relay.waow.tech, zlay.waow.tech, relay.feeds.blue (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://atproto.africa/xrpc/_health
  - IETF ATP charter deliverable: 'Standards track document(s) describing a low-latency synchronization protocol' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://datatracker.ietf.org/group/atp/about/
  - npm downloads, week of 2026-11-01: @atproto/tap 5,194; @atproto/sync 7,415; @skyware/jetstream 1,144 (as of 2026-11-07). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/@atproto/tap
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 3.5 to 3: it is the integration surface everything in atproto uses, but still one ecosystem. adoption_trajectory stays 3.5: sync is the one piece named as a standards-track deliverable in the confirmed charter, and Jetstream v2 shipped in August 2026. no_aggregator 1 to 2: the confirmed replay design (stateless, filter by collection, no false negatives) allows an exhaustive client-side scan with no running service, so 'you must run a consumer' is no longer the only option; it stays low because the archive has one operator, needs a Bluesky-issued key, and quota and price are unpublished. reverse_lookup and publisher_effort unchanged. keri_fit 3 to 2.5.

#### Lexicon publication and resolution (NSID to DNS TXT to DID to com.atproto.lexicon.schema record)

atproto's production system for publishing protocol schemas without a registry. A schema named by an NSID such as app.bsky.feed.post is a record of type com.atproto.lexicon.schema in the repository of whichever DID the DNS TXT record _lexicon.&lt;authority&gt; names; the record key is the NSID. It is the closest live precedent for what MAD wants, and the natural way to publish the schema of MAD's own declaration record.

- **Publish.** Control a domain, add TXT _lexicon.&lt;reversed authority&gt; with value did=&lt;your DID&gt;, and write one com.atproto.lexicon.schema record per NSID into that DID's repo (goat lex publish does both checks and the write). This publishes a schema under a name; it has no notion of 'I rely on' or 'I endorse' a schema, and no place for a digest as the name.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not by hash. By name: DNS TXT lookup, resolve the DID, getRecord with rkey = NSID. The response carries the record's sha-256 CID, and @atproto/lex pins it: the manifest is described as 'tracking installed Lexicons and their versions (CIDs)' and `lex install --ci` will 'Error if the installed lexicons do not match the CIDs in the lexicons.json manifest.' That is the same pin-by-digest workflow MAD wants, but with atproto's digest, not a SAID. I verified the chain live: dig TXT _lexicon.feed.bsky.app returned did=did:plc:4v4y5r3lwsbtmsxhile2ljac and getRecord on that DID's PDS returned the schema with cid bafyreia7rgxu7467phwpcgzaw62l5bzmxufkpikrc5zm4q3xhjmuhq4pti.
- **Reverse lookup.** None in the mechanism. Resolution goes name to schema only; the spec adds that 'resolvers should not recurse up or down the DNS hierarchy looking for TXT records.' 'Who uses this lexicon' is answered elsewhere: by listReposByCollection (who holds records of that collection) or by firehose consumers. Enumeration ('what lexicons exist') is likewise only available from sites that consume the firehose.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new lexicon is resolvable by anyone who knows its NSID as soon as DNS and the record exist. Nobody learns that it exists unless they see records of that type on the firehose or the author tells them.
- **Identity.** Authority is DNS control of the NSID's domain, delegated to a DID; the record is authenticated by that DID's repo signature. This is domain-rooted identity, the thing the maintainer wants to move away from, and a KERI AID has no role except as the controller behind the DID.
- **Digest fit.** None. The name must be an NSID (authority segments are letters, digits and hyphens, case-insensitive; the name segment is letters and digits only), and the version identifier is the record's sha-256 dag-cbor CID. A SAID cannot be the key of a com.atproto.lexicon.schema record because that collection's key type is nsid.
- **Governance.** Defined in the Lexicon specification owned by Bluesky PBC; the meta-schema 'is defined and governed as part of the protocol'. Explicitly outside the IETF working group's first charter.
- **Publisher cost.** A domain you control, one DNS TXT record per authority, a free account, and one record per schema.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @atproto/lex (lex install, lex build, CID-pinned lexicons.json), @atproto/lexicon-resolver, atcute's lexicon-resolver; the Go tool goat for publishing.
- **Failure modes.** Loss of the domain transfers authority over the namespace. Most lexicons in use are not published this way, so resolution fails for them. Schemas are mutable records; only the consumer's pinned CID detects a change. No reverse direction at all.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. For reverse lookup and enumeration, yes, and the ecosystem's own history shows it: directory sites appeared. Lexicon Garden (January 2026) says 'Until now, there hasn't been a central place to browse, search, and learn about these schemas. That's why I built Lexicon Garden.' lexicon.store calls itself 'The AT Protocol schema registry'. UFOs lists every collection seen. What changed relative to openhaven is how they are built: all three are generated automatically from the firehose with no submission and no editor (lexicon.store: 'automatically discovered and indexed via the AT Protocol Firehose'), so they are interchangeable, disposable views rather than curated authorities. That is the realistic ceiling for the maintainer's objective: the protocol can remove hand curation and make any index replaceable, but it did not remove indexes.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - 753 repositories hold at least one com.atproto.lexicon.schema record (my count via the Bluesky relay), against 3,470 collection NSIDs active in the last 7 days per UFOs (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://relay1.us-west.bsky.network/xrpc/com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection?collection=com.atproto.lexicon.schema&limit=2000
  - Spec: 'Lexicon schemas are published publicly as records in atproto repositories, using the `com.atproto.lexicon.schema` type.' 'The record key is the NSID of the schema.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://atproto.com/specs/lexicon
  - Publishing guide describes publication as 'strongly advised' so that 'a single canonical & authoritative representation' is available; it is not required (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://atproto.com/guides/publishing-lexicons
  - IETF charter excludes it: 'this initial charter does not include non-public data, application-specific data schemas or APIs, lexicon publication, the labeling moderation system, etc.' (as of 2026-04-02). Source: https://atproto.com/blog/kicking-off-the-atp-working-group
  - @atproto/lex 55,653 weekly npm downloads; @atproto/lexicon-resolver 640 (as of 2026-11-07). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/@atproto/lex
  - Lexicon Garden aggregates 'from across the ATProtocol network by consuming the relay firehose directly'; its browse list shows 'Page 1 of 168'; it exposes an MCP endpoint for AI agents: 'The endpoint is read-only and requires no authentication' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://lexicon.garden/help/mcp
  - Lexicon Garden launch post: 'Until now, there hasn't been a central place to browse, search, and learn about these schemas. That's why I built Lexicon Garden.' (as of 2026-01-05). Source: https://ngerakines.leaflet.pub/3mbogpi62k22d
  - lexicon.store: lexicons are 'automatically discovered and indexed via the AT Protocol Firehose.' (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://lexicon-store.pages.dev/docs
- **Skeptic's re-score.** no_aggregator 1 to 2: the report's 1 blends two things. Name-to-schema resolution needs no aggregator at all (I ran it live through DNS and one PDS); enumeration, reverse lookup and readable reference needed one, and at least three directory sites were built. The score reflects the job, but should not read as 'this mechanism requires an index to function'. keri_fit 2 to 1.5: authority is DNS control of the NSID's domain, the key type is nsid and the version identifier is a sha-256 CID, so neither the AID nor the SAID has any place in it. Other scores unchanged; the 753-versus-3,470 framing understates adoption among serious lexicon authors but I could not quantify coverage well enough to raise the score.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (atproto)

The skeptic re-checked 30 claims; 17 held as stated. The rest:

- *partly* · Digest-keyed records in a PDS repository (per-publisher lookup and local-first crawl) · claim: com.atproto.sync.getRecord returns a signed proof of existence or non-existence
  - Correction: The bsky.social entryway returned 401 AuthMissing for the same call. A client must first resolve the DID to its actual PDS host; the handle's entryway hostname does not serve the proof endpoint anonymously.
  - Checked: Called sync.getRecord for a non-existent SAID-keyed record. The real PDS host returned HTTP 200, application/vnd.ipld.car, 4,577 bytes (a CAR with a commit block). I did not verify the MST proof or the commit signature.
- *partly* · Digest-keyed records in a PDS repository (per-publisher lookup and local-first crawl) · claim: Any account can publish a custom record type today at no cost, with no registration of the type
  - Correction: No write was performed by me either, so this rests on indirect evidence. It is strong indirect evidence.
  - Checked: createRecord.json read raw: validate text is verbatim as quoted. describeRepo on did:plc:ewvi7nxzyoun6zhxrhs64oiz (atproto.com, hosted on a Bluesky PDS) lists 29 collections, about 20 of them third-party. UFOs lists 39,917 collection NSIDs. Rate-limit doc read raw: 'The limit is 5,000 points per hour and 35,000 points per day', CREATE 3 points; read limit 3000 per 5 minutes per IP.
- *partly* · Digest-keyed records in a PDS repository (per-publisher lookup and local-first crawl) · claim: 39,915 collection NSIDs observed since 2025-05-30; 3,470 seen in the last 7 days across 574 two-segment authorities
  - Correction: As a measure of third-party lexicons in use the figure overstates by an order of magnitude. In the last 7 days only 270 third-party collections had 10 or more distinct writers, 51 had 100 or more, and 6 had 1,000 or more; only 96 authorities had any collection with 10 or more writers.
  - Checked: Paged https://ufos-api.microcosm.blue/collections myself: 39,917 all-time; 3,487 in the last 7 days across 573 authorities. Counts match.
- *partly* · Digest-keyed records in a PDS repository (per-publisher lookup and local-first crawl) · claim: Bluesky PBC still operates plc.directory; the Swiss-association handover is unconfirmed
  - Correction: The steward organisation exists, which the report could not confirm. None of the three pages says who operates the directory servers today or names a board, so whether operation has actually moved remains unverifiable.
  - Checked: did-method-plc README and web.plc.directory both now say 'In 2026 the PLC Organization was founded to be a neutral steward for the DID PLC system'. https://plcred.org describes a Swiss association (c/o address in Zug) 'established in early 2026'.
- *partly* · Relay collection directory (com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection) plus per-repo fan-out · claim: Fan-out is one request per publisher and cheap at hundreds to low thousands of publishers
