  - Correction: It is two requests per publisher (DID document, then getRecord). 137 of 150 gave a definite answer; 13 (8.7%) gave none (7 PDS unreachable, 5 HTTP errors, 1 DID resolution failure). In a separate sample of 60 DIDs for another collection, 2 had no records at all (stale directory entries) and 5 PDSes errored. Expect 'unknown' for roughly one publisher in eleven.
  - Checked: Timed it with /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-seriouscoderone-code-bpm/0df03f74-64d8-4f0b-9040-da278e74b62c/scratchpad/fanout.py: 150 DIDs from Bluesky's list, 8 workers, 25.7 seconds, 93 distinct PDS hosts.
- *partly* · Relay collection directory (com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection) plus per-repo fan-out · claim: site.standard.publication is held by 14,758 DIDs
  - Correction: In a random sample of 60 of those DIDs, 25 were hosted on atproto.brid.gy, the Bridgy Fed bridge. The number is not 14,758 independent adopters; something like 40% are bridged accounts.
  - Checked: Paged the Bluesky relay: 14,758 DIDs returned.
- *partly* · Generic backlink index (Constellation, microcosm.blue) · claim: Any digest written inside a URI is indexed; normalization touches scheme and host, not the path; urn or custom scheme not confirmed end to end
  - Correction: New finding: the query side is not normalized. Target HTTPS://WWW.YOUTUBE.COM/watch?v=dQw4w9WgXcQ returned 0 while the canonical spelling returned 851. Lookup is an exact string match against the stored normalized form, so every publisher and every querier must emit byte-identical URI text, and a digest placed in the authority position (scheme://DIGEST) would be lower-cased and destroyed. I found no mixed-case urn: target in the index; a SAID-bearing URI is still untested end to end.
  - Checked: Read links/src/lib.rs and record.rs raw: every JSON string is tried as at-uri, then DID, then fluent_uri Uri::parse(...).normalize(). Live: opaque non-http targets are indexed (about:blank 227 records, javascript:void(0) 23, mailto:, and 'localhost:3000' parsed as a scheme, 20). Path and query case is preserved: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dQw4w9WgXcQ has 851 records, the lower-cased ID has 0.
- *partly* · Generic backlink index (Constellation, microcosm.blue) · claim: Serves 'hundreds of requests per second from dozens of apps'; 'At least a half-dozen third-party self-hosted instances'; one maintainer, community funded
  - Correction: I found no second publicly usable Constellation instance. For the purpose of 'many independent parties already operate it', the count of public operators I can verify is one.
  - Checked: atproto.com blog of 2026-03-20 (guest post by fig, 'creator and maintainer of microcosm infrastructure'): the first quote and the $20,000 Hubble grant are confirmed. updates.microcosm.blue post of 2026-01-02: 'Indexed over 11 BILLION links!' and the half-dozen instances sentence are confirmed as the operator's own statements.
- *partly* · Firehose, Jetstream and Tap (subscribe or replay, index it yourself) · claim: All relays are now non-archival; nine public relays answered a health probe
  - Correction: I did not verify that each is a full-network relay, nor who operates waow.tech, upcloud.world or feeds.blue. Hubble itself serves getRepo, listRepos and getRepoStatus only; it does not add a query surface.
  - Checked: Hubble blog post: 'All relays in the network now run this way, and are called non-archival' confirmed. All eleven relay hostnames I queried responded (six with data, five with 404 for the directory endpoint).
- *partly* · Lexicon publication and resolution (NSID to DNS TXT to DID to com.atproto.lexicon.schema record) · claim: Only 753 repos publish schemas against about 3,470 collection NSIDs active in the last week
  - Correction: The comparison mixes units (repositories versus NSIDs). In comparable units, 573 two-segment authorities were active in the week and only 96 of them had a collection with 10 or more writers; 63 DIDs wrote schema records that week. Lexicon publication is less marginal among serious lexicon authors than the sentence implies. I could not measure coverage exactly.
  - Checked: Both numbers reproduce (753; 3,487).
- *partly* · Lexicon publication and resolution (NSID to DNS TXT to DID to com.atproto.lexicon.schema record) · claim: Directory sites appeared anyway (Lexicon Garden, lexicon.store, UFOs), generated from the firehose with no submission and no editor
  - Correction: The firehose sentence for Lexicon Garden is on /help, not on /help/mcp as cited. lexicon.store also accepts manual indexing requests for an NSID, domain or DID, gated by DNS authority verification, so it is not purely submission-free. Each site has a single named operator (Nick Gerakines; vayumandala.com).
  - Checked: Lexicon Garden launch post (2026-01-05): 'Until now, there hasn't been a central place to browse, search, and learn about these schemas' confirmed. https://lexicon.garden/mcp is described as 'read-only and requires no authentication' with seven tools. Front page shows 'Page 1 of 168'. lexicon.store docs: 'automatically discovered and indexed via the AT Protocol Firehose' confirmed.
- *unverifiable* · Digest-keyed records in a PDS repository (per-publisher lookup and local-first crawl) · claim: The definition record doubles as the human-readable reference: 'generic record browsers render it, no site needed'
  - Correction: Any such browser is itself a third-party site, and none would present a MAD definition as documentation without a view written for that record type. Treat the human-readable-reference requirement as not met by this family; atproto's own answer to it was Lexicon Garden.
  - Checked: Nothing in the report cites a source for this and I opened no record browser.
- *partly* · Generic backlink index (Constellation, microcosm.blue) · claim: The only agent-facing or machine-readable reverse surface is Constellation plus Lexicon Garden's MCP endpoint; no agent-discovery standard exists on atproto
  - Correction: There is one more reverse index, operated by Bluesky PBC: https://api.bsky.app/xrpc/app.bsky.feed.searchPosts answered unauthenticated queries filtered by url=, tag= and domain=. The url filter is exact and case-sensitive (1,546 hits for the mixed-case YouTube URL, 1 for the lower-cased one). It covers posts only, and the same call on public.api.bsky.app returns 403.
  - Checked: Searched for agent-discovery work on atproto: only community proposals and small registries (for example an AI-disclosure lexicon proposal and a site.filae.agent.card crawler). No adopted standard.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (atproto)

- **Bluesky search AppView as a by-URL and by-tag reverse index (app.bsky.feed.searchPosts with url, tag and domain filters).** It is a second reverse index keyed by an exact URL, run by Bluesky PBC at full network scale. I queried it unauthenticated: the url filter is exact and case-sensitive (1,546 hits for a mixed-case URL, 1 for the lower-cased form), so a digest in a URL path would survive. If each declaration is also expressed as a post with a link to the digest URL, the by-digest lookup stops depending on one volunteer. It does not change the conclusion: it covers posts only, has one corporate operator, caps hitsTotal at 10,000, and the same call on public.api.bsky.app already returns 403, so anonymous access is not assured. Source: https://api.bsky.app/xrpc/app.bsky.feed.searchPosts (live queries, 2026-11-08)
- **Jetstream v2 archive replay used as an on-demand, client-side scan of one collection.** The report describes it but leaves it out of the best pick and scores the firehose mechanism as 'someone has to run the consumer'. The replay docs describe a stateless plan-and-download flow filtered by collection whose planner 'guarantees no false negatives'. For a small declaration collection that is an exhaustive reverse lookup with no service to run, and it is the only path with a stated completeness guarantee. It belongs in the design as the completeness backstop. Limits: one operator, a Bluesky-issued API key, unpublished quota, price and archive depth. Source: https://bsky.network/docs/jetstream-replay
- **Web-origin binding as deployed by standard.site (/.well-known/site.standard.publication returning an AT-URI, and a link rel tag for documents).** It is a deployed pattern for tying a record to a web domain with a static file, implemented by Leaflet, pckt and Offprint; 14,758 repositories hold the publication record (roughly 40% of my sample were bridged accounts). It shows how a first cut could be a static .well-known file under a domain, with an atproto record as an optional second carrier, which keeps atproto replaceable instead of foundational. It does not provide a reverse lookup by itself. Source: https://standard.site/
- **Spacedust (microcosm): a real-time stream of every link extracted from the firehose.** It lets a client watch for new declarations that reference H without operating an index. It is a watch, not a query: the readme lists it as v0 and 'missing cursor replay', so it cannot answer who already declared H. Same single operator as Constellation. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/at-microcosm/microcosm-rs/main/readme.md
- **Labels (com.atproto.label.defs#label).** A label is a statement by a DID about a URI ('AT URI of the record, repository (account), or other resource'), with its own signature field and a short value: structurally the closest thing atproto has to a signed endorsement by a key that is not the host-held repo key. It does not help the reverse lookup, because queryLabels is served per labeler and each endorser would have to run a labeler service. Worth knowing so it is not mistaken for a solution. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bluesky-social/atproto/main/lexicons/com/atproto/label/defs.json

### What the researcher could not verify (atproto)

- No write was performed (read-only constraint), so 'any account can publish a custom record type at no cost' rests on the createRecord lexicon text, the published Bluesky PDS rate limits, and the 39,915 collection NSIDs observed by UFOs, not on a test record.
- Constellation indexing of a urn-style or custom-scheme digest URI was not confirmed end to end. Evidence is the parser source (no scheme restriction) plus live index entries for mailto: and did:web targets and a live https-target query; a real record with a SAID-bearing URI was not published.
- WebFetch summarises pages through a small model. Items marked verbatim came back as quoted text, but only these were read raw by me: the listReposByCollection, createRecord, sync.getRecord and lexicon.schema JSON files, Constellation's links/src/lib.rs, the DNS TXT answer, all live API responses, npm download counts, and the Lexicon Garden MCP help page text.
- Current status of the PLC directory handover to the Swiss association (announced 2025-09-19). An April 2026 podcast says a board was being set up; I found nothing confirming that operation has transferred as of November 2026.
- TechCrunch/Similarweb usage figures and the $100M Series B (disclosed 2026-03-19) come from search-result summaries; I did not open the articles.
- Lexicon Garden's total schema count: the page shows 'Page 1 of 168' but I could not determine the page size. A third-party claim of '900+ schemas' is unverified.
- compare.hose.cam (the community list of public relays) is client-rendered and returned no content. My relay list is from probing /xrpc/_health on hostnames I found; I did not verify that each is a full-network relay.
- Which collection directory is closest to the truth. Bluesky's returned the most DIDs, but lightrail's README says it removes repos whose last record in a collection was deleted while Bluesky's does not, so the larger number may include stale entries.
- Jetstream v2 archive depth, API-key issuance terms, quotas and pricing are not stated in the docs I read.
- How many public Constellation instances exist besides microcosm's. The January 2026 post claims at least six third-party self-hosted instances but names none that are open to the public.
- PDS read rate limits that would bound the fan-out fallback were not checked.
- The UFOs NSID counts include junk and test NSIDs (the first sample returned was 'C.d.e.f'), so they overstate the number of real third-party lexicons.
- Agent-related atproto work: 'The State of Agents on ATProto: January 2026' (longform.social) did not resolve, and the '#aiproto working group' appears only in a search summary. I found no primary source showing an adopted agent-discovery standard on atproto.
- attested.network (third-party attestation records) is labelled 'Draft — Not yet published'; I did not read the underlying badge.blue specification, so I did not rely on it.
- Serverless PDS options (Cirrus on Cloudflare Workers, described as experimental and did:web only) are from search summaries, not from the project's documentation.
- The multicodec code for a Blake3 multihash (0x1e) is from memory; I did not fetch the multicodec table. It does not affect the recommendation, since atproto's blessed CID set excludes it either way.

<a id="nostr"></a>

## Nostr

**Researcher's summary.** Nostr answers the crux natively: NIP-01 says all single-letter tags "are expected to be indexed by relays", so any relay answers a filter such as {"kinds":[K],"#d":["&lt;digest&gt;"]} with every stored event carrying that tag, from any author, with no crawler or separate index. I confirmed this on 2026-11-08 with read-only queries against stock public relays (mostly strfry): a hash query on `#x` and arbitrary-string queries on `#I` and `#d` were all served directly. The same tests show the limit: answers are per relay and partial. One exact-tag filter returned 157, 153, 91, 89, 41, 36 and 0 events on seven relays, and for the wiki key d="bitcoin" 8 of 12 authors were visible on only one relay (the topic relay held 10; relay.primal.net and nos.lol held none). So nothing purpose-built is required, but completeness depends on publishers and readers converging on relays; Nostr's own answers are NIP-65 inbox delivery, relay hints inside naddr/nprofile strings, and issuer-named relay lists as in NIP-34, all of which work from one introduction. Digest fit is good for the identifier and poor for byte storage: a 44-character CESR SAID fits unchanged in a `d` tag, while NIP-94 `x` tags and Blossom are SHA-256 hex only, so bytes travel inline in event content or are addressed a second time by sha256. Adoption is real but narrow: NIP-66 monitors reported about 1,300 to 1,480 live relays on 2026-11-08, the last sourced activity figure is 144,202 daily active pubkeys (15-31 Dec 2025), and nostr-tools had 599,453 npm downloads in the week of 2026-11-01. Outside the Bitcoin-adjacent core the notable 2026 adopter is Block's Buzz (announced 2026-07-21; 36,814 GitHub stars), which uses Nostr keys and events on self-hosted relays; agent work (ContextVM, Clawstr, 17 agent-titled NIP pull requests) is active, but none of those NIPs appears in the merged list and NIP-90 is now marked unrecommended. There is no standards body (NIP-01 itself is labelled `draft`), and I found no mainstream agent runtime that reads Nostr by default. Identity is a bare secp256k1 BIP-340 key with no rotation; CESR defines only ECDSA codes for secp256k1, so a Nostr key cannot be a KERI signing key today, but an event can carry a CESR-signed endorsement so that readers count AIDs instead of Nostr keys. Nothing in the protocol stops spam claims for a popular digest; the defences are author filters and web of trust on the reader side, relay write policies, and later KERI signature checks. Net: Nostr meets priorities 1, 3 and 5 better than a crawler plus index, meets priority 2 poorly, and fits priority 4 only as a transport for KERI-signed statements.

**Researcher's best pick.** Tag-indexed signed events on relays: one addressable declaration event per party keyed d=&lt;SAID&gt;, with rendezvous through the issuer's NIP-65 read relays and issuer-named relays. It is the strongest mechanism in this family and it does answer "who else declares H" without a purpose-built index. It does not meet the "very widely adopted" bar, so I would treat Nostr as one binding of a transport-neutral signed declaration, not as the sole substrate.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** Adoption and completeness, together. The parties who would have to publish (businesses and tool vendors authoring authorization protocols) and the agent tooling that should read are not on Nostr: the network is roughly 1,300-1,480 relays and about 144,000 daily active keys concentrated in one community, there is no standards body, NIP-01 is still labelled draft, and none of the 2026 agent NIPs has merged. mad-discovery would therefore have to bring its own publishers, and once it does, the "shared infrastructure" is a few donated relays with no retention guarantee (the 2023 measurement found 20% of relays down more than 40% of the time and 95% of free relays unable to cover costs). My live tests show that the same query returns anything from 0 to 157 events depending on the relay, and that a dedicated topic relay is what actually gives coverage, which is an aggregator by another name if only one exists. A reader can never tell silence from absence, free keys make spam declarations for a popular digest unbounded without a trust graph, and Nostr's key model (one secp256k1 key, no rotation) is the model KERI rejects, so Nostr contributes transport only. If the declaration is a KERI-signed statement anyway, it could ride on a substrate that the target parties already operate.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** The report is factually careful: of roughly 45 claims I re-opened, almost all matched the primary source exactly, including every live relay count, the npm and GitHub figures, the NIP, CESR and strfry quotations, and the arXiv numbers. The one material error is durability. The researcher took strfry's three-year default as the upkeep horizon and did not measure retention; three of six large free relays in fact keep events for about five to six weeks, which undoes "the relays host it" and "nothing is required to run". The framing of Block's Buzz as adoption outside the core community is also too generous. With those corrected, I score the best pick lower on no_aggregator, publisher_effort and keri_fit, and agree with the researcher's own closing judgement: Nostr should not be the substrate. At most it is one optional binding or mirror for a transport-neutral signed declaration, and only if the maintainer accepts running or naming a retention relay. Trust this family for the native query shape and as a design precedent (addressable events keyed by a shared tag, relay hints inside an introduction), not for adoption, durability or KERI alignment.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

Partly. The query is real and nobody has to write an indexer, but a dependable answer needs someone to run a relay.

Walking "given H, list everyone who declares H" as a stranger:

1. Holding only H: Nostr has no routing from a tag value to a relay. The reader needs an introduction that carries relay hints, or falls back to a hard-coded list of large relays (a seed list again, of relays instead of publishers), or to an aggregating index. The main such index, relay.nostr.band, is one operator and was unreachable for both the researcher and me; relay.damus.io refused WebSocket upgrades with HTTP 503.

2. From an introduction to the issuer's announcement and relay list: answered by the hinted relay, or by relay-list indexers such as purplepag.es (one operator; it rejects filters without a kind). relay.primal.net keeps few relay lists older than about 40 days.

3. REQ {"kinds":[B],"#d":[H]} on each candidate relay: answered from that relay's own tag index. I reproduced the researcher's counts exactly (157, 153, 91, 89, 41, 36, 0). These relays accept arbitrary kinds in practice (79 to 121 distinct addressable kinds per relay in a 7-day sample, many unassigned) and arbitrary tag strings up to 1024 bytes on strfry defaults. Reading and writing are free on relay.primal.net, nos.lol, relay.ditto.pub, nostr.mom, relay.nostr.net and offchain.pub. Storage is not durable: relay.primal.net, nos.lol and offchain.pub hold almost nothing older than 42.0, about 40.7 and 36.0 days respectively, including addressable and replaceable kinds. The relays that do retain are single operators with no commitment.

4. Snowball through each new author's relay list: this only reaches declarers who share a relay with someone already found. A party that published to its own relays is invisible, and silence never means absence. In the one live multi-author precedent, 10 of 11 authors were on the dedicated topic relay and most of about 25 general relays had none.

5. Filtering spam: nothing in the protocol; the reader needs its own list of trusted keys, which does not exist for MAD's parties.

What would have to exist or be run: an agreed kind number and tag convention; a TypeScript client; a relay that every party writes to and that commits to keeping these events (stock strfry, mirrorable with NIP-77, but operated for this purpose, which is an index by function); or, instead of that relay, a re-broadcast job per publisher about every four weeks plus acceptance of patchy coverage; and a reader-side trust list. So the honest answer is that somebody must run one relay, though not write one.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

The free shared infrastructure that the "no aggregator" claim rests on does not keep the data, and the people who would publish and read are not there.

Measured on 2026-11-08: three of six large free relays (relay.primal.net, nos.lol, offchain.pub) hold almost nothing older than 36 to 42 days, for every kind I tried, including addressable declarations, profiles and relay lists. Declarations are long-lived, low-traffic records, exactly what such pruning removes. Combined with the wiki test, where the dedicated topic relay was the only one with real coverage, the design reduces to "run a relay everyone writes to". That is the draft's DynamoDB index again, with the crawler replaced by publishers pushing to it, and it still gives no proof of completeness and no spam bound.

In return for that, every publisher must adopt a second identity that cannot rotate, in a network with no standards body, whose agent proposals are all unmerged, and which agent tooling does not read (MCP SDK downloads are 147 times nostr-tools; the one large 2026 adopter, Block, is the community's own sponsor and uses private relays). If the declaration is going to be a KERI-signed statement anyway, Nostr contributes only a tag-indexed mailbox, and the same mailbox could be any store the target parties already use.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (nostr)

What is written, and where
1. Definition announcement (issuer): an addressable event of a new kind A (number to be chosen and proposed as a NIP pull request or registry-of-kinds entry). Tags: ["d","&lt;SAID&gt;"], ["title",...], ["summary",...], ["relays","wss://...",...] naming where declarations about this definition should be sent (NIP-34 pattern), optional ["x","&lt;sha256 hex of the exact bytes&gt;"] for Blossom. Content: the definition bytes, if they fit in about 60 KB; otherwise empty, with the bytes on Blossom servers listed in the issuer's kind 10063 event.
2. Declaration (any party): an addressable event of kind B. Tags: ["d","&lt;SAID&gt;"], ["L","mad.role"], ["l","issue|rely|implement|endorse","mad.role"] (several l tags allowed), ["p",&lt;issuer key&gt;,&lt;relay hint&gt;], ["a","A:&lt;issuer key&gt;:&lt;SAID&gt;",&lt;relay hint&gt;], optional ["expiration",&lt;unix time&gt;]. Sent to the party's own write relays, the issuer's read relays, and the relays named in the announcement. One event per party per digest; re-signing replaces it.
3. Human reference: a kind 30023 Markdown article with d=&lt;SAID&gt;, rendered by existing gateways and long-form clients.
4. Identity, first cut: kind 0 profile with nip05 "_@domain", a static /.well-known/nostr.json on the party's domain, and a kind 10002 relay list.
5. Identity, later and additive: the declaration's content becomes a CESR stream, a statement naming AID, SAID, role, date and the Nostr key, with attached KERI signatures. Readers take all meaning from the signed payload, check the outer tags match it, verify against the KEL obtained by KERI means, and count AIDs. Events without a valid payload simply rank lower; nothing already published breaks.

Queries
- Fetch H: REQ {"kinds":[A],"#d":["&lt;SAID&gt;"]} to the hint relays; accept any copy whose content recomputes to the SAID.
- Who else declares H: REQ {"kinds":[B],"#d":["&lt;SAID&gt;"]} sent to the relays in every announcement's relays tag, the issuer's read relays and the reader's own relays; then, for each new author, fetch kind 10002 and repeat on their write relays until no new authors appear. Narrow with "#l":["endorse"] or "authors":[trusted keys]; use NIP-45 COUNT for sizes; page with until, since strfry caps a filter at 500 events.
- Enumerate definitions: REQ {"kinds":[A]} paged by until on the known relays, or NIP-77 negentropy sync where advertised.

What the maintainer runs
Nothing is required: a TypeScript library and CLI built on nostr-tools that builds and signs the events, publishes them and runs the snowball query (Node 24's built-in WebSocket sufficed for all my tests), plus a static nostr.json on a domain. Optional: one relay (strfry on a small host, or a managed relay) used as the default entry in the relays tag, to stop depending on the retention policy of donated relays. That relay is generic software, mirrorable by anyone, and each issuer can name a different one.

### Mechanisms (nostr)

#### Tag-indexed signed events on relays (NIP-01 filters) with an addressable declaration event keyed d=&lt;digest&gt;

Nostr's only object is a JSON event signed with a secp256k1 BIP-340 key and sent over WebSocket to relays, which store it and answer filters. NIP-01: "all single-letter (only english alphabet letters: a-z, A-Z) key tags are expected to be indexed by relays" and "Only the first value in any given tag is indexed." Kinds 30000-39999 are addressable: relays keep the latest event per (kind, pubkey, d tag). NIP-54 (wiki) and NIP-85 (trusted assertions) already use the d tag as a shared subject key that many authors write under.

- **Publish.** A party signs one addressable event of an agreed kind (no kind exists for this; a number would have to be chosen and proposed) with tags ["d","&lt;44-char SAID&gt;"], the role as ["l","issue|rely|implement|endorse","&lt;namespace&gt;"] (NIP-32 self-label form), ["p",&lt;issuer key&gt;,&lt;relay hint&gt;] and optionally ["expiration",...]. It sends the event to its own write relays (NIP-65), to the read relays of the issuer it p-tags, and to any relays the issuer's announcement names. The relays host it. Cost is zero on open relays (the NIP-11 documents of relay.damus.io, nos.lol, relay.primal.net and relay.ditto.pub, fetched 2026-11-08, show no payment requirement); paid relays such as nostr.wine advertise payment_required and an admission fee.
- **Fetch by digest.** The issuer puts the definition bytes in the content of its own announcement event (strfry's default maxEventSize is 65,536 bytes; relay.damus.io advertises max_message_length 1,000,000). A reader sends REQ {"kinds":[K],"#d":["&lt;SAID&gt;"]}, takes any returned copy, recomputes the Blake3-256 SAID over the content and discards mismatches. The Nostr event id (sha256) and signature play no part in integrity. Documents too large for an event go to Blossom (next mechanism).
- **Reverse lookup.** Native query, partial coverage. Steps: (1) the reader starts from one introduction, an naddr/nprofile string or an event, which carries relay hints (NIP-19 TLV type 1); (2) it fetches the issuer's announcement and kind 10002 relay list and builds a candidate set: relays named in the announcement, the issuer's read relays, the introducer's write relays, its own relays; (3) it sends ["REQ",id,{"kinds":[K],"#d":["&lt;SAID&gt;"]}] to each, and each relay answers from its own tag index, optionally narrowed with "#l":["endorse"] or "authors":[trusted keys]; NIP-45 COUNT gives sizes (advertised by damus, nos.lol, primal, ditto); (4) for every new author it fetches that author's kind 10002 list and repeats on their write relays until no new authors appear; (5) it deduplicates by pubkey. The infrastructure is whichever public relays the publishers chose. The answer is the union of the relays asked, never provably complete. Live evidence 2026-11-08: one exact-tag filter returned 157, 153, 91, 89, 41, 36 and 0 events on seven relays; for wiki d="bitcoin", 12 authors in the union, 10 on relay.wikifreedia.xyz, 4 on relay.ditto.pub, 0 on relay.primal.net and nos.lol. nostr.wine refused a kind-less tag query with "auth-required", so filters should name the kind.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher becomes findable by pushing its declaration to relays that readers of H already query: the issuer's read relays (NIP-65: "Send the event to all read relays of each tagged user") and relays the issuer's announcement lists (the NIP-34 pattern: "relays that this repository will monitor for patches and issues"). No registration step. A publisher that posts only to its own obscure relay and tags nobody connected to H is not findable.
- **Identity.** Each event is signed by a secp256k1 key (BIP-340 Schnorr); there is no rotation or recovery. First cut: bind the key to a domain with NIP-05 (status `final`), a static https://&lt;domain&gt;/.well-known/nostr.json. KERI: the CESR code table has only ECDSA entries for secp256k1 (1AAA, 1AAB, signature 0C) and the spec text contains no Schnorr code, so the Nostr key cannot itself be an AID signing key. Additive path (my proposal; I found no spec or implementation): the event content carries a CESR stream, a statement naming AID, digest, role and date with attached KERI signatures. Readers verify it against the KEL obtained by KERI means and count AIDs; the outer Nostr key becomes a transport key and the outer tags an index that must match the signed payload.
- **Digest fit.** Good for the identifier. Tag values are arbitrary strings; NIP-01 restricts only ids, authors, #e and #p to 64-character hex, and strfry's default maxTagValSize is 1024. A 44-character CESR SAID goes unchanged in the d tag. Nostr's own ids are sha256 and the conventional x tag is sha256 hex, so Blake3 bytes should not be placed there. If a hex form is wanted: replace the leading 'E' with 'A', base64url-decode to 33 bytes, drop the first byte, hex-encode the remaining 32. NIP-73 `i` tags have no digest type (the table lists URLs, ISBN, geohash, ISO 3166, ISAN, DOI, hashtags, podcast GUIDs, blockchain transactions and addresses).
- **Governance.** No standards body. NIPs live in a public-domain GitHub repository; commit access belongs to "a list of notable Nostr software developers" and the repository owner "can revoke membership and rewrite history". Nearly every NIP, including NIP-01, is labelled `draft`; NIP-05 is `final`. New kinds need no permission; a pull request or an entry in nostr-protocol/registry-of-kinds is the only collision control. The W3C Nostr Community Group's did:nostr describes itself as "a work in progress".
- **Publisher cost.** No money on open relays. One keypair and a few lines of TypeScript. Events should be re-signed at least every three years, because strfry's default rejectEventsOlderThanSeconds is 94,608,000 and older events cannot be re-broadcast to default relays.
- **TypeScript tooling.** nostr-tools 2.26.0 (Unlicense; 599,453 weekly downloads), @nostr-dev-kit/ndk 3.0.3 (14,751), applesauce-core 7.0.3 (5,094), @nostrify/nostrify (3,097); weekly figures for 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-07. Node 24's built-in WebSocket was enough for every query I ran. @noble/hashes provides Blake3; signify-ts (1,499 weekly) covers KERI. The nak CLI (Go, 426 stars) is a ready command-line client.
- **Failure modes.** Spam and poisoning: keys are free, so anyone can publish unlimited declarations for a popular digest. NIP-45 itself says clients "should not fetch these reaction counts from open relays that accept everything", and NIP-89 tells clients to "use spam-prevention mechanisms or querying high-quality restricted relays". Real defences are an authors filter, web of trust, relay write policies, and later KERI verification. Liveness: events outlive an offline publisher and anyone can re-broadcast them, but retention is each relay's choice and NIP-11 has no retention field. Completeness cannot be proven; silence is not absence. Chokepoints: a handful of large relays and relay-list indexers; stats.nostr.band, api.nostr.band and relay.nostr.band were unreachable from this environment on 2026-11-08. Privacy: relays see the reader's IP and the digest queried; NIP-09 says a deletion request "does not guarantee deletion". Key compromise ends an identity because there is no rotation.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. No index or crawler has to be built: the lookup is a stock relay query, and relays are generic infrastructure run by many independent parties (Damus, Primal, Soapbox/Ditto, nos.lol and roughly 1,300-1,480 others seen by NIP-66 monitors on 2026-11-08). But a relay returns only what was pushed to it, and the live tests show wide variance between relays. Reliable coverage needs a per-definition relay convention, and in the wiki test the dedicated topic relay beat every general relay. A designated relay is generic software and can be mirrored with NIP-77, but if only one exists it plays the aggregator's role.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - NIP-01: "As a convention, all single-letter (only english alphabet letters: a-z, A-Z) key tags are expected to be indexed by relays" ... "Only the first value in any given tag is indexed." Status line: `draft` `mandatory` `relay`. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/01.md
  - Live read-only query: NIP-66 kind 30166 events from the last 24 hours named 1,478 distinct relay URLs; eight monitor keys each reported between 1,311 and 1,366. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.nostr.watch
  - Nostr Biweekly Review for 15-31 Dec 2025: "the total daily active pubkeys reached 144,202"; "Data source: https://stats.nostr.band/". This is the latest sourced activity figure I found. (as of 2026-01-02). Source: https://nostr.ae/naddr1qq2h24j3fafkga2gf9grv3jp2gm9yntv8pfy6q3qwkrfe7kyksfn8s4zmnkr9c9ueakc8hz2fhu4mdayc0gfxa5pnxrsxpqqqp65w5kcxwa
  - Live NIP-45 COUNT of kind 1 notes with created_at in the last 24 hours: relay.primal.net 254,119; relay.ditto.pub 296,076. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.primal.net
  - Live test of one exact-tag filter ({"#I":["https://clawstr.com/c/mcp"]}) returned 157 (relay.ditto.pub), 153 (relay.primal.net), 91 (relay.nostr.net), 89 (nos.lol), 41 (offchain.pub), 36 (nostr.mom) and 0 (theforest.nostr1.com) events. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.ditto.pub
  - Live query {"kinds":[30818],"#d":["bitcoin"]} on 8 relays: 12 distinct authors in the union, 8 of them seen on only one relay; relay.wikifreedia.xyz held 10 authors, relay.ditto.pub 4, relay.primal.net and nos.lol 0. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.wikifreedia.xyz
  - Relay information documents: relay.damus.io, nos.lol, relay.primal.net, relay.nostr.net, nostr.mom and offchain.pub report software strfry; relay.ditto.pub reports Ditto Relay 2.5.1; nostr.wine reports payment_required and restricted_writes true. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://relay.damus.io/

Quotes as returned by the fetch tool. (as of 2025-09-29). Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2402.05709v2
  - nostr-tools 2.26.0 was published 2026-11-07 and had 599,453 npm downloads in the week 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-07. (as of 2026-11-07). Source: https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-week/nostr-tools
  - nostr-protocol/nips: 3,136 stars, 808 forks, 759 open issues and pull requests, last push 2026-11-07. Acceptance criterion 1: "They should be fully implemented in at least two clients and one relay -- when applicable." License: "All NIPs are public domain." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/README.md
  - Nostr Compass #3 states "2,500+ relays across 50 countries" and "140+ clients" without naming a source; this is higher than my live NIP-66 measurement. (as of 2026-01-01). Source: https://buttondown.com/nostrcompass/archive/nostr-compass-3-6694
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 2 to 1.5: every figure checked out, but they describe one community (about 1,300 relays, 144,202 unfiltered daily keys ten months ago and falling, two thirds of new relay lists from a bridge), the flagship relay refused connections, the main statistics and index service is unreachable, and agent tooling does not read it (MCP SDK downloads are 147 times nostr-tools). adoption_trajectory 3 to 2.5: tooling downloads rose 20x in a year and Buzz is real, but downloads fell 38% since August, 0 of 17 agent proposals merged, the two MCP proposals have been idle for eight months, NIP-90 was withdrawn, and Buzz runs on private relays. no_aggregator 3.5 to 2.5: no indexer has to be written, but half the large free relays I measured drop events after about six weeks, the only good coverage in the live precedent is a dedicated topic relay, and cold start needs a hard-coded relay list or a single-operator index. reverse_lookup 3.5 to 3: the query is native and I reproduced it exactly, but the answer is partial, unprovable, decays with relay pruning and has no spam bound. publisher_effort 4 to 3: no server or domain is needed, but the publisher takes on a new key, must know the issuer's relays, and must re-broadcast about monthly or depend on a retention relay. keri_fit 3 to 2: Nostr adds a mandatory second identity that cannot rotate, replacement and spam controls are keyed to that identity instead of the AID, delegated signing is deprecated, and the KERI path is the researcher's own unspecified proposal with no implementation.

#### NIP-94 file metadata (kind 1063, x tag) plus Blossom blob servers (BUD-01 to BUD-03, NIP-B7)

Blossom is a set of HTTP endpoints for blobs addressed by SHA-256: GET /&lt;sha256&gt; on any server. NIP-B7: "servers that store files addressable by their SHA-256 sums". Users list their servers in a replaceable kind 10063 event. NIP-94 kind 1063 events describe a file, with `x` "containing the SHA-256 hexencoded string of the file". NIP-96 is marked "unrecommended: replaced by Blossom".

- **Publish.** Upload the exact definition bytes with PUT /upload to one or more Blossom servers (authorised by a signed kind 24242 event), publish a kind 10063 server list, and optionally a kind 1063 event with url, m and x tags. The servers host the bytes. Public servers set their own limits and may answer 402 Payment Required (BUD-07).
- **Fetch by digest.** GET https://&lt;server&gt;/&lt;sha256 hex&gt; from any server in the author's kind 10063 list, then check the sha256; NIP-B7: clients "SHOULD verify that the sha256-hash of its contents matches the 64-character hex string". For MAD the reader would then recompute the Blake3 SAID as a second check.
- **Reverse lookup.** Blossom itself has none: HEAD /&lt;sha256&gt; tells whether one server holds a blob, and GET /list/&lt;pubkey&gt; is marked unrecommended. On relays, {"kinds":[1063],"#x":["&lt;sha256 hex&gt;"]} is a native query (in my live test a hash first seen on relay.primal.net was also returned by relay.ditto.pub and by none of eight other relays). It answers who published file metadata for these bytes, keyed by sha256 only, and it carries no role such as rely or endorse.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Through the author's kind 10063 list, found via the author's relays. BUD-03 allows a fallback "to using a well-known popular blossom server".
- **Identity.** Uploads are authorised by a Nostr-signed kind 24242 event; downloads are verified by hash alone. KERI adds nothing here and is not needed for integrity.
- **Digest fit.** Poor for SAIDs. The address is always lowercase hex SHA-256 over the exact bytes. A Blake3-256 SAID cannot be the Blossom address or an honest `x` value; MAD would carry both identifiers (SAID in d, sha256 in x). A search of the Blossom repository's issues for "blake3" returned nothing.
- **Governance.** BUDs live in one maintainer's public-domain GitHub repository (hzrd149/blossom); BUD-01 is `draft` `mandatory`. NIP-B7 is `draft` `optional`.
- **Publisher cost.** Free or paid per server; one HTTP PUT plus one signed list event.
- **TypeScript tooling.** blossom-client-sdk 5.1.0 (MIT); nak can upload, download and run a local Blossom server.
- **Failure modes.** Bytes vanish when every listed server drops them; the top server in my sample (blossom.primal.net) is named in 85% of lists, a concentration point. Servers may transform or refuse content types. No reverse lookup. SHA-256 only.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Fetching needs no aggregator once one publisher of the bytes is known. The reverse lookup is not a Blossom function; it falls back to relay tag queries, with the same partial coverage as the first mechanism and with sha256 as the only key.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - NIP-B7: "Blossom is a set of standards (called BUDs) for dealing with servers that store files addressable by their SHA-256 sums." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/B7.md
  - NIPs index: "NIP-96: HTTP File Storage Integration --- unrecommended: replaced by Blossom". (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/README.md
  - Live NIP-45 COUNT of kind 10063 server lists: relay.ditto.pub 19,882; nos.lol 6,277; relay.primal.net 4,164. A sample of 1,002 lists named 131 distinct hosts; blossom.primal.net appeared in 85.1%, blossom.ditto.pub in 37.5%, cdn.hzrd149.com in 33.9%. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.ditto.pub
  - Live NIP-45 COUNT of kind 1063 file-metadata events: relay.ditto.pub 316,079; relay.primal.net 253,966. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.primal.net
  - hzrd149/blossom: 326 stars, last push 2026-09-25; blossom-client-sdk 5.1.0 had 961 npm downloads in the week of 2026-11-01. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/hzrd149/blossom
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 2 to 1.5: 19,883 server lists on the largest relay and 131 hosts, with one operator in 85% of lists. adoption_trajectory 3 to 2.5: Blossom did replace NIP-96, but the specification is one maintainer's repository with 326 stars and no push since 2026-09-25. no_aggregator 3 to 2.5: fetching needs a known publisher or the BUD-03 fallback to a 'well-known popular' server, which in practice is blossom.primal.net. reverse_lookup 2 to 1.5: Blossom has none, and the relay-side #x query is SHA-256 only, carries no role, and inherits the retention problem. publisher_effort 4 to 3.5: a second hash, a signed upload authorisation, and untested acceptance of non-media content. keri_fit 1.5 to 1: the address can never be the SAID and Blake3 is absent from the project.

#### Rendezvous layer: NIP-65 relay lists (outbox and inbox), NIP-05 DNS names, NIP-19 relay hints, NIP-66 relay monitors

NIP-65 kind 10002 is a replaceable list of the relays a key writes to and reads mentions from. NIP-05 (status `final`) maps name@domain to a key through https://&lt;domain&gt;/.well-known/nostr.json, which may also list relays. NIP-19 nprofile and naddr strings embed relay hints. NIP-66 kind 30166 events are relay liveness reports signed by monitors.

- **Publish.** One kind 10002 event with r tags (NIP-65 asks for 2-4 relays per category), a nip05 field in the kind 0 profile, and a static nostr.json on the publisher's own domain. Cost: a static file and two events.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not applicable; this layer locates relays, not bytes.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not a reverse lookup. The outbox model tells a reader where a known key writes; it does not find unknown keys that tagged a digest. The useful half is the inbox rule: "Send the event to all read relays of each tagged user". If every declaration p-tags the issuer, the issuer's read relays become the place to ask {"#d":[H]}, exactly as replies to a note are found today.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** From one introduction: resolve name@domain (NIP-05) or decode an nprofile to get a key and relay hints, fetch kind 10002, then follow tags outward. NIP-65 admits the soft centre: clients should spread relay lists "paying attention to relays that, at any moment, serve naturally as well-known public indexers for these relay lists".
- **Identity.** A domain vouches for a key (NIP-05: "not intended to verify a user, but only to identify them", with the stated exception of organisations tied to a well-known domain). This is the domain-first step the maintainer allows. A KERI AID could be linked later with a NIP-39 style kind 10011 identity claim, though no keri claim type is defined.
- **Digest fit.** Not applicable.
- **Governance.** NIP-05 is `final`; NIP-65 and NIP-66 are `draft` `optional`.
- **Publisher cost.** A static JSON file on a domain and two small events.
- **TypeScript tooling.** nostr-tools (nip05, nip19 and SimplePool modules), NDK and applesauce implement outbox relay selection.
- **Failure modes.** Relay lists go stale; inbox relays may reject strangers (web-of-trust or paid inboxes), which blocks exactly the unknown newcomer; indexer relays are few; NIP-66 warns that "A monitor may publish erroneous `30166` events".
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Relay hints inside the introduction avoid any central list. Without hints, finding a stranger's kind 10002 list relies in practice on a few indexer relays such as purplepag.es, which exist already and are generic, but are few.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Live NIP-45 COUNT of kind 10002 relay lists: 2,021,058 on relay.ditto.pub; lists updated in the last 30 days: 251,888 on relay.ditto.pub and 218,067 on relay.primal.net. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.ditto.pub
  - In the 3,000 most recently updated relay lists on relay.primal.net and relay.ditto.pub, nostr.data.haus appeared in 46.1% and relay.momostr.pink in 41.2% (relay.damus.io and relay.primal.net 11.0% each), which suggests many recent keys are bulk-generated or bridged; raw key counts overstate human publishers. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.primal.net
  - NIP-65: "Clients SHOULD spread an author's `kind:10002` event to as many relays as viable, paying attention to relays that, at any moment, serve naturally as well-known public indexers for these relay lists". (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/65.md
  - NIP-05 status line: `final` `optional`. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/05.md
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today unchanged at 2: this is the most deployed part of Nostr (2.02 million relay lists on one relay), though mostly bridged or bulk keys. adoption_trajectory 3 to 2.5: same network-level evidence as the best pick. no_aggregator 3 to 2.5: without a hint, finding a stranger's relay list depends on a few indexer relays, and relay.primal.net keeps few relay lists older than about 40 days. reverse_lookup 1.5 to 1: the researcher says it is not a reverse lookup, and I agree; the inbox rule helps only when a canonical issuer exists, which a content-addressed definition does not guarantee. publisher_effort 4 to 3.5: a static file and two events, plus keeping them alive on pruning relays. keri_fit 2.5 to 2: NIP-05 gives the allowed domain-first step, but NIP-39 has no KERI claim type and no rotation NIP has merged.

#### Human-readable reference: NIP-23 long-form (kind 30023) and NIP-54 wiki (kind 30818), rendered by generic gateways

Addressable text events: kind 30023 carries Markdown articles; kind 30818 carries Djot wiki entries where "it's expected that multiple people will write articles about the exact same subjects" under one normalized d tag. Generic gateways such as njump ("a Nostr-to-web HTML and OpenGraph gateway") and long-form clients render them without anyone building a site for the topic.

- **Publish.** The issuer, or anyone, signs a kind 30023 event with d set to the SAID plus title and summary tags and Markdown content, and sends it to the same relays as the declaration. Free on open relays.
- **Fetch by digest.** REQ {"kinds":[30023],"#d":["&lt;SAID&gt;"]}; the article is prose about the definition, not the verified bytes.
- **Reverse lookup.** The same native primitive: all articles any author wrote under d=&lt;SAID&gt;. The wiki live test is the direct precedent and shows the same partial coverage (12 authors in the union, 10 on the topic relay, none on two large general relays). NIP-51 kind 10102 lets a user list good wiki relays, a per-topic relay list that can be discovered from one user.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Same as the declaration event. For wiki, clients rank competing articles using reactions, contact lists and relay lists (NIP-54).
- **Identity.** Nostr key of the author; the same additive KERI path applies as for declarations.
- **Digest fit.** The d tag takes the SAID unchanged for kind 30023. NIP-54 normalization lowercases d tags, which would corrupt a case-sensitive base64url SAID, so kind 30818 is unsuitable as the key carrier.
- **Governance.** Both NIPs are `draft` `optional`.
- **Publisher cost.** One event; free on open relays.
- **TypeScript tooling.** nostr-tools; any Markdown renderer.
- **Failure modes.** Wiki usage is tiny. A gateway is somebody's website and can disappear, though any other gateway or client can render the same event. Search engines and agents index gateway pages, not relays.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. No reference site has to be built or hosted; existing gateways render the article. Coverage across authors still depends on shared relays, and the best wiki coverage today sits on one dedicated relay.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Live NIP-45 COUNT of kind 30023 articles: 134,077 on relay.ditto.pub and 49,920 on relay.primal.net; 44,162 and 30,513 respectively were created or updated in the last 30 days. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.ditto.pub
  - Live NIP-45 COUNT of kind 30818 wiki articles: 1,571 on relay.ditto.pub, 908 on nostr.mom, 738 on relay.nostr.net, 32 on nos.lol, 29 on relay.primal.net. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.ditto.pub
  - NIP-54: "All letters with uppercase/lowercase variants MUST be converted to lowercase." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/54.md
  - fiatjaf/njump: 167 stars, last push 2026-11-01, description "a Nostr-to-web HTML and OpenGraph gateway". (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/fiatjaf/njump
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today 2 to 1.5: 134,086 articles on the largest relay, 1,571 wiki entries in total, and the gateway is a 167-star project run by one person. adoption_trajectory unchanged at 2. no_aggregator 3.5 to 2.5: a human reader needs someone's gateway site, search engines and agents index gateway pages rather than relays, and listing 'all definitions' needs an agreed tag plus a viewer, which is a reference site in miniature. reverse_lookup 3 to 2.5: same partial coverage, shown directly by the wiki test. publisher_effort 4 to 3.5: one event, kept alive. keri_fit 2 to 1.5: prose signed by a Nostr key; the wiki kind cannot carry a case-sensitive SAID at all.

#### Existing endorsement vocabularies: NIP-32 labels, NIP-89 app handlers, NIP-85 trusted assertions, NIP-73 external IDs with NIP-22 comments

Merged NIPs that already express that one key says something about a subject. NIP-32 kind 1985 attaches namespaced labels to events, pubkeys, addresses, relays or topics. NIP-89 kinds 31989 and 31990 recommend and describe applications that handle an event kind. NIP-85 kinds 30382-30385 are provider-signed scores keyed by a d tag. NIP-73 defines `i` tags for external identifiers, and NIP-22 kind 1111 comments can be scoped to one with an `I` tag.

- **Publish.** Options without a new kind: a kind 1985 label on the issuer's announcement address (["a","&lt;kind&gt;:&lt;issuer&gt;:&lt;SAID&gt;"] with ["l","endorse","&lt;namespace&gt;"]), or a kind 1111 comment scoped with ["I","&lt;scheme&gt;:&lt;SAID&gt;"]. Both are free and are stored by general relays today.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not applicable; these events describe a subject, they do not carry it.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native tag queries: {"kinds":[1985],"#a":["&lt;address&gt;"]} or {"kinds":[1111],"#I":["&lt;scheme&gt;:&lt;SAID&gt;"]}. Limits: NIP-32 targets are e, p, a, r or t, so labels attach to one issuer's event rather than to the digest, and split if two issuers announce the same H. NIP-73 has no digest identifier type, so a said: prefix would be non-standard. Kinds 1985 and 1111 are regular events, so one author can leave many with no replace semantics.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Same relay conventions as the first mechanism.
- **Identity.** Nostr key. NIP-85 is the nearest thing to delegated trust: users name providers whose signed assertions they accept (kind 10040), which is the reader-side trust list that spam control needs.
- **Digest fit.** The d and I tag values accept the SAID string. NIP-73 would need a new type; open pull requests add other types (git permalinks #1859, Wikidata #2150, references to NIP specifications #2102), none for content digests.
- **Governance.** All four NIPs are `draft` `optional`.
- **Publisher cost.** One event each.
- **TypeScript tooling.** nostr-tools, NDK.
- **Failure modes.** Semantics are borrowed: a label or comment is not obviously a claim to issue or rely on a definition, and other clients will render these events as social content. Same spam and coverage limits as the first mechanism.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Same as the first mechanism: native query, partial per-relay coverage.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Live NIP-45 COUNT on relay.ditto.pub: kind 1985 labels 857,047; kind 1111 comments 995,366; kind 31990 handler information 4,168; kind 30382 trusted assertions 52,590. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: wss://relay.ditto.pub
  - NIP-73 title pull requests: 14 total; open ones include #2102 (2025-10-31), #2150 (2025-12-07), #2353 (2026-05-18) and #2468 (2026-09-16). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/nostr-protocol/nips/pulls?q=is%3Apr+NIP-73+in%3Atitle
  - NIP-89: "Clients SHOULD be careful doing this and use spam-prevention mechanisms or querying high-quality restricted relays to avoid directing users to malicious handlers." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/89.md
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today unchanged at 2: counts confirmed (857,049 labels, 995,546 comments on one relay). adoption_trajectory 3 to 2.5: seven NIP-73 extensions sit open, none for digests. no_aggregator 3.5 to 2.5 for the same retention and coverage reasons as the best pick. reverse_lookup 3 to 2.5: labels attach to one issuer's event and split when two parties announce the same digest, a 'said:' identifier would be non-standard, and regular kinds have no replace semantics, so one author can leave many conflicting claims. publisher_effort 4 to 3.5. keri_fit 2.5 to 2: NIP-85's provider lists are a useful reader-side trust pattern, but they are keyed to Nostr keys.

#### Agent-facing Nostr layers: ContextVM (MCP over Nostr), proposed agent NIPs, Block Buzz, Clawstr

2026 work that puts AI agents on Nostr. ContextVM "is a protocol for exposing and calling MCP servers over nostr". Block's Buzz is "a collaboration workspace built on the Nostr protocol" where agents hold their own keys. Clawstr is "a decentralized social network for AI agents built on the Nostr protocol" that reuses NIP-22 comments, NIP-73 URL identifiers and NIP-32 labels. Several NIP pull requests propose agent identity, messaging and MCP server announcements.

- **Publish.** Nothing here defines a record for declaring a definition digest. An MCP server can be announced under the open proposals (NIP-AD pull request #2221, ContextVM pull request #2246); neither is merged.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not provided.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not provided. These layers are evidence about who reads Nostr, not a lookup mechanism.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** By public key over relays; OpenSats describes ContextVM discovery as a client that "can find it through a public key and talk to it over relays".
- **Identity.** Nostr keys per agent. Block: "Every participant, human or agent, holds a cryptographic keypair that belongs to them, not to the platform they happen to be using."
- **Digest fit.** Not applicable.
- **Governance.** All agent NIPs are unmerged pull requests; the earlier agent-adjacent standard (NIP-90) has been withdrawn from recommendation.
- **Publisher cost.** Varies; ContextVM needs a running MCP server plus a gateway process.
- **TypeScript tooling.** @contextvm/sdk (TypeScript, LGPL-3.0); buzz-cli (agent-first, JSON in and out).
- **Failure modes.** Churn: competing proposals, none settled. Buzz deployments are private relays, so they do not make public declarations discoverable. I found no evidence that mainstream agent runtimes or registries read Nostr by default.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Not a reverse-lookup mechanism; any discovery built on it reduces to the relay tag queries of the first mechanism.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Block, 2026-07-21: "Buzz is a collaboration workspace built on the Nostr protocol." and "We chose Nostr because it solves the most fundamental problem in multi-agent collaboration: identity." (as of 2026-07-21). Source: https://block.xyz/inside/introducing-buzz-where-humans-and-agents-work-together
  - block/buzz on GitHub: 36,814 stars, 4,901 forks, Apache-2.0, created 2026-03-06, last push 2026-11-09. README: "It's a Nostr relay: every message, reaction, workflow step, review approval, and git event is a signed event in one log." Each community runs on its own relay, so this adds users of the protocol, not capacity on public relays. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/block/buzz
  - Soapbox, 2026-02-04: Clawstr is "a decentralized social network for AI agents built on the Nostr protocol". Live on relay.ditto.pub: 129,136 events labelled l=ai in total; 2,697 such kind 1111 events from 54 distinct authors in the last 7 days. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://soapbox.pub/blog/announcing-clawstr
  - nostr-protocol/nips has 17 pull requests with "agent" in the title; of the 12 most recent (2026-02-17 to 2026-11-03), 9 are open, 3 closed and none merged. ContextVM (#2246, opened 2026-02-27) and NIP-AD MCP Server and Skill Announcements (#2221, opened 2026-02-12) are open. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://github.com/nostr-protocol/nips/pulls?q=is%3Apr+agent+in%3Atitle
  - NIPs index: "NIP-90: Data Vending Machines --- unrecommended: this got totally out of control, prefer use-case-specific microstandards". (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/README.md
  - @contextvm/sdk 0.14.3 (published 2026-09-28) had 864 npm downloads in the week of 2026-11-01; ContextVM/sdk has 16 GitHub stars. OpenSats lists ContextVM under its "Fifteenth Wave of Nostr Grants" (2026-02-17). (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://opensats.org/projects/contextvm
  - OpenClaw docs: "Nostr is a downloadable channel plugin (`@openclaw/nostr`)" that "lets OpenClaw receive and answer NIP-04 encrypted direct messages over Nostr relays"; direct messages only. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://docs.openclaw.ai/channels/nostr.md
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_today unchanged at 1: 864 weekly SDK downloads, 54 active Clawstr authors in a week on its home relay, a DM-only plugin. adoption_trajectory 3 to 2.5: Buzz's 36,814 stars are real, but all 17 agent proposals are unmerged, the MCP ones are idle since March, and the previous agent standard was withdrawn. no_aggregator 3 to 2 and reverse_lookup 1 to 0.5: nothing here performs a lookup, and Buzz's relays are private. publisher_effort 3 to 2.5: ContextVM needs a running server and gateway. keri_fit 2 to 1.5: the pitch of these layers is 'a Nostr keypair is the agent's identity', which competes with an AID rather than carrying one.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (nostr)

The skeptic re-checked 25 claims; 20 held as stated. The rest:

- *refuted* · Tag-indexed signed events on relays (NIP-01 filters) with an addressable declaration event keyed d=&lt;digest&gt; · claim: Publisher upkeep: 'Events should be re-signed at least every three years' (strfry rejectEventsOlderThanSeconds 94,608,000), and 'What the maintainer runs: Nothing is required.'
  - Correction: On relay.primal.net, nos.lol and offchain.pub a declaration is likely gone within about six weeks unless someone re-broadcasts it, so the realistic upkeep is a re-broadcast roughly monthly (a scheduled job per publisher) or a relay that commits to retention. Caveats: this is one observation, so I cannot separate a rolling policy from one-off purges, and I found no operator documentation; three operators with three different edges makes independent pruning the likelier reading. The relays that do retain (ditto, nostr.mom, relay.nostr.net) are single operators with no stated commitment, and NIP-11 has no retention field (0 matches in 11.md).
  - Checked: The strfry default is real (strfry.conf line 27). But NIP-45 COUNT by created_at window shows three of six large free relays keep almost nothing older than five to six weeks. Kind 1 events created in a one-day window ending N days ago: relay.primal.net 89,726 (30d), 36,863 (40d), 1,782 (42d), 328 (90d), 244 (200d); nos.lol at the 10,000 cap through 40d, then 572 (42d), 72 (90d); offchain.pub 20,253 (30d), 120 (36d), 40 (90d). Controls that do retain: relay.ditto.pub 99,825 (30d), 79,560 (90d), 62,007 (200d); nostr.mom flat at 8,000 to 23,000; relay.nostr.net 11,703 (90d), 31,644 (200d). Six-hour windows put the edges at 42.0 days (primal), about 40.7 days (nos.lol) and 36.0 days (offchain.pub). The same edge appears for replaceable and addressable kinds on primal: kind 10002 relay lists 3,634 (30d) vs 164 (40d) while ditto has 4,598 vs 3,305; kinds 30023+30617+31990+30009 give 1,264 (30d) vs 4 (100d) while ditto has 1,413 vs 1,911.
- *partly* · Tag-indexed signed events on relays (NIP-01 filters) with an addressable declaration event keyed d=&lt;digest&gt; · claim: Relays will accept a new custom kind and a 44-character CESR SAID in a d tag (inferred from NIP-01, strfry defaults and reads of other arbitrary tag values; no write test).
  - Correction: Unknown kinds are stored today by these relays, which supports the inference. Acceptance of a SAID string specifically remains untested, and relay write policies are plugins that operators change without notice.
  - Checked: Read-only, so no write test here either. Supporting evidence: sampling addressable events from the last 7 days showed 79 distinct kinds on relay.ditto.pub, 85 on relay.primal.net, 121 on nos.lol, 85 on nostr.mom, 60 on relay.nostr.net, 34 on offchain.pub, including many numbers with no NIP (37195, 39697, 35000, 39999, 36943). strfry defaults maxTagValSize 1024 and maxEventSize 65536 confirmed.
- *partly* · Tag-indexed signed events on relays (NIP-01 filters) with an addressable declaration event keyed d=&lt;digest&gt; · claim: The reverse lookup works from one introduction by snowballing through NIP-65 relay lists 'until no new authors appear', with no purpose-built infrastructure.
  - Correction: It works without anyone writing an indexer. It does not work dependably without someone operating a relay that all parties write to and that keeps the events.
  - Checked: Walked the steps against live relays (see reverse_lookup_verdict). The per-relay query works. The snowball only reaches declarers who share a relay with someone already found; a party that learned H from a file and published to its own relays is invisible. With only H and no introduction there is no routing from a tag value to a relay: the reader falls back to a hard-coded list of big relays or to an aggregating index, and the main one (relay.nostr.band) was unreachable.
- *partly* · Rendezvous layer: NIP-65 relay lists (outbox and inbox), NIP-05 DNS names, NIP-19 relay hints, NIP-66 relay monitors · claim: kind 10002: 2,021,058 on relay.ditto.pub; 251,888 (ditto) and 218,067 (primal) updated in 30 days; in the 3,000 most recent lists nostr.data.haus appears in 46.1% and relay.momostr.pink in 41.2%.
  - Correction: Totals confirmed. The shares move with time of day, but the conclusion is stronger than stated: about two thirds of the newest relay lists belong to bridged accounts. Separately, relay.primal.net holds few relay lists older than about 40 days (3,634 created in the day 30 days ago, 164 for the day 40 days ago), so outbox lookups for quiet publishers depend on the retaining relays and on indexers such as purplepag.es.
  - Checked: COUNT: 2,021,097; 251,888; 218,064. Sample of the 1,500 most recent lists on each relay: relay.momostr.pink 72.4% (primal) and 67.7% (ditto); nostr.data.haus 12.1% and 11.4%; relay.damus.io 19.0% and 17.4%.
- *partly* · Agent-facing Nostr layers: ContextVM (MCP over Nostr), proposed agent NIPs, Block Buzz, Clawstr · claim: Block announced Buzz on 2026-07-21 as 'a collaboration workspace built on the Nostr protocol'; block/buzz has 36,814 stars, 4,901 forks, Apache-2.0, created 2026-03-06; each community runs its own relay. Presented as the notable adopter outside the Bitcoin-adjacent core.
  - Correction: Facts confirmed. The framing is not: Block is a Bitcoin-centred company whose own site lists Bitkey and Proto among its brands, so Buzz is the core community's largest sponsor building on Nostr, not evidence of spread to a different ecosystem. Buzz relays are authenticated and private, so they add nothing to public rendezvous.
  - Checked: Downloaded the block.xyz page: date July 21, 2026 and both quotes present. GitHub API: 36,814 stars, 4,901 forks, Apache-2.0, created 2026-03-06. README line 37 quote confirmed; relay stack is 'NIP-01, NIP-42 auth', single-community relays.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (nostr)

- **Aggregating and search relays as the existing generic index (relay.nostr.band, Primal's cache service, NIP-50 search on relay.ditto.pub and nostr.wine).** These are what a stranger holding only H would really ask, and they are the only existing infrastructure that could answer without a relay convention. Each is a single operator, and relay.nostr.band, stats.nostr.band and api.nostr.band were all unreachable on 2026-11-08. The report lists them as a chokepoint but not as the cold-start step, which is where the design depends on someone else's aggregator. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/50.md ; NIP-11 documents of relay.ditto.pub and nostr.wine advertise NIP-50; connection attempts to nostr.band endpoints timed out
- **Relay retention as a design input, with NIP-77 negentropy mirroring between retaining relays.** The report mentions NIP-77 in passing but never measures retention. Only relays that keep events are useful rendezvous points, and NIP-77 is advertised by relay.ditto.pub, relay.nostr.net, offchain.pub, relay.wikifreedia.xyz and purplepag.es but not by relay.damus.io, nos.lol or relay.primal.net. Any Nostr binding should name retaining, mirrorable relays rather than the largest ones. Source: NIP-45 COUNT by created_at window on six relays, 2026-11-09T03:30-03:45Z; NIP-11 supported_nips fields
- **One list event per party instead of one event per party and digest (NIP-51 style set with many indexed tags).** A single replaceable event carrying one indexed tag per declared digest (strfry allows 2,000 tags) answers both 'who declares H' and 'what does this party declare', and leaves one event per publisher to keep alive on pruning relays instead of one per definition. It would reduce the upkeep problem but not remove it. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/51.md ; strfry.conf maxNumTags = 2000
- **NIP-34 'earliest unique commit' r tag.** A merged, deployed precedent for the exact pattern: independent authors publish addressable events that share a content-derived identifier so clients can group 'repositories hosted elsewhere that may represent essentially the same project'. relay.ditto.pub holds 4,683 such announcements. It supports feasibility of the tag design; it does not solve coverage. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/34.md lines 26 and 36
- **NIP-67 EOSE completeness hint.** Merged since the researcher's protocol reading would suggest otherwise: a relay can now state 'finish' or 'more', so a reader can know a single relay's answer is complete. It does nothing for completeness across relays, so 'never provably complete' still holds. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/67.md
- **NIP-13 proof of work and NIP-42/NIP-43 relay membership as spam levers.** These are the only protocol-level spam controls and the report omits them. Both act per relay (minimum difficulty or an admitted-member list), so they work only on a relay someone curates, which again points to a designated relay. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/13.md ; NIP-11 fields min_pow_difficulty and restricted_writes
- **NIP-5A static websites (nsites) served from Blossom.** A merged way to publish a human-readable reference site with no hosting, addressed by pubkey, with an x tag for the manifest hash. It speaks to the 'nobody builds reference sites' objective better than a long-form article, but it needs a gateway, uses SHA-256 only, and limits the site identifier to 13 lowercase characters, so it cannot be keyed by a SAID. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/5A.md
- **NIP-78 application data (kind 30078) as a no-new-kind shortcut, ruled out.** It is the most common addressable kind on every relay I sampled and would avoid allocating a kind, but its text says these kinds 'are not meant to be used as a generic interchange format' and that relays should serve them only to the authenticated owner. Worth recording so nobody reaches for it. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nostr-protocol/nips/master/78.md

### What the researcher could not verify (nostr)

- I published nothing (read-only), so relay acceptance of a new custom kind and of a 44-character CESR SAID in a d tag is inferred from NIP-01 text, strfry's default configuration and live reads of other arbitrary-string tag values (URLs in I tags, slugs in d tags), not from a write test.
- No network-wide activity figure for the second half of 2026: stats.nostr.band refused connections and api.nostr.band and relay.nostr.band timed out from this environment on 2026-11-08. The latest sourced figure is 144,202 daily active pubkeys for 15-31 Dec 2025. I cannot say whether nostr.band is down generally or only unreachable from here.
- The WebSocket to relay.damus.io failed from this environment (its HTTP relay information document loaded), so it is missing from the live tag-query tests.
- Live counts come from NIP-45 COUNT on a handful of relays and are per-relay figures, not network totals; nos.lol caps counts at 10,000 and nostr.mom at 100,000. The 1,478-relay figure is what NIP-66 monitors reported on one monitor relay in 24 hours.
- The unattributed "2,500+ relays across 50 countries" in Nostr Compass #3 conflicts with my live measurement of about 1,300-1,480; I could not find its source.
- I did not confirm what nostr.data.haus and relay.momostr.pink are; the reading that they indicate bridged or bulk-generated keys is an inference from their share of recent relay lists.
- WebFetch summarised several pages (Nostr Compass, OpenSats, did:nostr, the arXiv paper, Soapbox, OpenClaw docs, the Nostr Review). Quotes from those are as returned by the summarising tool and were not checked against raw HTML; the arXiv abstract was not obtained verbatim. NIP, BUD, CESR, strfry and Buzz README texts were read raw.
- The Block announcement page failed in WebFetch (header overflow); its quotes come from a tag-stripped download of the page. I found no deployment or user numbers for Buzz, only GitHub stars.
- Clawstr publishes no agent counts. The 54 distinct authors in 7 days is from one relay.
- Of the 17 agent-titled NIP pull requests I saw the state of only the 12 most recent; the claim that none is merged rests on those 12 plus the absence of any agent NIP in the merged index.
- I found no evidence that any mainstream agent runtime or registry (MCP registry, A2A, major model vendors' tooling) reads Nostr natively; absence is not proven.
- No published specification or implementation carries KERI signatures or KELs in Nostr events (only a 2025 essay, disputed in its comments by KERI's author). The KERI path in the sketch is my own proposal. I did not assess the safety of using one secp256k1 key for both ECDSA (KERI) and BIP-340 (Nostr).
- The GitHub API rate limit stopped me from fetching metadata for NDK, applesauce, khatru, nostrify and serverless relay projects; their npm figures are included where I had them. I did not check whether a relay can be run on AWS serverless at near-zero idle cost.
- Kind numbers A and B are placeholders; no allocation exists and collision avoidance depends on a NIP pull request or a registry-of-kinds entry being accepted.

<a id="supply-chain"></a>

## Supply-chain registries and transparency logs

**Researcher's summary.** The supply-chain family is very good at fetching bytes by hash and at signed statements about a hash, but only three of its mechanisms answer "who else declares H" natively across unrelated publishers, and each of those is a generic service run by one operator, not by many independent parties. Rekor v1's search endpoint does it by sha256 today: a live query on 2026-11-09 UTC for the sha256 of the empty string returned 129,011 entry IDs signed by unrelated GitHub Actions identities, with entries as fresh as 2026-11-06, on a log of about 3.76 billion entries. That endpoint is marked experimental, deprecated and possibly incomplete in Rekor's own OpenAPI file; Rekor v2 (GA 2025-10-10) removed search and no longer records attestation subjects; the promised replacement index is still an open issue with no comments (opened 2025-02-24); and the 2026-10-02 post-quantum plan stores identities only as hashes and tentatively turns the classical instance down in 2029 or 2030. Rekor cannot key on Blake3: entries take sha256, sha384 or sha512, and the search API rejected a blake3-prefixed key with HTTP 422, so a SAID can only travel as data beside a sha256. The OCI referrers API is scoped to one repository by the specification ("in the same &lt;name&gt; namespace"), so it answers "what did this publisher attach to H" and never "who else"; when probed, Docker Hub served the endpoint and GHCR returned 404. Software Heritage's public "known" call only says whether bytes are archived (its origin lookup returned 401 without credentials), and deps.dev's hash query matches only whole release artifacts under MD5, SHA1, SHA256 or SHA512. npm's registry search is the one stable native tag lookup: keyword matching behaved as exact and case-sensitive when probed, so a 44-character SAID can be a keyword verbatim, and the pattern is already how agent tooling is found (87,668 packages carry the keyword mcp). GitHub code search already finds KERI SAIDs across repositories (415 hits for the vLEI QVI schema SAID) but needs sign-in, is capped and is explicitly not exhaustive, and topics cannot hold a SAID (lowercase, 50 characters, 20 per repository). Identity everywhere in this family is an account, an OIDC email or a CI workflow URI; a KERI-signed endorsement can ride as payload (a CESR file in a package or repository, a DSSE envelope signed with the AID's key, an OCI referrer artifact), which is additive, but nothing here verifies it. Only GitHub's web interface is usable by a non-developer; everything else needs a command line or CI. Net: the family can give a zero-infrastructure first cut (npm keyword plus a git repository), but it does so through one company's catalogue, and it is local-first only to the extent that each publisher's package or repository also lists its peers.

**Researcher's best pick.** npm registry keywords: one small package per publisher with each declared SAID as an exact keyword, mirrored as a git repository. This is a qualified pick. It is the only stable, documented, unauthenticated cross-publisher tag lookup in the family, but it is one company's catalogue. Rekor v1 search by hash is the only true by-digest alternative and is on a retirement path; the OCI referrers API does not answer the cross-publisher question at all.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** This swaps "our index" for "npm's index". The reverse lookup depends on a single company's JavaScript package registry that can delist packages, rate-limit search or change matching at will; the exact, case-sensitive keyword behaviour it relies on was observed in probes and is not documented. Identity is an npm account, not a key. Any account can attach any SAID as a keyword, so poisoning cannot be prevented, only filtered after the fact by signatures the registry never checks. Only developers can publish, and the choice brands a protocol-neutral registry as a JavaScript artefact. If npm search goes away, what remains is the peers walk, which is the publisher-to-publisher crawl the reviewers already said was missing. A skeptic can fairly say the family offers no reverse lookup that is at once keyed by the digest itself, operated by many parties and authenticated by self-held keys; the one true by-digest lookup (Rekor v1) is being retired and accepts only sha2.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** The researcher's facts are reliable: of 29 claims I re-opened against primary sources or live endpoints, 18 held outright, 11 needed correction, and none was simply false. The corrections mostly weaken the recommendation: the npm pick sits against npm's own terms on database use and is rate-limited, Rekor's search has no availability commitment and its sample hits were mostly one test workflow, and the 'second index' is a downstream copy. Two points came out stronger than reported: npm keyword matching is exact on every hit I inspected, and new packages are searchable within minutes.

Trust this family for what it was built for, which is fetching bytes by hash, verifying them and carrying signed statements about them. Do not trust it for the reverse lookup: the only versions that work today are one company's catalogue and one operator's deprecated endpoint. At most, treat an npm package or git repository as one optional publication channel among several, and look to another family for a lookup that many parties operate and that can carry key-based identity.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

Mechanically yes, today. By the maintainer's first criterion, only half.

Walking it as a stranger holding SAID H, for the researcher's pick (npm keywords):
