1. You must already know the convention that declarations are npm packages carrying H as a keyword. That knowledge lives in the client, which is acceptable.
2. GET https://registry.npmjs.org/-/v1/search?text=keywords:H. Operated by npm, Inc. (GitHub, Microsoft) and nobody else. It accepts any keyword string. I confirmed exact, case-sensitive matching on every returned hit across five probes (case, hyphen, underscore, dot, no prefix matching), paging to offset 20,000, and that packages created minutes earlier with no downloads already appear. Not confirmed: a 44-character mixed-case string, because none exists to probe and publishing was off limits. It is free and needs no sign-in, but I got HTTP 429 after ten back-to-back queries.
3. For each hit, fetch mad.json and the definition from the registry tarball (npm) or from unpkg or jsDelivr (third parties), then recompute the Blake3 SAID locally. Nothing in this chain verifies who is declaring: a hit is an npm account that typed a string.

What has to exist: a client library and the convention. No service, no index, no seed list. That part of the report holds.

What stands behind the answer: one company's index, used under terms that say 'Using the Public Registry as a general purpose database is not allowed' and that let administrators delete content they deem unacceptable. The second index at packages.ecosyste.ms works (it had a 20-minute-old package) but is a downstream copy of npm. Only parties who publish to npm can be found, and only developers can publish.

So nobody has to run an index, but 'generic infrastructure that many independent parties operate' is not met, and the search is a central catalogue, not local-first. The only local-first path is the peers list in each package, which is a publisher-to-publisher crawl with no index behind it. If npm's search behaviour or tolerance changes, the honest fallback is that somebody runs an index.

The by-digest alternative, Rekor v1, also works today (I found a four-day-old attestation by its subject digest), but only for sha2 digests, without roles, on an endpoint that is deprecated, outside the availability objective and absent from the successor log.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

The family never offers the three properties together: a lookup keyed by the digest itself, operated by many parties, and authenticated by self-held keys. Each mechanism has at most one.

- OCI is the only substrate many independent parties run, and its reverse lookup stops at the repository boundary by specification.
- Rekor v1 is the only lookup truly keyed by a digest. It takes sha2 only, shows login or workflow identities, and is being retired piece by piece: search deprecated and outside the availability objective, removed in v2, DSSE entries dropped in June 2026, identities to be hashed in the successor.
- The recommended pick is a string convention on one company's JavaScript package registry. The SAID is a label nobody checks, the identity is an npm account, and the registry's own terms say 'Using the Public Registry as a general purpose database is not allowed'. A registry that removed up to 150,000 spam packages in one sweep will not hesitate over a few hundred packages whose keywords are hashes.

The adoption argument also cuts the other way. Agent tooling was tagged with npm keywords at scale (87,669 packages), and that community still built a dedicated registry. Building mad-discovery on this family therefore means either accepting one company's catalogue as the meeting point, or rebuilding the index the maintainer set out to avoid, with non-developers shut out in both cases.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (supply-chain)

What is written, and where. Each party keeps one git repository and publishes it as one npm package (for example @acme/mad). It contains definitions/&lt;SAID&gt;.json (the definition bytes with the embedded SAID), mad.json (the party's declarations: SAID, role of issues, relies-on, implements or endorses, plus a peers list of other packages, repositories or AIDs it knows), and README.md, which npmjs.com and the forge render as the human-readable reference. package.json keywords hold the rendezvous tag mad-definition, the bare SAID for each declaration and optionally a role-qualified form such as mad-relies.&lt;SAID&gt;. Publishing is npm publish --provenance from CI, which also records a Sigstore attestation in Rekor at no extra effort.

Queries. Who else declares H: GET https://registry.npmjs.org/-/v1/search?text=keywords:&lt;SAID&gt;&size=250, paging with from=; for each hit fetch https://unpkg.com/&lt;pkg&gt;@&lt;version&gt;/mad.json and keep only declarations that verify. Fetch H: GET https://unpkg.com/&lt;pkg&gt;@&lt;version&gt;/definitions/&lt;SAID&gt;.json from any hit (or the tarball checked against dist.integrity), then recompute the Blake3 SAID. Enumerate: keywords:mad-definition. Local-first from one introduction: read that publisher's mad.json and walk its peers list, with no call to npm search at all; the search is an accelerator, not a requirement. The same keyword query can be repeated against packages.ecosyste.ms as a second, independently run index.

Identity path. First cut: npm scope plus provenance (a CI workflow identity). Later, additively: ship mad.cesr in the package holding the endorsement signed by the party's AID with an OOBI; the client treats registry hits as unverified hints and ranks or filters by verified AID signature. Nothing about the npm layout changes.

What the maintainer runs: nothing. The deliverable is a TypeScript library and CLI (publish, who &lt;SAID&gt;, fetch &lt;SAID&gt;, walk &lt;publisher&gt;) with no Lambda, no DynamoDB and no seed list.

Optional second channel, with an expiry date: also log a hashedrekord over the canonical string mad:v1:&lt;role&gt;:&lt;SAID&gt; to Rekor v1 and query POST /api/v1/index/retrieve with its sha256. This works today and yields signer identities and timestamps, but the endpoint is deprecated, absent from Rekor v2, and the classical instance is tentatively due for turndown in 2029 or 2030, so it should never be the only path.

Not recommended as the base: OCI. It is the cleanest fetch-by-hash and the only substrate many parties operate, and declarations-as-referrers is an elegant model, but 'who else' is unanswerable without a shared repository or a crawler.

### Mechanisms (supply-chain)

#### OCI registries, ORAS artifacts and the referrers API

Content-addressed blob and manifest storage behind the OCI Distribution HTTP API, operated independently by Docker Hub (Docker, Inc.), GHCR (GitHub/Microsoft), ECR (AWS), ACR (Microsoft), Artifact Registry (Google), Quay (Red Hat) and self-hosted Harbor, zot or CNCF Distribution. Version 1.1 (released 2024-02-15) added artifactType, a subject field on manifests and GET /v2/&lt;name&gt;/referrers/&lt;digest&gt;. ORAS is the CLI and library family for non-image artifacts.

- **Publish.** Push the definition as a blob plus a manifest (artifactType such as application/vnd.mad.definition.v1+json, annotation carrying the SAID) into the party's own repository, for example ghcr.io/acme/mad. Declare with a second small manifest (artifactType application/vnd.mad.declaration.v1+json, annotations for role and SAID, optional layer holding a signed CESR stream) whose subject is the definition manifest's digest. The spec lets the subject be absent from the repository ("references a manifest that does not exist in the repository"), so a relying party can declare from its own repository. Commands: oras push, oras attach. Free for public content on GHCR and on Docker Hub's free personal plan.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET /v2/&lt;name&gt;/blobs/sha256:&lt;hex&gt; from a repository known to hold it; the client recomputes sha256, then recomputes the embedded SAID. The digest is not routable by itself: there is no cross-registry 'get blob by digest', so the caller must already know a repository.
- **Reverse lookup.** GET /v2/&lt;name&gt;/referrers/&lt;digest&gt;?artifactType=... returns an index of manifests whose subject is that digest, but only within that repository. Spec text: "Each descriptor is of an image manifest or index in the same `<name>` namespace". It answers 'what is attached to H inside acme/mad', not 'who else declares H'. A cross-publisher answer needs either one shared repository into which everyone pushes declarations (someone must own it and grant write access, which is a purpose-built aggregator on free hosting) or a crawler over known repositories. Probes on 2026-11-09 UTC: Docker Hub answered /v2/library/alpine/referrers/&lt;digest&gt; with 200 and an empty index; GHCR answered /v2/homebrew/core/hello/referrers/&lt;digest&gt; with 404 MANIFEST_UNKNOWN, so clients there fall back to the sha256-&lt;hex&gt; tag schema.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None in the protocol. Registry search on the public hosts is by repository name and description, not by digest or artifactType. A new publisher is findable only by being told its repository reference.
- **Identity.** Write access to a registry namespace (an account or organisation). Signatures in this ecosystem are themselves referrer artifacts (cosign, Notation), so a KERI endorsement fits the same slot: a referrer whose layer is a CESR stream signed by the AID, with annotations naming the AID and an OOBI. Additive, but no registry verifies it.
- **Digest fit.** Digest grammar is algorithm:encoded, and sha256 is what registries implement. blake3 is in the registered-algorithms table only on image-spec main ("Implementations MAY implement BLAKE3 digest verification for use in descriptors"), not in released v1.1.0 or v1.1.1; the encoded part must be 64 lowercase hex. Re-encoding a SAID: base64url-decode 'A' + SAID[1:], drop the first byte, hex-encode the remaining 32 bytes (EBfdlu8R27Fbx-ehrqwImnK-8Cm79sqbAQ4MmvEAYqao becomes blake3:17dd96ef11dbb15bc7e7a1aeac089a72bef029bbf6ca9b010e0c9af10062a6a8). GHCR answered a blake3: blob read with 400 DIGEST_INVALID. In practice: sha256 for transport, the SAID as an annotation.
- **Governance.** Open Container Initiative, a Linux Foundation project; specifications are stable at 1.1.x. ORAS is a CNCF Sandbox project (accepted 2021-07-13 according to a secondary source).
- **Publisher cost.** Free public hosting. Effort: a registry account and token, then the oras CLI or about five HTTP calls.
- **TypeScript tooling.** No first-party ORAS JavaScript or TypeScript library: the oras-project organisation has Go, Python, Java, .NET and Rust libraries plus a Rust-backed Node binding repository (rust-oci-client-node). An npm search found no maintained general OCI registry client with meaningful downloads. The API is plain HTTPS and can be driven with fetch.
- **Failure modes.** Reverse lookup is blind across repositories. GHCR lacks the referrers endpoint, and the tag-schema fallback is a client-maintained index that races under concurrent writers. A shared repository invites referrer spam from anyone with write access. The host can delete, rate-limit or change free-tier terms. No human-readable page for a definition. Liveness depends on the registry host, though blobs can be mirrored by digest.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. The referrers API is per repository by specification, so 'who else' requires a shared repository someone administers or a crawler and index someone runs.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "The OCI Image Specification and Distribution Specification each had a 1.1.0 release on February 15, 2024." (as of 2024-03-13). Source: https://opencontainers.org/posts/blog/2024-03-13-image-and-distribution-1-1/
  - Latest releases are image-spec v1.1.1 (2025-03-03) and distribution-spec v1.1.1 (2025-01-29); blake3 appears in descriptor.md on main but in neither v1.1.0 nor v1.1.1 (GitHub API and raw-file check). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://github.com/opencontainers/image-spec/blob/main/descriptor.md
  - ORAS lists 12 compatible registries: CNCF Distribution, Amazon ECR, Azure Container Registry, Google Artifact Registry, GitHub Packages container registry, Docker Hub, Zot, Red Hat Quay, Artifactory, Harbor, DigitalOcean Container Registry, summ. (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://oras.land/docs/compatible_oci_registries
  - The official MCP registry schema describes package registryType as "Registry type indicating how to download packages (e.g., 'npm', 'pypi', 'cargo', 'oci', 'nuget', 'mcpb')", so agent tooling already resolves OCI and npm references. (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry/blob/main/docs/reference/api/openapi.yaml
  - "Container image storage and bandwidth for the Container registry is currently free." (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://docs.github.com/en/billing/concepts/product-billing/github-packages
  - Amazon ECR pull-through cache added discovery and sync of OCI referrers in April 2026 (from a search summary, not fetched verbatim). (as of 2026-04). Source: https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/04/amazon-ecr-pull-through-cache-referrers/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. Every load-bearing claim held when re-read or re-probed: the referrers API is per repository by specification, GHCR does not serve it, and blake3 exists only on the unreleased main branch. This is the one substrate in the family that many independent parties operate, and it still cannot answer 'who else'.

#### Sigstore: cosign keyless signing and the Rekor transparency log

A free public-good signing service. Fulcio issues short-lived certificates binding an OIDC identity to an ephemeral key; Rekor is the append-only log of signatures. Operated by the Sigstore community under OpenSSF with a volunteer on-call rotation and a 99.5% availability SLO. Rekor v1 (rekor.sigstore.dev) is still the default log; Rekor v2 (log2025-1.rekor.sigstore.dev, GA 2025-10-10) is tile-based and opt-in.

- **Publish.** Sign and log a statement about H on Rekor v1, in one of two shapes. (a) A DSSE in-toto statement (cosign attest-blob or sigstore-js) with subject digest sha256(definition bytes) and a custom predicate carrying role, SAID and AID; Rekor v1's dsse type indexes every subject digest. (b) A hashedrekord over the canonical string mad:v1:&lt;role&gt;:&lt;SAID&gt;, so the sha256 of that string is a per-role lookup key anyone can recompute. The signer is a Fulcio certificate (browser or CI OIDC login) or a self-managed public key. Free. Rekor does not keep the attestation body for dsse entries ("Rekor was not designed to be used as storage for verification metadata"), so the publisher still hosts the bundle somewhere.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not provided. Rekor stores hashes, signatures and verifiers, never the artifact.
- **Reverse lookup.** Rekor v1, native and unauthenticated: POST https://rekor.sigstore.dev/api/v1/index/retrieve with {"hash":"sha256:&lt;hex&gt;"} returns an array of entry UUIDs; GET /api/v1/log/entries/&lt;uuid&gt; returns the entry with the signer's certificate or public key, the integrated time and an inclusion proof. Verified live on 2026-11-09 UTC: the sha256 of the empty string returned 129,011 UUIDs; sampled entries were hashedrekord and dsse kinds dated up to 2026-11-06, signed by unrelated GitHub Actions workflow identities. Limits: the OpenAPI file marks the endpoint deprecated and "EXPERIMENTAL - this endpoint is offered as best effort only and may be changed or removed in future releases", with results that "may be incomplete"; the whole list comes back unpaginated; a dsse hit reveals signer and time but not the predicate, which is why shape (b) is needed to distinguish roles. Rekor v2 has no search at all ("Rekor no longer provides an API for online verification and search"), deprecates the DSSE entry type in favour of a hashedrekord over the hash of the DSSE PAE (so attestation subjects are no longer in the log), and its replacement search service is unshipped (rekor-tiles issue 15, open since 2025-02-24, zero comments).
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Logging the entry is the rendezvous: a publisher nobody has heard of appears in the next search for that hash. There is no enumeration of 'all definitions' short of scanning the log or its BigQuery mirror.
- **Identity.** A Fulcio certificate whose SAN is an email (Google, GitHub or Microsoft login) or a CI workflow URI; sampled entries showed URIs of the form https://github.com/&lt;org&gt;/&lt;repo&gt;/.github/workflows/&lt;file&gt;@refs/heads/main with issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com. GitLab CI, SPIFFE and Kubernetes issuers are also supported. Alternatively a raw public key: Rekor v1 and v2 both accept a self-managed key (v2 client doc: "Request with a self-managed key"), including Ed25519. So an AID's current signing key can sign the envelope and the AID can sit in the DSSE keyid or the predicate, but that is an extra signature made for Sigstore (hashedrekord verifies Ed25519 in prehash mode), not the KERI-native one; Rekor knows nothing about key event logs or rotation. The post-quantum plan of 2026-10-02 says user identities "will be stored as cryptographic hashes, preventing sensitive identifiers from being public", which removes the public 'who' from the successor log.
- **Digest fit.** sha2 only. Rekor v1 hashedrekord schema enum: sha256, sha384, sha512. Sigstore protobuf HashAlgorithm: SHA2_256, SHA2_384, SHA2_512 (SHA3 variants deprecated). The search field accepts only sha1, sha256 or sha512 hex; a body of {"hash":"blake3:&lt;64 hex&gt;"} returned HTTP 422 with the pattern error. A Blake3 SAID cannot be the lookup key; use the sha256 of the bytes, or of a canonical string that contains the SAID.
- **Governance.** OpenSSF graduated project (Linux Foundation). The public-good instance is run by the community's public-good operations group. Stated deprecation policy: a Rekor v1 freeze "will be announced one year in advance"; no freeze has been announced. Direction of travel: v2 rollout paused, PQC redesign under way, classical instance turndown tentatively 2029 or 2030.
- **Publisher cost.** Free. Needs cosign or sigstore-js plus an OIDC login or a CI workflow. The public instance limits attestation uploads to 100KB.
- **TypeScript tooling.** sigstore-js: the sigstore package is at 5.0.1 (modified 2026-11-05) with 13,974,823 downloads in the week 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-07, alongside @sigstore/sign, @sigstore/verify and @sigstore/bundle. The Rekor v1 search is a single fetch call.
- **Failure modes.** Anyone can log an entry against any hash (a search for the sha256 value 00...01 returned results), so hits are claims to verify, not facts. Entries are permanent and public, including email addresses. One operator. The search endpoint is deprecated and may be incomplete. Using a software supply-chain log as a registry of endorsements is off-label and could be unwelcome. The successor design hides identities, and the classical instance has a tentative end date.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Nothing has to be built or run today because Rekor v1's own index answers the query, but that index is one operator's deprecated best-effort feature with no successor in v2. When it goes, someone must tail the log and index it, which is a purpose-built aggregator again.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Rekor v1 holds about 3.76 billion entries: active shard treeSize 3,638,556,727 plus inactive shards of 117,740,831 and 4,163,431 (live GET). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://rekor.sigstore.dev/api/v1/log
  - Rekor v2 shard log2025-1 holds 190,379,505 entries and is cosigned by external witnesses (live checkpoint). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://log2025-1.rekor.sigstore.dev/api/v2/checkpoint
  - "The search index has been removed, since this was a best-effort service." (as of 2025-10-10). Source: https://blog.sigstore.dev/rekor-v2-ga/
  - "In the near future, we will spin up a separate service to support search." The tracking issue 'Implement search index as its own service, including client PoC' has been open since 2025-02-24 with no comments. (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://github.com/sigstore/rekor-tiles/issues/15
  - "the sigstore.dev public good instance will continue using Rekor v1 as the default log for the foreseeable future." (as of 2026-06-28). Source: https://blog.sigstore.dev/rekor-evolution/
  - The classical public instance stays operational until a turndown "tentatively scheduled for 2029 or 2030 to align with PQC mandates"; a PQC instance is targeted for mid-2027. (as of 2026-10-02). Source: https://blog.sigstore.dev/pqc-sigstore/
  - npm publish provenance still lands in Rekor v1: sigstore@5.0.1 carries an SLSA provenance v1 attestation at Rekor log index 3,692,194,993 (live GET of the npm attestations endpoint). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/-/npm/v1/attestations/sigstore@5.0.1
  - Sigstore became an OpenSSF graduated project in March 2024 (from a search summary of the Sigstore and OpenSSF posts). (as of 2024-03). Source: https://blog.sigstore.dev/sigstore-openssf-graduation/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** adoption_trajectory 2 to 1: for this job the direction is down on every count I checked. The search endpoint is deprecated, sits outside the 99.5% availability objective, was removed in v2, and its successor has been an untouched issue for 20 months; v2 also dropped the DSSE entry type in June 2026, and the post-quantum plan hashes identities. no_aggregator 4 to 3: nothing must be built today, but the answer comes from one operator's uncommitted feature, and using a software-signing log to register protocol endorsements is off-label. reverse_lookup stays 3: I confirmed it works on fresh data by subject digest, but only for sha2, without roles, and unpaginated. keri_fit stays 2: bare keys are accepted, though every one I saw was P-256, not Ed25519.

#### in-toto attestations (DSSE statements; GitHub artifact attestations, npm provenance, PyPI attestations)

A signed-statement format: a DSSE envelope around an in-toto Statement with subject (name and digest set), predicateType and predicate. It is exactly the shape 'principal P says X about digest H'. It is a format, not a store: lookup is whatever the hosting store offers.

- **Publish.** Write a Statement whose subject digest is the sha256 of the definition bytes and whose custom predicateType URI carries role, SAID and AID; sign it as DSSE; store it beside the artifact as an OCI referrer, in a package registry's attestation endpoint, in GitHub's attestation store (actions/attest) or as a file in a repository.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not applicable; the format carries digests, not bytes.
- **Reverse lookup.** Depends entirely on the store. Rekor v1 indexes the subject digests of dsse entries (its source builds index keys as algorithm + ":" + digest for each subject), which is the only cross-publisher path and is covered above. GitHub's API GET /users/{username}/attestations/{subject_digest} (with organisation and repository variants) is keyed by digest but scoped to attestations "associated with repositories owned by a user", so the owner must already be known. npm and PyPI serve attestations per package version. No store offers a global query by subject digest or by predicateType.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None of its own.
- **Identity.** DSSE is signature-scheme agnostic and its keyid field is free-form, so an AID or its key can be named there, and the predicate can embed or reference an ACDC by SAID. Sigstore-based verifiers, however, expect a Fulcio certificate or a pre-configured key.
- **Digest fit.** The DigestSet predefines sha256, sha512, sha1, blake2b, gitBlob and others, not blake3. Extension is allowed ("New algorithms MUST document how the value is encoded, e.g. URL-safe base64, lowercase hex, etc.") but consumers "MUST ignore unrecognized or unaccepted algorithms". A subject digest of both sha256 and blake3 hex is legal; only sha256 will be understood by existing tools.
- **Governance.** in-toto is a CNCF graduated project; the attestation framework is at spec v1 and SLSA provenance v1 is the dominant predicate.
- **Publisher cost.** Free; one signing step in a CLI or CI job.
- **TypeScript tooling.** sigstore-js (attest and verify), @sigstore/bundle, and the TypeScript GitHub action actions/attest.
- **Failure modes.** A format without a home: lookup inherits each store's limits. Generic verifiers ignore custom predicate types. The subject must be a sha2 digest to be found by anything that exists today.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Outside Rekor v1's deprecated index there is no cross-publisher query by subject digest; attestations would have to be crawled from each store.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - CNCF announced in-toto's graduation on 2025-04-23 (from a search summary). (as of 2025-04-23). Source: https://www.cncf.io/announcements/2025/04/23/cncf-announces-graduation-of-in-toto-security-framework-enhancing-software-supply-chain-integrity-across-industries/
  - npm serves two attestations for sigstore@5.0.1: predicate types https://slsa.dev/provenance/v1 and https://github.com/npm/attestation/tree/main/specs/publish/v0.1 (live GET). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/-/npm/v1/attestations/sigstore@5.0.1
  - GitHub artifact attestations for public repositories use the Sigstore public good instance and are "written to an immutable transparency log that is publicly readable on the internet". (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://docs.github.com/en/actions/concepts/security/artifact-attestations
  - PyPI reported more than 50,000 projects using trusted publishing in its 2025 review; at the November 2024 launch over 20,000 attestations had been uploaded (from search summaries). (as of 2025-12-31). Source: https://blog.pypi.org/posts/2025-12-31-pypi-2025-in-review
  - Third-party samples put npm provenance at about 12% of the most-downloaded packages (6 of the top 50 in an April 2026 audit); no registry-wide figure was found. (as of 2026-04). Source: https://dev.to/thecryptodonkey/i-audited-the-top-50-npm-packages-almost-none-ship-with-supply-chain-attestations-3ki8

#### npm registry keywords (a package registry used as a tag index; PyPI as the contrast)

The npm public registry, operated by npm, Inc. (a GitHub/Microsoft subsidiary), holds 4,583,845 packages and exposes a documented, unauthenticated search API with a keywords: qualifier. A package is a content-addressed tarball (sha512 integrity) with free-form keywords, a rendered README and optional Sigstore provenance. PyPI, by contrast, has no search API.

- **Publish.** Each party publishes one small package, for example @acme/mad, containing definitions/&lt;SAID&gt;.json, a mad.json listing its declarations (SAID, role, and peers it knows) and a README. package.json keywords carry a rendezvous tag (mad-definition) and one keyword per declared SAID, optionally role-qualified (mad-issues.&lt;SAID&gt;). npm publish, ideally with --provenance from CI. Free for public packages.
- **Fetch by digest.** From any package carrying the keyword: GET https://unpkg.com/&lt;pkg&gt;@&lt;version&gt;/definitions/&lt;SAID&gt;.json or the jsDelivr equivalent (both returned 200 for a test file), or download the tarball and check dist.integrity (sha512). Then recompute the Blake3 SAID from the bytes; the registry never checks it.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native tag query on npm's own index: GET https://registry.npmjs.org/-/v1/search?text=keywords:&lt;SAID&gt;&size=250 (page with from=) returns matching packages with name, publisher, maintainers and date. Probes on 2026-11-09 show matching is exact and case-sensitive: keywords:TypeScript returned 7,890 packages and keywords:typescript 159,881; JSON-LD 108 against json-ld 768; react-component 41,627 against react_component 1. A 44-character base64url SAID therefore works verbatim as a keyword. The client then fetches each hit's mad.json and verifies it. A second, independently operated index answers the same kind of query across ecosystems: https://packages.ecosyste.ms/api/v1/keywords/&lt;keyword&gt; returned npm and PyPI packages. A variant maps 'relies on' to an npm dependency and uses reverse dependencies, but deps.dev's dependents call returns only counts.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Publishing is the rendezvous: the package appears under its keywords with no registration elsewhere. Enumeration of everything is keywords:mad-definition.
- **Identity.** An npm account or organisation scope, optionally strengthened by Sigstore provenance naming the CI workflow that published. A KERI-signed CESR file can ship inside the tarball and the client can rank or filter hits by verified AID signature; that is additive, but npm will never verify it.
- **Digest fit.** The SAID is carried as an opaque, exact-match keyword string with no re-encoding. Transport integrity is npm's own sha512 of the tarball. Avoid a colon inside the keyword because the search syntax uses it for qualifiers.
- **Governance.** A proprietary service of npm, Inc. (GitHub/Microsoft). The API is documented in the npm/registry repository but belongs to no standards body, and the exact-match keyword behaviour is observed, not documented.
- **Publisher cost.** Free. An npm account with two-factor authentication and the CLI, or a CI workflow; minutes for a TypeScript developer.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Native territory: the npm CLI, the search API over fetch, pacote and libnpmsearch; @noble/hashes (142,794,614 weekly downloads) provides Blake3 for SAID checks.
- **Failure modes.** Anyone can attach any SAID as a keyword, so hits are unverified hints and keyword stuffing cannot be prevented. npm can delist packages it regards as spam and can change search ranking or matching. One company is the write path and the canonical index. Publishing needs a developer. Whether a brand-new package with no downloads appears promptly in keyword search was not tested. A party declaring very many SAIDs would need large keyword arrays or several packages. It brands a protocol-neutral registry as a JavaScript thing.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** no. The lookup is served by npm's general-purpose search (and mirrored by ecosyste.ms); nobody builds or runs anything for these definitions. The caveat is that this is one company's central catalogue, not infrastructure many parties operate.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - The registry replication endpoint reports doc_count 4,583,845 (live GET). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://replicate.npmjs.com/
  - Keywords are already the discovery tag for agent tooling: keywords:mcp matches 87,668 packages and keywords:mcp-server 11,216 (live search). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://registry.npmjs.org/-/v1/search?text=keywords:mcp&size=1
  - Search parameters are documented: size is "how many results should be returned (default 20, max 250)", and keywords: is a listed qualifier. (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://github.com/npm/registry/blob/main/docs/REGISTRY-API.md
  - PyPI has no equivalent: its XML-RPC search answers "PyPI no longer supports 'pip search' (or XML-RPC search)." (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://pypi.org/pypi
  - ecosyste.ms serves a keyword-to-packages API across registries; the sigstore keyword returned npm and PyPI packages (live GET). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://packages.ecosyste.ms/api/v1/keywords/sigstore

#### GitHub and plain git: repositories, topics, code search, commit search

git is a content-addressed store (SHA-1 object ids) with no global lookup; GitHub (Microsoft) adds search over public repositories: topics, code search and commit search. Other forges host the same repository but do not share the search.

- **Publish.** Create a public repository holding the definition files and a declarations file, and add a rendezvous topic such as mad-definitions. The SAID appears as text in the files. All of this can be done in the web interface without a command line. Free.
- **Fetch by digest.** Within a known repository only: GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/git/blobs/{sha} by git blob id, or a raw URL by path; verify the SAID after download. A git blob id is SHA-1 over a 'blob &lt;length&gt;' header plus the bytes, so it is a different hash of the same content.
- **Reverse lookup.** Three native queries, all run by GitHub. (1) Code search for the SAID string: GET /search/code?q="&lt;SAID&gt;" returned 415 hits across repositories for the vLEI QVI schema SAID EBfdlu8R27Fbx-ehrqwImnK-8Cm79sqbAQ4MmvEAYqao. It requires authentication (403 without), allows 10 requests per minute, sees only default branches and files under 384 KB, returns at most 1,000 results (100 in the web interface), and the documentation says "Exhaustive search is not supported" and "not all code is indexed". (2) Topic search GET /search/repositories?q=topic:&lt;t&gt; works unauthenticated, but a topic cannot hold a SAID: "Use lowercase letters, numbers, and hyphens", "Use 50 characters or less", "Add no more than 20 topics", and the lowercase base32 form of a 256-bit digest is 52 characters. Topics therefore serve to enumerate publishers, after which the client fetches each publisher's declarations file. (3) Commit search with tree:&lt;sha1&gt; matches commits by root tree hash across public default branches (the empty tree returned 69,804,942 commits unauthenticated); it is a real by-hash query but SHA-1 and requires a commit whose whole tree is exactly the definition, so it is a curiosity, not a design.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** The topic, or the string on a public default branch, once GitHub has indexed it; indexing delay is not documented.
- **Identity.** A GitHub account or organisation, optionally with signed commits. A KERI-signed CESR file can sit in the repository; additive.
- **Digest fit.** The SAID is found as an opaque string by code search (case-insensitive by default). It does not fit a topic. git object ids cannot be derived from a Blake3 digest.
- **Governance.** A proprietary service. git itself is open and forge-neutral, which keeps the publishing step portable to other forges, but the search does not travel with it.
- **Publisher cost.** Free; minutes in a browser. This is the only mechanism in the family a non-developer can realistically use.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Octokit (@octokit/rest) and isomorphic-git.
- **Failure modes.** A mention is not a declaration: most of the 415 hits were copies of the same fixture in forks and test data. Search is login-gated, capped and incomplete. One company, with account takedown risk. Anyone can write the string anywhere.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. A best-effort answer needs nothing purpose-built, but a complete one is not available: code search is login-gated and explicitly non-exhaustive, and topic-based lookup needs a client-side fan-out over every publisher.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Octoverse 2025 reports more than 180 million developers, 630 million repositories and 395 million public repositories (from a search summary of the GitHub post). (as of 2025-10-28). Source: https://github.blog/news-insights/octoverse/octoverse-a-new-developer-joins-github-every-second-as-ai-leads-typescript-to-1/
  - Topic search returns 41,729 repositories for topic:mcp-server, 486 for topic:sigstore and 40 for topic:keri (live, unauthenticated, limit 10 requests per minute). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://api.github.com/search/repositories?q=topic:mcp-server&per_page=1
  - "You must be logged in to a GitHub account to use code search, including for searching code in public repositories." (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://docs.github.com/en/search-github/github-code-search/about-github-code-search
  - "The `tree` qualifier matches commits with the specified SHA-1 git tree hash." (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://docs.github.com/en/search-github/searching-on-github/searching-commits

#### Software Heritage and SWHIDs (ISO/IEC 18670:2025)

A non-profit universal source-code archive (founded by Inria in partnership with UNESCO) that crawls forges and package registries and identifies every object by an intrinsic SWHID, for file contents swh:1:cnt:&lt;40 hex&gt;.

- **Publish.** Nothing to write: keep the definition file in any public repository and the archive's crawlers pick it up; a 'Save Code Now' request speeds that up. The implied declaration is only 'this exact blob exists in my public repository'.
- **Fetch by digest.** By SWHID through the archive's content endpoints, then verify the SAID. The SWHID for a file is SHA-1 over 'blob', a space, the length, a NUL byte and the bytes, so anyone holding the bytes can compute it, but it cannot be derived from the Blake3 digest.
- **Reverse lookup.** POST https://archive.softwareheritage.org/api/1/known/ with a list of SWHIDs returns known true or false per identifier: existence only, never who. Verified live. The origin lookup GET /api/1/provenance/whereis/&lt;swhid&gt;/ answered 401 "This API endpoint requires authentication.", and the documentation (via a search summary) adds that special permission is required and result quality is not guaranteed. The public therefore cannot ask which repositories contain H.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Automatic for repositories on the forges it lists, with no guaranteed delay.
- **Identity.** None. It records where bytes were seen, not who declares anything about them.
- **Digest fit.** Its own function and encoding (SHA-1, git-compatible, lowercase hex). A SAID cannot be re-encoded into a SWHID; the two are independent hashes of the same bytes.
- **Governance.** Non-profit; the identifier scheme is an ISO/IEC standard, with resolution explicitly out of the standard's scope.
- **Publisher cost.** Zero.
- **TypeScript tooling.** None identified; the REST API is plain JSON.
- **Failure modes.** Existence is not endorsement. Crawl lag. 120 anonymous requests per hour. SHA-1. The 'who' query is gated.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. The public API cannot say who holds or vouches for a blob; without the gated provenance endpoint someone must build an origin index.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Archive counters: 31,086,385,770 contents and 458,737,157 origins (live GET). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://archive.softwareheritage.org/api/1/stat/counters/
  - SWHID v1.2 was published as ISO/IEC 18670:2025 in April 2025 (from a search summary of the ISO listing). (as of 2025-04). Source: https://www.iso.org/standard/89985.html
  - The API returned X-Ratelimit-Limit: 120 for an anonymous caller (120 requests per hour per the documentation). (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://archive.softwareheritage.org/api/1/known/

#### deps.dev query by hash

Google's Open Source Insights service, which indexes package releases across npm, PyPI, Cargo, Maven, NuGet, RubyGems and Go and exposes a free API.

- **Publish.** Indirect: publish the definition as a package release on a covered registry and deps.dev ingests it.
- **Fetch by digest.** None; it returns package version keys and the bytes come from the registry.
- **Reverse lookup.** GET /v3/query with hash.type and a base64 hash.value returns package versions whose release artifact has that hash; it matches "multiple release artifacts (such as JAR files) that comprise package versions", not files inside an archive, with "At most 1000 results". The dependents call returns counts only (sigstore 5.0.1: 397 dependents, 57 direct). It cannot answer who declares H unless H is itself a whole package archive, and then it only names packages that redistribute the identical archive.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Whatever the underlying registry provides.
- **Identity.** None beyond registry package ownership.
- **Digest fit.** "Can be one of `MD5`, `SHA1`, `SHA256`, `SHA512`." No Blake3 and no free-form digest strings.
- **Governance.** A Google-operated service with a v3 API and an experimental v3alpha.
- **Publisher cost.** The cost of publishing a package.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Plain HTTPS and JSON; no dedicated library needed.
- **Failure modes.** Wrong granularity for this job; one operator; sha-family hashes only.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. It does not index arbitrary files, tags or declarations, so the reverse question would still need its own index.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "Querying by content hash is currently supported for npm, Cargo, Maven, NuGet, PyPI and RubyGems." (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://docs.deps.dev/api/v3/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** no_aggregator 2 to 1 for the same consistency reason. reverse_lookup stays 1: it does answer 'who ships these exact bytes' for single-file Maven artifacts, which is slightly more than the report credits, but only for sha-family hashes and only for parties who republish the file.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (supply-chain)

The skeptic re-checked 29 claims; 18 held as stated. The rest:

- *partly* · Sigstore / Rekor · claim: Rekor v1 search by sha256 works unauthenticated today: the sha256 of the empty string returned 129,011 entry IDs signed by unrelated GitHub Actions identities.
  - Correction: The count is exact, but the identities are not broadly unrelated. Of 14 sampled entries, 8 were one test workflow (sigstore/root-signing custom-test.yml or its dlorenc fork), 5 were bare public keys with no identity, and 1 was an unrelated project. That hash is mostly test noise. A real case does work: the sha512 of the sigstore@5.0.1 npm tarball returned exactly its two attestation entries dated 2026-11-05.
  - Checked: POST https://rekor.sigstore.dev/api/v1/index/retrieve on 2026-11-09 03:13 UTC returned HTTP 200 with exactly 129,011 IDs in one unpaginated 10.7 MB response. I then fetched 14 random entries and read the signer certificates.
- *partly* · Sigstore / Rekor · claim: The public instance is run with a volunteer on-call rotation and a 99.5% availability SLO.
  - Correction: The README says '24/7 oncall rotation', not volunteer. More important: the 99.5% objective lists five endpoints (/api/v1/log, /log/publicKey, /log/proof, /log/entries, /log/entries/retrieve) and does not include /api/v1/index/retrieve. The search the reverse lookup depends on has no availability commitment at all.
  - Checked: Read the Rekor README 'Public Instance' section.
- *partly* · Sigstore / Rekor · claim: Rekor v1 stays the default log for the foreseeable future; the v2 rollout is paused.
  - Correction: The default-log quote is there. 'Paused' is the researcher's word; the post says 'there's been a change of plans' and that v2 'is running and available in production today'.
  - Checked: Fetched blog.sigstore.dev/rekor-evolution/ (2026-06-28).
- *partly* · Sigstore / Rekor · claim: Rekor accepts a self-managed public key, including Ed25519, so an AID's signing key could sign the entry.
  - Correction: All 19 were 256-bit elliptic-curve keys; I saw no Ed25519 key in the log. Ed25519 acceptance rests on the schema and documentation only, as the researcher admitted.
  - Checked: Sampled live entries: bare public keys are accepted in 2026 (5 of 14 in one sample). I inspected 19 such keys.
- *partly* · OCI registries / referrers · claim: ORAS lists 12 compatible registries (used as adoption evidence for this job).
  - Correction: The page lists 12 registries that accept artifacts. It says nothing about referrers API support for 11 of them (only 'summ'). It is evidence for storing artifacts, not for the declaration-as-referrer model.
  - Checked: Fetched oras.land/docs/compatible_oci_registries.
- *partly* · npm registry keywords · claim: Keyword matching is exact and case-sensitive, so a 44-character SAID works verbatim as a keyword.
  - Correction: Case, hyphen, underscore and dot are each confirmed. A 44-character mixed-case string is still untested: I scanned 450 recent packages and found no such keyword to probe. The documentation only says the qualifier shows results 'that have `batman` in the keywords', so exactness remains undocumented behaviour.
  - Checked: Went further than result counts: pulled up to 250 hits each for keywords:TypeScript, JSON-LD, node.js, typescrip and react_component and checked every hit's keyword array. Every hit carried the exact string, in the exact case; 'typescrip' returned 6 packages with that literal keyword, so there is no prefix matching.
- *partly* · npm registry keywords · claim: The lookup is a stable, documented, unauthenticated, free service and nothing about the use is restricted.
  - Correction: Two things the report does not mention. First, I received HTTP 429 after ten back-to-back unauthenticated searches from one address, so clients must pace themselves. Second, the terms (last updated 2022-03-10) list as unacceptable content: 'Using the Public Registry as a general purpose database is not allowed', and packages that are 'a text document uploaded directly to the registry'; 'Administrators at npm reserve the right to delete content hosted on the npm Services that they deem unacceptable'. One keyword per declared digest is close to that line.
  - Checked: Read docs/REGISTRY-API.md (size max 250 and the keywords qualifier are documented), ran the queries, and read npm's Open Source Terms directly.
- *partly* · npm registry keywords · claim: packages.ecosyste.ms is a second, independently operated index answering the same keyword query.
  - Correction: It is a separate operator but a downstream copy of npm, not an independent source: if npm delists or stops indexing, it has nothing new. Its operator and funding were not verified. It also serves a dependents list (see missed mechanisms).
  - Checked: Called /api/v1/keywords/&lt;kw&gt;: it answers, treats TypeScript and typescript as different keywords, and already had a package first published 20 minutes earlier; a second fresh package's keyword returned nothing yet.
- *partly* · GitHub and git · claim: Code search finds 415 hits for the vLEI QVI schema SAID; it needs sign-in (403 without), is capped and not exhaustive; most hits were copies of the same fixture in forks and test data.
  - Correction: Unauthenticated returns 401, not 403. The web documentation says files over 350 KiB are excluded and results are limited to 100. The fork statement is wrong for what I saw: the first 100 hits span 37 repositories and 15 owners and none is a fork; many are real users of the schema (GLEIF verifier and tooling repositories). So code search is a better best-effort signal than the report allows, while still a mention and not a declaration.
  - Checked: Ran the search authenticated (415 hits, 10 requests per minute limit shown by the rate-limit endpoint), unauthenticated, and read the documentation page.
- *partly* · deps.dev · claim: Hash query accepts only MD5, SHA1, SHA256, SHA512 and matches only whole release artifacts, so it cannot say who declares H; the dependents call returns counts only.
  - Correction: The algorithm and dependents statements hold. The granularity statement is too strong for Maven, where one release artifact can be a single file: my SHA256 query returned Maven coordinates, and Maven Central's own checksum search returned 454 artifacts for the SHA-1 of the empty file. See missed mechanisms.
  - Checked: Queried a blake3 type ('invalid hash type', 404), the dependents call (397 / 57 / 340 counts only) and a SHA256 query for the empty file.
- *partly* · Family summary · claim: Only three mechanisms in the family answer 'who else declares H' natively across unrelated publishers (Rekor v1 search, npm keywords, GitHub code search).
  - Correction: There is a fourth: Maven Central (Sonatype) answers 'which coordinates ship these exact bytes' by SHA-1 across publishers, and deps.dev (Google) answers the same by SHA256. It does not change the conclusion (sha family only, heavy publishing, no role), but it is a true by-content lookup the report missed.
  - Checked: Probed Maven Central's checksum search and deps.dev's hash query for a single-file artifact.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (supply-chain)

- **IETF SCITT (RFC 9943, 'An Architecture for Trustworthy and Transparent Digital Supply Chains', June 2026, Standards Track).** It is the standards-body form of exactly what Rekor is being used for off-label here: a signed statement by an issuer about a subject, registered with a transparency service that returns a receipt. If any log-based design is chosen, this is the envelope with a future, not a Sigstore-specific entry. It does not change the conclusion: the architecture says discovery is 'out of scope', and the companion API draft (draft-ietf-scitt-scrapi-11, with the RFC Editor) defines only register and fetch-by-entry-id, with no query by subject or issuer. I did not look for a free public instance. Source: https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9943.html ; https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-scitt-scrapi/
- **Maven Central checksum search, with deps.dev as a second index.** A true by-content, cross-publisher lookup run by two unrelated operators (Sonatype and Google), in an ecosystem whose namespaces are tied to domains. Searching the SHA-1 of the empty file returned 454 artifacts from unrelated publishers; deps.dev returned Maven coordinates for the SHA256. It shows the report's 'only three' count is short by one. It does not rescue the family: sha family only, 'declaring' means republishing the same bytes, there is no role, and publishing to Maven Central is the heaviest path in the family. Source: https://search.maven.org/solrsearch/select?q=1:%22da39a3ee5e6b4b0d3255bfef95601890afd80709%22&wt=json ; https://docs.deps.dev/api/v3/
- **Dependency edges as the declaration (dependents listings).** The supply chain's own reverse edge is 'who depends on X'. If a definition is itself a tiny package, 'relies on H' becomes a real dependency and the reverse lookup is a dependents list, which is ordinary package use, not keyword-as-database. The report dismissed this on deps.dev alone (counts only), but packages.ecosyste.ms serves a dependents list by API (verified: /registries/npmjs.org/packages/sigstore/dependent_packages responded), and npm's website and GitHub's 'Used by' show the same to humans (not re-verified). Costs: npm package names must be lowercase so the SAID needs re-encoding, the listing API belongs to a small third party, and it is still npm underneath. Source: https://packages.ecosyste.ms/api/v1/registries/npmjs.org/packages/sigstore/dependent_packages
- **Go module ecosystem (index.golang.org, sum.golang.org, pkg.go.dev).** The only package ecosystem in the family with no publisher account: a module is a repository named by its own domain or forge path, and the first fetch through the public proxy puts it in a public feed (index.golang.org, verified live) and a public checksum log (sum.golang.org, 71,021,095 records, verified live). That is the closest thing here to 'publish from your own place and generic infrastructure picks it up', and pkg.go.dev shows importers (not verified). It is run by Google alone, hashes with SHA-256, and has no query by content hash, so it informs the design more than it changes the verdict. Source: https://index.golang.org/index?limit=3 ; https://sum.golang.org/latest
- **Rekor's BigQuery mirror.** A way to query the log that does not depend on the deprecated search endpoint. Announced 2025-08-15 by Google's open source security team as a public dataset for researchers to 'gather aggregate data'. It needs a Google Cloud account, its freshness and columns are not stated in the announcement, and it is positioned for research, not per-artifact lookup. It softens the 'search disappears' risk slightly; it is not something to build a product on. Source: https://blog.sigstore.dev/rekor-bigquery-dataset/
- **Sigsum transparency logs.** Closer to the KERI direction than Fulcio's login identities: a leaf is a checksum, an Ed25519 signature and a key hash, with no OIDC and no email. But leaves are read by position range, so finding everyone who signed a given checksum needs a monitor that reads the whole log, which is an index someone runs. From a search summary of the project's documentation only; not probed. Source: https://git.sigsum.org/log-go/tree/README.md
- **The official MCP Registry, as counter-evidence.** The report cites 87,668 packages tagged 'mcp' as proof that npm keywords are how agent tooling is found. The same community nevertheless built and runs a dedicated registry (registry.modelcontextprotocol.io answered live with server records). That is evidence that keyword tagging on a package registry was not judged sufficient for discovery, which weakens the best pick's adoption argument. Source: https://registry.modelcontextprotocol.io/v0/servers?limit=1

### What the researcher could not verify (supply-chain)

- The WebFetch tool summarises pages and caps quotations at roughly 125 characters; quotes marked verbatim from blog and documentation pages passed through that summariser. Text from Rekor, rekor-tiles, protobuf-specs and OCI sources, and all API results, were read directly with curl and are exact.
- Probe timestamps: server Date headers read 2026-11-09 02:56 to 03:10 GMT, so figures labelled 2026-11-09 are UTC.
- No official rate limit for the public Rekor instance was found; only the 99.5% availability SLO and the 100KB attestation limit are documented.
- No stated retention policy for inactive Rekor v1 shards or frozen Rekor v2 shards was found. Observed only: v1 still lists its two inactive shards (4,163,431 and 117,740,831 entries).
- Whether Rekor v1 would accept a self-signed certificate carrying an AID in its SAN, and whether a hashedrekord over a short canonical string is accepted as described, were not tested because the task was read-only. The acceptance of self-managed public keys is from the schema and client documentation, not from a write.
- Whether the Rekor v1 search index is complete for any given hash is unknown; its own description says results may be incomplete.
- The Sigstore BigQuery mirror of Rekor exists per the 2025-08-15 announcement, but its freshness, schema and query cost were not verified.
- npm: whether a newly published package with no downloads appears promptly under a keyword query, any limit on keyword count or length, and whether a 44-character SAID containing '-' and '_' is indexed exactly, were not tested (no publishing allowed). Exact and case-sensitive matching is inferred from differing result counts for existing keywords.
- ecosyste.ms: only confirmed that the keyword endpoint exists and returns npm and PyPI packages; exact or case-sensitive handling of SAID-like keywords and its operator's funding were not verified.
- Docker Hub referrers support rests on one anonymous probe (HTTP 200 with an empty index on library/alpine), and GHCR's lack of it on one probe (HTTP 404 on homebrew/core/hello) plus third-party compatibility tables; neither vendor's documentation was found stating it.
- Whether any public registry accepts a blake3 digest on push was not tested. GHCR returned 400 DIGEST_INVALID on a blake3 blob read; Docker Hub returned BLOB_UNKNOWN, which is inconclusive.
- Docker Hub free-plan terms and pull limits come from secondary sources that disagree with each other.
- ORAS's CNCF level (Sandbox, accepted 2021-07-13) and the support of ECR, ACR, Quay, Harbor and zot for the referrers API come from search summaries and third-party tables, not vendor pages fetched verbatim.
- Software Heritage's documentation site returned an Anubis access-denied page; the statements about the provenance endpoint needing special permission and the 120 and 1,200 requests-per-hour limits come from a search summary, backed by a live 401 and a live X-Ratelimit-Limit: 120 header. Lookup of contents by sha256 or other hashes was not probed.
- Registry-wide npm provenance adoption and current PyPI attestation counts were not found; the figures given are small third-party samples and PyPI numbers from 2024 and 2025 via search summaries.
- Sigstore's OpenSSF graduation date (March 2024), in-toto's CNCF graduation (2025-04-23), ISO/IEC 18670:2025 and the Octoverse 2025 figures were taken from search summaries, not fetched verbatim.
- Which digest algorithms GitHub's attestation API accepts in subject_digest is not stated on the page fetched (examples show sha256 and sha512).
- The claim that npm, Inc. is a GitHub (Microsoft) subsidiary and that deps.dev is run by Google's open source security team are from prior knowledge, not re-fetched.
- No public verifiable-index service over Rekor v2 was found; transparency-dev's VIndex incubator code was pushed on 2026-11-04 and names Sigstore as a target log, but no deployment was located.

<a id="backlink-percolation"></a>

## Backlinks and percolation

**Researcher's summary.** This family answers "who refers to X" in only two ways: ask X's source (Webmention, Pingback, an LDN inbox, ActivityPub likes/shares/replies collections) or walk outbound links from what you already know (blogrolls, peer lists, gossip). None of its members offers a query keyed by an arbitrary digest that existing third-party nodes answer. The widely deployed members lack the lookup: ActivityPub (FediDB on 2026-11-09: 14,496,673 accounts, 40,808 servers, 1,169,821 monthly active) keeps backlinks at the origin server, and Mastodon documents for likes and shares that "The actual activities are not exposed by Mastodon at this time"; hashtag timelines are per-instance views. The member with the right semantics, Webmention (W3C Recommendation, 12 January 2017), is tiny: the WordPress plugin shows "900+" active installations, BuiltWith lists roughly 2,000 live sites on webmention.io (secondary figure), and I found no AI-agent tooling that reads it. Webmention does fit technically, because the spec verifies JSON sources by exact property-value match of the target URL, so an adopter's catalog can be the source; but the target is a URL, not a digest, the spec defines no read API, and nothing is signed. Standards status is stable, not growing: the W3C Social Web Working Group was rechartered 15 January 2026 to 31 January 2028 to maintain ActivityPub, Activity Streams, LDN, Micropub, Webmention and WebSub; Threads' fediverse integration is "on maintenance mode" (Fediverse Report, 19 December 2025); Manyverse, the main SSB client, ended development in July 2024. The reviewer's percolating-catalog pattern is the best fit in the family for values 1, 3, 4 and 5: static JSON only, lookup is a local walk from one introduction, and it matches what the KERI v1.1 spec calls percolated discovery, with OOBIs defined for "a given AID or SAID". Its limits are structural and visible in every deployed analogue: BitTorrent PEX works only "once peers have bootstrapped via other mechanisms such as DHT or Tracker", Radicle ships two team-run bootstrap nodes, and a 2004 FOAF crawl study found that unconnected components "will never be discovered by traversal of any kind". On the 2000 web graph a directed path existed for only 24% of page pairs while over 90% of pages sat in one weakly connected component, so the backlink (the ping) that makes edges two-way is what keeps a walk from dying out. A walk therefore answers "who, among those reachable from my introductions, declares H" and cannot prove a negative; closing islands needs a digest-keyed rendezvous from outside this family. The pattern fails value 2 as a format: zero adopters and no standards body, with only its transport (HTTPS and well-known JSON) being universal.

**Researcher's best pick.** Percolating catalogs with a Webmention-style ping to the definition's source, signed by AID as an additive step. It is the best in this family, but only a partial answer: it meets values 1, 3, 4 and 5, fails value 2 (no existing adoption), and needs a digest-keyed rendezvous from another family to close the island problem.

**Researcher's strongest objection.** It is a new private convention with no adopters and no standards body, so it does not meet "very widely adopted", and nothing else in this family does either for this job: the widely deployed members (ActivityPub, feeds, schema.org) have no digest-keyed reverse lookup, and the one with the right semantics, Webmention, has on the order of a thousand to a few thousand deployments nine years after becoming a W3C Recommendation, with no AI tooling reading it. Worse, a walk cannot prove a negative. The question that matters for interoperation is "is anyone else using H?", and the pattern answers only for catalogs reachable from the reader's introductions; in an early network of a few dozen publishers, disconnected groups are the expected case, and the reader has no signal that an island exists. Every deployed percolation system I checked solved this with something outside percolation: BitTorrent with a tracker or DHT, Radicle with bootstrap nodes, FOAF and blogroll crawlers with seed lists, the fediverse with statistics crawlers. The likely outcome is that someone builds a crawler and index anyway, as happened for APIs.json and for linked-data vocabularies, which is the aggregator the maintainer set out to avoid, even if here it would be an optional cache anyone can rebuild.

**Skeptic's overall judgement.** The report is accurate where it quotes: of roughly thirty weight-bearing claims I re-opened, nearly all came back verbatim, including the adoption figures (FediDB, both WordPress plugin counts, the W3C dates and charter, the KERI text). Its errors are of emphasis. It scores its best pick too kindly: "static JSON only" and "optionally one small function" understate that the reverse lookup exists only where a source runs a ping receiver, "KERI's discovery model applied to definitions" covers fetching by SAID but not listing adopters, and the hopeful fallback of searching the web for the digest failed the one time I tried it. It also missed that the pattern has a finished standards-track twin (OpenID Federation 1.0, final February 2026) and that two adjacent networks (Nostr relays, AT Protocol's backlink index) already answer value-keyed queries on shared infrastructure. The maintainer should trust this family for two things only: the catalog as the publishing and human-readable reference format, and the local walk as a verifier of claims found by other means. He should not trust it as the reverse lookup or as the route to wide adoption; that has to come from another family, or he should accept plainly that he is running the index.

**Skeptic: does the reverse lookup work with nothing purpose-built?**

No. For the best pick (percolating catalogs with a ping to the source), "given digest H, list everyone who declares H" does not work with nothing purpose-built, and for a true stranger it does not start.

Step 0, a stranger holds only H. Nothing in the pattern turns a digest into a URL. I tested the obvious free fallback, an exact-phrase web search for a real KERI SAID that several parties publish, and it returned nothing relevant. The stranger needs an introduction (a catalog URL) from outside the system.

Step 1, fetch the introduced catalog. Answered by that publisher's own static host (S3, GitHub Pages or similar), run by the publisher. Free and as durable as that publisher.

Step 2, climb the "from" links toward the source. Each hop is another publisher's static host. Any dead hop ends the climb.

Step 3, read the source's list of adopters for H. This list exists only if adopters sent pings and the source ran a receiver that fetched each adopter's catalog, stored the result and rebuilt its own catalog. That receiver is a service the source must write and keep running. The only existing hosted receiver is webmention.io: operated by one person (Aaron Parecki), free, no stated guarantees, keyed by a target URL on a signed-in domain, not by a digest, and its verifier (XRay) searches unrecognised documents only as HTML, so a JSON catalog as the source would most likely be rejected.

Step 4, fetch each listed adopter and count it only if its own catalog declares H. Publishers' static hosts again.

Step 5, union across sources. If H was served from several origins, or adopters got it from intermediaries that take no pings, the lists are disjoint and nothing joins them.

What must be built: the catalog format, the walker, the ping sender and a ping receiver per source. What must be run: one receiver per source, indefinitely. In the early network the maintainer authors nearly every definition, so his catalog plus his receiver is the index for nearly every H; it is the draft's DynamoDB index fed by push instead of crawl, smaller and rebuildable but still something somebody runs. The answer it gives is "who pinged this source and is still reachable", never "everyone". For the stranger case, for joining groups that never linked, and for enumeration, the honest answer is that somebody must run an index or a generic one must be borrowed from outside this family.

**Skeptic's strongest objection.**

The family's only working reverse lookup is "ask the source", and for content-addressed definitions that puts the weight exactly where the maintainer did not want it. The whole point of a digest is that any holder can serve the bytes and no origin is special; the percolating pattern then reintroduces a special origin per definition, because adopters are listed only where a source accepted their pings. That source is a single point of failure for the list (when it is offline or stops rebuilding, later adopters are invisible and the remaining catalogs fall apart into leaves that know only their parent), it must run a public endpoint that fetches arbitrary URLs, and during the years that matter it is one party: the maintainer. So the design keeps an index, renames it a catalog, and adds a new unadopted format on top. On the maintainer's second value it scores nothing: every member that has the right semantics is tiny or dormant (Webmention at about 900 plugin installs nine years after Recommendation, Semantic Pingback abandoned, SSB's main client ended), every member that is large (ActivityPub at 1.17 million monthly active, structured data on 52% of crawled pages) has no digest-keyed lookup, and none of it is read by agent tooling. Finally the answer cannot be trusted in the direction that matters: a walk cannot distinguish "nobody else uses H" from "nobody I can reach uses H", and the precedents the report itself cites (FOAF, blogrolls, APIs.json, peer exchange, Radicle) all ended with a seeded crawler, a tracker or bootstrap nodes.

### How mad-discovery would look on this family (backlink-percolation)

What replaces the draft: in /Users/seriouscoderone/code/bpm/mad-discovery/MAD-DISCOVERY-SPEC.md, section 5 (crawler Lambda, DynamoDB index, query API, seed table) is deleted. Section 3's CIDv1 hash becomes the workspace SAID. Section 4's publisher stays, with a different catalog.

What is written where (all static, on the publisher's own host):
1. /mad/defs/&lt;SAID&gt;.json: the definition bytes, immutable.
2. /.well-known/mad/catalog.json: { self, publisher (domain now, AID later), entries: [ { d: &lt;SAID&gt;, claim: authors | issues | relies-on | implements | endorses, url: &lt;where I serve the bytes&gt;, title, from: &lt;catalog I got it from&gt;, peers: [&lt;catalogs I have verified declaring this SAID&gt;] } ], peers: [&lt;catalogs I know in general&gt;] }. The title and description make each entry a human-readable reference, so a reference page is a local rendering of the walk, not a site someone curates.
3. Optional: a Link header on each definition URL advertising a ping endpoint.

The query "who else declares H": a TypeScript library and CLI function, whoDeclares(H, introductions), run on the reader's machine. It fetches each introduced catalog, takes its entry for H, queues that entry's "from" and "peers" plus the catalog-level peers (bounded depth, visited set, per-host limits), and records a party only when that party's own catalog declares H and the bytes it serves re-derive H. Output: the list of (publisher, claim, verified) plus the list of catalogs that could not be reached. The same function can be exposed to agents as a tool. Enumeration is the same walk without the filter on H.

The ping: after adding H to its catalog, the adopter POSTs source=&lt;its catalog URL&gt;&target=&lt;the source's URL for H&gt;. The source fetches the catalog, checks it contains that URL as a property value (the Webmention JSON rule) under an entry for H, and adds the adopter to "peers" for H on its next build. This makes links two-way, which is what stops a walk from dying out.

What the maintainer still runs: static hosting for his own catalog (S3 and CloudFront, or any static host), and optionally one small function that receives pings and appends to a file. No crawler, no index, no seed table; his own catalog's peer list is an ordinary catalog like anyone's.

KERI step, additive: the catalog gets its own SAID and the publisher's AID, is signed with a CESR attachment, and the key event log is served at /.well-known/keri/oobi/&lt;AID&gt;. Peer links become OOBIs. The walker then counts a claim only if the signature verifies against the AID's current keys; the domain becomes a location, not the identity.

What this does not give: an answer that covers groups the reader has no link to. If that matters, add one digest-keyed lookup from another family as a second source of introductions; the walk and the verification stay the same.

### Mechanisms (backlink-percolation)

#### W3C Webmention (with predecessors Pingback and Trackback)

A W3C Recommendation: the linking page (source) form-POSTs source=...&target=... to an endpoint the target advertises through a Link header or &lt;link rel="webmention"&gt;; the receiver fetches the source and confirms it contains the exact target URL. Pingback (2002, XML-RPC pingback.ping(sourceURI, targetURI), verification only MAY) and Trackback (manual, sends an excerpt) are the older forms that WordPress still ships.

- **Publish.** The adopter puts the origin's URL for the definition into its own document (for example a catalog entry whose "url" is https://origin.example/mad/defs/&lt;H&gt;), then POSTs source=&lt;its catalog URL&gt;&target=&lt;that URL&gt; to the origin's endpoint. The adopter hosts a static file; the origin hosts the endpoint (self-run, or the hosted webmention.io service made by Aaron Parecki). Cost to the adopter: one HTTP POST.
- **Fetch by digest.** Not addressed. Webmention moves URLs, not bytes. Bytes come from a GET of the target URL and are verified by recomputing the SAID locally.
- **Reverse lookup.** Native, but only as "ask X's source". Step 1: know the origin URL for H. Step 2: the origin's receiver has stored the verified sources that pinged it. Step 3: read them, but the spec defines no read API (it says only that the receiver "MAY publish content from the source page on the target page"); webmention.io offers a public GET /api/mentions.jf2?target=&lt;URL&gt;. The key is a target URL at one origin, not a digest. If five origins host H there are five disjoint lists, adopters who never pinged are invisible, and no existing node answers a query for an arbitrary digest.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new publisher becomes findable only by pinging the origin of each definition it adopts. A new author with no adopters and no inbound link is invisible.
- **Identity.** Nothing beyond fetching the source over HTTPS, which proves control of a URL. There are no signatures. A KERI-signed catalog can be the source document, with the receiver checking the signature in addition to the link; that is additive.
- **Digest fit.** The target must be a URL. A 44-character SAID rides as a URL path segment (Base64url is URL-safe) and as a JSON property value; the spec says that in JSON "the receiver should look for properties whose values are an exact match for the URL". No hash function is imposed.
- **Governance.** W3C Recommendation, unchanged since 2017. It is in the maintenance scope of the Social Web Working Group chartered 15 January 2026 to 31 January 2028, with no expected completion date listed for a revision. Pingback 1.0 is a 2002 document by Stuart Langridge and Ian Hickson with no standards body.
- **Publisher cost.** Sender: none beyond one POST. Receiver: a small function plus storage (a Lambda function URL with near-zero idle cost), or the hosted webmention.io.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Small and stale: @remy/webmention 1.5.0 (sender, MIT, last release 2023-11-06); webmention-handler 0.0.9 (TypeScript receiver, MIT, last release 2023-11-11); send-webmention 2.0.0 (last release 2018-07-13). The protocol is small enough to write by hand.
- **Failure modes.** Spam: the spec offers only "Receivers MAY moderate Webmentions before publishing them", and Vouch never spread. Forced fetches: "a sender can cause a receiver to make GET requests to arbitrary URLs, opening up a potential DoS vector"; Pingback was abused exactly this way in 2014. Liveness: a ping to an offline origin is lost unless the sender retries, and a dead origin takes its backlink list with it. Link rot of sources is handled only if senders re-send or return 410. Centralization: many sites delegate to webmention.io, run by one person. Privacy: a ping tells the origin who adopted.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. No shared index is needed, but every origin must run or rent a receiver, store the mentions and expose the list in some agreed form, and the union across origins still needs a walk or a crawl.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "W3C Recommendation 12 January 2017"; abstract: "Webmention is a simple way to notify any URL when you mention it on your site." (as of 2017-01-12). Source: https://www.w3.org/TR/webmention/
  - WordPress Webmention plugin: active installations "900+", version 5.11.0, last modified October 21, 2026. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://wordpress.org/plugins/webmention/
  - BuiltWith lists about 2,019 live sites using webmention.io and 1,330 historical (from a search snippet; the BuiltWith page itself was blocked by a human test). (as of 2025-08). Source: https://trends.builtwith.com/framework/Webmention.io
  - The IndieWeb wiki page for Webmention gives no site counts; it lists implementations (Known, WordPress, Drupal, Kirby, Micro.blog, webmention.io, Bridgy, Telegraph). Page last edited 16 September 2026. (as of 2026-09-16). Source: https://indieweb.org/Webmention
  - The anti-spam extension Vouch lists four implementations; page last edited 30 December 2024. (as of 2024-12-30). Source: https://indieweb.org/Vouch
  - WordPress, which ships Pingback, "is used by 58.1% of all the websites whose content management system we know. This is 39.4% of all websites." (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://w3techs.com/technologies/details/cm-wordpress
  - Pingback abuse: in March 2014 Sucuri reported more than 162,000 legitimate WordPress sites used as reflectors in one DDoS through XML-RPC pingbacks (press coverage of Sucuri's post). (as of 2014-03). Source: https://www.computerweekly.com/news/2240215998/More-the-162000-WordPress-sites-used-in-DDoS-attack
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Only no_aggregator changes, 4 to 3. The spec has no read side, so every origin must run a receiver and invent a way to publish its list, or rent webmention.io, which one person runs with no stated guarantees and which would most likely reject a bare JSON catalog as a source. Adoption (900 plugin installs confirmed) and the other scores stand.

#### ActivityPub (Announce, Like, replies; Fediverse Enhancement Proposals)

The W3C federation protocol behind Mastodon and similar servers: actors with inboxes and outboxes exchange Activity Streams 2.0 activities. An object may have likes, shares and replies collections held by the server that hosts the object.

- **Publish.** Either (a) from an existing fediverse account, post a public Note that links the definition URL and carries a hashtag; or (b) run your own actor and deliver Announce{object: &lt;definition id&gt;} or a reply (inReplyTo: &lt;definition id&gt;) to the origin actor's inbox. Route (b) needs the definition to be an ActivityPub object on an always-on origin server, and an always-on inbox for the adopter.
- **Fetch by digest.** None native. Object ids are HTTPS URLs dereferenced at the origin. The draft FEP-ef61 adds hashlinks but states "The digest MUST be computed using the SHA-256 algorithm."
- **Reverse lookup.** On paper it is "ask the origin": the spec says the origin SHOULD add a received Like or Announce to the object's likes or shares collection, and Activity Streams defines replies as "a Collection containing objects considered to be responses to this object". In practice Mastodon exposes only totals for likes and shares ("The actual activities are not exposed by Mastodon at this time"), and replies are paged from the origin. A hashtag query (GET /api/v1/timelines/tag/:hashtag) returns only what that one instance has seen. A complete answer needs either your own origin server exposing full collections, which is then the Webmention model with far more machinery, or a crawler or relay.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new actor is seen only by servers whose users follow it or to which it delivers. Replying to or mentioning the origin actor delivers to the origin's inbox. Relays exist but are operator-run fan-out services.
- **Identity.** HTTP Signatures made with actor keys that the server holds, bound to an HTTPS actor URL, so identity is a domain. FEP-8b32 (Object Integrity Proofs, DRAFT since 2022-11-12) adds DataIntegrityProof with eddsa-jcs-2022 recommended and a verificationMethod that "can be an HTTP(S) URI or a DID URL". FEP-ef61 (DRAFT since 2023-12-06) uses ap://did:key:... ids and says implementers "MUST support the [did:key] method". Neither mentions KERI; a CESR-signed endorsement could ride as an extension property that other servers ignore.
- **Digest fit.** Ids are URLs, so the digest goes in a URL path or an extension property. As a hashtag it breaks: "Mastodon will also normalize hashtags to be case-insensitive lowercase strings", which destroys Base64url, so the raw 32 bytes would have to be re-encoded as hex (64 characters) or lowercase base32 (52 characters).
- **Governance.** W3C Recommendation with an active working group through January 2028. The enhancement proposals are community documents on Codeberg; all four I read (8b32, ef61, 7888, 044f) are DRAFT.
- **Publisher cost.** Route (a) is free with an existing account. Route (b) needs an always-on or serverless inbox with signatures, WebFinger, queues and storage, which is a real service to operate.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Fedify (@fedify/fedify 2.4.5, MIT; Node 22+, Deno 2+, Bun 1.1+; site also lists Cloudflare Workers). It implements HTTP Signatures, RFC 9421, FEP-8b32, WebFinger and NodeInfo, and states it is backed by a Sovereign Tech investment.
- **Failure modes.** Origin dependence: the backlink collections live on the origin server, so an offline origin answers nothing. Partial views: each instance knows only what was delivered to it. Moderation and blocklists can silently partition the network into islands. Secure mode requires signed fetches. Spam is handled by per-server filtering. Large instances are de facto chokepoints. All claims are public posts.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. Origin-held collections work only if every definition origin runs a server that exposes them; with the servers people already run, the answer needs a crawler or indexer.
- **Adoption evidence.**
