  - FediDB API: total_users 14,496,673; total_instances 40,808; monthly_active_users 1,169,821; last_updated_at 2026-11-09T02:45:02+00:00. (as of 2026-11-09). Source: https://api.fedidb.org/v1/stats
  - "W3C Recommendation 23 January 2018". (as of 2018-01-23). Source: https://www.w3.org/TR/activitypub/
  - Social Web Working Group charter: start "15 January 2026", end "31 January 2028", chair Darius Kazemi; ActivityPub, Activity Streams and Activity Vocabulary revisions expected Q3 2026 (I could not confirm publication). (as of 2026-01-15). Source: https://www.w3.org/groups/wg/social/charters/active
  - WordPress ActivityPub plugin by Automattic: active installations "6,000+", version 9.4.3, last modified November 5, 2026. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://wordpress.org/plugins/activitypub/
  - Fediverse Report #147: "Threads' integration with the fediverse is on maintenance mode, signalling the end to a tumultuous story". (as of 2025-12-19). Source: https://connectedplaces.online/?p=4765
  - Ghost 6.0 shipped ActivityPub support in August 2025 (press coverage; no count of federated Ghost sites found). (as of 2025-08-05). Source: https://techcrunch.com/2025/08/05/substack-rival-ghost-connects-to-the-open-social-web-with-its-latest-public-release
  - "Mastodon 4.5 enables the feature by default" (fetching missing replies to a remote status), which shows that reply backlinks were incomplete without asking the origin. (as of 2025-10). Source: https://blog.joinmastodon.org/2025/10/mastodon-4-5-for-devs/
  - FEP-044f (consent-respecting quote posts): status DRAFT, received 2025-04-03; QuoteRequest answered by an Accept carrying a QuoteAuthorization stamp; implemented in Mastodon. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://codeberg.org/fediverse/fep/raw/branch/main/fep/044f/fep-044f.md
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Trajectory 3 to 2: the Threads claim is secondhand but uncontradicted, and the revision the working group scheduled for Q3 2026 is not on the W3C page in November 2026. Reverse lookup stays 2: Mastodon's client API does list boosters, favouriters and replies at the origin, which is better than the report said, but it is per origin, product-specific and needs the definition to be a post.

#### WebSub

A W3C Recommendation for push delivery: a publisher advertises a hub and a self URL, subscribers register a callback at the hub for that exact topic URL, and the hub pushes updates.

- **Publish.** The publisher adds Link headers with rel=hub and rel=self to its feed or catalog URL and notifies the hub when the content changes.
- **Fetch by digest.** None.
- **Reverse lookup.** None. The subscriber list is private state inside the hub and there is no query for it. The topic "MUST be the "self" URL found during the discovery step", so nobody can subscribe by digest or tag. It is a forward push channel, not a backlink mechanism.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** None. A subscriber must already know the topic URL.
- **Identity.** An optional shared secret between hub and subscriber; no publisher signature. A signed catalog could be the pushed content.
- **Digest fit.** Topics are URLs only.
- **Governance.** W3C Recommendation, republished in 2026 with a security fix; maintained by the Social Web Working Group.
- **Publisher cost.** Two Link headers and a ping to a hub; public hubs exist, or run one.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Not checked.
- **Failure modes.** The hub is a chokepoint and sees all subscriptions. Useful here only to push catalog changes faster to peers that already follow a publisher.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. WebSub cannot answer the reverse lookup at all, so something else has to; and the hub is itself an operated service.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "W3C Recommendation 02 June 2026"; the status section says the update adds mitigations for cross-site scripting risk to the Security Considerations. (The original Recommendation dates from January 2018, from memory.) (as of 2026-06-02). Source: https://www.w3.org/TR/websub/
  - Listed as a Recommendation maintained by the Social Web Working Group, with no expected completion date for further work. (as of 2026-01-15). Source: https://www.w3.org/groups/wg/social/charters/active
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. The 2026 republication and the self-URL rule were confirmed; it is a push channel with no reverse lookup.

#### Linked Data Notifications (and its COAR Notify profile)

A W3C Recommendation: a resource advertises an inbox with the link relation http://www.w3.org/ns/ldp#inbox; senders POST JSON-LD notifications to it; consumers GET the inbox and follow ldp:contains to each notification. COAR Notify profiles it so repositories and review services tell each other about reviews and endorsements of scholarly items.

- **Publish.** The adopter POSTs a JSON-LD notification (for example an Activity Streams Announce whose object is the definition URL and whose actor is the adopter) to the inbox discovered from the origin's definition URL. The origin hosts the inbox.
- **Fetch by digest.** None.
- **Reverse lookup.** "Ask X's source", with a standard read side that Webmention lacks: "Consumers retrieve the URIs of notifications in an Inbox through making a GET request on the Inbox URL". It is still one list per target at one origin, not keyed by digest, the inbox may be access-controlled, and the receiver need not check the claim (the spec notes "anything can be posted to an Inbox").
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Same as Webmention: a new publisher is found only after it notifies an origin.
- **Identity.** None specified; authentication is optional. Because the payload is arbitrary JSON-LD, the notification body can itself be a signed endorsement, so a KERI-signed document fits as the payload.
- **Digest fit.** Identifiers are IRIs; the digest can be a property value or part of a URL. No hash function is imposed.
- **Governance.** W3C Recommendation in the Social Web Working Group's maintenance scope. COAR Notify is governed by the Confederation of Open Access Repositories, on a grant that began 1 July 2022 for four years (from a search summary).
- **Publisher cost.** Sender: one POST of JSON-LD. Receiver: an inbox service with storage.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Not checked.
- **Failure modes.** No required verification of claims, so spam and false claims are the receiver's problem ("Receivers SHOULD use constraints to filter unwarranted notifications"). Same origin-liveness and privacy limits as Webmention. Deployment is concentrated in scholarly repositories.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. No shared index, but every origin must run an inbox server, and the union across origins needs a walk or crawl.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - "W3C Recommendation 2 May 2017". (as of 2017-05-02). Source: https://www.w3.org/TR/ldn/
  - COAR Notify describes itself as "a set of profiles, constraints and conventions around the use of W3C Linked Data Notifications (LDN)" and shows 16 implementations: Preprints.org, Apollo, bioRxiv, Dataverse, DASH, Digital.CSIC, DSpace, Episciences, EPrints, HAL, Kotahi, Nakala, PCI, PREreview, SciELO Preprints, Sciety. (as of 2026-11-05). Source: https://coar-notify.net/
  - Current version "COAR Notify Protocol 1.0.1". (as of 2026-11-05). Source: https://coar-notify.net/specification/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** no_aggregator 4 to 3 for the same reason as Webmention: every origin runs an inbox server. Reverse lookup stays 2: the GET on the inbox is standard, but the spec lets receivers show a consumer only what it may access and requires no verification of claims.

#### Feeds with OPML blogrolls, and webrings

Peer-link discovery from the blogging web: a site's feed lists its items, an OPML file (linked with a proposed rel="blogroll") lists the feeds it recommends, and a webring is a member list kept by a ring operator.

- **Publish.** The publisher lists its definitions as feed items and lists peer feeds in an OPML file on its own site. Static files only.
- **Fetch by digest.** None; items carry URLs.
- **Reverse lookup.** None native. These are outbound links only, so "who lists H" means crawling every reachable feed. The Blogroll Network Map does exactly that: it "crawls web feeds and OPML recommendations starting from several hard-coded seed URLs", with new blogs added "after the next crawl (currently a manual, weekly process)". A webring answers membership from one operator's list, which is a small aggregator.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Get onto someone's blogroll or join a ring; otherwise invisible.
- **Identity.** Domain only.
- **Digest fit.** Any string fits in an item id or text.
- **Governance.** RSS, Atom and OPML are stable de facto formats; the blogroll link relation is an informal proposal.
- **Publisher cost.** Static files; effectively zero.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Feed and OPML parsers are plentiful on npm (specific packages not checked).
- **Failure modes.** Forward links only, so a walk from a random start often dies out; stale blogrolls; no claim semantics; no verification.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. Reverse lookup exists only if someone crawls from seeds and indexes, or a ring operator keeps the list.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Blogroll Network Map: "Explore 14,251 feeds"; "Last refreshed: 8 November 2026 at 16:17 (UTC)". (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://blogroll-network.alexsci.com/
  - rel="blogroll" is a 2024 proposal by Dave Winer with a sample at opml.org/blogroll.opml; Micro.blog added blogroll support in March 2024. No formal specification status found. (as of 2024-03-11). Source: https://manton.org/2024/03/11/recommendations-and-blogrolls.html
  - The IndieWeb webring calls itself a "proof-of-concept webring" and states no member count. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://xn--sr8hvo.ws/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Unchanged. The one deployed reverse view (Blogroll Network Map) was confirmed to be a seeded crawler refreshed by hand.

#### Secure Scuttlebutt and other gossip protocols (Radicle inventory gossip, BitTorrent peer exchange)

Each identity is a key pair with a signed append-only feed; peers replicate the feeds of those they follow, out to a few hops, and answer questions from their local copy. Radicle gossips which node hosts which repository; BitTorrent peer exchange lets peers in a swarm tell each other about other peers for the same infohash.

- **Publish.** Append a signed message to your own feed that names H in its content. Peers that follow you replicate it. You must run a node and connect to a pub, a room or a local peer.
- **Fetch by digest.** SSB blobs are fetched from any peer that has them, but "Blobs are immutable because their ID contains a hash of their contents" and that hash is sha256, so the SAID would not be the blob id.
- **Reverse lookup.** A local index over the feeds you have replicated: complete only inside your replication horizon ("Messages from feeds 3 hops out are replicated"), with no global query. Radicle is closer to a native lookup: "Inventory Announcements are used for broadcasting repository inventories and constructing the routing table", which maps repository ids to nodes. Peer exchange lists peers per infohash but only "once peers have bootstrapped via other mechanisms such as DHT or Tracker announces."
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** SSB: invite codes ("the most common way for new users to get started"), pubs, rooms, or UDP broadcast on a local network. Radicle: "pre-configured with two bootstrap nodes" run by the Radicle team. BitTorrent: a tracker or the DHT.
- **Identity.** Self-held Ed25519 keys and signed logs, the nearest thing to KERI in this family, but with no key rotation or pre-rotation and its own identifier encoding. A KERI AID does not map onto an SSB or Radicle identity; a signed KERI document could only ride as message content.
- **Digest fit.** SSB imposes sha256 with its own base64 form for message and blob ids; an arbitrary string fits only inside message content. Radicle uses git object ids. BitTorrent infohashes are SHA-1 or SHA-256.
- **Governance.** SSB is a community protocol with no standards body and a stalled reference client. Radicle is run by its own team. BitTorrent enhancement proposals are stable.
- **Publisher cost.** Run a node with a persistent key and storage; find a pub or bootstrap peer. Not serverless-friendly.
- **TypeScript tooling.** The SSB stack is JavaScript, but its maintenance state was not checked beyond the Manyverse announcement. Radicle is Rust.
- **Failure modes.** Reach limited to the follow graph; slow convergence; new users cannot join without an invite or pub; logs are permanent and public; small and shrinking community for SSB; bootstrap nodes are chokepoints.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. No index, but every deployed gossip network depends on bootstrap nodes, pubs or a DHT, and on peers that stay online to replicate.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Manyverse, the main SSB mobile client, ended development: "Personally I will not do any more work on Manyverse." (as of 2024-07-03). Source: https://www.manyver.se/blog/2024-07-03
  - "Secure Scuttlebutt just turned 10 years now on May 11th." No user counts found. (as of 2024-07-03). Source: https://www.manyver.se/blog/2024-07-03
  - Radicle: "Radicle's reference implementation is pre-configured with two bootstrap nodes that are connected to if the address book is empty." No network size published in the guide. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://radicle.dev/guides/protocol
  - BitTorrent BEP 11: "Data exchanged via PEX messages should be considered untrusted and potentially malicious" and "a client should avoid taking all its connection candidates from a single PEX source." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://www.bittorrent.org/beps/bep_0011.html
- **Skeptic's re-score.** no_aggregator 4 to 3: every deployed example depends on operated bootstrap points (Radicle's two team-run nodes, pubs, trackers). Reverse lookup 3 to 2: none of the three accepts an arbitrary digest as a key from a stranger; SSB answers only inside a 3-hop horizon and Radicle only for its own repository ids. keri_fit 3 to 2: self-held keys, but no rotation, sha256-only ids and a stalled reference client.

#### schema.org and rel-based links that general crawlers follow (with VoID, APIs.json and FOAF as the catalog precedents)

Declarations embedded where existing crawlers already look: JSON-LD in HTML pages, link relations, or a well-known description file. VoID (datasets declare the vocabularies they use), APIs.json (providers describe their APIs) and FOAF files linked by rdfs:seeAlso are the direct precedents for "a catalog that says what I use".

- **Publish.** The adopter adds JSON-LD to an HTML page naming the definition URL and the digest string, or serves a description file such as /.well-known/void or /.well-known/apis.json. Static hosting only.
- **Fetch by digest.** None; URLs only.
- **Reverse lookup.** Not native. It works only if some crawler indexed the page: a general search engine queried for the exact 44-character string, Common Crawl's bulk data, or a domain crawler such as APIs.io. None offers a digest-keyed query with any completeness promise, and JSON at well-known paths is not reliably indexed by web search (my assessment, not measured).
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** Be crawled: inbound links, sitemaps, or submission to a crawler's seed list.
- **Identity.** Domain only.
- **Digest fit.** Any string. A unique 44-character token is a good exact-match search key in principle; how search engines tokenize Base64url with '-' and '_' was not tested.
- **Governance.** schema.org is run by its sponsor search companies; VoID is a 2011 W3C note; APIs.json is a community format at version 0.25.
- **Publisher cost.** A static page or file.
- **TypeScript tooling.** JSON-LD libraries exist on npm (not checked for this report); nothing is needed to publish.
- **Failure modes.** The reverse lookup is whatever a third party's crawler chose to index, with no way to tell missing from absent. In the VoID and APIs.json precedents the reverse lookup ended up at an aggregator (a vocabulary index, APIs.io), which is the outcome the maintainer wants to avoid.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** yes. A dependable answer needs a crawler that targets these declarations. Best-effort answers can come from existing general crawlers (Google, Bing, Common Crawl), which are generic and already operated, but they give no guarantee.
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - Web Data Commons, October 2024 Common Crawl (released 2025-01-10): 1,245,622,627 of 2,391,039,772 parsed URLs and 16,525,070 of 37,447,141 domains carry structured data (52.1% and 44.1%, derived). (as of 2024-10). Source: https://webdatacommons.org/structureddata/
  - VoID is a "W3C Interest Group Note 03 March 2011"; "Every value of void:vocabulary must be a URI that identifies a vocabulary or ontology that is used in the dataset"; it registers /.well-known/void. (as of 2011-03-03). Source: https://www.w3.org/TR/void/
  - APIs.json v0.25: "155 providers publish an authoritative APIs.json", indexing 473 APIs; APIs.io is described as an "APIs.json-powered API search engine". (as of 2026-11-07). Source: https://apisjson.org/
  - FOAF crawl study (URLs harvested 3 to 7 March 2004): a scutter "is generally bootstrapped with a list of known files", and unconnected components "will never be discovered by traversal of any kind." (as of 2004-03). Source: https://www.w3.org/2001/sw/Europe/events/foaf-galway/papers/fp/challenges_of_foaf_characterization/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** no_aggregator 3 to 2. The 3 rested on general crawlers giving best-effort answers; in my test an exact-phrase search for a real, multiply-published KERI SAID returned nothing relevant, so a usable answer needs a crawler aimed at these declarations. The adoption scores describe structured data in general (confirmed at 52% of crawled URLs), not anyone declaring protocol digests.

#### Percolating catalogs with an optional ping to the source (the reviewer's pattern)

Each publisher serves one static catalog that lists the digests it authored, the digests it adopts with the kind of claim, and for each digest the peer catalogs it knows that also declare it, including where it first got it. A reader starts from any one catalog and walks these links locally. Optionally an adopter pings the source so the source can list it. It is the KERI discovery model applied to definitions: the KERI v1.1 spec says an OOBI "provides a discovery mechanism that associates a given URI or URL with a given AID or SAID" and that "Percolation means that each discoverer, in turn, may share what it discovers with any subsequent discoverers."

- **Publish.** The party writes a JSON catalog on its own static host (for example /.well-known/mad/catalog.json): per entry the SAID H, the claim (authors, issues, relies on, implements, endorses), a URL where it serves the bytes, "from" (the catalog it got H from) and "peers" (other catalogs it has verified declaring H). Optionally it POSTs source=&lt;its catalog URL&gt;&target=&lt;the source's URL for H&gt; to the source. Hosting is any static site; cost is near zero.
- **Fetch by digest.** GET &lt;any listed peer&gt;/defs/&lt;H&gt; and recompute the SAID locally (Blake3-256 over the document with the digest field replaced by the placeholder). Because the bytes are content-addressed, any holder can serve them, so the first publisher being offline is not fatal while one known peer is up.
- **Reverse lookup.** A local breadth-first walk by the reader's own client. Step 1: fetch the introduced catalog. Step 2: collect its entry for H, its "from" and its "peers" for H. Step 3: fetch each of those catalogs and count a party only if its own catalog declares H (a third party's statement is a hint, never evidence). Step 4: repeat until no new catalogs appear or a budget is hit. With pings, one GET of the source's catalog returns every adopter that pinged. Nothing here is a query that existing nodes answer for an arbitrary digest: it is a crawl of H's neighbourhood using only the publishers' static hosting, and it is complete only within the set of catalogs connected to the reader's starting point.
- **Rendezvous for an unknown publisher.** A new adopter becomes findable when the source (or any peer already in the graph) lists it, which is what the ping is for. A new author with a new definition and no adopters is findable only through an introduction made outside the system. The pattern has no way to join two groups that never exchanged a link.
- **Identity.** First cut: the catalog is served from the party's domain, so TLS authenticates the claim. Additive step to KERI: give the catalog its own SAID and the publisher's AID, sign it with a CESR attachment, serve the key event log at /.well-known/keri/oobi/&lt;AID&gt;, turn each peer link into an OOBI (URL plus AID), and optionally anchor the catalog SAID in the key event log. The KERI spec's rule carries over directly: "an OOBI itself is not trusted but MUST be verified."
- **Digest fit.** Full fit. The digest is an opaque string in JSON and in a URL path, so the 44-character CESR SAID is used as is; no hash function or encoding is imposed. If a peer needs another form, the same 32 Blake3 bytes can be re-encoded (hex or base32), but a CID built from them would not verify under byte-hash semantics because a SAID is computed over the document with a placeholder.
- **Governance.** None for the catalog format; it would be the maintainer's own convention. The parts it leans on are governed: HTTPS and well-known URIs (IETF), KERI v1.1 (Trust over IP), and, if the ping is profiled on it, Webmention (W3C).
- **Publisher cost.** Static hosting of one JSON file and the definition files. An optional ping receiver is one function with near-zero idle cost. Ongoing effort: keeping peer lists current, which a command-line tool can do on each build.
- **TypeScript tooling.** Nothing existing for the catalog walk; it is fetch plus JSON plus a visited set. For signing later: signify-ts is the TypeScript KERI client (not re-checked today). The ping can reuse Webmention semantics, where the existing npm packages are small and stale.
- **Failure modes.** Islands: two groups that never linked never learn of each other, and the reader cannot tell "nobody else" from "nobody I can reach". Sparse early network: with a few dozen publishers and few links each, disconnection is likely. Stale peer lists and link rot. Poisoning: a catalog can list fake peers or point to slow hosts, so the walker needs budgets and must verify every claim at the claimant. Spam pings: mitigated by fetching the source and checking it declares H, by rate limits and later by signatures. Source offline: its backlink list is unavailable, though the bytes survive elsewhere. Privacy: "relies on H" is a public statement of a business dependency.
- **Needs a purpose-built aggregator?** partly. The walk needs nothing shared or purpose-built. But global enumeration and joining disconnected groups need a rendezvous the pattern does not supply, and a source that wants backlinks must accept pings (a tiny function, or a manual step).
- **Adoption evidence.**
  - As a catalog format it has no adopters; I found no prior art applying exactly this pattern (per-digest adopter lists percolated between publisher catalogs) to schemas, protocols or packages. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://apisjson.org/
  - KERI specification status "v1.1": "This provides what we call zero-trust percolated discovery or speedy percolated endpoint discovery (SPED)." (as of 2026-01-21). Source: https://trustoverip.github.io/kswg-keri-specification/
  - OOBI draft: "To clarify, the minimum information in an OOBI is the pair, (url, aid)"; OOBIs bootstrap "Percolated Information Discovery (PID)" which is "based on Invasion Percolation Theory"; the Data OOBI section is still "ToDo" in that draft. (as of 2023-07). Source: https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-ssmith-oobi-01.html
  - GLEIF's testnet schema server serves ACDC schemas at an HTTP GET endpoint "/oobi/{SAID}" (from a search summary of the GLEIF document, not fetched). (as of 2025-07-11). Source: https://www.gleif.org/media/pages/newsroom/events/gleif-vlei-hackathon-2025/1f5781d569-1764935127/2025-07-11-gleif-testnet-schema-server-documentation-v1.0._final.pdf
  - The draft's container, AI Catalog, already allows the link type: "Catalog entries can point to other catalogs, enabling hierarchical organization and multi-artifact packaging"; it is "Maintained by the Agent Card Working Group" under the Linux Foundation and names no adopters on its site. (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://ai-catalog.io/
  - Walk completeness evidence, web graph of 2000 (203 million pages): "the probability that any path exists from the source to the destination is only 24%"; a forward crawl from a random start "would either "die out" after reaching a small set of nodes" or explode to about 100 million; "Most (over 90%)" of nodes are in one weakly connected component. (as of 2000). Source: https://archives.iw3c2.org/www9/w9cdrom/160/160.html
  - Deployed percolation by peer lists: Mastodon's public GET /api/v1/instance/peers returns "Domains that this server is aware of." (as of 2026-11-08). Source: https://docs.joinmastodon.org/methods/instance/
- **Skeptic's re-score.** Trajectory 1 to 0: nothing I checked shows anyone converging on this format. no_aggregator 4 to 3: no shared index, but backlinks exist only where a source runs a ping receiver, and while the maintainer authors most definitions his receiver is the index. Reverse lookup 3 to 2: it is Webmention's 'ask the source' plus copied peer lists, so it deserves the same score; without pings a walk reaches ancestors only. publisher_effort 4 to 3: adopters need static files and a POST, sources need a public function that fetches arbitrary URLs. keri_fit 5 to 4: signing and OOBI links fit cleanly, but KERI specifies only the forward resolution of a SAID and existing KERI software has no SAID route or adopter listing.

### Claims the skeptic corrected (backlink-percolation)

The skeptic re-checked 23 claims; 14 held as stated. The rest:

- *partly* · W3C Webmention (with predecessors Pingback and Trackback) · claim: The reverse lookup is native as 'ask X's source', and webmention.io offers a public GET /api/mentions.jf2?target=&lt;URL&gt;.
  - Correction: Nothing about reading is native to Webmention: retrieval is a feature of one hosted service run by one person, keyed by a target URL on a domain whose owner signed in. Also, XRay searches an unrecognised document only as HTML (a, img, video, audio attributes); it walks a parsed tree only for formats it knows (microformats, JSON Feed, feeds). A bare JSON catalog as the source would most likely fail verification on webmention.io, so 'the adopter's catalog can be the source' holds for the spec and for a self-written receiver, not for the existing hosted one.
  - Checked: webmention.io README (target= example carries no token; domain= needs one), webmention.io home page ('Made by aaronpk', sign in with your website, no pricing or guarantees), and the source of XRay, the parser webmention.io uses to verify a source (lib/XRay/Parser.php).
- *partly* · ActivityPub (Announce, Like, replies; Fediverse Enhancement Proposals) · claim: Mastodon exposes only totals for likes and shares ('The actual activities are not exposed by Mastodon at this time'); hashtags are normalised to lowercase; a complete answer needs your own origin server or a crawler.
  - Correction: The quote is about the ActivityPub collections. Mastodon's own REST API does list backlinks held at the origin: GET /api/v1/statuses/:id/reblogged_by, /favourited_by and /context are public for public statuses; /quotes (since 4.5.0) needs a user token. There is even a URL-keyed timeline, GET /api/v1/timelines/link?url=, but only for a 'currently-trending article' and only from accounts that opted in to discovery. All of it is per origin, Mastodon-specific, and requires the definition to be a post on a fediverse server, so the conclusion stands.
  - Checked: https://docs.joinmastodon.org/spec/activitypub/ (both quotes present), plus the Mastodon client API pages for statuses and timelines.
- *partly* · ActivityPub (Announce, Like, replies; Fediverse Enhancement Proposals) · claim: Threads' fediverse integration is 'on maintenance mode' (Fediverse Report, 19 December 2025).
  - Correction: It is the report author's framing of an interview the Threads head gave to Alex Heath, not a direct quote and not a Meta statement.
  - Checked: https://connectedplaces.online/?p=4765: title 'Fediverse Report -#147', 19 December 2025, sentence present.
- *partly* · Secure Scuttlebutt and other gossip protocols (Radicle inventory gossip, BitTorrent peer exchange) · claim: Summary: no member of the family offers a query keyed by an arbitrary digest that existing third-party nodes answer.
  - Correction: True for the members the report covered. Two systems the report left out do answer such a query today: Nostr relays (NIP-01: single-letter tags 'are expected to be indexed by relays', filter key '#&lt;letter&gt;') and Constellation, a lexicon-agnostic backlink index for AT Protocol whose targets may be ordinary https URLs. See missed mechanisms; both have caveats I could not close.
  - Checked: Nostr NIP-01 mirrors and NIP-73 on GitHub; the Constellation service page for AT Protocol.
- *refuted* · schema.org and rel-based links that general crawlers follow (with VoID, APIs.json and FOAF as the catalog precedents) · claim: A unique 44-character token is a good exact-match search key in principle; general crawlers can give best-effort answers.
  - Correction: The exact-phrase search returned eight unrelated pages and nothing about the schema. Only after adding descriptive words (vLEI, schema, oobi) did the search find GitHub repositories and GLEIF documents, which means the digest was not the key. One search tool and one digest, but it is the only measurement anyone has made, and it failed.
  - Checked: A live test with a real KERI SAID that several independent parties publish: an exact-phrase web search for the vLEI Qualified vLEI Issuer schema SAID 'EBfdlu8R27Fbx-ehrqwImnK-8Cm79sqbAQ4MmvEAYqao'.
- *partly* · Percolating catalogs with an optional ping to the source (the reviewer's pattern) · claim: It is the KERI discovery model applied to definitions; KERI v1.1 defines percolated discovery and OOBIs for 'a given AID or SAID'.
  - Correction: KERI covers the forward direction only. The spec calls endpoints for items identified by SAID 'a secondary use case'; the IETF draft expired 28 January 2024 and its Data OOBI section is 'ToDo'; keripy registers /oobi, /oobi/{aid}, /oobi/{aid}/{role} and /oobi/{aid}/{role}/{eid} and no SAID route. Nothing in KERI enumerates who uses a SAID, so the reverse lookup is new design, not KERI applied.
  - Checked: KERI specification v1.1 at trustoverip.github.io (all four quotes present), draft-ssmith-oobi-01, and the route table in the local keripy checkout (/Users/seriouscoderone/code/KERI/keripy/src/keri/end/ending.py lines 631-634).
- *partly* · Percolating catalogs with an optional ping to the source (the reviewer's pattern) · claim: Static JSON only; the maintainer runs static hosting and 'optionally one small function'; the walk needs nothing shared or purpose-built.
  - Correction: Without the ping, an adopter's catalog links only upward ('from') and to whatever peers it copied on the day it adopted, so a walk reaches ancestors and misses every later adopter. The reverse lookup therefore depends on each source running a receiver that fetches arbitrary URLs, stores the result and rewrites its catalog, for as long as the definition is in use. That is a service, per source, and the report's own data (24% directed reachability) says the ping is not optional.
  - Checked: Traced the report's own walk step by step against its catalog fields (from, peers) and its ping.
- *partly* · Percolating catalogs with an optional ping to the source (the reviewer's pattern) · claim: No prior art applies this pattern to schemas, protocols or packages.
  - Correction: The exact combination keyed by a digest is new, but the shape is standardised elsewhere. OpenID Federation 1.0 (Final, 17 February 2026) has a signed statement at /.well-known/openid-federation, 'authority_hints' as upward links, a Subordinate Listing endpoint filterable by trust_mark_type, Trust Marks as signed conformance statements, and a Trust Marked Entities Listing that 'lists all the Entities for which Trust Marks have been issued and are still valid'. Semantic Pingback (2010) was typed backlink pings for linked data and did not spread.
  - Checked: OpenID Federation 1.0 final text, search results for Semantic Pingback (EKAW 2010), APIs.json format documents.
- *unverifiable* · All · claim: Smaller cited figures: BuiltWith 2,019 sites; Sucuri 162,000 sites in the 2014 pingback attack; Vouch has four implementations; Ghost 6.0 shipped ActivityPub; Mastodon peers API wording; GLEIF schema server path /oobi/{SAID}.
  - Checked: Not re-opened; none of them carries the conclusion. For the GLEIF point, a third-party repository found by search lists the vLEI schema OOBI as a static GitHub Pages URL ending in /oobi/&lt;SAID&gt;, which is consistent with the claim.

### Mechanisms the researcher missed (backlink-percolation)

- **OpenID Federation 1.0 (signed entity statements, authority hints, listing endpoints, Trust Marks).** It is the reviewer's pattern already standardised: a signed statement at /.well-known/openid-federation, upward links (authority_hints), a Subordinate Listing endpoint filterable by trust_mark_type, Trust Marks as signed statements of conformance (a federation MAY allow self-signed ones), and a Trust Marked Entities Listing that returns every entity holding a given mark. Status Final, 17 February 2026; production use in Italy's national identity federations, an eduGAIN pilot from July 2025, nine implementations at the February 2026 interop, and a TypeScript implementation (@oidfed, quality not checked). It removes the 'no standards body, no prior art' part of the conclusion and shows the maintainer what the mature form costs: the listing endpoints are still run by the issuer, so it does not improve value 1, and it signs with JOSE keys bound to HTTPS identifiers, not KERI. Source: https://openid.net/specs/openid-federation-1_0.html ; https://oidfed.pages.dev/ecosystem
- **Nostr relays (signed events queried by tag).** It contradicts the report's summary that nothing here answers a value-keyed query on existing third-party nodes. An adopter publishes one signed event carrying H in a single-letter tag; any relay answers a filter on that tag, because NIP-01 says such tags 'are expected to be indexed by relays'. Relays are run by many independent parties and keys are self-held. Caveats I could not close: NIP-73 (external content ids, still draft and optional) has no generic hash type, so this would be a custom event kind; relay policies on unknown kinds, retention and spam vary; the only relay counts I found are from 2023 (600 to 712) and activity figures are secondhand (144,202 daily active pubkeys, late 2025). Keys are secp256k1 Schnorr, not KERI AIDs. It probably belongs to a sibling family, but it is the nearest thing to the 'small generic rendezvous' the brief asked about. Source: https://github.com/nostr-protocol/nips/blob/master/01.md ; https://github.com/nostr-protocol/nips/blob/master/73.md
- **AT Protocol: lexicon publication, relay listReposByCollection, and the Constellation backlink index.** AT Protocol already publishes schemas the way MAD wants to ('Lexicon schemas are published publicly as records in atproto repositories', authority resolved by a _lexicon DNS TXT record), relays answer 'which repositories hold records of collection X' (com.atproto.sync.listReposByCollection), and Constellation is a running, lexicon-agnostic backlink index: 19,676,489,819 links to 4,344,884,851 targets over 649 days, with targets that may be ordinary https URLs. A claim record in a custom collection whose field holds a URL ending in H would be findable by anyone with no new service. Caveats: Constellation is one volunteer project, most relay capacity is Bluesky's, registered users are about 43 to 44 million with monthly actives near 10.4 million and falling (secondary sources), and identity is DID-based, not KERI. Also a sibling family, and also an aggregator, but a generic one that exists. Source: https://atproto.com/specs/lexicon ; https://constellation.microcosm.blue/ ; https://docs.bsky.app/docs/api/com-atproto-sync-list-repos-by-collection
- **Semantic Pingback (typed backlink pings for linked data, 2010).** Direct prior art for the ping half of the pattern: a resource advertises a service, the linker pings it with a typed RDF link, and the target records who refers to it and how. It was implemented in OntoWiki and Triplify, was later absorbed into Linked Data Notifications, and never spread beyond its authors' tools. It is evidence that this exact idea has been tried for vocabularies and data and did not reach adoption, which supports the objection rather than rescuing the pick. Source: https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-16438-5_10 ; https://lists.w3.org/Archives/Public/public-lod/2010Mar/0107.html
- **General web search and code search as a zero-build rendezvous (tested).** The report left untested the one generic rendezvous that needs nothing built: search for the digest string. I tested it with a real vLEI schema SAID that GLEIF and several GitHub repositories publish, and an exact-phrase search returned only unrelated pages. If it had worked, the island problem would have had a free answer inside this family; it did not, in one test with one search tool. GitHub code search was not tested and needs a signed-in account. Source: Exact-phrase web search for "EBfdlu8R27Fbx-ehrqwImnK-8Cm79sqbAQ4MmvEAYqao" run on 2026-11-08

### What the researcher could not verify (backlink-percolation)

- My fetch tool returns short excerpts chosen by a summarising model; every 'verbatim' quote is what that tool reported as verbatim, and I could not view raw pages. KERI spec quotes were limited to about 125 characters each.
- BuiltWith's webmention.io count (about 2,019 live sites, mid-2025) comes from a search snippet; the BuiltWith page was blocked by a human test. I found no dated census of Webmention-enabled sites.
- WordPress.org install counts ('900+' for Webmention, '6,000+' for ActivityPub) are as the fetch tool reported them; I could not cross-check them.
- Threads federation figures: only low-quality secondary pages gave numbers (for example 18.2 million federated profiles); I did not use them. The 'maintenance mode' status is from Fediverse Report #147, quoting the Threads head secondhand.
- Whether the W3C working group published its Q3 2026 revisions of ActivityPub and Activity Streams.
- The full FEP index (Codeberg served an anti-scraper page and the README table was too long); I verified only FEP-8b32, FEP-ef61, FEP-7888 and FEP-044f individually. Other relevant proposals (object links, conversation backfill, relays) were not read.
- Whether webmention.io accepts a JSON document as a source; its README does not say.
- Secure Scuttlebutt active-user counts and the maintenance state of its JavaScript libraries; Radicle network size.
- COAR Notify's notification pattern list and inbox discovery text (the specification index page did not show them); the 16 implementations are logos on the home page, not a census.
- The 2014 pingback DDoS figure (162,000 sites) is from press coverage of Sucuri's post, not Sucuri's page itself.
- The GLEIF '/oobi/{SAID}' schema endpoint is from a search summary of a GLEIF PDF I did not fetch.
- ads.txt and sellers.json adoption numbers (a bilateral-declaration precedent) and Linked Open Vocabularies statistics (the page returned 404); I left both out of the evidence.
- Whether APIs.json has an 'include' property for linking catalogs; the home page does not mention it.
- No evidence was found that AI agents or their tooling read Webmention, ActivityPub collections, LDN inboxes or OPML; this is absence of evidence from a limited search, not a proof.
- TypeScript tooling for LDN and WebSub was not checked; signify-ts was not re-checked today.
- The CESR decoding of a 44-character Blake3-256 SAID to 32 raw bytes, the original January 2018 date of WebSub, and the random-graph connectivity reasoning behind 'islands are likely in a sparse early network' are from prior knowledge, not fetched today.
- How web search engines tokenize a Base64url digest containing '-' or '_', and whether they index JSON at well-known paths, was not tested.
